For those days are in the far distance which I myself witnessed, when men like Aurispa and Guarino went out to Greece as to a storehouse, and came back laden with manuscripts which every scholar was eager to borrow—and, be it owned with shame, not always willing to restore. Nay, even the days when erudite Greeks flocked to our shores for refuge seem far-off now—farther off than the on-coming of my blindness. But doubtless, young man, research after the treasures of antiquity was not alien to the purpose of your travels?"

"Assuredly not," said Tito. "On the contrary, my companion—my father—was willing to risk his life in his zeal for the discovery of inscriptions and other traces of ancient civilization."

"And I trust there is a record of his researches and their results," Bardo said eagerly, "since they must be even more precious than those of Ciriaco, which I have diligently availed myself of, though they are not always illuminated by adequate learning."

"There *was* such a record," Tito replied, "but it was lost, like everything else, in the shipwreck I suffered below Ancona. The only record left is such as remains in our—in my memory."

"You must lose no time in committing it to paper, young man," Bardo said, his interest growing. "Doubtless you remember much, if you aided in transcription; for when I was your age, words wrought themselves into my mind as if fixed by the tool of the graver; wherefore I constantly marvel at the capriciousness of my daughter's memory, which grasps certain objects with tenacity and lets fall all those minutiae whereon depends accuracy, the very soul of scholarship. But I apprehend no such danger with you, young man, if your will has seconded the advantages of your training."

When Bardo made this reference to his daughter, Tito ventured to turn his eyes toward her. At the accusation against her memory, his face broke into its brightest smile, which was reflected as inevitably as sudden sunbeams in Romola's. Conceive the soothing delight of that smile to her! Romola had never dreamed that there was a scholar in the world who would smile at a deficiency for which she was constantly made to feel herself a culprit. It was like the dawn of a new sense to her—the sense of comradeship. They did not look away from each other immediately, as if the smile had been stolen; they looked and smiled with frank enjoyment.

"She is not really so cold and proud," Tito thought.

"Does *he* forget too, I wonder?" Romola thought, "Yet I hope not, else he will vex my father."

But Tito was obliged to turn away to answer Bardo's question. "I have had much practice in transcription," he said. "But in the case of inscriptions copied in memorable scenes, rendered doubly impressive by the sense of risk and adventure, it may have happened that my retention of written characters has been weakened. On the plain of the Eurotas, or among the gigantic stones of Mycenae and Tyrins—especially when the fear of the Turk hovers over one like a vulture—the mind wanders, even though the hand writes faithfully what the eye dictates. But something doubtless I have retained," he added with a modesty that was not false, though he was conscious it was politic, "something that might be of service if illustrated and corrected by a wider learning than my own."

"That is well spoken, young man," Bardo said, delighted. "And I will not withhold from you such aid as I can give, if you like to communicate with me concerning your recollections. I foresee a work which will be a useful supplement to the 'Isolario' of Christoforo Buondelmonte, and which may take rank with the 'Itineraria' of Ciriaco and the admirable Ambrogio Traversari. But we must prepare ourselves for calumny, young man," Bardo went on with energy, as if the work were already growing so fast that the time of trial was near; "if your book contains novelties you will be charged with forgery; if my elucidations should clash with any principles of interpretation adopted by another scholar, our personal characters will be attacked, we shall be impeached with foul actions; you must prepare yourself to be told that your mother was a fish-woman, and that your father was a renegade priest or a hanged malefactor. I myself, for having shown error in a single preposition, had an invective written against me wherein I was taxed with treachery, fraud, indecency, and even hideous crimes. Such, my young friend—such are the flowers with which the glorious path of scholarship is st

Bartolommeo Scala, the secretary of the Florentine Republic, resided in a stately palace near the Porta Pinti, now known as the Casa Gherardesca. His coat of arms, an azure ladder crossing a golden field with the motto *Gradatim* above the gate, declared to all who passed that the miller's son had climbed to his high station solely through his own exertions—a truth he wore with unashamed pride. The secretary was a man of vanity and pomp, yet fundamentally honest; he believed so deeply in his own worth that he saw no need for pretense. By this time, he had ascended to the very top rung of his ladder. For twenty years he had served as secretary, delivering his orations from the *ringhiera*, the balcony of the Old Palace, before princely guests while the Marzocco, the lion of the republic, wore a golden crown and the populace shouted, "Viva Messer Bartolommeo!" He had served as an ambassador to Rome, earning titles such as Titular Senator, Apostolical Secretary, and Knight of the Golden Spur. Eight years prior, he had become Gonfaloniere, the ultimate ambition of any Florentine citizen. Along with his honors, his wealth had grown, and so had his gout, a common affliction of successful mortals. Often, the Knight of the Golden Spur was forced to sit with his cushioned heel helpless beneath the magnificent loggia he had constructed, overlooking the vast gardens behind his home.

It was on such a day that he granted the requested audience to Tito Melema. The May sun warmed the flowers and grass beyond the pleasant shade of the loggia. Scala had discarded his heavy silk *lucco* for a light, loose mantle over his tunic. Seated beside him were his beautiful daughter, Alessandra, and her husband, the Greek soldier-poet Marullo. On the opposite side sat two friends, distinguished enough to be respectable but not so illustrious as to dominate the conversation, making them ideal listeners. Yet, despite the gout and the sunny afternoon, Messer Bartolommeo's felicity was far from complete. It was embittered by a pile of papers before him, mostly a correspondence between himself and the scholar Poliziano.

It was a peculiar foible of that era—incredible as it may seem today—to recite quarrels and offer scholarly visitors the full text of lengthy disputes. This was not the first time Scala had sought the candid opinions of his friends regarding the balance of right and wrong in a dozen Latin letters exchanged with Poliziano, all stemming from playful epigrams. It was the tale of a typical, petty quarrel, one that served precisely as the thorn necessary to prick the sluggish horse of friendship, as Nello had once noted.

Poliziano, having been a rejected suitor for the hand of Scala's daughter, kept a sharp, learned tooth ready against the prosperous secretary who had refused to marry the greatest scholar of the age to his daughter. Scala was a meritorious public servant and a lucky man—qualities naturally exasperating to an offended academic. However, Scala also possessed an itch for authorship, albeit a poor one. He was one of those delightful individuals who, sitting in gout-ridden slippers, penned poetic trifles purely for amusement, sending them to friends who acted as the literary periodicals of the fifteenth century. Scala had many friends eager to praise his work, such as Ficino and Landino, who found his Latin prose elegant and masculine. The terrifying Joseph Scaliger, who would later declare him totally ignorant of Latinity, was still a century away. But when does the coquetry of superfluous authorship ever find contentment with the ready praise of friends? That critical, haughty Poliziano—a fellow-browser who was far from amiable—had to be shown that the solid secretary possessed a fertile mind for verse, hinting at the potential greatness he might achieve if he were not so consumed by affairs.

What an ineffable moment it is when the man you secretly hate sends you a Latin epigram with a false gender, hendecasyllables with a questionable elision, or poetic figures that are manifest solecisms. That moment had arrived for Poliziano. The secretary had poked his soft head out of the official shell, and the terrible lurking crab descended upon him. Poliziano had used the freedom of a friend to correct Scala's mistake in gendering the *culex* (the mosquito, ubiquitous on the banks of the Arno) as feminine. Scala replied with a bad joke in suitable Latin verses, referencing Poliziano's unsuccessful suit. Poliziano found the verses charming and facetious, though he pitied the fact that they were seriously incorrect. Since Scala claimed to have written them imitating a Greek epigram, Poliziano, on such friendly terms, enclosed a Greek epigram of his own on the same insect. It contained a lively conceit about Venus, Cupid, and the mosquito, based on the zoological fact that the gnat, like Venus, was born from the waters.

In reply, Scala begged to state that his verses were never intended for a scholar with such delicate olfactory senses as Poliziano, the nearest living man to the perfection of the ancients, whose taste was so fastidious that even sturgeon would seem insipid to him. He defended his verses, noting they were written hastily without correction, intended merely as a distraction during the summer heat for himself and friends satisfied with mediocrity. Unlike others who courted publicity through booksellers, Scala was content to keep his work private. Furthermore, he admitted he barely had enough Greek to understand the sense of the epigram Poliziano had sent, let alone taste its elegance. Yet, the epigram was Poliziano's; what more needed to be said? Still, as a postscript, he feared Poliziano's comparison of the gnat to Venus, due to its watery origin, was problematic. Venus might be offended; on the other hand, unless the poet alluded to the doctrine of Thales, the cold, damp origin seemed doubtful for a creature so fond of warmth. Perhaps a fish was a better comparison, or, regarding flight, an eagle; considering the darkness, a bat or an owl would be a less obscure parallel.

Here was a golden opportunity for Poliziano. He wrote that he was unaware Scala had any question of sturgeon, but rather of frogs and gudgeons. He made short work of Scala's defense of his Latin and mangled him terribly over the stupid criticisms he had ventured on the Greek epigram kindly forwarded as a model. These were wretched cavils! Regarding the damp origin of the gnat, there was the authority of Virgil himself, who called it the "alumnus of the waters." As for Scala's comments on the fish, the eagle, and the rest, it was *nihil ad rem*; just because an eagle could fly higher did not mean the gnat could not fly at all. Poliziano was ashamed to dwell on such trivialities and swell a gnat into an elephant, but he added that he had nothing deceitful or double about him, nor was he to be caught by the false blandishments of those who slandered him in his absence. He agreed with a Homeric sentiment on this head, furnishing a Greek quotation to serve as powder for his bullet.

The quarrel could not end there. The logic grew harder to follow, but the secretary became more pompously self-asserting, while the scholarly poet's temper turned more venomous. Poliziano had been generously willing to hold up a mirror, hoping the too-inflated secretary, beholding his own likeness, might cease defending his ignorant Latin against ancient authorities confirmed by centuries of consent. Unless, indeed, he designed to sink in literature as he rose in honors, so that men of letters might feel themselves his equals. In return, Poliziano was begged to examine Scala's writings; nowhere would he find a more devout admiration of antiquity. The secretary was ashamed of the age in which he lived and blushed for it. Some wanted their works exalted to the level of divine monuments, but Scala could not oblige them. As for the honors offensive to the envious, they had been well earned, witnessed by his life since coming to Florence in penury. The elegant scholar replied that he was not surprised Scala found the Age distasteful, since he himself was so distasteful to the Age. Indeed, he had accurately called Scala a "branny monster," formed from the off-scourings of monsters, born amidst the refuse of a mill, and eminently worthy of the long-eared office of turning paternal millstones (*in pistrinis sordibus natus et quidem pistrino dignissimus*).

It was not without reference to Tito's appointed visit that these papers were brought out that day. Here was a new Greek scholar whose accomplishments were to be tested, and on nothing did Scala more desire a dispassionate opinion from persons of superior knowledge than on that Greek epigram of Poliziano's. After sufficient introductory talk concerning Tito's travels, a survey of the gems, and an easy transition from the lamented Lorenzo's eagerness for ancient art to classical tastes in general, it was inevitable to mention Poliziano. He was a man of eminent ability, indeed, but a little too arrogant—assuming to be a Hercules whose office was to destroy all literary monstrosities of the age, writing letters to his elders without signing them as if they were miraculous revelations. Were not his own criticisms often questionable and his tastes perverse? He was fond of saying pungent things about men who thought they wrote like Cicero because they ended every sentence with "esse videtur," yet while boasting of freedom from servile imitation, did he not fall into the other extreme, chasing strange words and affected phrases? Even in his much-lauded *Miscellanea*, was every point tenable? Tito, who had just looked into the *Miscellanea*, found so much agreeable to the secretary that he showed himself quite worthy to be made a judge in the notable correspondence concerning the *culex*. Here was the Greek epigram which Poliziano undoubtedly thought the finest in the world, though he had pretended to believe the "transmarini," the Greeks themselves, would make light of it. Had he not been unintentionally speaking the truth in his false modesty?

Tito was ready and scarified the epigram to Scala's content. Oh, wise young judge! He could doubtless appreciate satire even in the vulgar tongue. Scala, an excellent man who never sought publicity through booksellers, was never unprovided with "hasty uncorrected trifles" as a sort of sherbet for a visitor on a hot day, or a cordial if the weather were cold. He had a few little matters in the shape of Sonnets turning on well-known foibles of Poliziano's, which he would not like to pursue further but which might amuse the company.

Enough: Tito took his leave under an urgent invitation to return. His gems were interesting, especially the agate with the *lusus naturae* inside—a wonderful semblance of Cupid riding a lion—and the "Jew's stone," with a lion-headed serpent encased within. Scala agreed to buy both; the latter as a reinforcement of his preventives against the gout, which gave him such severe twinges that it was plain he needed rings of rare virtue and an amulet worn close under the right breast. But Tito was assured that he himself was more interesting than his gems. He had won his way to the Scala Palace by the recommendation of Bardo de' Bardi, who was indeed Scala's old acquaintance and a worthy scholar, despite his tendency to overvalue himself—a frequent foible in the secretary's friends. Yet, he must come again on the ground of his own manifest accomplishments.

The interview could hardly have ended more auspiciously for Tito. As he walked out at the Porta Pinti, laughing lightly at the affair of the *culex*, he felt that fortune could hardly mean to turn her back on him again at present, since she had taken him by the hand in this decided way.

A Face in the Crowd.

To the northern soul, the dawn of Midsummer is merely a season of dew upon the grass, fresh shoots among the oaks, and a view of the shorn meadow. They rise to greet the Nativity of Saint John the Baptist with the casual indifference of men who measure time by the turning of the wheel rather than the turning of the page. Not so the Florentine, and not so the Florentine of the fifteenth century. To him, the eastern sun breaking over the Arno possessed a specific, electric quality; the bells did not merely ring—they articulated a declaration, announcing the great summer festival of Florence, the day of San Giovanni.

San Giovanni had been the patron of Florence for eight centuries, a lineage stretching back to the Lombard Queen Theodolinda, and according to the chronicler Villani, perhaps even to the days of Constantine and Sylvester. In those ancient times, the Florentines had been so prudent that they did not discard their old idol, Mars, with contempt. Instead, they had consecrated their temple to God and the "Beato Messere Santo Giovanni" while placing Mars respectfully upon a high tower near the river, fearing that if the god of the spear and shield were broken, the city itself would suffer mutation and ruin. But by the fifteenth century, such superstitious caution had vanished. The defences of the Republic were no longer found in the spear and shield, but in craft and coffers. For spear and shield could be hired with gold florins, and on every gold florin, the image of San Giovanni shone.

Much good had come to Florence since that struggle between the old patron and the new. There had been the inevitable quarrels and bloodshed between Guelf and Ghibelline, Black and White, orthodox and heretic; there had been floods, famine, and pestilence. Yet, amidst the chaos, there had been immense wealth and glory. Florence had conquered walled cities mightier than itself, notably Pisa, whose marble buildings and honored masts were now overshadowed by the Florentine name. That name had grown prouder in the courts of Europe and Africa, built upon purest gold coinage, finest dyes, pre-eminent scholarship, and wits of the most serviceable sort for statesmanship and banking. A Pope with a turn for epigram had once called Florentines "the fifth element." And for this high destiny, though it might partly depend on the stars and the Madonna dell'Impruneta, the praise was largely due to San Giovanni, whose image crowned the fair gold florins.

Therefore, it was fitting that the day of San Giovanni should be a day of peculiar rejoicing, ushered in by a vigil kept in strict old Florentine fashion. This vigil involved dancing, street jesting, perhaps some stone-throwing and window-breaking, but above all, sights provided by a city that employed a clever engineer named Cecca. By Cecca's hand, the saints, surrounded by almond-shaped glory and floating clouds with winged cherubs, seemed to bring a piece of heaven down into the narrow streets. More wonderfully, saints of gigantic size, with attendant angels, moved in a slow, mysterious manner along the thoroughfares, like a procession of colossal figures descended from the high domes. The clouds were woven of fine stuff; the saints were mortals supported by firm bars; and those mysterious giants were men of steady brains balancing themselves on stilts, enlarged by huge masks and stuffed shoulders. To the unimaginative Florentine, this was impiety, for in the images of sacred things lay some of the virtue of the sacred things themselves. Yet, the Tuscan mind slipped from the devout to the burlesque as readily as water round an angle. After the saints had done their turn before the gates of San Giovanni, a company of merry black demons, armed with claws and thongs, would perform impromptu farces of bastinadoing and clothes-tearing. That was their way of keeping vigil, and they, too, had descended from the domes.

On the morrow, the great day, the tributary symbols paid by all dependent cities and villages were offered at the shrine of San Giovanni in the octagonal baptistery. Every Florentine, from the white-haired man to the lisping child, would be clothed in their best to honor the great sight. When the sun sloped and the streets cooled, there would be the glorious race or *Corso*, where unsaddled horses, clothed in rich trappings, would run from the Porta al Prato to the Porta Santa Croce. There, the richest *Palii*—velvet and brocade banners with silk linings and gold fringe—would await the winner on a triumphal car. And thereafter, more dancing, weddings, and grand gatherings, with piping, music, and song, so much gladness and ornament that the earth might be mistaken for Paradise.

In this year of 1492, however, it was perhaps a little less easy to make that mistake. Lorenzo the Magnificent was dead, replaced by an arrogant and incautious Piero, an evil change for Florence unless, indeed, the wise horse prefers the bad rider, as he is more easily thrown. Regrets for Lorenzo were giving way to murmured desires for a broader government, one where corruption might be arrested and free play given to everyone's jealousy and ambition. Lorenzo was dead; Pope Innocent was dying; a troublesome Neapolitan succession and an intriguing Milan threatened to set Italy by the ears. The times were likely to be difficult. Still, there was all the more reason for the Republic to keep its religious festivals.

Midsummer morning in 1492 was not less bright than usual. Betimes, the symbolic offerings were assembled at the Piazza della Signoria, where stood the massive Palazzo Vecchio and the spacious Loggia built by Orcagna. The sky made a fairest blue tent, and the bells swung so vigorously that every evil spirit with sense enough to be formidable must have taken flight. Windows and terraced roofs were alive with human faces; sombre stone houses were bright with hanging draperies. The boldly soaring palace tower, the older square tower of the Bargello, and the spire of the neighboring Badia seemed to keep watch above. Below, on the broad polygonal flags of the piazza, was the glorious show of banners, horses with rich trappings, and gigantic *ceri*, or tapers, fitly called towers—strangely aggrandized descendants of the torches by whose faint light the Church worshipped in the Catacombs.

But where the procession was to pause, the magnificent city had provided another tent than the sky. The whole of the Piazza del Duomo, from the octagonal baptistery to the façade of the cathedral, was covered at a height of forty feet or more with blue drapery adorned with yellow lilies and coats of arms. Sheaves of many-colored banners drooped beneath this superincumbent blue, creating a gorgeous, rainbow-lit shelter for the waiting spectators.

One of these spectators was Tito Melema. Bright, in the midst of brightness, he sat at the window of the room above Nello's shop. His right elbow rested on the red drapery hanging from the window-sill, his head supported in a backward position by the right hand, which pressed the curls against his ear. His face wore that bland liveliness, far removed from excitability or gloom, which marks the companion popular alike amongst men and women—the companion who is never obtrusive or noisy from uneasy vanity, and whose brow is never contracted by resentment. He showed no other change from the two months since his first appearance in the weather-stained tunic and hose than an added radiance of good fortune, like the just-perceptible perfecting of a flower after it has drunk a morning's sunbeams.

Close behind him stood the slim figure of Nello in holiday suit, and at his left the younger Cennini—Pietro, the erudite corrector of proof-sheets, not Domenico the practical. Tito looked alternately down on the scene below and upward at the knot of gazers around him. Piero di Cosimo was raising a laugh among them by his grimaces and anathemas at the noise of the bells, declaring that he would bury himself in the most solitary spot of the Valdarno if he were not condemned, as a painter, to lie in wait for the secrets of color caught from the floating of banners.

Tito had just turned his laughing face away from the whimsical painter to look down at the small drama among the checkered border of spectators when, at the angle of the marble steps in front of the Duomo, nearly opposite Nello's shop, he saw a man's face upturned towards him. It fixed on him a gaze that seemed to have more meaning than the ordinary passing observation of a stranger. It was a face with a tonsured head, rising above the black mantle and white tunic of a Dominican friar—a common sight in Florence. But the glance had something peculiar in it for Tito. There was a faint suggestion, certainly not of an unpleasant kind. Yet, what pleasant association had he ever had with monks? None. The glance and the suggestion hardly took longer than a flash of lightning.

"Nello!" said Tito, hastily, but immediately added, in a tone of disappointment, "Ah, he has turned round. It was that tall, thin friar who is going up the steps. I wanted you to tell me if you knew aught of him?"

"One of the Frati Predicatori," said Nello, carelessly; "you don't expect me to know the private history of the crows."

"I seem to remember something about his face," said Tito. "It is an uncommon face."

"What? You thought it might be our Fra Girolamo? Too tall; and he never shows himself in that chance way."

"Besides, that loud-barking 'hound of the Lord' is not in Florence just now," said Francesco Cei, the popular poet; "he has taken Piero de' Medici's hint to carry his railing prophecies on a journey for a while."

"The Frate neither rails nor prophesies against any man," said a middle-aged personage seated at the other corner of the window; "he only prophesies against vice. If you think that an attack on your poems, Francesco, it is not the Frate's fault."

"Ah, he's gone into the Duomo now," said Tito, who had watched the figure eagerly. "No, I was not under that mistake, Nello. Your Fra Girolamo has a high nose and a large under-lip. I saw him once—he is not handsome; but this man..."

"Truce to your descriptions!" said Cennini. "Hark! See! Here come the horsemen and the banners. That standard, carried on the horse with white trappings—that with the red eagle holding the green dragon between his talons, and the red lily over the eagle—is the Gonfalon of the Guelf party, and those cavaliers close round it are the chief officers of the Guelf party. That is one of our proudest banners, grumble as we may; it means the triumph of the Guelfs, which means the triumph of Florentine will, which means triumph of the popolani."

"Nay, go on, Cennini," said the middle-aged man, "which means triumph of the fat popolani over the lean, which again means triumph of the fattest popolano over those who are less fat."

"Cronaca, you are becoming sententious," said the printer. "Fra Girolamo's preaching will spoil you, and make you take life by the wrong handle. Trust me, your cornices will lose half their beauty if you begin to mingle bitterness with them; that is the *maniera Tedesca* which you used to declaim against when you came from Rome."

"That is a goodly show of cavaliers," said Tito, who had learned by this time the best way to please Florentines; "but are there not strangers among them? I see foreign costumes."

"Assuredly," said Cennini; "you see there the Orators from France, Milan, and Venice, and behind them are English and German nobles; for it is customary that all foreign visitors of distinction pay their tribute to San Giovanni in the train of that gonfalon. For my part, I think our Florentine cavaliers sit their horses as well as any of those cut-and-thrust northerners, whose wits lie in their heels and saddles; and for yon Venetian, I fancy he would feel himself more at ease on the back of a dolphin. We ought to know something of horsemanship, for we excel all Italy in the sports of the Giostra, and the money we spend on them. But you will see a finer show of our chief men by-and-by, Melema; my brother himself will be among the officers of the Zecca."

"The banners are the better sight," said Piero di Cosimo, forgetting the noise in his delight at the winding stream of color as the tributary standards advanced. "The Florentine men are so-so; they make but a sorry show at this distance with their patch of sallow flesh-tint above the black garments; but those banners with their velvet, and satin, and minever, and brocade, and their endless play of delicate light and shadow!—*Va*! your human talk and doings are a tame jest; the only passionate life is in form and colour."

"Ay, Piero, if Satanasso could paint, thou wouldst sell thy soul to learn his secrets," said Nello. "But there is little likelihood of it, seeing the blessed angels themselves are such poor hands at chiaroscuro, if one may judge from their *capo-d'opera*, the Madonna Nunziata."

"There go the banners of Pisa and Arezzo," said Cennini. "Ay, Messer Pisano, it is no use for you to look sullen; you may as well carry your banner to our San Giovanni with a good grace. 'Pisans false, Florentines blind'—the second half of that proverb will hold no longer. There come the ensigns of our subject towns and signories, Melema; they will all be suspended in San Giovanni until this day next year, when they will give place to new ones."

"They are a fair sight," said Tito; "

A Man's Ransom.

The Piazza was alive with the heat of the midday sun, and Tito moved swiftly through the throng, his steps carrying him from the square toward the Corso degli Adimari. Though he offered only a casual answer to Nello's inquiry regarding his recent encounter, a quiet longing stirred within him. As he navigated the crowd, his mind drifted briefly to the pair of blue eyes he had seen gazing up from beneath the white linen hood of a country woman at the festival. He knew well that face belonged to Tessa, yet he chose silence. Why should Nello concern himself with such trivialities? Tito possessed a natural aptitude for reticence, a talent that operated without conscious intent. Like many who find concealment effortless, he sometimes hid things that required no hiding at all, such as the mere sight of a flock of crows.

However, this fleeting thought of Tessa was quickly overshadowed by a more persistent memory: the face of the friar, pale and fanatical, who had stared at him with an intensity that felt irrecoverably linked to his own past. Why had that sickly figure singled him out? Where, in all his travels, had he seen such a visage before? It was folly to dwell on such vague, cobweb-like impressions; they demanded to be swept away. By the time he turned from the Corso into a narrow side street, his thoughts had shifted entirely. He cared only that the sun burned high and that the procession had delayed him from his intended visit to the room in the Via de' Bardi. He knew his arrival was anxiously awaited there.

In his imagination, he rehearsed the scene of his entrance: the blind man's face, usually obscured, glowing with a diffuse joy like light filtering through a translucent lamp; the faint, transient flush on Romola's cheeks and neck, which added a delicate, feminine charm to her inherent majesty, enhanced by a boyish frankness in her gaze and smile. Together, they were the finest companions, circling the blind man's chair. She appealed to him constantly, and he informed her, yet he felt a strange subjugation to Romola, bowing before the simplicity of her nature. For the first time, he experienced a loving awe in the presence of noble womanhood—a reverence akin to the ancient worship of a great goddess, whose power was deeper and more primordial than mere knowledge. They had never been alone, and he could conjure no probable image of love between them. He could only imagine wildly, though he knew it impossible, that one day Romola would speak her love.

He recalled a day in Greece, leaning against a sun-drenched wall, when a small peasant girl with dark eyes had crept closer until she shyly asked for a kiss, offering her round olive cheek with innocent trust. Tito was accustomed to love arriving in such unsought ways. But Romola's love would never come thus; would it ever come at all? Yet, it remained the highest apple upon which his mind had fixed itself. In his fresh youth, though not yet passionate, he was deeply impressionable. It was as inevitable that he should feel love for Romola as it was for white irises to reflect in a sunlit stream. He possessed no vanity, yet he held an intimate sense that she stood far above him. Many men have felt this same distance before a large-eyed, simple child.

Nevertheless, Tito had achieved rapid success. Some men might have grown presuming, or at least entertained the hope of becoming Romola's husband, or even convinced her father, Bartolommeo Scala, of such a future. His first meeting with the secretary had indeed marked the beginning of growing favor. Politian, professor of both Greek and Latin in Florence, found himself without a rival since the departure of Demetrio Calcondila to Milan. Friends wished to teach the renowned scholar humility, and it was agreed that Tito should be supported in a Greek chair, following the precedent set by Lorenzo the Magnificent for Calcondila. There was nothing invidious in this arrangement, only a zeal for true learning and the education of Florentine youth.

Tito sailed under the fairest breeze. He convinced fair judges that his talents matched his fortune, wearing his good luck with such ease and lack of pretense that no offense was taken. He was destined for the best Florentine society, a circle where fine plate abounded and the Signory permitted the wearing of rich brocade. Where else would a handsome young scholar be welcomed, one who could play the lute and sing a gay song? His bright face, easy smile, and liquid voice seemed to bring a holiday to the city, making the work-worn and the sad ashamed of their own gloom. He was a professor likely to render the Greek classics amiable to the sons of great houses.

This was not all his fortune; he had sold his jewels, keeping only one ring, and now held five hundred gold florins. Yet, the moment he first possessed this sum marked the crisis of the first serious struggle his facile, good-humored nature had ever known. An importunate thought, previously seen only as a shadow, suddenly grasped him. He was forced to pause and decide: would he surrender to duty, or offer a refusal with irrevocable consequences?

It was in the room above Nello's shop, which Tito now rented, that the elder Cennini handed him the final portion of the funds on behalf of Bernardo Rucellai, the buyer of the two precious gems.

"Ecco, giovane mio!" the respectable printer and goldsmith declared. "You now possess a pretty little fortune. If you will take my advice, let me place your florins in a safe quarter where they may increase, rather than slipping through your fingers for banquets and follies common among our Florentine youth. It has become the fashion of scholars, especially when, like our Pietro Crinito, they believe their scholarship needs scenting and embroidery, to squander with one hand until they must beg with the other. I have brought you the money, and you are free to make a wise or unwise choice; I shall see which way the balance tips. We Florentines hold no man a member of an Art until he has shown his skill and been matriculated; and no man is matriculated to the art of life until he has been well tempted. If you decide to put your florins out to usury, let me know tomorrow. A scholar may marry, and should have something in readiness for the *morgen-cap*. Addio."

As Cennini closed the door, Tito turned, the smile fading from his face, and fixed his eyes on the table where the florins lay. He made no other movement, standing with his thumbs in his belt, looking down in a transfixed state, his consciousness concentrated on some inward image.

"A man's ransom!" Who had said that five hundred florins was more than a man's ransom? If, under this midday sun on some hot coast far away, a man somewhat aged, yet possessing high thoughts and a passionate heart—a man who had once rescued a boy from beggary and cruelty, raising him tenderly as a father—were now toiling as a slave, hewing wood and drawing water, perhaps struck down because he lacked agility? If that man were thinking, "Tito will find me; he need only carry our manuscripts and gems to Venice; he will raise money and never rest until he finds me"? If that were certain, could Tito, seeing the price of the gems before him, say, "I will stay in Florence, fanned by soft airs of promised love and prosperity; I will not risk myself for his sake"? No, surely not, *if it were certain*.

But nothing was certain. The galley had been captured by a Turkish vessel on its way to Delos, known only by the report of the companion galley that had escaped. There had been resistance, probable bloodshed, and a man seen falling overboard. Who were the survivors, and what had befallen them amidst the multitude of possibilities? Had not Tito himself suffered shipwreck and narrowly escaped drowning? He had good cause to feel the omnipresence of casualties threatening all projects with futility. Rumors of pirate settlements in Delos were unreliable. What would likely happen if he left Florence for Venice, obtained authoritative letters, and set out for the Archipelago? Why, that he himself would be seized, spend all his florins on preliminaries, and become again a destitute wanderer with no gems to sell.

Tito had a clearer vision of this disastrous result than of the moment he might find his father and carry him deliverance. It would surely be an unfairness for him, in his full ripe youth, to whom life had hitherto held some of the restraint of a school, to turn his back on promised love and distinction, perhaps never to be visited by that promise again. "And yet," he told himself, "if I were certain that Baldassarre Calvo was alive, and that I could free him by whatever exertions or perils, I would go now—now that I have the money. I would go now to Bardo and Bartolommeo Scala and tell them the whole truth."

He did not explicitly tell himself that if those two men knew the truth, he would have no alternative but to search for his benefactor, the rightful owner of the gems, whom he had always equivocally spoken of as "lost." He did not acknowledge that Greeks of distinction had made sacrifices, undertaken repeated voyages, and sought aid from crowned and mitred heads to free relatives from Turkish slavery. Public opinion did not regard this as exceptional virtue.

This was his first real colloquy with himself. He had followed the impulses of the moment, one of which had been to conceal half the truth. He had never considered this part of his conduct long enough to face the consciousness of his motives for concealment. What was the use of telling the whole? It was true, the thought had crossed his mind several times since leaving Nauplia that it was a great relief to be quit of Baldassarre, and he would have liked to know *who* had fallen overboard. But such thoughts spring inevitably from a relationship that has become irksome. Baldassarre was exacting and had grown stranger as he aged; he constantly scrutinized Tito's mind to see if it met his own exaggerated expectations. Age—the age of a thickset, heavy-browed, bald man beyond sixty, whose intensity and eagerness in the grasp of ideas had long taken the character of monotony and repetition—could be viewed from many angles without being found attractive. Such a man, stranded among new acquaintances, unless he possessed the philosopher's stone, would hardly find rank, youth, and beauty at his feet. The feelings that gather fervor from novelty would be of little help in making the world a home for dimmed and faded human beings. Unless they retained a love rooted in memories, distilling perpetually the sweet balms of fidelity and forbearing tenderness, they were widowed of all affection.

But surely such memories were not absent from Tito's mind? Far in the backward vista of his remembered life, when he was only seven years old, Baldassarre had rescued him from blows, taking him to a home that seemed like opened paradise, filled with sweet food and soothing caresses, all centered on Baldassarre's knee. From that time until the hour they parted, Tito had been the sole center of Baldassarre's fatherly cares.

He had been docile, pliable, quick of apprehension, and ready to acquire: a very bright, lovely boy, a youth of splendid grace, who seemed quite without vices, as if that beautiful form represented a vitality so exquisitely poised and balanced that it could know no uneasy desires, no unrest—a radiant presence for a lonely man to have won for himself. If he was silent when his father expected a response, he did not look moody; if he declined some labor, he flung himself down with such a charming, half-smiling, half-pleading air that the pleasure of looking at him made amends to one who had watched his growth with a sense of claim and possession. The curves of Tito's mouth held ineffable good humor. And then, the quick talent to which everything came readily, from philosophical systems to the rhymes of a street ballad caught up at a hearing! Would anyone have said that Tito had not made a rich return to his benefactor, or that his gratitude and affection would fail on any great demand?

He did not admit that his gratitude had failed; but *it was not certain* that Baldassarre was in slavery, nor certain that he was living.

"Do I not owe something to myself?" Tito said inwardly, with a slight movement of his shoulders, the first he had made since turning to look down at the florins. "Before I quit everything and incur again all the risks of which I am even now weary, I must at least have a reasonable hope. Am I to spend my life in a wandering search? *I believe he is dead.* Cennini was right about my florins: I will place them in his hands tomorrow."

When, the next morning, Tito put this determination into action, he had chosen his color in the game and given an inevitable bent to his wishes. He had made it impossible that he should not from henceforth desire it to be the truth that his father was dead; impossible that he should not be tempted to baseness rather than that the precise facts of his conduct should not remain forever concealed.

Under every guilty secret is hidden a brood of guilty wishes, whose unwholesome, infecting life is cherished by the darkness. The contaminating effect of deeds often lies less in the commission than in the consequent adjustment of our desires—the enlistment of self-interest on the side of falsity. On the other hand, the purifying influence of public confession springs from the fact that by it the hope in lies is forever swept away, and the soul recovers the noble attitude of simplicity.

Besides, in this first distinct colloquy with himself, the ideas which had previously been scattered and interrupted had now concentrated. The little rills of selfishness had united to make a channel, so that they could never again meet with the same resistance. Hitherto, Tito had left in vague indecision the question whether, with the means in his power, he would not return to ascertain his father's fate. He had now made a definite excuse to himself for not taking that course; he had avowed to himself a choice which he would have been ashamed to av

Under the Plane-Tree.

Three weeks had passed since Tito handed his florins to Cennini, and as he set out toward the Via de' Bardi, he wore the outward marks of a mind entirely at ease. How could it be otherwise? His nature was too buoyant, too unburdened by foresight, for the hidden dangers or distant terrors of the world to seize him. Turning from the hot sun into the cool shadow of a narrow street, he removed his black cloth cap, pushing back his brown curls to court the breeze. There was no deceit upon his brow, nor any trace of naive simplicity; it was merely a finely formed, smooth young forehead. His slow, absent glance at the upper windows of the houses possessed neither cunning nor innocence, but simply the wide, unwearying breadth of a youthful gaze, framed by dark lashes and a cerulean-white angle of the eye. Did his face attract or repel depending on the observer's mood? Was he a cipher with many keys? The strong, unmistakable expression of his entire person was a negative one, perfectly truthful, declaring an absence of restless vanity or uneasy claims. It commanded a willing tribute of admiration from the holiday-makers crowding the streets.

By now, the stir of the Festa was felt even in the smallest alleys. The throng, once concentrated for the procession, now streamed in all directions, chasing new distractions. These intervals of a festival are precisely when the vague animal spirits of a crowd become petulant, ready to sacrifice a stray individual for the greater happiness of the many. As Tito entered the neighborhood of San Martino, he found the crowd denser. Near the hostelry of the *Bertucce*, or Baboons, a knot of people had gathered around some arresting object. Tito prepared to turn aside when, amidst loud laughter, his ear caught a distressed childish voice crying, "Loose me! Holy Virgin, help me!" This sound determined him to push his way into the knot of gazers.

He perceived the voice came from a young peasant girl, her white hood fallen off in her struggle to free her hands from the grasp of a man in the parti-colored dress of a *cerretano*, or conjuror. The conjuror made laughing attempts to soothe and cajole her, carrying with him the amused sympathy of the spectators, who argued with the girl against her obstinacy using various terms for "simpleton" rich in the Florentine dialect. At first, the girl's face was turned away, revealing only her light-brown hair plaited with a long silver pin. But the struggle brought her face opposite Tito's, and he saw the baby features of Tessa, her blue eyes filled with tears and her lower lip quivering. Tessa saw *him*, and through the mist of her swelling tears, a sudden hope beamed in her face, like that of a child held by a stranger who spots a familiar hand reaching out.

In an instant, Tito pushed through the barrier of bystanders, whose curiosity made them step aside at his sudden interference. He grasped Tessa's waist and said, "Loose this child! What right have you to hold her against her will?"

The conjuror—a man with a face where the angles of the eyes, eyebrows, nostrils, and jaw all tended upward—showed his small, regular teeth in an impish but not ill-natured grin. He released Tessa's hands, stretching his own backward, shrugging his shoulders in a half-apologetic, half-protesting manner.

"I mean the ragazza no evil in the world, Messere: ask this respectable company," the conjuror said. "I was only going to show them a few samples of my skill, in which this little damsel might have helped me the better because of her kitten face, which would have assured them of open dealing; and I had promised her a lapful of confetti as a reward. But what then? Messer has doubtless better confetti at hand, and she knows it."

A general laugh among the bystanders accompanied these words, raised probably by the look of relief and confidence with which Tessa clung to Tito's arm as he drew her close. She cared only for the laugh as she might have cared for the roar of wild beasts from which she was escaping, attaching no meaning to it. But Tito, having no sooner got her on his arm than he foresaw some embarrassment in the situation, hastened to clear himself from observers who, having been despoiled of an expected amusement, were sure to re-establish the balance by jests.

"See, see, little one! here is your hood," said the conjuror, throwing the bit of white drapery over Tessa's head. "*Orsù*, bear me no malice; come back to me when Messere can spare you."

"Ah! Maestro Vaiano, she'll come back presently, as the toad said to the harrow," called out one of the spectators, seeing how Tessa started and shrank at the action.

Tito pushed vigorously toward the corner of a side-street, a little vexed at this delay in his progress to the Via de' Bardi, intending to get rid of the poor little contadina as soon as possible. But the next street had its own passengers inclined to make holiday remarks on so unusual a pair. They had no sooner entered it than he said, in a kind but hurried manner, "Now, little one, where were you going? Are you come by yourself to the Festa?"

"Ah, no!" said Tessa, looking frightened and distressed again; "I have lost my mother in the crowd—her and my father-in-law. They will be angry—he will beat me. It was in the crowd in San Pulinari—somebody pushed me along and I couldn't stop myself, so I got away from them. Oh, I don't know where they're gone! Please, don't leave me!"

Her eyes swelled with tears again, and she ended with a sob.

Tito hurried along; the Church of the Badia was not far off. They could enter it by the cloister that opened at the back, and in the church he could talk to Tessa—perhaps leave her. No! it was an hour when the church was not open. They paused under the shelter of the cloister, and he said, "Have you no cousin or friend in Florence, my little Tessa, whose house you could find; or are you afraid of walking by yourself since you have been frightened by the conjuror? I am in a hurry to get to Oltrarno, but if I could take you anywhere near—"

"Oh, I *am* frightened: he was the devil—I know he was. And I don't know where to go. I have nobody: and my mother meant to have her dinner somewhere, and I don't know where. Holy Madonna! I shall be beaten."

The corners of her pouting mouth went down piteously, and the poor little bosom with the beads on it above the green serge gown heaved so that a loud sob *would* come, and the big tears fell as if making up for lost time. Here was a situation! It would have been brutal to leave her, and Tito's nature was all gentleness. He wished at that moment that he had not been expected in the Via de' Bardi. As he saw her lifting up her holiday apron to catch the hurrying tears, he laid his hand on the apron, rubbed one of her cheeks, and kissed the baby-like roundness.

"My poor little Tessa! leave off crying. Let us see what can be done. Where is your home—where do you live?"

There was no answer, but the sobs began to subside a little and the drops to fall less quickly.

"Come! I'll take you a little way, if you'll tell me where you want to go."

The apron fell, and Tessa's face began to look as contented as a cherub's budding from a cloud. The diabolical conjuror, the anger, and the beating seemed a long way off.

"I think I'll go home, if you'll take me," she said, in a half whisper, looking up at Tito with wide blue eyes and something sweeter than a smile—with a childlike calm.

"Come, then, little one," said Tito, in a caressing tone, putting her arm within his again. "Which way is it?"

"Beyond Peretola—where the large pear-tree is."

"Peretola? Out at which gate, pazzarella? I am a stranger, you must remember."

"Out at the Por del Prato," said Tessa, moving along with a very fast hold on Tito's arm.

He did not know all the turnings well enough to venture on an attempt at choosing the quietest streets; and besides, it occurred to him that where the passengers were most numerous there was, perhaps, the most chance of meeting with Monna Ghita and finding an end to his knight-errant-ship. So he made straight for Porta Rossa and on to Ognissanti, showing his usual bright propitiatory face to the mixed observers who threw their jests at him and his little heavy-shod maiden with much liberality. Mingled with the more decent holiday-makers were frolicsome apprentices, rather envious of his good fortune; bold-eyed women with the badge of the yellow veil; beggars who thrust forward their caps for alms in derision at Tito's evident haste; dicers, sharpers, and loungers of the worst sort; boys whose tongues were used to wag in concert at the most brutal street games. For the streets of Florence were not always a moral spectacle in those times, and Tessa's terror at being lost in the crowd was not wholly unreasonable.

When they reached the Piazza d'Ognissanti, Tito slackened his pace. They were both heated with their hurried walk, and here was a wider space where they could take breath. They sat down on one of the stone benches frequent against the walls of old Florentine houses.

"Holy Virgin!" said Tessa; "I am glad we have got away from those women and boys; but I was not frightened, because you could take care of me."

"Pretty little Tessa!" said Tito, smiling at her. "What makes you feel so safe with me?"

"Because you are so beautiful—like the people going into Paradise: they are all good."

"It is a long while since you had your breakfast, Tessa," said Tito, seeing some stalls near with fruit and sweetmeats upon them. "Are you hungry?"

"Yes, I think I am—if you will have some too."

Tito bought some apricots, cakes, and comfits, and put them into her apron.

"Come," he said, "let us walk on to the Prato, and then perhaps you will not be afraid to go the rest of the way alone."

"But you will have some of the apricots and things," said Tessa, rising obediently and gathering up her apron as a bag for her store.

"We will see," said Tito aloud; and to himself he said, "Here is a little contadina who might inspire a better idyl than Lorenzo de' Medici's 'Nencia da Barberino,' that Nello's friends rave about; if I were only a Theocritus, or had time to cultivate the necessary experience by unseasonable walks of this sort! However, the mischief is done now: I am so late already that another half-hour will make no difference. Pretty little pigeon!"

"We have a garden and plenty of pears," said Tessa, "and two cows, besides the mules; and I'm very fond of them. But my father-in-law is a cross man: I wish my mother had not married him. I think he is wicked; he is very ugly."

"And does your mother let him beat you, poverina? You said you were afraid of being beaten."

"Ah, my mother herself scolds me: she loves my young sister better, and thinks I don't do work enough. Nobody speaks kindly to me, only the Pievano (parish priest) when I go to confession. And the men in the Mercato laugh at me and make fun of me. Nobody ever kissed me and spoke to me as you do; just as I talk to my little black-faced kid, because I'm very fond of it."

It seemed not to have entered Tessa's mind that there was any change in Tito's appearance since the morning he begged the milk from her, and that he looked now like a personage for whom she must summon her little stock of reverent words and signs. He had impressed her too differently from any human being who had ever come near her before, for her to make any comparison of details; she took no note of his dress. He was simply a voice and a face to her, something come from Paradise into a world where most things seemed hard and angry; and she prattled with as little restraint as if he had been an imaginary companion born of her own lovingness and the sunshine.

They had now reached the Prato, which at that time was a large open space within the walls, where the Florentine youth played at their favorite *Calcio*—a peculiar kind of football—and otherwise exercised themselves. At this mid-day time it was forsaken and quiet to the very gates, where a tent had been erected in preparation for the race. On the border of this wide meadow, Tito paused and said—

"Now, Tessa, you will not be frightened if I leave you to walk the rest of the way by yourself. Addio! Shall I come and buy a cup of milk from you in the Mercato to-morrow morning, to see that you are quite safe?"

He added this question in a soothing tone, as he saw her eyes widening sorrowfully and the corners of her mouth falling. She said nothing at first; she only opened her apron and looked down at her apricots and sweetmeats. Then she looked up at him again and said complainingly—

"I thought you would have some, and we could sit down under a tree outside the gate, and eat them together."

"Tessa, Tessa, you little siren, you would ruin me," said Tito, laughing, and kissing both her cheeks. "I ought to have been in the Via de' Bardi long ago. No!

When Fra Luca fell silent, Tito remained standing beside him, paralyzed by indecision. It was only after the crowd of pilgrims had thinned and the friar rose to walk slowly toward the church of Santa Felicita that Tito followed, turning his steps down the Via de' Bardi.

"If this monk is truly Florentine," he murmured to himself, "and if he intends to stay in Florence, then everything must be revealed." He sensed a new crisis had arrived, yet he was not so visibly shaken that he could not proceed with his scheduled visit to Bardo to apologize for his recent absence. Tito's gift for concealment was rapidly evolving from a passive trait into an active weapon. It was conceivable—perhaps inevitable—that he could now accept the changed circumstances openly and confess Baldassarre's existence. However, doing so would cast a harsh retroactive light on his initial silence, framing it as a calculated equivocation designed to evade a recognized claim. Furthermore, it would expose his quiet settlement of funds and investments, actions taken while his benefactor's fate remained unverified. For the moment, prudence dictated acting as if nothing had occurred. He would suspend decisive thought; the night offered ample time for meditation, and no one would know the exact hour he received the letter.

He ascended to the second-floor room where Romola and her father sat amidst parchment and marble, isolated from the street's holiday bustle just as they were on ordinary days. Tito entered with a countenance only slightly brighter than usual, masking a regret at his lateness—a regret requiring no proof, since he had sacrificed the spectacle of the Corso to express it. He then endeavored to inject extra animation into the evening conversation, though his consciousness operated like a complex machine, depositing thoughts entirely distinct from the flow of speech. By the time he descended the stone stairs and emerged from the grim door into the starlight, his mind had genuinely reached a new stage in forming a purpose.

The following day, once freed from his professorial duties, he turned up the Via del Cocomero toward the convent of San Marco, his purpose now fully crystallized. He intended to ascertain from Fra Luca precisely what he suspected of the truth and on what grounds; furthermore, he needed to know how long the friar planned to remain at San Marco. With this fuller knowledge, he hoped to craft a statement that would spare him the necessity of leaving Florence. Tito had never before had occasion to fabricate an ingenious lie, yet the opportunity had arrived—the specific circumstance that always breeds tacit falsehood. His ingenuity was ready. He had convinced himself that he was not bound to search for Baldassarre. He had once declared that upon receiving a fair assurance of his father's existence and whereabouts, he would unhesitatingly pursue him. But why, after all, was he bound to go? What, upon close inspection, was the ultimate end of life but to extract the maximum sum of pleasure? Was not his own blooming existence a promise of incomparably greater joy—not merely for himself, but for others—than the withered, wintry life of a man past the age of keen enjoyment, whose ideas had stiffened into barren rigidity? Those ideas had been sown in the fresh soil of Tito's mind; they were lively germs there. That was the proper order of things—the order of nature, which treats maturity as merely a nesting ground for youth. Baldassarre had done his work, had drawn his fill of life; Tito declared it was his turn now.

The prospect loomed vague yet enticing: "I think they are taking me to Antioch." Here was a vista! After a long voyage, to spend months, perhaps years, in a search for which there was no guarantee it would not prove vain, and to leave behind at the starting point a life of distinction and love. And if he found anything, he would encounter the old, exacting companionship known by rote. Certainly, the gems—and therefore the florins—were, in a narrow legal sense, Baldassarre's. But in that larger, more radically natural view where the world belongs to youth and strength, they belonged to whoever could extract the most pleasure from them. He was conscious that this was not the sentiment engendered by the complicated play of human feelings in society. The men around him would expect him to immediately apply those florins to his benefactor's rescue. But what was the sentiment of society? A mere tangle of anomalous traditions and opinions, which no wise man would take as a guide, except so far as his own comfort was concerned. Did he care for the florins save perhaps for Romola's sake? He would give them up readily enough. It was the joy due to him, hovering close to his lips, which he felt he was not bound to thrust away and thus travel on, thirsting. Any maxims requiring a man to fling away the good needed to make existence sweet were merely the lining of human selfishness turned outward; they were crafted by men who desired others to sacrifice themselves for their sake. He would rather that Baldassarre should not suffer; he liked no one to suffer. But could any philosophy prove to him that he was bound to care for another's suffering more than his own? To do so, he must have loved Baldassarre devotedly, and he did not. Was that his fault? Gratitude, seen closely, made no valid claim. His father's life would have been dreary without him; were we convicted of a debt to men for the pleasures they give themselves?

Once he began to explain away Baldassarre's claim, Tito's thought showed itself as active as a virulent acid, eating its rapid way through all the tissues of sentiment. His mind was destitute of that dread erroneously decried as nothing higher than a man's animal care for his own skin: the awe of Divine Nemesis felt by religious pagans, and, though taking a more positive form under Christianity, still felt by the mass of mankind simply as a vague fear of anything called wrongdoing. Such terror of the unseen was so far above mere sensual cowardice that it would annihilate it; it was the initial recognition of a moral law restraining desire, checking the hard, bold scrutiny of imperfect thought into obligations that could never be proved to possess sanctity in the absence of feeling. "It is good," sang the old Eumenides in Aeschylus, "that fear should sit as the guardian of the soul, forcing it into wisdom—it is good that men should carry a threatening shadow in their hearts under the full sunshine; else, how should they learn to revere the right?" That guardianship might become needless, but only when all outward law has become needless—only when duty and love have united in one stream to make a common force.

As Tito entered the outer cloister of San Marco and inquired for Fra Luca, there was no shadowy presentiment in his mind; he felt himself too cultured and skeptical for such fears. He had been nurtured in contempt for the tales of priests whose impudent lives were proverbial, and in erudite familiarity with disputes concerning the Chief Good, which he considered ultimately a matter of taste. Yet fear was a strong element in Tito's nature—the fear of what he believed or saw was likely to rob him of pleasure. He harbored a definite fear that Fra Luca might be the means of driving him from Florence.

"Fra Luca? Ah, he has gone to Fiesole—to the Dominican monastery there. He was taken on a litter in the cool of the morning. The poor Brother is very ill. Could you leave a message for him?"

This answer came from a *fra converso*, or lay brother, whose accent plainly marked him as a raw countryman, and whose dull glance implied no curiosity.

"Thanks; my business can wait."

Tito turned away with a sense of relief. "This friar is not likely to live," he said to himself. "I saw he was worn to a shadow. And at Fiesole there will be nothing to recall me to his mind. Besides, if he should return, my explanation will serve as well then as now. But I wish I knew what it was about his face that recalled to me."

The Prize is Nearly Grasped.

Tito moved with a light, buoyant step, the shadow of fear having vanished from his mind. The usual joyousness of his nature returned to dominate his spirit, and he hurried to see Romola. Yet, as he approached, he felt a pang of hesitation. Romola's life appeared to him a mirror of that loving, pitying devotion and patient endurance of tedious duties from which he had previously shrank. He was not, however, out of love with goodness, nor prepared to plunge into vice; he was still in his fresh youth, with soft pulses responsive to all charm and loveliness. He retained a healthy appetite for ordinary human joys, and the poison could only work upon him by degrees. Though he had sold himself to evil, life seemed so nearly unchanged to him that he was unconscious of the bond he had forged. He intended for all things to proceed as they had before, both within and without him; he meant to win golden opinions through meritorious exertion, ingenious learning, and amiable compliance. He was not going to do anything that would throw him out of harmony with the beings he cared for. And he cared supremely for Romola; he wished to have her for his beautiful and loving wife. While there might be wealthier alliances within the reach of his successful accomplishments, there was no woman in all Florence like Romola. When she was near him, looking at him with her sincere hazel eyes, he was subdued by a delicious influence as strong and inevitable as those musical vibrations which take possession of us with a rhythmic empire that no sooner ceases than we desire it to begin again.

As he trod the stone stairs, still outside the door with no one but Maso near him, the influence seemed to have begun its work merely through the nearness of anticipation.

"Welcome, Tito mio," said the old man's voice before Tito had spoken. There was a new vigor in the voice, a new cheerfulness in the blind face, since that first interview more than two months ago. "You have brought fresh manuscript, doubtless; but since we were talking last night I have had new ideas: we must take a wider scope—we must go back upon our footsteps."

Tito, paying his homage to Romola as he advanced, went, as his custom was, straight to Bardo's chair. He placed his hand in the palm held out to receive it, positioning himself on the cross-legged leather seat with scrolled ends, close to Bardo's elbow.

"Yes," he said in his gentle way. "I have brought the new manuscript, but that can wait your pleasure. I have young limbs, you know, and can walk back up the hill without any difficulty."

He did not look at Romola as he said this, but he knew quite well that her eyes were fixed on him with delight.

"That is well said, my son." Bardo had already addressed Tito in this way once or twice of late. "And I perceive with gladness that you do not shrink from labour, without which, the poet has wisely said, life has given nothing to mortals. It is too often the 'palma sine pulvere,' the prize of glory without the dust of the race, that attracts young ambition. But what says the Greek? 'In the morning of life, work; in the mid-day, give counsel; in the evening, pray.' It is true, I might be thought to have reached that helpless evening; but not so, while I have counsel within me which is yet unspoken. For my mind, as I have often said, was shut up as by a dam; the plenteous waters lay dark and motionless; but you, my Tito, have opened a duct for them, and they rush forward with a force that surprises myself. And now, what I want is, that we should go over our preliminary ground again, with a wider scheme of comment and illustration: otherwise I may lose opportunities which I now see retrospectively, and which may never occur again. You mark what I am saying, Tito?"

He had just stooped to reach his manuscript, which had rolled down, and Bardo's jealous ear was alive to the slight movement.

Tito might have been excused for shrugging his shoulders at the prospect before him, but he was not naturally impatient; moreover, he had been bred up in that laborious erudition, at once minute and copious, which was the chief intellectual task of the age; and with Romola near, he was floated along by waves of agreeable sensation that made everything seem easy.

"Assuredly," he said. "You wish to enlarge your comments on certain passages we have cited."

"Not only so; I wish to introduce an occasional *excursus*, where we have noticed an author to whom I have given special study; for I may die too soon to achieve any separate work. And this is not a time for scholarly integrity and well-sifted learning to lie idle, when it is not only rash ignorance that we have to fear, but when there are men like Calderino, who, as Poliziano has well shown, have recourse to impudent falsities of citation to serve the ends of their vanity and secure a triumph to their own mistakes. Wherefore, my Tito, I think it not well that we should let slip the occasion that lies under our hands. And now we will turn back to the point where we have cited the passage from Thucydides, and I wish you, by way of preliminary, to go with me through all my notes on the Latin translation made by Lorenzo Valla, for which the incomparable Pope Nicholas the Fifth—with whose personal notice I was honoured while I was yet young, and when he was still Thomas of Sarzana—paid him (I say not unduly) the sum of five hundred gold scudi. But inasmuch as Valla, though otherwise of dubious fame, is held in high honour for his severe scholarship, whence the epigrammatist has jocosely said of him that since he went among the shades, Pluto himself has not dared to speak in the ancient languages, it is the more needful that his name should not be as a stamp warranting false wares; and therefore I would introduce an *excursus* on Thucydides, wherein my castigations of Valla's text may find a fitting place. My Romola, thou wilt reach the needful volumes—thou knowest them—on the fifth shelf of the cabinet."

Tito rose at the same moment with Romola, saying, "I will reach them, if you will point them out," and followed her hastily into the adjoining small room, where the walls were also covered with ranges of books in perfect order.

"There they are," said Romola, pointing upward. "Every book is just where it was when my father ceased to see them."

Tito stood by her without hastening to reach the books. They had never been in this room together before.

"I hope," she continued, turning her eyes full on Tito, with a look of grave confidence—"I hope he will not weary you; this work makes him so happy."

"And me too, Romola—if you will only let me say, I love you—if you will only think me worth loving a little."

His speech was the softest murmur, and the dark beautiful face, nearer to hers than it had ever been before, was looking at her with beseeching tenderness.

"I do love you," murmured Romola. She looked at him with the same simple majesty as ever, but her voice had never in her life before sunk to that murmur. It seemed to them both that they were looking at each other a long while before her lips moved again; yet it was but a moment till she said, "I know *now* what it is to be happy."

The faces just met, and the dark curls mingled for an instant with the rippling gold. Quick as lightning after that, Tito set his foot on a projecting ledge of the book-shelves and reached down the needful volumes. They were both contented to be silent and separate, for that first blissful experience of mutual consciousness was all the more exquisite for being unperturbed by immediate sensation.

It had all been as rapid as the irreversible mingling of waters, for even the eager and jealous Bardo had not become impatient.

"You have the volumes, my Romola?" the old man said, as they came near him again. "And now you will get your pen ready; for, as Tito marks off the scholia we determine on extracting, it will be well for you to copy them without delay—numbering them carefully, mind, to correspond with the numbers in the text which he will write."

Romola always had some task which gave her a share in this joint work. Tito took his stand at the *leggio*, where he both wrote and read, and she placed herself at a table just in front of him, where she was ready to give into her father's hands anything that he might happen to want, or relieve him of a volume that he had done with. They had always been in that position since the work began, yet on this day it seemed new; it was so different now for them to be opposite each other; so different for Tito to take a book from her, as she lifted it from her father's knee. Yet there was no finesse to secure an additional look or touch. Each woman creates in her own likeness the love-tokens that are offered to her; and Romola's deep calm happiness encompassed Tito like the rich but quiet evening light which dissipates all unrest.

They had been two hours at their work, and were just desisting because of the fading light, when the door opened and there entered a figure strangely incongruous with the current of their thoughts and with the suggestions of every object around them. It was the figure of a short, stout, black-eyed woman, about fifty, wearing a black velvet *berretta*, or close cap, embroidered with pearls, under which surprisingly massive black braids surmounted the little bulging forehead, and fell in rich plaited curves over the ears, while an equally surprising carmine tint on the upper region of the fat cheeks contrasted with the surrounding sallowness. Three rows of pearls and a lower necklace of gold reposed on the horizontal cushion of her neck; the embroidered border of her trailing black velvet gown and her embroidered long-drooping sleeves of rose-coloured damask were slightly faded, but they conveyed to the initiated eye the satisfactory assurance that they were the splendid result of six months' labour by a skilled workman; and the rose-coloured petticoat, with its dimmed white fringe and seed-pearl arabesques, was duly exhibited in order to suggest a similar pleasing reflection. A handsome coral rosary hung from one side of an inferential belt, which emerged into certainty with a large clasp of silver wrought in niello; and, on the other side, where the belt again became inferential, hung a *scarsella*, or large purse, of crimson velvet, stitched with pearls. Her little fat right-hand, which looked as if it had been made of paste, and had risen out of shape under partial baking, held a small book of devotions, also splendid with velvet, pearls, and silver.

The figure was already too familiar to Tito to be startling, for Monna Brigida was a frequent visitor at Bardo's, being excepted from the sentence of banishment passed on feminine triviality, on the ground of her cousinship to his dead wife and her early care for Romola, who now looked round at her with an affectionate smile, and rose to draw the leather seat to a due distance from her father's chair, that the coming gush of talk might not be too near his ear.

"_La cugina_?" said Bardo, interrogatively, detecting the short steps and the sweeping drapery.

"Yes, it is your cousin," said Monna Brigida, in an alert voice, raising her fingers smilingly at Tito, and then lifting up her face to be kissed by Romola. "Always the troublesome cousin breaking in on your wisdom," she went on, seating herself and beginning to fan herself with the white veil hanging over her arm. "Well, well; if I didn't bring you some news of the world now and then, I do believe you'd forget there was anything in life but these mouldy ancients, who want sprinkling with holy water if all I hear about them is true. Not but what the world is bad enough nowadays, for the scandals that turn up under one's nose at every corner—I don't want to hear and see such things, but one can't go about with one's head in a bag; and it was only yesterday—well, well, you needn't burst out at me, Bardo, I'm not going to tell anything; if I'm not as wise as the three kings, I know how many legs go into one boot. But, nevertheless, Florence is a wicked city—is it not true, Messer Tito? for you go into the world. Not but what one must sin a little—Messer Domeneddio expects that of us, else what are the blessed sacraments for? And what I say is, we've got to reverence the saints, and not to set ourselves up as if we could be like them, else life would be unbearable; as it will be if things go on after this new fashion. For what do you think? I've been at the wedding to-day—Dianora Acciajoli's with the young Albizzi that there has been so much talk of—and everybody wondered at its being to-day instead of yesterday; but, *cieli*! such a wedding as it was might have been put off till the next Quaresima for a penance. For there was the bride looking like a white nun—not so much as a pearl about her—and the bridegroom as solemn as San Giuseppe. It's true! And half the people invited were *Piagnoni*—they call them *Piagnoni* (funeral mourners: properly, paid mourners) now, these new saints of Fra Girolamo's making. And to think of two families like the Albizzi and the Acciajoli taking up such notions, when they could afford to wear the best! Well, well, they invited me—but they could do no other, seeing my husband was Luca Antonio's uncle by the mother's side—and a pretty time I had of it while we waited under the canopy in front of the house, before they let us in. I couldn't stand in my

The afternoon of the seventh of September hung heavy and lazy over Florence, a silence broken only by the distant hum of the city, two months since Romola and Tito had dared to speak their love aloud.

Tito stepped into Nello's shop, finding the barber in a state of profound repose. Stretched upon the bench, a cap served as a blindfold over his eyes, one leg drawn up while the other had slipped toward the floor, dragging a manuscript volume of verse beneath it until the leaves were crushed. In the corner, Sandro sat alone, his fingers moving with solemn, rhythmic precision as he played *mora*, his left hand responding to the arithmetical demands of his right with the gravity of a priest.

Tito moved with a whisper of a step, snatching up the lute. He bent over the sleeping barber, plucking the strings softly as he sang Lorenzo de' Medici's *Carnival Song*:

*"Beauteous is life in blossom!
And it fleeteth—fleeteth ever;
Whoso would be joyful—let him!
There's no surety for the morrow."*

Nello woke as easily as a startled bird. The cap flew from his eyes, and he sprang upright, stretching his arms to their full length before curling them around Tito's curls in a playful grip.

"Ah, my Apollino! I am somewhat late with my siesta on this scorching day," Nello chuckled, rubbing his eyes. "That comes of not sleeping naturally, but taking a potion of potent poetry. Listen to me matching my words by their initial letters, like a *Trovatore*.
