A bad symptom, I fear; my wine of understanding is running off at the spigot of authorship, leaving me an empty cask smelling of dregs, like many a genius I know. What is it, my Orpheus?"

"It is but a tailor's image of your poet's," Tito replied, letting his fingers drift lightly across the strings. "Yet you have divined my impatience. I seek an extra touch of your art—not on my chin, but on the *zazzera*, which is as tangled as Florentine politics. Your comb works wonders there, stirring the animal spirits, and a hint of your orange-scent would be fitting. I am bound for the Scala palace; the young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici attends, bringing with him Bernardo Dovizi of Bibbiena, whose wit is so rapid I can only hope to outshine it with the fragrance of orange blossoms."

Nello seized his comb, flourishing it before pushing Tito gently back into the chair and wrapping him in a cloth.

"Never speak of rivalry, *bel giovane mio*," Nello said, working the comb with practiced ease. "Bernardo Dovizi is a keen youth, but he will never cast a net to catch the wind. He bears the sharp-muzzled look of his brother, Ser Piero, the weasel Piero de' Medici keeps to slip through small holes. No! You distance all rivals; you may soon touch the sky with your forefinger. They say you have carried enough honey to sweeten the sour Messer Angelo, who has pronounced you less of an ass than expected, given your understanding with the Secretary."

"And between ourselves, Nello," Tito murmured, "Messer Angelo possesses more genius and erudition than all other Florentine scholars combined. It suits them to praise me now that Poliziano is bowed by grief or illness. I might challenge a sparrow-hawk like Pietro Crinito, but Poliziano is a large-beaked eagle who would swallow me, feathers and all, without noticing the difference."

"I will not contradict your modesty," Nello teased. "But we clever Florentines do not repeat the same truths daily; we cry down Dante and cry up Francesco Cei merely for variety. If we hail you as a new Poliziano, heaven ensures the lie is not quite so great. Are you not a pattern of virtue in this wicked city? With your ears double-waxed against siren invitations that would lure you from the Via de' Bardi and your great work destined to astonish posterity?"

"Posterity," Tito smiled wryly, "whom it will probably astonish as the universe does, by the impossibility of seeing what the plan ever was."

"Yes, something like that was prophesied recently. Cristoforo Landino said the excellent Bardo lies overthrown in his learning like a cavalier in heavy armor, angry because he is over-ridden. A pithy remark, though it seems Messer Cristoforo feeds one with an empty spoon and gags one with vain expectation. *Ecco!* You are perfect now." Nello drew away the cloth. "Impossible to add more grace! But love is not always fed on learning, is it? I shall have to dress the *zazzera* for the betrothal soon, shall I?"

"Perhaps," Tito said, smiling faintly, "unless Messer Bernardo recommends that Bardo require me to yoke a lion and a wild boar to the car of the Zecca before I can win my Alcestis. But I confess he is right: I am unworthy of Romola. She is a Pleiad who may grow dim by marrying any mortal."

"*Gnaffè*," Nello laughed. "Your modesty is in the right place. Yet fate seems to have measured and chiselled you for the niche left empty by the old man's son. By the way, *Cronaca* told me he is now at San Marco. Did you know?"

A slight electric shock passed through Tito as he rose, though he hid it by stooping to flatten the crushed leaves of the fallen book.

"No; I thought he was at Fiesole. Are you sure he has returned to San Marco?"

"Cronaca is my authority," Nello shrugged. "I do not frequent that sanctuary, but he does. Ah," he added, taking the book from Tito's hands, "my poor Nencia da Barberino! It jars your scholarly feelings to see the pages dog-eared. I was lulled by her rustic charms—'prettier than the turnip-flower,' 'with a cheek more savoury than cheese.' But to get such a notion of a *contadina*, one must lie on velvet cushions in the Via Larga, not watch the *Fierucoloni* stumping into the Piazza della Nunziata this evening."

"And pray, who are the *Fierucoloni*?" Tito asked, settling his cap.

"The country women from Pistoia and the Casentino, come to keep vigil in the church of the Nunziata and sell their yarn and dried mushrooms at the *Fierucola*, the little Fair. They make a queer show with paper lanterns, howling hymns to the Virgin on her nativity. If you had the leisure..." Nello paused, his expression darkening slightly. "No? Well, I have had enough. There is wild work in the Piazza tonight. One may get stones about the ears or shins without asking for them. I was never fond of that pressing attention. *Addio*."

Tito left the shop carrying a quiet uneasiness. His visit had ended sooner than anticipated. The boy-cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, the youngest of red-hatted fathers who would later present his broad dark cheek to posterity as Pope Leo the Tenth, had been detained by his favorite pastime, the chase, and failed to appear at the Scala palace.

It was nearly sunset when Tito left the palace, intending to proceed to the Via de' Bardi. He had not gone far when, to his astonishment, he saw Romola advancing toward him along the Borgo Pinti.

She wore a thick black veil and a black mantle, yet her figure and her walk were unmistakable. Beside her walked a short, stout form, recognized instantly as Monna Brigida, despite the unusual plainness of her attire. Romola had not been bred for devotional observances, and her appearances outside the loggia on the roof of her house were so rare, and so heavily discussed beforehand due to Bardo's dislike of being left alone, that Tito felt certain there must be some sudden, urgent cause for her absence.

She saw him through her veil and hastened her steps.

"Romola, has anything happened?" Tito asked, turning to walk beside her.

She did not answer immediately. Monna Brigida broke the silence.

"Ah, Messer Tito, you do well to turn round, for we are in haste. And is it not a misfortune? We are obliged to go round by the walls and turn up the Via del Maglio because of the Fair; the *contadine* block the way by the Nunziata, which would have taken us to San Marco in half the time."

Tito's heart gave a violent bound.

"Romola," he said in a lower tone, "are you going to San Marco?"

They had emerged from the Borgo Pinti and were now under the city walls, where wide gardens lay to their left and all was quiet. Romola lifted her veil to breathe the air, and Tito saw the subdued agitation in her face.

"Yes, Tito mio," she said, looking directly at him with sad eyes. "For the first time I am doing something unknown to my father. It comforts me that I have met you, for at least I can tell *you*. But if you are going to him, it will be well for you not to say that you met me. He thinks I am only gone to my cousin, because she sent for me. I left my godfather with him; *he* knows where I am going and why. You remember that evening when my brother's name was mentioned and my father spoke of him to you?"

"Yes," Tito whispered. A strange complication seized his mental state. His heart sank at the probability of a great change in his prospects, yet his thoughts darted over a hundred details of the course he would take when that change arrived. Still, he returned her gaze with a hungry sense that it might be the last time she would bend it upon him with full, unquestioning confidence.

"The *cugina* heard he had returned. The evening before—the evening of San Giovanni—I afterwards found he had been seen by our good Maso near our door. But when Maso went to inquire at San Marco, Dino, that is, my brother—christened Bernardino after our godfather, but now calling himself Fra Luca—had been taken to the monastery at Fiesole because he was ill. This morning, however, a message came to Maso saying he had returned to San Marco. Maso went to him there. He is very ill, and he has adjured me to go and see him. I cannot refuse, though I hold him guilty. I still remember how I loved him when I was a little girl, before I knew he would forsake my father. Perhaps he has some word of penitence to send by me. It cost me a struggle to act against my father's feeling, which I have always held to be just. I am almost sure you will think I have chosen rightly, Tito, because I have noticed your nature is less rigid than mine, and nothing makes you angry. It would cost you less to be forgiving. Though, if you had seen your father forsaken by one to whom he gave his chief love—by one in whom he planted his labor and his hopes—forsaken when his need was becoming greatest—even you, Tito, would find it hard to forgive."

What could he say? He was not equal to the hypocrisy of telling Romola that such offenses ought not to be pardoned, nor did he have the courage to utter words of dissuasion.

"You are right, my Romola; you are always right, except in thinking too well of me."

There was genuine truth in those words. Tito looked beautiful as he spoke them, his face unusually pale, his lip quivering slightly. Romola, interpreting all things largely, like a mind prepossessed with high beliefs, had a tearful brightness in her eyes as she looked at him, touched with keen joy that he felt so strongly whatever she felt.

"And now, Tito, I wish you to leave me," she said, continuing her walk. "The *cugina* and I shall be less noticed if we enter the piazza alone."

"Yes, it were better you should leave us," Monna Brigida interjected. "To say the truth, Messer Tito, all eyes follow you. Let Romola muffle herself as she will, everyone wants to see what is under her veil, for she has that way of walking like a procession. Not that I find fault with her for it, only it doesn't suit my steps. And indeed, I would rather not have us seen going to San Marco. That is why I am dressed as if I were one of the *Piagnoni* themselves, and as old as Sant' Anna. For if it had been anybody but poor Dino, who ought to be forgiven if he's dying, for what's the use of having a grudge against dead people? Make them feel while they live, say I—"

No one made a scruple of interrupting Monna Brigida. Tito, having just raised Romola's hand to his lips and said, "I understand, I obey you," turned away, lifting his cap. It was a sign of reverence rarely made by native Florentines at that time, exciting Bernardo del Nero's contempt for Tito as a fawning Greek, while to Romola, who loved homage, it gave him an exceptional grace.

He was half glad of the dismissal, half disposed to cling to Romola to the last moment in which she would love him without suspicion. It seemed certain that this brother would before all things want to know, and that Romola would before all things confide to him her father's position and her own after the years that must have brought so much change. She would tell him she was soon to be publicly betrothed to a young scholar, filling the place left vacant long ago by a wandering son. Tito foresaw the impulse that would prompt Romola to dwell on that prospect, and what would follow on the mention of the future husband's name. Fra Luca would tell all he knew and conjectured, and Tito saw no possible falsity by which he could now ward off the worst consequences of his former dissimulation.

The roar of the crowd and the chaotic symphony of sounds that greeted Tito at the Piazza's entrance reminded him of Nello's warnings regarding the Fierucoloni. With a strange sense of relief, he thrust himself into the throng, enjoying the jostling and the raucous noise that filled the void. It dulled the bleak calculations of his future—the terrifying prospect of wandering alone in search of fortune—and momentarily extinguished the thought of seeking out Baldassarre.

Through the opposing archways, people surged into the square, illuminated by paper lanterns waved on sticks. A monotonous chant formed a steady thread beneath a cacophony of screams, whistles, mocking songs from boys, drumbeats, and tinkling bells. Occasionally, a lantern shattered with a crash as a stone flew blindly through the air, followed by shrieks and renewed shouting. Yet, on the periphery of this whirlwind, groups observed the Nativity of the Virgin with a more orderly devotion. Ragged men darted sharp glances around while their tongues ran on, inviting country folk to games; two figures on stilts, having snatched lanterns from the crowd, swung the lights like meteors as they paraded; a shrewd merchant sold hot *berlingozzi* from a small stall; another man, leaning against a pillar of the Foundling Hospital, hawked miracle pills guaranteed to cure toothache and drowning; a tumbler performed on a platform nearby; and a handful of apprentices, bored by the casual stone-throwing, engaged in a fierce, concentrated match of their favorite Florentine sport at the narrow entrance of the Via de' Febbrai.

Tito, forced to weave through random gaps in the crowd, found himself beside a procession of barefoot peasant women. Their sun-baked, bronzed faces and fragmentary, dirt-stained garments of obsolete fashion made them appear to the city dwellers as a weary ancestry returning from a pilgrimage begun a century prior. These were the hardy women from the Pistoia mountains, carrying a year's labor in a modest bundle of yarn on their backs. In their hearts lay a meager hope and a vast, dim fear, both entrusted to the Blessed Virgin, whose miraculous image was to be unveiled on this Eve of her Nativity, allowing her power to flow unobstructed.

At another moment, Tito was pushed toward the edge of the square, where peddlers stationed themselves safely behind pillars. There he recognized Bratti, who stood with his back to the stone, lips pursed in disdainful silence. He eyed every approacher with a cold superiority, clutching a serge cloth that hid the contents of his basket. Surprised by such unusual composure in an anxious trader, Tito stepped closer. Two women approached the basket with curiosity, prompting Bratti to pull the cloth tighter and look away. Provoked, one woman asked what was inside.

"Before I answer, Monna, I must know if you intend to buy," Bratti replied sharply. "I cannot display such wares to every fly that settles and pays nothing. My goods are too choice. Besides, I have only two left, and I have no mind to soil them. With the rumors of pestilence, they might be worth their weight in gold. No, no: *andate con Dio*."

The women exchanged glances. "And what is the price?" asked the second.

"Not within what you likely carry, buona donna," Bratti said in a tone of compassionate condescension. "Trust in Messer Domeneddio and the saints; poor people can do no better."

"Not so poor!" the second woman retorted indignantly, producing her money-bag. "Come, what do you say to a *grosso*?"

"I say you may get twenty-one *quattrini* for it," Bratti replied coolly, "but not from me, for I lack such small change."

"Two, then?" the woman demanded, growing exasperated, while her companion looked on with envy. "It won't be more than two, I think."

After further haggling and mercantile coquetry, Bratti feigned concession. "Since you are set on it," he said slowly, lifting the cover, "I would be loath to cause you harm. Maestro Gabbadeo used to say that when a woman desires something and fails to obtain it, she falls into greater danger of the pestilence. *Ecco!* I have but two left. Let me tell you, the companion to these is on the finger of Maestro Gabbadeo, who has gone to Bologna—a wise doctor indeed."

The objects were two clumsy iron rings, beaten into the shape of ancient Roman rings sometimes unearthed. Their rust and the hidden nature of their supposed potency were so convincing that the *grossi* were paid without complaint. The first woman, lacking such coins, succeeded after much feigned reluctance in trading some of her yarn for the remaining ring, carrying it off in triumph. Bratti covered his basket, now filled with miscellaneous items likely acquired under similar circumstances, and moved from his pillar, suddenly confronting Tito, who would have preferred to avoid recognition.

"By the head of San Giovanni," Bratti said, pulling Tito back to the pillar, "what luck! I was speaking of you this morning, Messer Greco. I said he has risen among the gentlemen—and I am glad, for I was at the bottom of his fortune—but I rarely catch him lying on the stones now. But it is your luck, not mine, Messer Greco, save for a small trifle to satisfy me for my trouble."

"You speak in riddles, Bratti," Tito said. "Remember, I do not sharpen my wits by haggling over iron rings; you must be plain."

"By the Holy Gospels! It was an easy bargain I gave them. If a Hebrew gets thirty-two percent, I hope a Christian may get a little more. Without a conscience, I would have doubled the money and the yarn. But speaking of rings, it is your ring—that very ring on your finger—that I could find a buyer for. Yes, a buyer with a deep purse."

"Truly?" Tito asked, examining his ring.

"A Genoese heading straight to Hungary. He came to my shop searching for old things I didn't know the price of; I wager he thought I had a pumpkin on my shoulders. He had rummaged every shop in Florence. He wore a ring—not quite like yours, but of the same fashion. As we spoke of rings, I mentioned a fine young man, a particular friend of mine, who owned such a ring. He asked, 'Who is he? Tell him I'll give him his price.' I thought of coming to Nello's tomorrow. In my opinion, Messer Greco, you are not the type to watch the Arno turn to broth and stand idle."

Tito had listened intently, though his mind raced. Why keep the ring? It had been a mere sentiment preventing him from selling it with the other gems. Had he sold it, perhaps he would have avoided Fra Luca's identification. True, it had been taken from Baldassarre's finger and placed on his own as his hand grew, but there was no valid good in such superstitious scruples regarding inanimate objects. The ring had facilitated his recognition, and Tito had begun to dislike recognition—a claim from the past. This foreigner's offer, if genuine, was an opportunity to dispose of the ring without the trouble of finding a buyer.

"You speak with your usual wisdom, Bratti," Tito said. "I have no objection to hearing what your Genoese offers. But when and where shall I meet him?"

"Tomorrow, three hours after sunrise, at my shop. If your wits are as sharp as I believe, Messer Greco, you will ask for a heavy price; he cares little for money. I believe he buys for someone else, perhaps a great *signor*."

"It is well," Tito agreed. "I will be at your shop, if nothing hinders."

"And you will doubtless deal nobly by me for old acquaintance's sake," Bratti added, refusing to fix the small fee for his service. "It seems a thousand years since I left the piazza; a fair is dull, not to say wicked, when one has no goods to sell."

Tito made a hasty sign of assent and moved away, once again swept toward the center of the square and back again, unable to determine his own course. In this zigzag motion, he was carried to the end of the piazza opposite the church, where an entertainment drew a deep crowd. Loud bursts of laughter interrupted a monologue that shifted from slow oratory to rattling buffoonery. A girl was pushed into an inner circle with apparent reluctance, while a laughing minx found her way with her elbows. A strange light bathed the square: pale stars above, dim waving lanterns below, rendering objects indistinct except when caught by the fitful glow. But in a recess, a stronger light cast the heads of spectators in dark relief as Tito was pushed toward them. Above them rose the head of a man wearing a white mitre with yellow cabalistic figures.

"Behold, my children!" the speaker cried. "Behold your opportunity! Neglect not the holy sacrament of matrimony when available for the small sum of a white *quattrino*—the cheapest marriage ever offered, dissolved by special bull at every man's will. Behold the bull!" He held up a parchment with huge seals. "Behold the indulgence granted by His Holiness Alexander the Sixth, newly elected for his piety, intending to reform the Church by abolishing the priestly abuse that hoards matrimony for the clergy. Spit once, my sons, and pay a white *quattrino*! This is the whole price. The *quattrino* is the only difference the Holy Father allows between us and the clergy—who spit and pay nothing."

Tito recognized the sharp ring of the voice, though the face remained shadowed. Stepping closer, he saw within the circle an altar-like table draped in red fabric stitched with yellow cabalistic figures. Half a dozen thin tapers burned behind it, alongside a conjuring apparatus, a large open book, and a monkey fastened by a cord to a small ring, holding a taper that flickered in its restless movements. An impish boy in a white surplice cuffed the monkey and adjusted the taper. The man in the mitre wore a surplice and a chasuble with rudely marked zodiac signs. Tito was certain now; it was Maestro Vaiano, the mountebank from whom he had rescued Tessa. *Pretty little Tessa!* Perhaps she was among the peasant women.

"Come, my maidens!" the conjuror continued. "This is the time for the pretty who have many chances, and the ill-favored who have few. Matrimony to be had—hot, eaten, and done with as easily as *berlingozzi*! And see!" He held up a cluster of tiny bags. "To every bride I give a *Breve* with a secret inside—the secret alone worth the money you pay. The secret how to... no, no, I will not tell you, making it a double secret. Hang it round your neck if you like; I don't say that won't be best, for then you will see unexpected things: though if you open it you may break your leg, *è vero*, but you will know a secret! Something nobody knows but me! Mark—I give you the *Breve*, I do not sell it. The *quattrino* is for the matrimony, and the *Breve* is free. *Orsù, giovanetti*, come like dutiful sons of the Church and buy the Indulgence of His Holiness Alexander the Sixth."

This buffoonery suited the audience perfectly. The *fierucola* was a minor occasion, so the townsfolk were content with jokes less indecent than those at carnival, rhymed by the Magnifico and his satellites. The women, however, truly began to crave the *Brevi*. Several couples had already undergone the ceremony, where the conjuror's solemn gibberish, grimaces over the open book, the monkey's antics, and the preliminary spitting had elicited peals of laughter. Now, a merry-eyed youth of seventeen in a loose tunic and red cap pushed forward, holding the hand of a plump brunette whose ragged dress revealed her round arms and legs.

"Fetter us without delay, Maestro!" the youth said. "I must take my bride home and paint her under a lantern's light."

"Ha! Mariotto, my son, I commend your pious observance..." The conjuror began, when a loud chattering warned him of a crisis with his monkey.

The temper of the imperfect acolyte was tested by his colleague's over-active discipline. A sudden cuff, delivered as the taper fell horizontally, caused the boy to leap back with violence, snapping the slackened knot of his cord. His first leap threw him to the other end of the table, his remonstrances so threatening that the imp in the surplice brandished a wand. The monkey leaped onto the head of a tall woman in the foreground, dropping his taper and chattering emphatically from that height. Screaming and confusion ensued; many spectators feared the Maestro's monkey, suspecting it of hidden mischief beyond mere teeth and claws. The conjuror himself was alarmed lest harm befall his familiar. In the scuffle to seize the monkey's string, Tito slipped out of the circle and, unwilling to contend for his place, allowed himself to be pushed toward the church of the Nunziata, joining the worshippers.

The brilliant illumination within pressed upon his eyes with palpable force after the pale, scattered lights of the piazza. For the first minute or two, he saw nothing distinct. That yellow splendor was supernatural and heavenly to many peasant women, accustomed to sleeping in twilight with mountains hiding half the sky; the uninterrupted chant from the choir was repose to the ear after the hellish hubbub outside. Gradually, the scene cleared, though a thin yellow haze from incense mingled with the breath of the multitude. In a chapel on the left, wreathed with silver lamps, the miraculous fresco of the Annunciation stood unveiled, appearing dark with the excess of surrounding light. The entire nave was filled with peasant women,

When Romola reached the threshold of San Marco, a friar awaited her. Monna Brigida withdrew into the adjacent church, and Romola was ushered to the door of the chapter-house in the outer cloister, the boundary beyond which no woman was permitted to pass.

As the heavy door swung open, the dim light from outside merged with the glow of two tapers set behind a narrow bed. There lay Fra Luca, his face wasted to an emaciated skeleton, the golden tonsure framing a skull-like visage. His deep-sunken hazel eyes were fixed upon a small crucifix held before him. He was propped up in a semi-sitting position. As Romola threw aside her veil, she became aware of another monk standing beside the bed, his black cowl pulled low, who retreated toward the door as she entered. In the background, a crucified figure loomed pale and high on the frescoed wall, its lower half surrounded by ghostly, sorrowful faces.

Then, her eyes met Fra Luca's as he lifted his gaze from the cross. A sharp pang of recognition struck her, binding this haggard monastic form to the memory of her fair young brother.

"Dino!" she cried, her voice a low, pained exclamation. Yet she did not lean toward him; she remained rigid, holding her distance at two yards. An insurmountable repulsion gripped her at the sight of him in this garb. To her, it seemed the mark of a cowardly abandonment that had left her father desolate—a groveling superstition that could rebrand such betrayal as piety. Her father, a man of proud sincerity and simple living, had raised her to ignore the Church's claim to govern the minds of rational beings. In her view, the Church belonged to the chaotic masses from which they had always separated. Consequently, she felt nothing but incurious, indignant contempt for her brother's path. Still, the loving nature of her soul clung to the image of the past, and despite her rigid stance, a yearning search flickered in her eyes for something too faint to grasp.

But the monk's face offered no such warmth. He regarded the little sister, returned to him in her full womanly beauty, with the distant gaze of a spirit revisiting a lost world.

"My sister!" he managed, his voice feeble and broken yet distinct. "It is well you have not delayed further. I have a message for you, and my time is short."

Romola stepped closer. She expected a confession of affectionate penitence directed at her father. Her heart began to soften; nothing could erase the long years of desertion, but a sinner looking back with the weight of irremediable wrong might evoke pity. At the end, surely, there would be understanding and forgiveness. She imagined Dino pouring out natural filial feelings, asking about his father's blindness, the rapidity of its onset, the long dark days, and the life in the home where he had been nourished. She anticipated a final message of tenderness and regret.

"Romola," Fra Luca began, "I have had a vision concerning you. Three times in the last two months, and each time it has grown clearer. I came from Fiesole believing it a divine mandate to deliver this. And I find a promise of mercy in this, that my breath is preserved to—"

His speech was cut short by the difficult, rattling breath that constantly interrupted him.

Romola felt her heart chill. It was a vision, then—a message born of those delusions her father had bitterly alluded to. Indignation surged in her throat.

"Dino, I thought you had words for my father. You abandoned him when his sight failed; you made his life a desert. Did you never care? Never repent? What is this religion of yours that places visions above natural duties?"

The deep-sunken eyes turned slowly toward her, resting upon her in silence for several moments, as if weighing the cost of an answer.

"No," he said finally, in a low, passionless tone, sounding less like a dying man and more like a spirit speaking through mortal organs. "No. I have never repented for fleeing the stifling, poisonous breath of sin that choked me, threatening to overwhelm my senses like besotting wine. My father could not hear the voice calling me night and day; he knew nothing of the demon-tempters trying to drag me back. He lived amidst human sin and misery without believing in them, like one busy picking shining stones in a mine while a world died of plague above him. I spoke, but he listened with scorn. I told him the studies he wished me to pursue were either childish trifles or must be fueled by worldly ambition and fleshly lusts—the very substances of the poetry and history he demanded I read."

"Has my father not led a pure and noble life?" Romola burst out, unable to remain silent against this implied accusation. "He sought no worldly honors; he was truthful; he denied himself all luxuries; he lived like an ancient sage. He never wished you to live for worldly ambitions or lusts. He wanted you to live as he did, according to the purest maxims of philosophy, in which he raised you."

Romola spoke partly from rote, as ardent young creatures often do, but with intense conviction. A pink flush colored her cheeks, and she quivered from head to foot. Her brother was slow to answer, regarding her passionate face with strange, unfeeling eyes.

"What were the maxims of philosophy to me? They told me to be strong when I felt weak; to roll myself among thorns and court smarting wounds as deliverance from temptation. For Divine love had sought me, penetrating me and creating a great need within me, like a seed demanding room to grow. I was raised in carelessness of the true faith; I had not studied our doctrines, yet they seemed to possess me like a rising flood. I felt there existed a life of perfect love and purity for the soul, free from uneasy hunger for pleasure, tormenting questions, or fear of suffering. Before knowing the history of the saints, I had a foreshadowing of their ecstasy. The same truth permeated even pagan philosophy: that it is a bliss within man's reach to die to mortal needs and live in the life of God as the Unseen Perfection. But to attain this, I must forsake the world. I must have no affection, no hope wedded to that which passes away. I must live with my fellow-beings only as human souls related to the eternal unseen life. This need urged me continually. It came over me in visions when my mind grew weary of the vain words recording the passions of dead men; it seized me after I had been tempted into sin and turned away with loathing from the scent of the emptied cup. In these visions, I saw the meaning of the Crucifix."

He paused, breathing hard for a minute or two. Romola did not speak. It was futile for her mind to attempt contact with this unearthly brother, as useless as trying to grasp a shadow. When he spoke again, his heaving chest was quieter.

"I felt whom I must follow. But I saw that even among the servants of the Cross who claimed to have renounced the world, my soul would be stifled by the fumes of hypocrisy, lust, and pride. God had not chosen me, as He chose Saint Dominic and Saint Francis, to wrestle with evil in the Church and the world. He called upon me to flee. I took the sacred vows and I fled—to lands where danger, scorn, and want bore me continually, like angels, to repose on the bosom of God. I lived as a hermit, ministering to pilgrims, but my task was short. The veil dividing me from my everlasting rest has worn very thin. I returned to Florence that—"

"Dino, you *did* want to know if my father was alive," Romola interrupted, the picture of that suffering life stirring in her again, sparking a desire for union and forgiveness.

"—That before I died, I might urge our brethren to study Eastern tongues, as I failed to do, and go forth to greater ends. I find them already bent on the work. And since I came, Romola, I have felt I was sent partly to you—not to renew bonds of earthly affection, but to deliver the heavenly warning conveyed in a vision. For I have had that vision thrice. Through all the years since the Divine voice first called me, while I was still in the world, I have been taught and guided by visions. For in the painful linking of our waking thoughts, we can never be sure we have not mingled our own errors with the light we prayed for; but in visions and dreams, we are passive, and our souls become instruments in the Divine hand. Therefore, listen, and speak no more—for the time is short."

Romola's mind recoiled from listening to this vision. Her indignation had subsided, only because she felt the chasm between herself and her brother widening. As Fra Luca spoke, the figure of another monk entered, standing on the opposite side of the bed with his cowl drawn low.

"Kneel, my daughter, for the Angel of Death is present and waits while the message of heaven is delivered. Bend your pride before it is bent for you by a yoke of iron," a strong, rich voice declared, startlingly contrasting with Fra Luca's frailty.

The tone was not imperious but one of quiet self-possession and assured right, blended with benignity. Romola, vibrating at the sound, turned to the figure on the other side. His face was barely discernible under the shadow of the cowl, but her eyes fell immediately on his hands, folded across his breast and resting on the edge of his black mantle. They possessed a marked physiognomy that enforced the voice's authority: very beautiful, almost of transparent delicacy. Romola's disposition to rebel against command, doubly active in the presence of monks she had been taught to despise, would have latched onto any repulsive detail as a point of support. But the face was hidden, and the hands seemed to hold an appeal against all hardness. The next moment, the right hand took the crucifix to relieve Fra Luca's fatigued grasp, while the left touched his lips with a wet sponge lying nearby. As he bent, the cowl was pushed back, revealing the features of the monk to the full light of the tapers. They were striking features, lending themselves to popular description: a high-arched nose, a prominent under-lip, a coronet of thick dark hair above the brow—all suggesting energy and passion. There were blue-grey eyes, shining mildly beneath auburn lashes, seeming, like the hands, to radiate acute sensitiveness. Romola was certain these were the features of Fra Girolamo Savonarola, the prior of San Marco, whom she had chiefly considered more offensive than other monks because he was more noisy. Her rebellion rose against the first impression, which had almost forced her to kneel.

"Kneel, my daughter," the penetrating voice repeated. "The pride of the body is a barrier against the gifts that purify the soul."

He looked at her with mild fixedness, and again she felt that subtle, mysterious influence of personality by which rare men move their fellows. Slowly, Romola fell to her knees. In the very act, a tremor seized her; in the renunciation of her proud erectness, her mental attitude seemed transformed, and she found herself in a new state of passiveness.

Her brother began to speak again.

"Romola, in the deep night, as I lay awake, I saw my father's room—the library—with all the books and the marbles and the leggio where I used to stand and read. And I saw you—you were revealed to me as I see you now, with fair long hair, sitting before my father's chair. And at the leggio stood a man whose face I could not see. I looked, and looked, and it was a blank to me, even as a painting effaced. I saw him move and take you, Romola, by the hand; and then I saw you take my father by the hand; and you all three went down the stone steps into the streets, the man whose face was a blank leading the way. You stood at the altar in Santa Croce, and the priest who married you had the face of death. The graves opened, and the dead in their shrouds rose and followed you like a bridal train. You passed on through the streets and gates into the valley, and it seemed to me that he who led you hurried you more than you could bear, and the dead were weary of following you, turning back to their graves. Finally, you came to a stony place where there was no water, no trees, no herbage. Instead of water, I saw written parchment unrolling everywhere; instead of trees and herbage, I saw men of bronze and marble springing up and crowding round you. My father was faint for want of water and fell to the ground. The man whose face was a blank loosed your hand and departed. As he went, I could see his face; it was the face of the Great Tempter. You, Romola, wrung your hands and sought for water, but there was none. The bronze and marble figures seemed to mock you and hold out cups of water. When you grasped them and put them to my father's lips, they turned to parchment. The bronze and marble figures seemed to turn into demons and snatch my father's body from you, and the parchments shriveled up, and blood ran everywhere instead of them, and fire upon the blood, till they all vanished, and the plain was bare and stony again, and you were alone in the midst of it. Then it seemed that the night fell, and I saw no more... Thrice I have had that vision, Romola. I believe it is a revelation meant for you: to warn you against marriage as a temptation of the enemy; it calls upon you to dedicate yourself—"

His pauses had grown longer and more frequent, until he was compelled to cease by a severe fit of gasping, his eyes turned on the crucifix as on a light vanishing. Presently, he found strength to speak again, but in a feebler, scarcely audible tone.

"To renounce the vain philosophy and corrupt thoughts of the heathens: for in the hour of sorrow and death their pride will

A Florentine Joke.

The morning sun rose warm and golden over the Piazza di San Giovanni, casting a spell of festive idleness upon the city. Yet, for Tito, the light offered no comfort. He had just returned from Bratti's shop in the narrow thoroughfare of the Ferravecchi, his pockets heavy with fifty florins but his spirit hollow. The Genoese stranger had departed with the onyx ring, leaving Tito with a bitter taste in his mouth. A fleeting thought crossed his mind: if Fortune had truly spared him the necessity of fleeing Florence, perhaps he should not have parted with the ring, for it was worn not merely as jewelry, but as a vessel for cherished memories and predilections. Still, the loss was a trivial matter compared to the weight of his current shame. The feverish excitement that had once driven him to look toward the future had cooled into a dull, regretful lassitude. Now, he envied the ignorance of those who had never risked ignominy by shrinking from the obligations his peers demanded.

Deeds, he realized with a sinking heart, were like children born to us; they lived and acted apart from our own will. While children might be strangled, deeds possessed an indestructible vitality, pressing hard upon the conscience whether we acknowledged them or not. This dreadful vitality was crushing Tito for the first time.

He turned away from the Mercato Vecchio, avoiding the nearest path lest he encounter Tessa. He was not in the mood to seek anything, only to await the first sign of his altered lot. The piazza itself was a tableau of life: stalls laden with fruit and flowers surrounded the octagonal Baptistery, while mules stood patiently in their nose-bags, their drivers perhaps already kneeling before the Virgin inside the sacred doors. On the broad marble steps of the Duomo, beggars and gossipers mingled. An old crone encouraged a round-capped baby to feel the warmth of the stone, while a suspicious dog sniffed nearby; shaggy-headed boys watched a pale cripple carving a face into a cherry-stone; and above them, men knotted ribbons in desultory laughter or gesticulated in earnest conversation.

Tito's destination, however, was the barber's shop of Nello. It was the busiest hour of the day, and under the pretty red and white awning, men preferred the fresh air to the narrow confines of the shop. To sit with a lathered chin thrown backward, having one's nose made a handle, was not a sublime posture, yet it was a fashion of Florentine respectability, and the men looked at each other with grave seriousness while undergoing the ritual. The barber's tongue was in its glory, the deft activity of the razor and the lather seeming set in motion by a common spring. Tito knew he could not escape the circle of conversation; he would have to smile, retort, and appear perfectly at ease. It was merely the ordeal of swallowing bread and cheese pills, he told himself. The man who let the anticipation of discovery choke him was simply weak of nerve.

Just as he prepared to enter, a hand laid heavily upon his shoulder. No amount of prior resolution could prevent the unpleasant shock of the sudden touch. Tito turned, his face betraying an inward start, only to find the owner of the hand breaking into a light laugh. It was a young man of Tito's own age, with keen features, a small, close-clipped head, and a close-shaven lip and chin that suggested a mind unburdened by non-essential matters. His eyes were bright with hope and friendliness, a brightness that would later fade into bitterness for many; at this moment, however, Niccolò Machiavelli was hailed as a man of promise destined to mend the fortunes of his ancient family.

"Why, Melema," Machiavelli said, "what evil dream did you have last night that you took my light grasp for that of an *sbirro* or something worse?"

"Ah, Messer Niccolò!" Tito recovered himself instantly. "It must have been an extra amount of dulness in my veins this morning that shuddered at the approach of your wit. But the fact is, I have had a bad night."

"That is unlucky, because you will be expected to shine without any obstructing fog today in the Rucellai Gardens. I take it for granted you are to be there."

"Messer Bernardo did me the honor to invite me," Tito replied, "but I shall be engaged elsewhere."

"Ah! I remember, you are in love," Machiavelli shrugged, "else you would never have such inconvenient engagements. Why, we are to eat a peacock and ortolans under the loggia among Bernardo Rucellai's rare trees; there are to be the choicest spirits in Florence and the choicest wines. Only, as Piero de' Medici is to be there, the choice spirits may happen to be swamped in the capping of impromptu verses. I hate that game; it is a device for the triumph of small wits, who are always inspired the most by the smallest occasions."

"What is that you are saying about Piero de' Medici and small wits, Messer Niccolò?" asked Nello, whose light figure dominated the Herculean frame of Niccolò Caparra.

The famous worker in iron, whom they had last seen with bared muscular arms and a leathern apron in the Mercato Vecchio, was now dressed in holiday attire. As he sat submissively while Nello skipped around him, lathering his chin, seizing his nose, and scraping him with magical quickness, he looked much as a lion might if it had donned linen and tunic to prepare for society.

"A private secretary will never rise in the world if he couples great and small in that way," Nello continued. "When great men are not allowed to marry their sons and daughters as they like, small men must not expect to marry their words as they like. Have you heard the news Domenico Cennini has been telling us?—that Pagolantonio Soderini has given Ser Piero da Bibbiena a box on the ear for setting on Piero de' Medici to interfere with the marriage between young Tommaso Soderini and Fiammetta Strozzi, and is to be sent ambassador to Venice as a punishment?"

"I don't know which I envy him most," Machiavelli mused, "the offense or the punishment. The offense will make him the most popular man in all Florence, and the punishment will take him among the only people in Italy who have known how to manage their own affairs."

"Yes, if Soderini stays long enough at Venice," Cennini added, "he may chance to learn the Venetian fashion, and bring it home with him. The Soderini have been fast friends of the Medici, but what has happened is likely to open Pagolantonio's eyes to the good of our old Florentine trick of choosing a new harness when the old one galls us; if we have not quite lost the trick in these last fifty years."

"Not we," said Niccolò Caparra, rejoicing in the free use of his lips again. "Eat eggs in Lent and the snow will melt. That's what I say to our people when they get noisy over their cups at San Gallo, and talk of raising a *romor* (insurrection): I say, never do you plan a *romor*; you may as well try to fill the Arno with buckets. When there's water enough, the Arno will be full, and that will not be till the torrent is ready."

"Caparra, that oracular speech of yours is due to my excellent shaving," Nello remarked. "You could never have made it with that dark rust on your chin. Ecco, Messer Domenico, I am ready for you now. By the way, my bel erudito," he continued as he saw Tito moving toward the door, "here has been old Maso seeking for you, but your nest was empty. He will come again presently. The old man looked mournful, and seemed in haste. I hope there is nothing wrong in the Via de' Bardi."

"Doubtless Messer Tito knows that Bardo's son is dead," said Cronaca, who had just come up.

Tito's heart gave a leap—had the death occurred before Romola saw him?

"No, I had not heard it," he said, with no more discomposure than the occasion warranted, turning and leaning against the doorpost as if he had abandoned his intention of leaving. "I knew that his sister had gone to see him. Did he die before she arrived?"

"No," said Cronaca. "I was in San Marco at the time, and saw her come out from the chapter-house with Fra Girolamo, who told us that the dying man's breath had been preserved as by a miracle, that he might make a disclosure to his sister."

Tito felt that his fate was decided. Again his mind rushed over all the circumstances of his departure from Florence, and he conceived a plan of retrieving his money from Cennini before the disclosure became public. If he once had his money, he need not stay long enduring scorching looks and biting words. He would wait now, go away with Cennini, and get the money from him at once. With this project in his mind, he stood motionless, hands in his belt, eyes fixed absently on the ground. Nello, glancing at him, felt sure that he was absorbed in anxiety about Romola. He thought Tito such a pretty image of self-forgetful sadness that he just perceptibly pointed his razor at him and gave a challenging look at Piero di Cosimo, whom he had never forgiven for refusing to see any prognostics of character in his favorite's handsome face. Piero, leaning against the other doorpost close to Tito, shrugged his shoulders; the frequent recurrence of such challenges from Nello had changed the painter's first declaration of neutrality into a positive inclination to believe ill of the much-praised Greek.

"So you have got your Fra Girolamo back again, Cronaca? I suppose we shall have him preaching again this next Advent," said Nello.

"And not before there is need," said Cronaca, gravely. "We have had the best testimony to his words since the last Quaresima; for even to the wicked, wickedness has become a plague; and the ripeness of vice is turning to rottenness in the nostrils even of the vicious. There has not been a change since the Quaresima, either in Rome or at Florence, but has put a new seal on the Frate's words—that the harvest of sin is ripe, and that God will reap it with a sword."

"I hope he has had a new vision, however," said Francesco Cei, sneeringly. "The old ones are somewhat stale. Can't your Frate get a poet to help out his imagination for him?"

"He has no lack of poets about him," said Cronaca, with quiet contempt, "but they are great poets and not little ones; so they are contented to be taught by him, and no more think the truth stale which God has given him to utter, than they think the light of the moon is stale. But perhaps certain high prelates and princes who dislike the Frate's denunciations might be pleased to hear that, though Giovanni Pico, and Poliziano, and Marsilio Ficino, and most other men of mark in Florence, reverence Fra Girolamo, Messer Francesco Cei despises him."

"Poliziano?" said Cei, with a scornful laugh. "Yes, doubtless he believes in your new Jonah; witness the fine orations he wrote for the envoys of Siena, to tell Alexander the Sixth that the world and the Church were never so well off as since he became Pope."

"Nay, Francesco," said Machiavelli, smiling, "a various scholar must have various opinions. And as for the Frate, whatever we may think of his saintliness, you judge his preaching too narrowly. The secret of oratory lies, not in saying new things, but in saying things with a certain power that moves the hearers—without which, as old Filelfo has said, your speaker deserves to be called, 'non oratorem, sed aratorem.' And, according to that test, Fra Girolamo is a great orator."

"That is true, Niccolò," said Cennini, speaking from the shaving-chair, "but part of the secret lies in the prophetic visions. Our people—no offense to you, Cronaca—will run after anything in the shape of a prophet, especially if he prophesies terrors and tribulations."

"Rather say, Cennini," answered Cronaca, "that the chief secret lies in the Frate's pure life and strong faith, which stamp him as a messenger of God."

"I admit it—I admit it," said Cennini, opening his palms as he rose from the chair. "His life is spotless: no man has impeached it."

"He is satisfied with the pleasant lust of arrogance," Cei burst out bitterly. "I can see it in that proud lip and satisfied eye of his. He hears the air filled with his own name—Fra Girolamo Savonarola, of Ferrara; the prophet, the saint, the mighty preacher, who frightens the very babies of Florence into laying down their wicked baubles."

"Come, come, Francesco, you are out of humor with waiting," said the conciliatory Nello. "Let me stop your mouth with a little lather. I must not have my friend Cronaca made angry: I have a regard for his chin; and his chin is in no respect altered since he became a Piagnone. And for my own part, I confess, when the Frate was preaching in the Duomo last Advent, I got into such a trick of slipping in to listen to him that I might have turned Piagnone too, if I had not been hindered by the liberal nature of my art; and also by the length of the sermons, which are sometimes a good while before they get to the moving point. But, as Messer Niccolò here says, the Frate lays hold of the people by some power over and above his prophetic visions. Monks and nuns who prophesy are not of that rareness. For what says Luigi

Under the Loggia.

The upper loggia of Bardo's house rose like a crown above the surrounding structures, forming a sheltered gallery around its quadrangular walls. On the street-facing side, columns bore the weight of the roof, while the rear opened into a series of arched windows, offering a view over the chaotic jumble of poorly built dwellings toward the hill of Bogoli. It was here, near one of these arches close to the entrance, that Tito waited. The sunlight slanted across him with a sickening intensity, mingling with the ruin of his hopes. He had never truly believed that Romola's passion could overcome the repulsion born of his secret; he lacked the vanity to imagine her love rooted in ignorance rather than truth. Yet, as he pictured her approaching in her radiant beauty, softened by those soft hazel eyes, a selfish wish took hold: he wished she were lower, merely mortal enough to allow him to clasp and kiss her before they parted. He had known only fleeting glances and brief touches from her, a hunger for solitude with her that had grown insatiable. Now, they were to be alone, yet she stood there, seemingly inexorably distant.

Then the door moved. Romola appeared. It struck Tito not as a sight but as a flash of lightning; he felt the glory haloing her head, the tearful appeal in her gaze, and heard the cry of love on her lips—"Tito!"—before she was already in his arms, sobbing against him.

How desperately Romola had longed through the sleepless nights to see that bright face! The new specter of death, the bewildering doubt stirred by tales of a life alien to her understanding, and the haunting vision she seemed to inhabit as her own dream had sharpened her awareness. It was Tito who had first brought the warm stream of hope into her life, turning away the keen edge of pain regarding her brother. She would tell him everything; there was no one else. Restrained all morning before her father, the dam of her emotions finally broke. Proud and controlled to the outside world, Romola was as simple and unreserved as a child in her love for Tito. Content with days of silent glances, she now felt the urgent need to cling to him, with no thought hindering her.

"My Romola! my goddess!" Tito murmured, clasping her gently and kissing the thick golden ripples of her hair. He was in paradise; disgrace, shame, and parting held no fear. This happiness was too potent to be marred by the knowledge that she was deceived; indeed, he rejoiced in her delusion, for concealment had been wisdom. He could only regret his needless dread, or perhaps, that the dread had been worth suffering for the sake of this sudden rapture.

The sobbing ceased, and Romola raised her head. They stood gazing at one another with the sweet wonder of young love—she with her long white hands in his dark-brown curls, he with his fingers bathed in streaming gold. Each was beautiful to the other, experiencing that undisturbed mutual consciousness for the first time. A new, cold sadness pressed on Romola's heart, making her linger in the silent soothing of their nearness, while Tito dared not press his lips to hers, for that would be a change.

"Tito," she said finally, "it has been altogether painful, but I must tell you everything. Your strength will help me to resist impressions that reason cannot shake."

"I know, Romola—I know he is dead," Tito replied. His long, lustrous eyes revealed nothing of the many wishes that would have ended that life long ago if mere desire held such potency. Romola read her own pure thoughts in their dark depths as one reads letters in a happy dream.

"So changed, Tito! It pierced me to think it was Dino. And so strangely hard: not a word to my father, nothing but a vision he wanted to tell me. Yet it was piteous—the struggling breath, the eyes that seemed to look toward the crucifix and yet not see it. I shall never forget it; it seems as if it will stand between me and everything I shall ever look at."

Her heart swelled again, forcing her to pause, but the need to disburden her mind urged her to repress the rising anguish. When she spoke again, her thoughts had traveled slightly.

"It was strange, Tito. The vision was about our marriage, and yet he knew nothing of you."

"What was it, my Romola? Sit down and tell me," Tito said, leading her to the nearby bench. A fear had crossed him that the vision might somehow relate to Baldassarre, prompting this sudden shift in position.

Romola recounted everything from her entry into San Marco, omitting not a single word of her brother's speech, which had burned itself into her memory. But at the end of the vision, she paused; the rest remained too vivid to utter, and she sat staring into the distance, almost unaware of Tito's presence. His mind was now at ease; the vague vision had passed over him like white mist, leaving no mark. He remained silent, waiting for her to continue.

"I took it," she continued, as if he had read her thoughts. "I took the crucifix; it is down below in my bedroom."

"And now, my Romola," Tito entreated, "banish these ghastly thoughts. The vision was an ordinary monkish hallucination, bred of fasting and fanatical ideas. It surely holds no weight for you."

"No, Tito; no. But poor Dino, *he* believed it was a divine message. It is strange," she mused, "this life of men possessed by fervid beliefs that seem like madness to others. Dino was not a vulgar fanatic; and that Fra Girolamo—his very voice seems to have penetrated me with a sense that there is some truth in what moves them: some truth of which I know nothing."

"It was only because your feelings were highly wrought, my Romola. Your brother's state of mind was no more than a form of that theosophy which has been the common disease of excitable dreamers in all ages; the same ideas your father's old antagonist, Marsilio Ficino, pores over in the New Platonists. Only your brother's passionate nature drove him to act out what others merely write and talk about. As for Fra Girolamo, he is simply a narrow-minded monk with a gift for preaching and instilling terror. Any words or voice would have shaken you at that moment. When your mind has rested, you will judge such things as you have always done."

"Not about poor Dino," Romola said. "I was angry with him; my heart seemed to close against him while he spoke, but since then I have thought less of my own mind and more of his. Oh, Tito! it was very piteous to see his young life ending thus. That yearning look at the crucifix when he gasped for breath—I can never forget it. Last night I stared at the crucifix for a long while, trying to see how it could help him, until by the lamplight it seemed to me as if the suffering face shed pity."

"My Romola, promise me to resist such thoughts; they are fit for sickly nuns, not for my golden-tressed Aurora, who looks made to scatter all such twilight fantasies. Try not to think of them now; we shall not long be alone together."

His final words were uttered in a tone of tender beseeching, and he turned her face toward him with a gentle touch of his right hand. Romola had been gazing absently at the arched opening, failing to see the distant hill, having remained mentally in the chapter house, contemplating the pale images of sorrow and death.

Tito's touch and voice recalled her. Now, in the warm sunlight, she perceived that rich dark beauty that seemed to gather around it all images of joy—purple vines festooned between elms, strong corn perfecting itself under the vibrating heat, bright winged creatures hurrying among flowers, round limbs beating the earth in gladness with cymbals aloft, light melodies chanted to the thrilling rhythm of strings—all signs of Nature reveling in her force. It was a strange, bewildering transition from those pale images of sorrow to this bright youthfulness, like a sun-god knowing nothing of night. What thought could reconcile the worn anguish in her brother's face, that straining after something invisible, with this satisfied strength and beauty, making it intelligible that they belonged to the same world? Or was there never any reconciliation, only a blind worship of clashing deities, first in mad joy and then in wailing? For the first time, Romola felt this questioning need like a sudden uneasy dizziness, a want of something to grasp. It was an experience hardly longer than a sigh, for the eager theorizing of ages is compressed, as in a seed, into the momentary want of a single mind. But there was no answer to meet the need, and it vanished before the returning rush of young sympathy with the glad, loving beauty beaming upon her in new radiance, like the dawn after one has looked away from it to the grey west.

"Your mind lingers apart from our love, my Romola," Tito said with a soft, reproachful murmur. "It seems a forgotten thing to you."

She looked at the beseeching eyes in silence until the sadness melted from her own.

"My joy!" she said in her full, clear voice.

"Do you really care for me enough, then, to banish those chill fancies, or shall you always be suspecting me as the Great Tempter?" Tito asked, smiling brightly.

"How should I not care for you more than for everything else? Everything I had felt before in all my life—about my father, about my loneliness—was a preparation to love you. You would laugh at me, Tito, if you knew what sort of man I used to think I should marry—some scholar with deep lines in his face, like Alamanno Rinuccini, with rather grey hair, who would agree with my father in taking the side of the Aristotelians and be willing to live with him. I used to think about the love read of in poets, but I never dreamed anything like that could happen to me here in Florence in our old library. And then *you* came, Tito, and were so much to my father, and I began to believe that life could be happy for me too."

"My goddess! is there any woman like you?" Tito exclaimed, admiring the blended majesty and simplicity in her.

"But, dearest," he continued, somewhat timidly, "if you minded more about our marriage, you would persuade your father and Messer Bernardo not to think of any more delays. But you seem not to mind."

"Yes, Tito, I will, I do mind. But I am sure my godfather will urge more delay now because of Dino's death. He has never agreed with my father about disowning Dino, and you know he has always said we ought to wait until you have been at least a year in Florence. Do not think hardly of my godfather. I know he is prejudiced and narrow, yet he is very noble. He has often said it is folly in my father to want to keep his library apart so it may bear his name; yet he would try to carry out my father's wish. That seems to me very great and noble—that power of respecting a feeling which he does not share or understand."

"I have no rancour against Messer Bernardo for thinking you too precious for me, my Romola," Tito said, and that was true. "But your father, then, knows of his son's death?"

"Yes, I told him—I could not help it. I told him where I had been and that I had seen Dino die, but nothing else; and he has commanded me not to speak of it again. But he has been very silent this morning, and has had those restless movements which always go to my heart; they look as if he were trying to get outside the prison of his blindness. Let us go to him now. I had persuaded him to try to sleep, because he sleeps little at night. Your voice will soothe him, Tito: it always does."

"And not one kiss? I have not had one," Tito said in his gentle, reproachful tone, which gave him an air of dependence very charming in a creature with such rare gifts that seemed to excuse presumption.

A sweet pink blush spread quickly over Romola's face and neck as she bent toward him. It seemed impossible that their kisses could ever become common things.

"Let us walk once round the loggia," Romola said, "before we go down."

"There is something grim and grave to me always about Florence," Tito remarked as they paused before the house, looking over the opposite roofs to the other side of the river. "Even in its merriment there is something shrill and hard—biting rather than gay. I wish we lived in Southern Italy, where thought is broken, not by weariness, but by delicious languors such as never seem to come over the 'ingenia acerrima Florentina.' I should like to see you under that southern sun, lying among the flowers, subdued into mere enjoyment, while I bent over you and touched the lute and sang to you some little unconscious strain that seemed all one with the light and the warmth. You have never known that happiness of the nymphs, my Romola."

"No; but I have dreamed of it often since you came. I am very thirsty for a deep draught of joy—for a life all bright like you. But we will not think of it now, Tito; it seems to me as if there would always be pale sad faces among the flowers, and eyes that look in vain. Let us go."

The Portrait.

Tito departed from the Via de' Bardi that day with a heart swelling in exultant satisfaction, convinced at last that the shadow of threatened peril had been entirely dispelled. Yet, as the immediate presence of Romola and her father faded from his view, his thoughts drifted back to those futile hours of dread. He recalled with a shudder the actions he had taken, realizing now that he would never have parted with his ring had he possessed true foresight. To surrender a gem he had professed to cherish would have appeared sordid to Romola and her circle; he would have needed to feign a noble purpose, a desire to make a special gift with the proceeds. At that moment, however, Tito felt a nauseating weariness of all simulation. He was safely clear of the consequences that might have arisen from that initial deception, and the weight of it had vanished. Kind fortune had granted him immunity, and he felt it was only fair that she should reap the reward. Who truly suffered? The results for Baldassarre remained too murky to calculate, but Tito desired now to be free from any hidden shackles that might chafe beneath his ties to Romola. Unaware that the very delight in this immunity was deadening the sensibilities required to save him from future entanglements, he moved forward with a lightness he did not fully comprehend.

After all, the sale of the ring was a trifling matter. Was it equally slight that little Tessa lived under a delusion, her small head filled with expectations destined for disappointment? Should he seek her out alone to undeceive her immediately, or leave the revelation to time and chance? Happy dreams were pleasant, yet they ended swiftly with the daylight and the stir of life. The image of her—sweet, pouting, innocent, and round—was impossible to banish from his mind. He considered taking her a present to cheer her, perhaps to observe if her stepfather treated her more cruelly now that her mother was gone. Or, he mused, should he undeceive her at once and then inform Romola, so they might secure a happier fate for the poor child? No. That unfortunate incident involving the *cerretano* and the marriage, compounded by his allowing Tessa to depart in delusion, must never be known to Romola. Since no enlightenment could erase the memory from Tessa's mind, there would always be a risk of betrayal; moreover, even a child might harbor gall when her love proved unrequited. Yes, she must be in love with him, and that made it prudent that he see her no more. Yet, it was merely a trifling adventure, the sort a country girl might ponder until some ruddy peasant won her affection, at which point she would break her silence and discover her freedom. *Dunque*—good-bye, Tessa! Kindest wishes! Tito resolved that the silly affair of the *cerretano* would bear no further consequences for himself. People often believed their own resolutions to be ironclad. As for the fifty-five florins, the purchase-money for the ring, he had already decided what to do with a portion of it. He conceived an ingenious plan that would ease his conscience regarding the missing ring and serve as a safeguard against the recurrence of Romola's monkish fancies, which were especially repugnant to him. With this purpose fixed in his mind, he turned toward the Via Gualfonda to seek Piero di Cosimo, the artist whose mastery of fantastic mythological design was essential to Tito's scheme.

Entering the courtyard where Piero's dwelling stood, Tito observed the heavy iron knocker, thickly bound in wool and ingeniously secured with cords. Remembering the painter's habit of stuffing his ears against intrusive noise, Tito was unsurprised by this defense against visitors' thunder. He tapped modestly with his knuckles, then escalated to a more importunate shaking of the door. In vain! Just as Tito prepared to withdraw, blaming himself for wasting time on a visit he could wait for at Nello's, a little girl emerged carrying a basket of eggs. She approached the door, stood on tiptoe, and pushed up a small iron plate sliding in grooves. Peering through the aperture, she called out in a piping voice, "Messer Piero!"

Moments later, the bolts clicked, the door swung open, and Piero appeared in a red night-cap and a loose brown serge tunic, sleeves rolled to the shoulder. He darted a look of surprise at Tito but, without further acknowledgment, reached out to take the basket from the child. Re-entering the house, he soon returned with the empty basket, asking, "How much to pay?"

"Two grossoni, Messer Piero; they are all ready boiled, my mother says."

Piero extracted the coins from the leather pouch at his belt, and the little maiden trotted away, casting upward glances of awed admiration at the surprising young signor.

Piero's gaze, however, was far less complimentary as he addressed Tito. "What do you want at my door, Messer Greco? I saw you this morning at Nello's; if you had asked me then, I could have told you that I see no man in this house without knowing his business and agreeing with him beforehand."

"Pardon, Messer Piero," Tito replied with his characteristic imperturbable good humor. "I acted without sufficient reflection. I remembered nothing but your admirable skill in inventing pretty caprices, when a sudden desire for something of that sort prompted me to come to you."

The painter's manners were notoriously odd to everyone, so this reception was not a special affront. Even if Tito had suspected offensive intent, the impulse to resentment would have been weaker than his desire to conquer goodwill. Piero made a grimace habitual to him when spoken to with flattering suavity. He grinned, stretching the corners of his mouth and pressing down his brows to defy any divination of his feelings beneath that kind of stroking.

"And what may that need be?" he asked after a pause. In his heart, he was tempted by the hinted opportunity to apply his invention.

"I require a very delicate miniature device drawn from certain fables of the poets, which you will know how to combine for me. It must be painted on a wooden case—I will show you the size—in the form of a triptych. The interior may be simple gilding; it is on the exterior that I wish the device. It is a favorite subject with you Florentines—the triumph of Bacchus and Ariadne—but I desire it treated in a new way. A story in Ovid will provide the necessary hints. Young Bacchus must be seated in a ship, his head bound with clusters of grapes, a spear entwined with vine-leaves in his hand: dark-berried ivy must wind about the masts and sails, the oars must be thyrsi, and flowers must wreathe themselves about the poop; leopards and tigers must crouch before him, and dolphins sport around. But I want the fair-haired Ariadne beside him, made immortal with her golden crown—that is not in Ovid's story, but no matter, you will conceive it all—and above them, young Loves, such as you know how to paint, shooting with roses at the points of their arrows—"

"Say no more!" Piero interrupted. "I have Ovid in the vulgar tongue. Find me the passage. I love not to be choked with other men's thoughts. You may come in."

Piero led the way through the first room, where a basket of eggs rested on the open hearth near a heap of broken shells and a bank of ashes. In strange keeping with that sordid litter stood a low bedstead of carved ebony, covered carelessly with a piece of rich oriental carpet that looked as if it had once served to cover the steps to a Madonna's throne. Beside it was a carved *cassone*, or large chest, adorned with painted devices on its sides and lid. There was hardly any other furniture in the large room except casts, wooden steps, easels, and rough boxes, all festooned with cobwebs.

The next room was larger but even more crowded. Apparently, Piero was keeping the Festa, for the double door beneath the window, which admitted the painter's light from above, was thrown open, revealing a garden—or rather a thicket—where fig-trees and vines grew in tangled, trailing wildness among nettles and hemlocks, and a tall cypress lifted its dark head from a stifling mass of yellowish mulberry leaves. It seemed as if that dank luxuriance had begun to penetrate even within the walls of the wide, lofty room. In one corner, amidst a confused heap of carved marble fragments and rusty armor, tufts of long grass and dark feathery fennel had made their way, and a large stone vase, tilted on one side, seemed to be pouring out the ivy that streamed around. Hanging about the walls were pen and oil sketches of fantastic sea-monsters; dances of satyrs and maenads; Saint Margaret's resurrection from the devouring dragon; Madonnas bathed in supernal light; studies of plants and grotesque heads. On irregular, rough shelves, a few books were scattered among great drooping bunches of corn, bullocks' horns, pieces of dried honeycomb, stones with patches of rare-colored lichen, skulls and bones, peacock feathers, and large birds' wings. Rising from the dirty litter of the floor were life-sized figures: one in the frock of a Vallombrosan monk, strangely surmounted by a helmet with a barred visor, another smothered in brocade and skins hastily tossed over it. Among this heterogeneous still life, several speckled and white pigeons were perched or strutting, too tame to fly at the entrance of men; three corpulent toads crawled in an intimate, friendly manner near the door-stone; and a white rabbit, apparently the model for the creature frightening Cupid in the picture of Mars and Venus placed on the central easel, twitched its nose with content on a box full of bran.

"And now, Messer Greco," Piero said, gesturing for Tito to sit on a low stool near the door, then standing over him with folded arms, "do not try to see everything at once, like Messer Domeneddio, but let me know how large you would have this same triptych."

Tito indicated the required dimensions, and Piero marked them on a piece of paper.

"And now for the book," Piero said, reaching down a manuscript volume.

"There's nothing about the Ariadne there," Tito replied, handing over the passage. "But you will remember I want the crowned Ariadne by the side of the young Bacchus: she must have golden hair."

"Ha!" Piero said abruptly, pursing his lips again. "And you want them to be likenesses, eh?" he added, looking down into Tito's face.

Tito laughed and blushed. "I know you are great at portraits, Messer Piero; but I could not ask Ariadne to sit for you, because the painting is a secret."

"There it is! I want her to sit to me. Giovanni Vespucci wants me to paint him a picture of Oedipus and Antigone at Colonos, as he has expounded it to me: I have a fancy for the subject, and I want Bardo and his daughter to sit for it. Now, you ask them; and then I'll put the likeness into Ariadne."

"Agreed, if I can prevail with them. And your price for the Bacchus and Ariadne?"

"Baie! If you get them to let me paint them, that will pay me. I'd rather not have your money: you may pay for the case."

"And when shall I sit for you?" Tito asked. "For if we have one likeness, we must have two."

"I don't want your likeness; I've got it already," Piero replied. "Only I've made you look frightened. I must take the fright out of it for Bacchus."

As he spoke, Piero laid down the book and went to look among some paintings propped against the wall. He returned with an oil-sketch in his hand.

"I call this as good a bit of portrait as I ever did," he said, examining it as he advanced. "Yours is a face that expresses fear well, because it's naturally a bright one. I noticed it the first time I saw you. The rest of the picture is hardly sketched; but I've painted *you* in thoroughly."

Piero turned the sketch and held it toward Tito's eyes. He saw himself with his right hand uplifted, holding a wine-cup, in the attitude of triumphant joy, yet with his face turned away from the cup, displaying an expression of such intense fear in the dilated eyes and pallid lips that a cold stream ran through his veins, as if he were being thrown into sympathy with his imaged self.

"You are beginning to look like it already," Piero said with a short laugh, moving the picture away again. "He's seeing a ghost—that fine young man. I shall finish it some day, when I've settled what sort of ghost is the most terrible—whether it should look solid, like a dead man come to life, or half transparent, like a mist."

Tito, somewhat ashamed of himself for a sudden sensitiveness strangely opposed to his usual easy self-command, said carelessly, "That is a subject after your own heart, Messer Piero—a revel interrupted by a ghost. You seem to love the blending of the terrible with the gay. I suppose that is the reason your shelves are so well furnished with death's-heads, while you paint those roguish Loves running away with the armor of Mars. I begin to think you are a Cynic philosopher in the pleasant disguise of a cunning painter."

"Not I, Messer Greco; a philosopher is the last sort of animal I should choose to resemble. I find it enough to live, without spinning lies to account for life. Fowls cackle, asses bray, women chatter, and philosophers spin false reasons—that's the effect the sight of the world brings out of them. Well, I am an animal that paints instead of cackling, or braying, or spinning lies. And now, I think, our business is done; you'll keep to your side of the bargain about the Oedipus and Antigone?"

"I will do my best,"

Messer Bernardo del Nero remained as steadfast in his counsel as Romola had anticipated: the marriage must wait until Easter. In this, Bardo yielded entirely to the guidance of his wise and pragmatic friend. Yet, even Bernardo, though immune to the personal charm Tito exerted over others, could not wholly ignore the seductive argument of success that holds sway among men of the world. Tito was ascending rapidly through the highest circles. He had won the favor of the young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, who had even hinted at inviting him into his learned retinue for an upcoming journey to Rome. This bright Greek, whose tongue was ever ready yet never quarrelsome, became a cherished presence at the lavish suppers of the Via Larga and at Florentine games where he did not claim to excel, yet admired the incomparable skill of Piero de' Medici with the utmost grace. Through an unbroken sequence of events, Tito's reputation as an agreeable companion in "magnificent" society rendered his learning and talent all the more radiant; he possessed genuine accomplishment sufficient to prevent any exaggerated estimation from proving hazardous. Messer Bernardo, burdened by old prejudices and attachments, found these arguments slowly dismantling his newer, weaker reservations regarding the young Greek stranger, who was perhaps a little too supple. To the aged Florentine, it became impossible to disregard the recommendation of standing well with the most distinguished families. Since Tito was now fully received into that circle whose views constituted the unquestioned standard of social value, it seemed irrational to deny that there was no longer any obstacle to satisfaction in the prospect of such a son-in-law for Bardo, and such a husband for Romola.

It was undeniable that Tito's arrival had marked the dawn of a new life for both father and daughter, surpassing even the first promise.
