The blind old scholar—whose proud truthfulness would never descend into the commerce of feigned and preposterous admiration that formed the fabric of learned intercourse, varied only by a corresponding excess in vituperation—had fallen into neglect even among his fellow citizens. When mentioned, it was usually said that, while his blindness and the loss of his son were pitiable misfortunes, he was tiresome in contending for the value of his own labors; that his discontent was somewhat inconsistent in a man who had once been openly indifferent to religious rites and who, in days past, had refused offers from various quarters on the slight condition that he take orders, without which it was difficult for patrons to support a scholar. But since Tito's coming, the monotony of thought surrounding Bardo's name had vanished. The old man was understood to have ceased his plaints, and the fair daughter was no longer to be confined in dowerless pride, waiting for a *parentado*. The winning manners and growing favor of the handsome Greek, expected to enter into the dual relation of son and husband, helped transform the new interest into something thoroughly friendly. It was no longer rare for a visitor to enliven the quiet library. Elderly men came from an indefinite prompting to renew former intercourse that arises when an old acquaintance begins to be newly talked about; and young men whom Tito had asked permission to bring once found it easy to return when they overtook him on his way to the Via de' Bardi, resting their hands on his shoulder and falling into easy chat with him. For it was pleasant to behold Romola's beauty; to see her, like the type of womanly majesty described by old Firenzuola, "sitting with a certain grandeur, speaking with gravity, smiling with modesty, and casting around, as it were, an odour of queenliness;" and she seemed to unfold like a strong white lily under this genial breath of admiration and homage. It mattered little to her amidst her new bright life in Tito's love.

Tito had even become the instrument of strengthening the hope in Bardo's mind that he might, before his death, receive the longed-for security concerning his library: that it should not be merged into another collection, nor transferred to a body of monks to bear the name of a monastery, but should remain forever the Bardi Library, for the use of the Florentines. For the old habit of trusting in the Medici could not die out while their influence remained the strongest lever in the State; and Tito, once possessing the ear of Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, might accomplish more even than Messer Bernardo towards winning the desired interest, for he could demonstrate to a learned audience the peculiar value of Bardi's collection. Tito himself spoke sanguinely of such a result, eager to cheer the old man, and conscious that Romola repaid those gentle words to her father with a sort of adoration that no direct tribute to herself could have won.

This question of the library became the subject of numerous discussions between Bernardo del Nero and Romola as Christmas passed and the prospect of the marriage drew near—but always out of Bardo's hearing. For Bardo nursed a vague belief, which they dared not disturb, that his property, apart from the library, was adequate to meet all demands. He would not even, except under a momentary pressure of angry despondency, admit to himself that the will by which he had disinherited Dino would leave Romola the heir of nothing but debts; or that he needed anything from patronage beyond the security that a separate locality should be assigned to his library, in return for a deed of gift by which he made it over to the Florentine Republic.

"My opinion is," said Bernardo to Romola during a consultation beneath the loggia, "that since you are to be married, and Messer Tito will have a competent income, we should begin to wind up affairs and ascertain exactly the sum necessary to save the library from being touched, instead of letting the debts accumulate any longer. Your father needs nothing but his shred of mutton and his macaroni every day, and I think Messer Tito may engage to supply that for the years that remain; he can let it be in place of the *morgen-cap*."

"Tito has always known that my life is bound up with my father's," said Romola. "And he is better to my father than I am; he delights in making him happy."

"Ah, he is not made of the same clay as other men, is he?" said Bernardo, smiling. "Thy father has thought of shutting woman's folly out of thee by cramming thee with Greek and Latin; but thou hast been as ready to believe in the first pair of bright eyes and the first soft words that have come within reach of thee, as if thou couldst say nothing by heart but Paternosters, like other Christian men's daughters."

"Now, godfather," said Romola, shaking her head playfully, "as if it were only bright eyes and soft words that made me love Tito! You know better. You know I love my father and you because you are both good, and I love Tito too because he is so good. I see it, I feel it, in everything he says and does. And if he is handsome, too, why should I not love him the better for that? It seems to me beauty is part of the finished language by which goodness speaks. You know *you* must have been a very handsome youth, godfather"—she looked up with one of her happy, loving smiles at the stately old man—"you were about as tall as Tito, and you had very fine eyes; only you looked a little sterner and prouder, and—"

"And Romola likes to have all the pride to herself?" said Bernardo, not inaccessible to this pretty coaxing. "However, it is well that in one way Tito's demands are more modest than those of any Florentine husband of fitting rank that we should have been likely to find for you; he wants no dowry."

Thus it was settled between Messer Bernardo del Nero, Romola, and Tito. Bardo assented with a wave of the hand when Bernardo told him that he thought it would be well now to begin to sell property and clear off debts; being accustomed to think of debts and property as a sort of thick wood that his imagination never even penetrated, still less got beyond. And Tito set about winning Messer Bernardo's respect by inquiring, with his ready faculty, into Florentine money-matters, the secrets of the *Monti* or public funds, the values of real property, and the profits of banking.

"You will soon forget that Tito is not a Florentine, godfather," said Romola. "See how he is learning everything about Florence."

"It seems to me he is one of the *demoni*, who are of no particular country, child," said Bernardo, smiling. "His mind is a little too nimble to be weighted with all the stuff we men carry about in our hearts."

Romola smiled too, in happy confidence.

The final week of the Carnival had swollen the veins of Florence to bursting. The streets thrummed with a chaotic vitality, a cacophony of masqued processions chanting songs introduced by Lorenzo the Magnificent, and the *rigoletto*, a favored round dance footed beneath a pale, frosty sky. Practical jokes ranged from the harmless scattering of comfits to the malicious hurling of stones. The boys and striplings, a formidable element in Florentine crowds, had reached the height of their unmanageable energy, acting like tree-crickets in their noise. Their immemorial privilege was to bar the path with poles until a tribute was paid for suppers and bonfires, culminating in the standing entertainment of stone-throwing. This was not a monotonous affair; the resulting maiming varied, and it was not uncommon for a single casualty to be killed. Thus, the pleasures of the Carnival were checkered, a grotesque tapestry of grossness and barbarity that a painter would struggle to render without turning the canvas to the wall, save for the grave historical purpose of justifying a reforming zeal often condemned for its narrowness. Yet, amidst this, there remained a more innocent, picturesque merriment, born of a people with quick animal spirits and sensitive organs, lacking the heavy sottishness of the north or the stealthy fierceness of the south that turns a brawl into a dagger-thrust.

It was high morning, yet the carnival spirits lingered in a lazy recapitulation of the night's jests. Tito Melema walked briskly toward the Via de' Bardi, accompanied by Young Bernardo Dovizi—the keen-eyed Cardinal da Bibbiena of later portraits. They held an animated discourse on a subject seemingly unrelated to the sights and sounds pushing past them along the Por' Santa Maria. Yet, as they discussed, smiled, and gesticulated, both cast frequent, quick glances around. At the turn toward the Lung' Arno, leading to the Ponte Rubaconte, Tito's rapid survey revealed someone he desperately wished to avoid recognition at that precise moment. His mind was thoroughly preoccupied; he looked forward to a unique occasion in his life: his betrothal, set for the evening. The ceremony had been resolved upon suddenly. While preparations had been underway for some time—chiefly Tito's application of florins to furnish rooms in Bardo's dwelling, which had always been scantily furnished save for the library—it had been intended to defer both betrothal and marriage until after Easter, when Tito's year of probation, insisted upon by Bernardo del Nero, would conclude. However, an express proposition arrived inviting Tito to follow Cardinal Giovanni to Rome to assist Bernardo Dovizi with his superior knowledge of Greek in arranging a library. With no possibility of declining what lay so plainly on the road to advancement, Tito became urgent in his entreaties that the betrothal occur before his departure. There would be less delay upon his return, and it would be less painful to part if he and Romola were outwardly as well as inwardly pledged, possessing a claim that defied Messer Bernardo or anyone else to nullify. For the betrothal, with rings exchanged and mutual contracts signed, constituted more than half the legality of marriage, to be completed later by the nuptial benediction. Romola's feelings met Tito's in this wish, and the consent of the elders was won.

Now, Tito hastened, amidst arrangements for his departure the next day, to snatch a morning visit to Romola, to exchange any last words needed before their evening meeting. It was not a time when any recognition could be pleasant, let alone one by Tessa. It was unmistakably Tessa whom he caught sight of moving with a timid, forlorn look toward that very turn of the Lung' Arno. As he continued his talk with the young Dovizi, an uncomfortable undercurrent of consciousness told him Tessa had seen him and would certainly follow. There was no escaping her along this direct road by the Arno and over the Ponte Rubaconte. She would not dare to speak or approach while he was not alone, so he continued to keep Dovizi with him until they reached Bardo's door. He quickened his pace, weaving new threads of conversation, yet the sense that Tessa trailed behind him, though physically unseen, grew stronger and more irritating. Perhaps it was worse because a certain tenderness and pity for the poor little thing made the determination to escape without visible notice an unagreeable resource. Yet Tito persevered, carrying his companion to the door, cleverly managing his "addio" without turning his face in a direction where importunate blue eyes might catch him. Ascending the stone steps, he tried to banish unpleasant thoughts by convincing himself that Tessa might not have seen him, or, if she had, might not have followed.

But perhaps because that possibility could not be relied upon strongly, when the visit ended, he emerged with a quick step and an air of unconsciousness regarding anything to his right or left. Human eyes, however, take in a wide angle without asking leave of the will, and Tito knew there was a little figure in a white hood standing near the doorway. He knew it quite well before he felt a hand laid firmly on his arm. It was a real grasp, not a light, timid touch; for poor Tessa, seeing his rapid step, had started forward with a desperate effort. When he stopped and turned, her face wore a frightened look, as if she dreaded the effect of her boldness.

"Tessa!" Tito said, with more sharpness in his voice than she had ever heard. "Why are you here? You must not follow me—you must not stand about door-places waiting for me."

Her blue eyes widened with tears, and she said nothing. Tito feared something worse than ridicule if he were seen in the Via de' Bardi with a girlish *contadina* looking pathetically at him. It was a street of high, silent dwellings, not of traffic, but Bernardo del Nero, or someone almost as dangerous, might appear at any moment. Even if it had not been the day of his betrothal, the incident would have been awkward and annoying. Yet it would be brutal—impossible—to drive Tessa away with harsh words. That accursed folly with the *cerretano* should have lain buried for months and now spring up as this unseasonable crop of vexation! He could not speak harshly, but he spoke hurriedly.

"Tessa, I cannot—must not talk to you here. I will go on to the bridge and wait for you there. Follow me slowly."

He turned and walked fast to the Ponte Rubaconte, leaning against the wall of a quaint little house rising at even distances on the bridge, looking toward the way Tessa would come. It would have softened a much harder heart than Tito's to see the little thing advancing, her round face much paled and saddened since he had parted from it at the door of the "Nunziata." Happily, it was the least frequented of the bridges, and there were scarcely any passengers. He lost no time in speaking as she approached.

"Now, Tessa, I have very little time. You must not cry. Why did you follow me this morning? You must not do so again."

"I thought," Tessa whispered, struggling against a sob that rose immediately at the new timbre of Tito's voice, "I thought you wouldn't be so long before you came to take care of me again. And the *patrigno* beats me, and I can't bear it any longer. And always when I come for a holiday I walk about to find you, and I can't. Oh, please don't send me away from you again! It has been so long, and I cry so now, because you never come to me. I can't help it, for the days are so long, and I don't mind about the goats and kids, or anything—and I can't—"

Sobs came fast now, accompanied by great tears. Tito felt he could do nothing but comfort her. Send her away—yes, he must do that, at once. But it was all the more impossible to tell her anything that would leave her in a state of hopeless grief. He saw new trouble in the background, but the difficulty of the moment was too pressing to weigh distant consequences.

"Tessa, my little one," he said in his old caressing tones, "you must not cry. Bear with the cross *patrigno* a little longer. I will come back to you. But I'm going now to Rome—a long, long way off. I shall come back in a few weeks, and then I promise you to come and see you. Promise me to be good and wait for me."

It was the well-remembered voice again, and the mere sound was half enough to soothe Tessa. She looked up at him with trusting eyes, still glittering with tears, sobbing all the while despite her utmost efforts to obey. Again he said, gently, "Promise me, my Tessa."

"Yes," she whispered. "But you won't be long?"

"No, not long. But I must *go now*. And remember what I told you, Tessa. Nobody must know that you ever see me, else you will lose me for ever. And now, when I have left you, go straight home, and never follow me again. Wait till I come to you. Good-bye, my little Tessa: I *will* come."

There was no help for it; he must turn and leave her without looking behind to see how she bore it, for he had no time to spare. When he did look round, he was in the Via de' Benci, where he could not see what was happening on the bridge; but Tessa was too trusting and obedient not to do exactly what he had told her.

Yes, the difficulty was at an end for that day, yet this return of Tessa, at a moment when it was impossible to put an end to all difficulty by undeceiving her, was an unpleasant incident to carry in his memory. But Tito's mind was just now thoroughly penetrated with a hopeful first love, associated with all happy prospects flattering to his ambition; and that future necessity of grieving Tessa could be scarcely more to him than the far-off cry of some little suffering animal buried in the thicket to a merry cavalcade in the sunny plain. When, for the second time that day, Tito hastened across the Ponte Rubaconte, the thought of Tessa caused no perceptible diminution of his happiness. He was well muffled in his mantle, less perhaps to protect him from the cold than from the additional notice that would have been drawn upon him by his dainty apparel. He leaped up the stone steps by two at a time and said hurriedly to Maso, who met him, "Where is the damigella?"

"In the library; she is quite ready, and Monna Brigida and Messer Bernardo are already there with Ser Braccio, but none of the rest of the company."

"Ask her to give me a few minutes alone; I will await her in the *salotto*."

Tito entered a room fitted up in the utmost contrast to the half-pallid, half-sombre tints of the library. The walls were brightly frescoed with "caprices" of nymphs and loves sporting under the blue among flowers and birds. The only furniture besides the red leather seats and the central table were two tall white vases and a young faun playing the flute, modeled by a promising youth named Michelangelo Buonarotti. It was a room that gave a sense of being in the sunny open air.

Tito kept his mantle round him and looked toward the door. Not long after, Romola entered, all white and gold, more than ever like a tall lily. Her white silk garment was bound by a golden girdle falling with large tassels, and above that was the rippling gold of her hair, surmounted by the white mist of her long veil, fastened on her brow by a band of pearls—the gift of Bernardo del Nero—which was now parted from her face so that it all floated backward.

"Regina mia!" said Tito, taking her hand and kissing it, still keeping his mantle round him. He could not help going backward to look at her again, while she stood in calm delight, with that exquisite self-consciousness which rises under the gaze of admiring love.

"Romola, will you show me the next room now?" Tito said, checking himself with the remembrance that time might be short. "You said I should see it when you had arranged everything."

Without speaking, she led the way into a long, narrow room painted brightly like the other, but only with birds and flowers. The furniture was all old; there were old, faded objects for feminine use or ornament arranged in an open cabinet between the two narrow windows. Above the cabinet hung the portrait of Romola's mother, and below this, on top of the cabinet, stood the crucifix Romola had brought from San Marco.

"I have brought something under my mantle," Tito said, smiling. Throwing off the large loose garment, he showed the little tabernacle painted by Piero di Cosimo. The painter had carried out Tito's intention charmingly, thus far atoning for his long delay. "Do you know what this is for, my Romola?" he added, taking her hand and leading her toward the cabinet. "It is a little shrine, which is to hide away from you for ever that remembrancer of sadness. You have done with sadness now; and we will bury all images of it—bury them in a tomb of joy. See!"

A slight quiver passed across Romola's face as Tito took hold of the crucifix. But she had no wish to prevent his purpose; on the contrary, she herself wished to subdue certain importunate memories and questionings which still flitted like unexplained shadows across her happier thought.

He opened the triptych and placed the crucifix within the central space; then, closing it again, he took out the key, set the little tabernacle in the spot where the crucifix had stood, and said, "Now, Romola, look and see if you are satisfied with the portraits old Piero has made of us. Is it not a dainty device? and the credit of choosing it is mine."

"Ah! it is you—it is perfect!" Romola said, looking with moist, joyful eyes at the miniature Bacchus

November 17, 1494. Eighteen months had passed since Tito and Romola were wedded under the Easter sun, a union blessed with a shower of comfits that promised sweetness for their double life. Yet, since that spring, the air of Florence had shifted, thickening with the weight of history. Just as every blossom depends on the sap flowing from the root, the private fortunes of the couple were now tethered to the grand, trembling currents of Italian politics.

Only a week prior, the ancient spirit of the Republic had surged back into the hearts of the Florentines. The great bell in the palace tower had shattered the silence with an alarm, summoning the populace to arms. With rusted weapons and improvised clubs, they had driven the Medici from their throne. The gate of San Gallo slammed shut on Piero, fleeing toward Bologna with his mercenaries in panic, and barred the path of his younger brother, the Cardinal, who escaped disguised as a Franciscan monk. A price was placed on their heads, houses were sacked in accordance with old precedents, and the ignominious portraits of past conspirators were scrubbed from public walls. The exiles were welcomed home, and the half-grown tyrants were ejected from their splendid nest in the Via Larga. The Republic had, for the moment, reclaimed its will.

Now, however, the great palace in the Via Larga was being dressed for a different occupant. Drapery draped the streets in unfamiliar colors; banners spilled from windows; carpets and tapestries covered every step where exceptional feet might tread. This was the visible proof of a city brimming with anticipation. The stream of color flowed from the palace, past the Cathedral, through the Piazza della Signoria, and across the Ponte Vecchio to the Porta San Frediano. There, near the gate facing Pisa, a platform and canopy awaited the Signoria. Messer Luca Corsini, a doctor of law, felt his heart flutter with the anticipation of delivering a Latin oration. Every chief elder prepared himself, smoothing their chins and adjusting their silk *luccos*, while the well-born youths inspected their new tunics, styled after the French fashion, hoping to impress the stranger with a peculiar grace. A vast procession of clergy, from the Archbishop in his radiance to trains of black, white, and grey monks, consulted early in the morning on how to marshal their relics and sacred banners, ready to greet the illustrious visitor at three o'clock in the afternoon.

This was an unprecedented guest. He had crossed the Alpine passes with an army Italy had never witnessed: thousands of terrible Swiss, hardened to fight for love, hate, or coin; a host of gallant cavaliers; and an infantry where every hundredth man carried an arquebus. More terrifying still were the bronze cannons, hurling iron balls instead of stones, drawn by horses rather than oxen, capable of firing twice before a city could repair the breach. Some compared this newcomer to Charlemagne, the rebuilder of Florence and conqueror of degenerate kings; others saw in him the likeness of Cyrus, the liberator of the chosen people. He arrived with glorious projects: to march through Italy amid jubilation, to satisfy conflicting complaints at Rome, to seize the Kingdom of Naples by hereditary right and sword, and from there to launch a crusade against the Turks, cutting them down or converting them to Christ. This was the scheme of the Most Christian King, Charles the Eighth of France, son of the subtle Louis XI, whose shadow loomed over the strongest Christian monarchy of the age.

On a general level, the spectacle seemed grandiose, fit to revive memories of divine dispensations. A widespread conviction held that the arrival of the French king and his host was an event beyond the ordinary order of Providence—a moment when marble statues might sweat, phantom warriors fight in the air, and beasts bear monstrous offspring. This belief rested less on the sheer magnitude of the invasion than on the moral emotions of the times, finding its most remarkable voice in a single man.

That man was Fra Girolamo Savonarola, Prior of the Dominican convent of San Marco. On a September morning, as news of the French army's entry rang in men's ears, he preached in the Cathedral from the text, "Behold I, even I, do bring a flood of waters upon the earth." He believed this was supreme guidance, interpreting the "flood" as a symbol of both avenging wrath and purifying mercy. His audience, comprising the most cultivated spirits of the age, listened with shuddering awe. Savonarola possessed a rare power to imprint his beliefs upon others, swaying varied minds. Four years prior, he had proclaimed from Florence's chief pulpit that a scourge was descending to purify the Church. He appeared to hold a mission akin to the Hebrew prophets, viewing the Florentines as a second chosen people. While prophecy in that age was often like a farthing candle illuminating small corners of destiny, Savonarola's vision was a mighty beacon shining far out to warn and guide. To the soberest minds, the supernatural character of his insight was attested by the peculiar conditions of the age.

At the close of 1492, following the death of Lorenzo de' Medici, Italy had enjoyed a peace unthreatened by near danger. There was no fear of famine, for the seasons had been bountiful; new palaces rose in fair cities, and villas dotted pleasant slopes. Those who had more than their share showed no fear for those who had less. Citizens' armor grew rusty, and populations seemed tame, licking the hands of masters who paid for ready-made armies as easily as goods from Smyrna. Even the fear of the Turk had faded, replaced by the Pope's willingness to accept bribes for prospective poisoning rather than plan conquest. Altogether, the world seemed a handsome establishment for the lucky and wise, a realm where lust, obscenity, lying, and treachery were pleasant and useful when properly managed. Patronage of learning and fine arts adorned this substantial delight of tyranny, allowing flattery to be spoken in the choicest Latin and sublime artists to paint the holy and the unclean with equal skill. The Church, though disgraced in its head and lacking vital belief in its lower members, was more prosperous than in past days. The heavens smiled above, and below, there were no signs of earthquake.

Yet, even then, Savonarola had been preaching for two years that a scourge was at hand, that the world was not framed for hypocrites, libertines, and oppressors. From amidst those smiling heavens, he saw a sword hanging—the sword of God's justice, soon to descend with purifying punishment. In brilliant Ferrara, seventeen years prior, the contradiction between men's lives and their professed beliefs had destroyed his appetite for the world, driving him into the cloister at twenty-three. He believed God had entrusted the Church with the sacred lamp of truth, but saw that corruption had turned it into a sepulcher. As scandals multiplied and hypocrisy gave way to impudence, he asked if the world had ceased to have a righteous Ruler. Reason itself declared vengeance imminent; unless the Church were reclaimed, how could the promises of converting heathens and subjecting the world to the one true law be fulfilled? He had seen his belief reflected in visions, frequent since his youth.

The real force of Savonarola's demonstration lay in his burning indignation at wrong, his fervent belief in an Unseen Justice that would end it, and an Unseen Purity that abhorred uncleanness. To his ardent, power-loving soul, the faith in a supreme Ruler merged with the faith in speedy divine interposition to punish and reclaim.

Meanwhile, beneath the splendid masquerade of dignities, conditions worked slowly to disturb the general festivity. Ludovico Sforza, holding the ducal crown of Milan and desiring to wear it himself rather than let it rest on a feeble nephew, feared the Spanish-born King Ferdinand and Crown Prince Alfonso of Naples. These Neapolitans, disliking the cruelty and treachery useless to themselves, objected to poisoning a near relative for a Lombard usurper. The Neapolitan royalties feared their suzerain, Pope Alexander Borgia. All three anxiously watched Florence, lest its midway territory determine the game by underhand backing. All four, along with every small state in Italy, feared Venice—the cautious, stable, and strong power stretching its arms across the Adriatic to the western ports.

It was thought that Lorenzo de' Medici had prevented the fatal outbreak of these jealousies by maintaining the old Florentine alliance with Naples and the Pope while persuading Milan of its general advantage. But young Piero de' Medici's rash vanity quickly nullified his father's wary policy. Ludovico Sforza, roused to suspicion of a league against him, decided to invite the French king to march into Italy and, as heir of the house of Anjou, take possession of Naples. Ambassadors, orators as they were called in those haranguing times, went and came. A recusant cardinal, determined not to acknowledge a Pope elected by bribery and his own particular enemy, also traveled, seconding the invitation with hot rhetoric. The young king seemed to lend a willing ear. By 1493, the rumor spread that Charles the Eighth of France was about to cross the Alps with a mighty army. The Italian populations, accustomed since the fall of the Roman Empire to look for an arbitrator from afar, began to vaguely regard his coming as a means of avenging wrongs and redressing grievances.

In that rumor, Savonarola heard the assurance that his prophecy was being verified. What filled the ears of ancient prophets but the distant tread of foreign armies coming to do the work of justice? He no longer looked vaguely to the horizon for the coming storm; he pointed to the rising cloud. The French army was the new deluge to purify the earth from iniquity; Charles the Eighth was the instrument elected by God, as Cyrus had been of old. All men who desired good rather than evil were to rejoice in his coming, for the scourge would fall destructively on the impenitent alone. Let any city of Italy, let Florence above all—Florence beloved of God, to whose ear the warning voice had been specially sent—repent and turn from its ways like Nineveh, and the storm-cloud would roll over it leaving only refreshing raindrops.

Fra Girolamo's word was powerful, yet now that the new Cyrus had been three months in Italy and was not far from Florence's gates, his presence was expected with mixed feelings, where fear and distrust certainly predominated. It was not understood that he had redressed any grievances; the Florentines clearly had nothing to thank him for. He held their strong frontier fortresses, which Piero de' Medici had surrendered without securing honorable terms. He had done nothing to quell the alarming revolt of Pisa, encouraged by his presence to throw off the Florentine yoke. Orators, even with a prophet at their head, could win no assurance from him except that he would settle everything once within the walls of Florence. Still, there was satisfaction in knowing that the exasperating Piero had been pelted out for the ignominious surrender of the fortresses, and in that act of energy, the spirit of the Republic had recovered some of its old fire.

The preparations for this equivocal guest were not entirely those of a city resigned to submission. Behind the bright drapery and banners symbolic of joy, preparations of another sort were made with common accord by government and people. Hidden within the walls were hired soldiers of the Republic, hastily called from surrounding districts. Old arms were furbished, sharp tools and heavy cudgels laid carefully at hand to be snatched up on short notice. Excellent boards and stakes formed potential barricades, and a good supply of stones waited to make a surprising hail from upper windows. Above all, the people were in a humor for fighting any personage supposed to have designs of hectoring over them, having lately tasted that new pleasure with much relish. This mood was not diminished by the sight of occasional parties of Frenchmen arriving beforehand to choose their quarters, some with a hawk on their left wrist and metaphorically a piece of chalk in their right hand to mark Italian doors. Especially as credible historians imply that many sons of France were characterized by a swagger that must have whetted the Florentine appetite for a little stone-throwing.

This was the temper of Florence on the morning of November 17, 1494.

The sky above the Piazza del Duomo was a dull, slate grey, yet the square itself blazed with the artificial brilliance of a festival. Blue drapery hung heavy in the air, and constellations of yellow lilies and coats of arms dotted the canopy. Banners unfurled at the angles of the Baptistery, though the marble steps of the Duomo remained bare, trodden by the multitude. It was the hour of the Advent sermons, a solemn duty that could not be dispensed with even as the streets wore their holiday colors.

Yet, not all feet moved with the purpose of worship. High and low mingled in a brisk, restless tide, driven by errands rather than piety. Groups clustered in conversation, some eager to miss the sermon, others intent on ignoring it entirely. But the faces of these apparent loungers betrayed no leisurely contentment. Instead, they wore the sharp tension of men waiting for a storm. They listened with keen interest to a single speaker, yet constantly scanned the periphery, their eyes darting at every new arrival.

At the corner where the Piazza's artificial light faded into the sombre grey of the Via de' Cerretani, a distinct cluster of workers stood out against the festive backdrop. Their bare, brawny arms and soiled garments marked them as men of labor, and almost all carried tools that could easily serve as weapons. They gathered around Ser Cioni, a notary whose biting words had once drawn contempt in the Mercato but now commanded a complacent humor from a populace that felt the wind shifting in their favor.

"Never talk to me of bloodthirsty Swiss or fierce French infantry," Ser Cioni declared, his voice incisive. "They might as well be in mountain passes as in our streets. Peasants have destroyed the finest armies of our condottieri when they trapped them between precipices. Tell me, Florentines, need we fear any army in our own streets?"

"That is true, Ser Cioni," replied a dyer, his hands stained with crimson that mimicked blood, a small hatchet tucked into his belt. "Those French cavaliers who strutted in their doublets recently saw a sample of our dinner. I was carrying my cloth near Ognissanti when I saw my masters looking round as if the houses of the Vespucci and the Agli were poor lodging. They eyed us Florentines like top-knotted cocks, pitying our inability to strut. 'Yes, my fine Galli,' I said, 'stick out your stomachs; I have a meat-axe in my belt that will go inside you all the easier.' Then the great bell lowed, and I knew something had happened. I threw down my cloth, grabbed my axe, and ran after them toward the Vigna Nuova. 'What is it, Guccio?' I asked. 'I think it's the Medici coming back,' he said. *Bembè!* I expected so! We reared a barricade, trapping them, and a swarm of our Ciompi arrived, one wielding a great scythe that mowed off a cavalier's feather. It is true! The girls peppered stones to frighten them. Alas, Piero de' Medici never returned; it was a pity, for we would have left him neither legs nor wings."

"Well spoken, Oddo," agreed a young butcher, knife at his belt. "Piero will be a long while before he dares return; he looked as frightened as a hunted chicken when we hustled him in the piazza. He is a coward, or he would have made a better stand with his horsemen. But we shall swallow no Medici again, whatever the French King demands."

"But I dislike those French cannons they speak of," interjected Goro, his face swollen with two years of grievances. "San Giovanni defend us! If Messer Domeneddio means so well by us as your Frate claims, Ser Cioni, why not send the French to Naples instead?"

"Ay, Goro," the dyer nodded. "That is a question worth asking. Why not send them to Naples by Bologna? Or to Arezzo? We would not have minded their going to Arezzo."

"Fools!" Ser Cioni began, attempting to rally them to the cause of Florence's glory, but his words were drowned by a sudden cry: "Look there!"

All heads turned toward the Via de' Cerretani. Advancing was a brilliant party: Lorenzo Tornabuoni, distinguished and imposing; a French nobleman in elaborate white linen and gorgeous embroidery; two other Florentines of high birth in handsome dresses; and, to the left of the Frenchman, a figure that eclipsed all brocade. He wore only black, for he was in mourning, though a red mantle awaited him for the procession as Latin Secretary to the Ten. Tito Melema had become indispensable in his dealings with the French guests, his familiarity with Southern Italy and fluency in the French tongue earning him favor. He had visited the French camp at Signa often, and now the lustre of good fortune shone upon him. He smiled, listened, and explained with his usual graceful ease, though a keen observer might have noted a subtle change—the final departure of moral youthfulness, the distinct self-conscious adoption of a role in life. His face remained soft, his eyes pellucid, yet something indefinable had vanished, like the light in the morning twilight.

The Frenchman gathered instructions on ceremony before riding back to Signa, pausing now for a final survey of the Piazza del Duomo, where the royal procession would halt for religious rites. The party attracted every eye, yet the gaze was not entirely cordial. Whispers flew regarding the Frenchman's hoof-shaped shoes—a delicate flattery of royal superfluity—while snarls against the "Mediceans" were carefully suppressed. Lorenzo Tornabuoni possessed the power to disguise annoyance, and Tito, with his natural disposition to overcome ill-will through good humor, felt the unimpassioned detachment of an alien toward the native's deepest passions.

Pausing where they could view the Duomo obliquely, the group noticed the festoons over the central doorway. Tornabuoni beckoned to Piero di Cosimo, who lounged before Nello's shop. An animated discussion ensued, becoming highly amusing as the Frenchman marveled at Piero's pungent statements, which Tito translated literally. Even the snarling onlookers grew curious, their faces wearing expressions of half-smiling humiliation as they realized they were excluded from the joke. It was a delightful moment for Tito; he was the only one capable of making such an amusing interpreter, and he basked in the approving glances of the crowd, unaware of the shifting tides beneath the surface.

While the rainbow light bathed the laughing group in the Piazza, the grey light of the unadorned streets told a darker tale. Here, the French dress and hoofed shoes were conspicuous targets for a growing number of non-admiring Florentines. In the van of the crowd walked three men in scanty clothing, their hands bound by cords, ropes fastened around their necks and bodies so that a tug could throttle them. Their captors were French soldiers, stimulating the prisoners to beg with broken Italian phrases and strokes from the knotted ends of their ropes.

Two of the prisoners obeyed, holding out their bound hands to every Florentine they met, pleading in piteous tones: "For the love of God and the Holy Madonna, give us something towards our ransom! We are Tuscans: we were made prisoners in Lunigiana."

But the third man remained obstinately silent under the lashes of the knotted cord. He was vastly different from his companions. While they were young and hardy, looking like sturdy mendicants stripped by avarice, this man had passed the boundary of old age, perhaps fifty or sixty. His beard, grown long in neglect, and the thick, straight hair framing his baldness were nearly white. His thickset figure remained firm and upright despite emaciation, expressing a strange energy in spite of his years. This was carried in his dark eyes and strong dark eyebrows, which held a strangely isolated intensity against his yellow, bloodless, deep-wrinkled face and lank grey hairs. Yet, there was a fitfulness in his eyes that contradicted his flashes of energy; after looking round with quick fierceness at windows and faces, they fell again with a lost, wandering look. His lips were motionless, his hands held resolutely down. He would not beg.

The sight provoked growing exasperation among the Florentines. Initially, many gave money, some in automatic response to the appeal of God, others in unquestioning awe of the French soldiery, a fear that the soldiers themselves relied upon as a guarantee of immunity. But as the group moved deeper into the city, this compliance vanished. The crowd swelled into a troop of men and boys, chanting a chorus of intelligible jeers. The soldiers, inclined to use their weapons, were checked by the necessity of holding their prisoners securely, hurrying now in hopes of finding shelter in a hostelry.

"French dogs!"
"Bullock-feet!"
"Snatch their pikes from them!"
"Cut the cords and make them run! They'll run as fast as geese—don't you see they're web-footed?"

These cries, vaguely understood as threats, seemed to invite action rather than provoke it.

"Santiddio! Here's a sight!" cried the dyer, recognizing the tumult. "And the fools do nothing but hoot. Come along!" He snatched his axe from his belt and ran to join the crowd, followed by the butcher and his companions, save for Goro, who hastily retreated up a narrow passage.

The sight of the dyer, advancing with blood-red arms and an uplifted axe, stirred the crowd. He did nothing more than thrust himself among his fellow citizens, flourishing his blade, serving as a stirring symbol of street-fighting, like the waving of a gonfalon. The first sign that fire was ready to burst forth was as rapid as a leaping tongue of flame: it was the act of the conjuror's impish lad, Lollo. Dancing and jeering before the ingenuous boys who made up the majority of the crowd, Lollo possessed no great compassion for the prisoners, but he was conscious of an excellent knife. To jump forward, cut a rope, and leap back before the soldier could strike seemed an amusing and dexterous piece of mischief. As the people jostled more vigorously, Lollo felt his moment had come. He was close to the eldest prisoner. In an instant, he severed the cord.

"Run, old one!" he piped into the prisoner's ear as the cord split, then set the example of running as if aided by wings, like a scared fowl.

The prisoner's senses were not too slow to seize the opportunity. The idea of escape had been a constant presence, and the temper of the crowd had fueled his hope. He ran immediately, but his speed alone would have sufficed only if the Florentines had not instantly rushed between him and his captor. He fled into the Piazza, but quickly heard the tramp of feet behind him. The other two prisoners had been released, and the soldiers struggled and fought their way after them in a tardigrade fashion, impeded by their hoof-shaped shoes and attacked, though not resolutely, by the people. One of the younger prisoners turned up the Borgo di San Lorenzo, diverting some of the hubbub, but the main struggle remained toward the Piazza, where all eyes turned with alarmed curiosity. The cause could not be precisely guessed, for the French dress was screened by the impeding crowd.

"An escape of prisoners!" exclaimed Lorenzo Tornabuoni as he and his party turned just against the steps of the Duomo, seeing a prisoner rush past them. "The people are not content with having emptied the Bargello the other day. If there is no other authority in sight, they must fall on the sbirri to secure freedom for thieves. Ah! There is a French soldier: that is more serious."

The soldier struggled along the north side of the Piazza, but his prey had taken the other direction. That object was the eldest prisoner, who had wheeled round the Baptistery and was running toward the Duomo, determined to take refuge in that sanctuary rather than trust to his speed. But as he mounted the steps, his foot received a shock; he was precipitated toward the group of signori, whose backs were turned to him, and could recover his balance only by clutching one of them by the arm.

It was Tito Melema who felt that clutch. He turned his head and saw the face of his adoptive father, Baldassarre Calvo, close to his own.

The two men looked at each other, silent as death. Baldassarre, with dark fierceness and a tightening grip of the soiled, worn hands on the velvet-clad arm; Tito, with cheeks and lips all bloodless, fascinated by terror. It seemed a long while to them—it was but a moment.

The first sound Tito heard was the short laugh of Piero di Cosimo, who stood close by and was the only person who could see his face.

"Ha, ha! I know what a ghost should be now."

"This is another escaped prisoner," said Lorenzo Tornabuoni. "Who is he, I wonder?"

"Some madman, surely," said Tito.

He hardly knew how the words had come to his lips; there are moments when our passions speak and decide for us, and we seem to stand by and wonder. They carry an inspiration of crime that accomplishes in an instant the work of long premeditation.

The two men had not taken their eyes off each other, and it seemed to Tito, after he had spoken, that some magical poison had darted from Baldassarre's eyes, rushing through his veins. But the next instant, the grasp on his arm relaxed, and Baldassarre had disappeared within the church.

"You are easily frightened, though," Piero remarked, his laughter cutting through the tension like a blade. "My portrait lacks the ferocity of the original. Yet, the old man truly possessed a tiger's gaze; I must return to the Duomo to study him once more."

"It is hardly pleasant to be seized by a madman, if indeed he is one," Lorenzo Tornabuoni replied, offering a polite defense for Tito's trembling. "Perhaps he is merely a ruffian. We shall discover the truth soon. However, we must determine if we possess the authority to halt this disturbance between our citizens and your countrymen," he added, turning his gaze toward the French soldier.

The group moved forward, swords drawn, a silent phalanx of quiet onlookers parting to clear their path. Tito followed, compelled by necessity to learn what others already knew of Baldassarre. The initial paralysis of terror was rapidly giving way to the frantic calculations that mortal danger forces upon the timid.

The mob of men and boys, prone more to jeering and tormenting the soldier than inflicting serious harm, parted instantly at the sight of the nobles with steel in hand. The Frenchman was interrogated. He and his companions had merely escorted their prisoners into the city to solicit ransom: two were Tuscan soldiers captured in Lunigiana, while the third, an elderly man, had accompanied a party of Genoese who had clashed with French foragers near Fivizzano. He might be mad, but he posed no threat. The soldier knew nothing further, unable to comprehend a single word the old man uttered. Tito listened, deaf to the surrounding chatter until Tornabuoni addressed him directly.

"Will you return with us, Melema? Or, since Messere is departing for Signa, would it be wise to follow the current fashion and attend the Friar? He will be like a torrent in full flood this morning. It is what we must all do to preserve our Medicean skins. I myself would go if I had the leisure."

Tito's face had regained its color, and he managed a smile of forced gaiety.

"Naturally, I am among the admirers of the inspired orator," he said smoothly. "Unfortunately, I shall be occupied with the Segretario until the procession begins."

"I shall enter the Duomo to examine that savage old man again," Piero declared.

"Then, Piero mio, have the charity to show him to one of the hospitals for travelers," Tornabuoni suggested gently. "The monks may ascertain whether he requires confinement in a cage."

The party dispersed. Tito turned toward the Palazzo Vecchio, where he intended to meet Bartolommeo Scala. The distance was short, yet for Tito, it stretched out like the agonizing minutes of a waking dream. The brief intervals of street and piazza were filled with memories, premonitions, and torturing fears that could have spanned months. He felt as though a serpent had begun to coil around his limbs.

Baldassarre alive and present in Florence was a living revenge, a coiling viper that would not rest until it had crushed its prey. It was not in that man's nature to let an injury pass unavenged. His love and hatred burned with a passionate fervor that subjugated all else, driving a man to sacrifice himself to his passion as if worshipping a deity through self-destruction. Baldassarre had loosened his grip and vanished. Tito understood this well: it meant the vengeance was being studied, refined to ensure its certainty.

If he had not spoken those decisive words—"He is a madman"—if he could have summoned the mental fortitude to admit his recognition of Baldassarre, would the risk have been less? He might have claimed he believed Baldassarre dead. The only witnesses capable of contradicting him were Fra Luca, who was deceased, and the crew of the companion galley that had delivered the news of the pirate encounter. The odds were infinite against Baldassarre having met any of that crew again. Tito thought with bitter resentment that a timely, well-crafted falsehood might have spared him from fatal consequences. Yet, uttering such a lie would have demanded perfect self-command during a moment of convulsive shock. He seemed to have spoken without preconception; the words had leaped forth like a sudden birth, nurtured in the darkness.

Tito was subject to that inexorable law of human souls: we prepare ourselves for sudden deeds through the reiterated choice of good or evil, which gradually hardens character.

Now, there was but one chance: Baldassarre's failure to find his revenge. And then, a thought more actively cruel than any he had previously entertained struck him: might there be some truth in his own unpremeditated words? Enough truth, at least, to support his denial should Baldassarre attempt to identify him? The old man appeared strange and wild; with his eager heart and fevered brain, suffering had likely driven him to madness. If so, the vengeance seeking to inflict disgrace might be baffled.

Yet, there existed another form of vengeance that no ingenious lying could parry. Baldassarre belonged to a race for whom the thrust of the dagger feels as natural an impulse as the tiger's leap. Tito shrank with shuddering dread from disgrace, but he also harbored a physical terror inseparable from a soft, pleasure-loving nature—a fear that prevents a man from meeting wounds and death as a welcome relief from shame. His thoughts immediately sought some hidden defensive armor to protect him from a vengeance no subtlety could deflect.

He marveled at the power of the passionate fear that possessed him. It was as if he had been struck by a blighting disease, suddenly transforming the joyous sense of youth into pain.

One resource remained. He could have turned back, sought Baldassarre again, confessed everything—to Romola, to the world. But he never considered this. True repentance, which severs all moorings to evil, demands more than selfish fear. He possessed no sense that strength and safety lay in truth; the only strength he trusted was his ingenuity and his dissimulation. Now that the initial shock, which had betrayed the treacherous signs of fear, had passed, he hoped to be prepared for all emergencies through cool deceit and defensive armor.

It was a defining characteristic of Tito's experience at this crisis that no direct measure for removing Baldassarre ever occurred to him. Every other possibility swept through his mind, even flight from Florence itself, yet he never conceived a scheme to eliminate his enemy. His dread generated no active malignity; he would still have preferred not to cause pain to any mortal. He had simply chosen to make life easy for himself—to carry his human lot in a way that pinched him nowhere. That choice had, at various times, landed him in unexpected positions. The question now was not whether he should share the common pressure of destiny with his suffering fellow-men, but whether all the resources of lying would suffice to save him from being crushed by the consequences of that habitual choice.

Within the Duomo.

As Baldassarre forced his way behind the heavy curtain, his wrists bound tight and the rough hemp rope constricting his neck and torso, he halted abruptly. The interior of the cathedral did not match the hollow, dead expectation he had carried in his mind. He had imagined a cavern of silence, lined with lifeless idols and unlit candles, where only a few distant figures might murmur a monotone chant. Instead, he found himself confronted by a sea of living, breathing humanity.

The crowd was a tapestry of Florence itself: magistrates in fine wool, ladies of noble bearing, and the rough-hewn faces of artisans and peasants alike. They sat in breathless stillness, their gazes fixed upward toward the pulpit nestled between the nave and the choir. There, a Dominican friar with dark hair and stern features stood holding a crucifix, his voice cutting through the air.

For the first few moments, Baldassarre remained oblivious to the sermon. Though his entry had been silent, the rope around his neck marked him instantly as a fugitive. Yet, in a sanctuary, a prisoner was safe. His gaunt, aged face, etched with the lines of wild misery, invited pity rather than fear. As he stood frozen, his eyes drifting aimlessly from the vast congregation to the stone floor beneath him, the initial suspicion faded. The crowd, captivated by the preacher's rising power, turned their attention back to the pulpit, leaving Baldassarre to drift into the periphery of their collective awe.

Among those who had glanced at him was Romola. She had entered late through a side door, her position granting her a clear view of the main entrance. Her gaze lingered on Baldassarre with a profound intensity. The grey hairs framing his temples and the visible weight of suffering on his face struck a chord deep within her, awakening a lifelong sensitivity to the sorrows of the aged. She seemed certain that their eyes had met in that fleeting moment, but Baldassarre had seen nothing but a blur of faces. His consciousness was fractured, a mere flash of startled awareness lost in the violent turmoil of his own emotions. The memory of Tito burned like a fever dream, blending with a desperate thirst and physical anguish. No thought of the future could take root in this soil; only a bitter realization of his own weakening strength and a vague, all-encompassing distrust shaped his mind.

Suddenly, the air seemed to crackle with a sound that resonated like the thunder of his own inner rage. A voice, dripping with triumphant certainty, pierced his marrow: "The day of vengeance is at hand!"

Baldassarre shuddered, lifting his head. Too distant to discern the preacher's features clearly, he saw only an outstretched arm and the raised crucifix. Yet, he panted for those threatening words as if they were a promise of salvation. The preacher paused, slowly lowering his arm until he rested the cross upon the pulpit's edge. He crossed his arms over his chest and scanned the multitude, challenging every eye to meet his gaze.

"All ye in Florence are my witnesses," the voice boomed, echoing off the vaulted ceiling. "I spoke not in a corner. Four years ago, before the signs of war and tribulation appeared, I proclaimed the coming scourge. I lifted my voice like a trumpet to the princes and people of Italy, declaring that your cup of iniquity is full. Behold, the thunder of the Lord gathers to shatter it. What you drink as pleasant wine shall be poured out upon you as molten lead."

He turned his gaze specifically toward the clergy. "To you, O priests, who mock the Presence in the sanctuary, who declare the Mercy-seat bare and believe you may sin behind the veil unpunished: I say, God's presence shall return as a consuming fire. Your sacred vestments shall become winding-sheets of flame. Where there should be sweet music, there shall be shrieks; where soft couches, thorns; where the breath of wantons, pestilence. Trust not in your gold or silver, nor in your high fortresses. Though built of iron and adamant, the Most High shall instill terror into your hearts, causing you to flee like women. He shall break mighty men without number and replace them with others."

"And since Scripture declares that God reveals His will to His prophets, He has chosen me, His unworthy servant, to make His purpose known through the living Word and the deeds of Providence. By the ministry of angels, He has shown me visions. His word possesses me so completely that I am but a forest branch shaken by the wind of heaven; I cannot remain silent, even if I am mocked. For four years, in defiance of scoffers, I have preached three truths delivered by the Lord: that in these times God will regenerate His Church, that before this regeneration comes a scourge over all Italy, and that these things will come quickly."

"But hypocrites, cloaking their hatred of the truth with a show of love, have told me, 'Leave your prophecies; it is enough to teach virtue.' To them I answer: 'Yes, in your hearts you say God lives afar off, His word is a parchment written by dead men, and He does not rebuke nations as He did in the days of old. But I cry again in your ears: God is near, not afar. His judgments do not change. He is the God of armies; the storm, the fire, and the pestilence are His ministers, driven by the breath of His angels to strike the chosen land that has forsaken the covenant. And thou, O Italy, art that chosen land. Has not God placed His sanctuary within thee, and hast thou polluted it? Behold, the ministers of His wrath are upon thee—they are at thy very doors!'"

Savonarola's voice had climbed to a peak of impassioned force before falling suddenly into a profound silence. His hands dropped to his sides, then clasped quietly before him. This silence was not empty; it was a spell as potent as his voice. Men and women sat motionless, faces upturned like breathing statues, until the voice returned in clear, low tones.

"Yet there is a pause—even as in the days when Jerusalem was destroyed, there was a pause that the children of God might flee. There is stillness before the storm: lo, there is blackness above, yet not a leaf quakes. The winds are stayed that the voice of God's warning may be heard. Hear it now, O Florence, chosen city in the chosen land! Repent and forsake evil; do justice; love mercy; cast out all uncleanness from among you. Then the spirit of truth and holiness shall fill your souls, breathe through your streets, and the pestilence shall not enter, nor shall the sword pass over you unhurt."

"For the sword hangs from the sky; it quivers; it is about to fall! The sword of God upon the earth, swift and sudden! Did I not tell you years ago that I beheld the vision and heard the voice? And behold, it is fulfilled! Is there not a king with his army at your gates? Does not the earth shake with the tread of horses and the wheels of swift cannon? Is there not a fierce multitude that can lay bare the land as with a sharp razor? I tell you the French king with his army is the minister of God. God shall guide him as a hand guides a sharp sickle, and the joints of the wicked shall melt before him, mown down as stubble. He that flees shall not escape, he that escapes shall not be delivered. And the tyrants who built their thrones upon the vices of the multitude, and the unbelieving priests who traffic in souls and fill the sanctuary with fornication, shall be hurled from their soft couches into burning hell. The pagans and those who sinned under the old covenant shall stand aloof and say: 'Lo, these men have brought the stench of a new wickedness into the everlasting fire.'"

"But thou, O Florence, take the offered mercy. See! The Cross is held out to you; come and be healed. Which nation in Italy has received a token like yours? The tyrant is driven out; the men who held bribes in their left hand and rods in their right have gone forth, and no blood has been spilled. Now, cast away every other abomination, and you shall be strong in the strength of the living God. Wash yourselves from the black pitch of your vices, which have made you like heathens; put away the envy and hatred that have made your city a nest of wolves. Then no harm shall happen to you. The passage of armies shall be to you as a flight of birds, rebellious Pisa shall be restored to you, and famine and pestilence shall stay far from your gates. You shall be a beacon among the nations. But mark this: while you suffer the accursed thing to lie in the camp, you shall be afflicted and tormented, even though a remnant among you may be saved."

These warnings had been spoken with incisive authority, but in the next sentence, the preacher's voice melted into a strain of desperate entreaty.

"Listen, O people, over whom my heart yearns as a mother yearns over the children she has travailed for! God is my witness that but for your sakes I would willingly live as a turtle in the depths of the forest, singing low to my Beloved, who is mine and I am his. For you I toil, for you I languish, for you my nights are spent in watching, and my soul melts away for very heaviness. O Lord, thou knowest I am willing—I am ready. Take me, stretch me on thy cross. Let the wicked who delight in blood, who rob the poor, and who defile the temple of their bodies, wag their heads and sneer at me. Let the thorns press upon my brow, and let my sweat be anguish—I desire to be made like thee in thy great love. But let me see the fruit of my travail—let this people be saved! Let me see them clothed in purity; let me hear their voices rise in concord as the voices of angels; let them see no wisdom but in thy eternal law, no beauty but in holiness. Then they shall lead the way before the nations, and the people from the four winds shall follow them, gathered into the fold of the blessed. For it is thy will, O God, that the earth shall be converted unto thy law; it is thy will that wickedness shall cease and love shall reign. Come, O blessed promise; and behold, I am willing—lay me on the altar. Let my blood flow and the fire consume me; but let my witness be remembered among men, that iniquity shall not prosper forever."

During this final appeal, Savonarola stretched out his arms and lifted his eyes to heaven. His strong voice alternated between trembling emotion and renewed energy, but the passion with which he offered himself as a victim became too overwhelming for further speech, ending in a sob. Every shifting tone vibrated through the audience, shaking them into answering emotion. Even those with moderate faith in the Friar's prophetic mission, who in cooler moments loved him little, were swept along by the great wave of feeling rising from sympathies deeper than theory. A loud, responding sob rose instantly from the vast multitude as Savonarola fell to his knees, burying his face in his mantle. In that moment, he felt the rapture and glory of martyrdom without its agony.

In that great sob of the multitude, Baldassarre's joined theirs. Perhaps no other human present vibrated more strongly to the preacher's tones than he, yet his vibration was like a harp with all strings wrenched away save one. That threat of fiery, inexorable vengeance—a future where the hated sinner might be pursued and held by an avenger in an eternal grapple—came to him like the promise of an unquenchable fountain to an unquenchable thirst. The doctrines of the sages, his old contempt for priestly superstition, had fallen away from his soul like a forgotten language. If he could have remembered them, what answer could they have given to his great need compared to the answer given by this voice of energetic conviction?

The thunder of denunciation fell upon his passion-wrought nerves with the force of self-evidence. His thought never ventured beyond it into questions; he was possessed by it as a war-horse is possessed by the clash of sounds. No word that was not a threat touched his consciousness; he had no fiber to be thrilled by anything else. But the fierce, exultant delight he felt at the idea of perpetual vengeance found its climax and relieving outburst in the preacher's words of self-sacrifice. To Baldassarre, those words brought only a vague, triumphant sense that he too was devoting himself—signing with his own blood the deed by which he gave himself over to an unending fire, which would seem but coolness to his burning hatred.

"I rescued him—I cherished him—if I might clutch his heart-strings forever! Come, O blessed promise! Let my blood flow; let the fire consume me!"

The single remaining cord vibrated to its utmost. Baldassarre clutched his own palms, driving his long nails into the flesh, and burst into a sob with the rest.

While Baldassarre stood transfixed by the thunderous voice of Savonarola, he remained unaware that another figure had slipped through the rear doorway, standing in the shadows to watch him. It was Piero di Cosimo. The painter cared little for the sermon itself; his purpose was singular—to observe the escaped prisoner. As the preacher paused, allowing the congregation to surrender to a wordless emotional swell, the newcomer stepped forward and laid a hand upon Baldassarre's arm.

Baldassarre turned, his face still streaked with slow-moving tears, though a vigorous sigh suggested the storm within him was spent. The painter spoke softly.

"Shall I cut your cords? I have heard how you were taken."

Baldassarre hesitated, his gaze sharpening with suspicion at the intruder. Finally, he nodded. "If you will."

"Better step outside," Piero suggested.

Baldassarre eyed him warily once more. Seeing the doubt, Piero smiled, drew a knife, and severed the bonds. He began to suspect that the prisoner's madness was not entirely unfounded; there was a peculiar intensity in the man's expression. *Well,* he mused, *if he causes trouble, he will be tied again quickly. Let the poor devil have a chance, at least.*

"You fear me," Baldassarre murmured, his voice low. "You do not wish to know my name."

Baldassarre crossed his arms, savoring the long-departed sensation of muscle and strength. His look softened, taking on a quiet resolve. "No. I have nothing to tell."

"As you wish," Piero replied, though he offered a shrug. "Yet perhaps you seek shelter? We Florentines are hospitable to travelers with torn doublets and empty bellies. There is a hospital for the poor outside our gates. If you like, I can guide you there. The French soldier who captured you has been sent away."

Baldassarre nodded silently, accepting the offer, and the two men walked out together.

As they moved along the Via dell' Oriuolo toward the gate of Santa Croce, Piero broke the silence. "Would you not sit for a portrait? I am a painter, and I would pay well for such a likeness."

Suspicion flared again in Baldassarre's eyes. "No."

"Ah!" Piero said, his tone curt. "Very well. Go straight ahead; you will find the Porta Santa Croce. Outside lies the hospital for travelers. So, you decline my services?"

"I thank you for what you have already done. I require nothing more."

"It is as it should be," Piero muttered, turning away.

*A mysterious old tiger,* the artist thought, watching Baldassarre's retreating back. *Truly worth painting. Ugly, carved with deep lines, as if the plow and the harrow had tilled over his very heart. A stark contrast to my bland, smiling Messer Greco—my *Bacco trionfante*, who has wed the fair Antigone against all history and reason. Ah! His scholar's blood curdled uncomfortably at the sight of that old fellow's grip!*

When Piero re-entered the Piazza del Duomo, the vast multitude that had listened to Fra Girolamo was streaming out through every door. Their haste to resume their daily duties testified to the profound weight of the sermon that had held them captive. The artist leaned against the angle of the Baptistery, observing the departing throng with delight. He marveled at the variety of their garments and the sharp, characteristic faces—faces reminiscent of those Masaccio had captured fifty years prior, and those Domenico Ghirlandaio had not yet fully left behind.

This morning marked a peculiar occasion. The Frate's audience, usually multifarious, displayed an even more complete cross-section of Florence's classes and political factions. There were men of high birth, accustomed to public office, who had become conspicuous not only as enemies of the Medici and champions of popular government but as thorough *Piagnoni*, embracing the Frate's doctrines with fervor. Among them were men of authoritative presence, like Francesco Valori, perhaps sharing a hot, arrogant temperament, gratified by the immediate divine authority to bring about freedom in their own image. Others, like Soderini, possessed less of the ardent *Piagnone* and more of the wise politician.

At their sides stood men of family, like Piero Capponi—brave, unindoctrinated lovers of sober republican liberty who preferred action to argument, holding no specific grudge against any idea that kept the Medici out and made room for public spirit. Beside them were doctors of law, whose studies of Accursius had not extinguished their ardour, preventing them from becoming enthusiastic *Piagnoni*; Messer Luca Corsini, for instance, who was destined to raise his learned arms in street stone-throwing for the cause of religion, freedom, and the Frate.

Among the dignitaries carrying their black *lucco* or furred mantles with habitual authority, there were many with contemplative, sensitive faces: scholars inheriting names like Strozzi and Acciajoli, already minded to take the cowl and join the community of San Marco; artists driven to new heights by Savonarola's teaching, such as the young painter who had recently surpassed himself in his fresco of the divine child in the Frate's bare cell—unconscious yet that he would one day wear the tonsure himself and be known as Fra Bartolommeo. There was the mystic poet Girolamo Benevieni, perhaps hastening to carry tidings of the beloved Frate's imminent arrival to his friend Pico della Mirandola, who was never to see another morning.

Well-born women appeared, attired with such scrupulous plainness that their refined grace was the sole distinction separating them from their less aristocratic sisters. A predominant proportion belonged to the genuine *popolani* or middle class, belonging to both the Major and Minor Arts, conscious of purses threatened by war-taxes. More striking, perhaps, than all other classes were the long stream of poorer tradesmen and artisans. Their faith and hope in his Divine message varied from the rude, undiscriminating trust in him as the friend of the poor and enemy of the oppressive rich, to an eager tasting of biblical subtleties that took a peculiar hold on the sedentary artisan, illuminating the long, dim spaces beyond the board where he stitched with a pale flame that seemed to him the light of Divine science.

Yet, scattered among these devoted disciples were many who were not his followers at all. Some were Mediceans who, from fear and policy, had begun to feign deference to the prevailing spirit of the popular party. Others were sincere advocates of free government who viewed Savonarola merely as an ambitious monk—half sagacious, half fanatical—who had made himself a powerful instrument with the people and must be accepted as an important social fact. There were even bitter enemies: members of the old aristocratic anti-Medicean party determined to seize the reins once more for certain chief families, or licentious young men who detested him as the killjoy of Florence.

The sermons in the Duomo had already become political incidents, attracting the ears of curiosity and malice as well as faith. Men of ideas, like young Niccolò Machiavelli, went to observe and write reports to friends in country villas. Men of appetites, like Dolfo Spini, bent on hunting down the Frate as a public nuisance who made game scarce, went to feed their hatred and lie in wait for grounds of accusation.

Perhaps no preacher ever wielded such massive influence as Savonarola, yet no preacher ever worked with such heterogeneous materials. One secret of this influence lay in the highly mixed character of his preaching. Baldassarre, wrought into an ecstasy of self-martyring revenge, was only an extreme case among the partial and narrow sympathies of that audience. In Savonarola's sermons, there were strains that appealed to the finest susceptibilities of human nature, alongside elements that gratified low egoism, tickled gossiping curiosity, and fascinated timorous superstition. His need for personal predominance, his labyrinthine allegorical interpretations of Scripture, his enigmatic visions, and his false certitude regarding Divine intentions never ceased, in his own large soul, to be ennobled by fervid piety, a passionate sense of the infinite, active sympathy, and a clear-sighted demand for the subjection of selfish interests to the general good—a quality he shared with the greatest of mankind.

But for the mass of his audience, the entire weight of his preaching lay in his strong assertion of supernatural claims, his denunciatory visions, and the false certitude that gave his sermons the urgency of a political bulletin. Having once held that audience in his mastery, it was necessary to his nature—and necessary for their welfare—that he should keep it. The effect was inevitable. No man ever struggles to retain power over a mixed multitude without suffering vitiation; his standard must become their lower needs rather than his own best insight.

The mysteries of human character were seldom presented in a way more fitted to check the judgments of facile knowingness than in Girolamo Savonarola. Yet, we can afford him a reverence that requires no shutting of the eyes to fact if we regard his life as a drama accompanied by great inward modifications during its outward changes. Up to this period, when his direct action on political affairs had only just begun, it is probable that his imperious need of ascendancy burned undiscernibly within the strong flame of his zeal for God and man.

It was the fashion of old, when an ox was led out for sacrifice to Jupiter, to chalk the dark spots and give the offering a false show of unblemished whiteness. Let us fling away the chalk and boldly say: the victim is spotted, but it is not therefore in vain that his mighty heart is laid on the altar of men's highest hopes.

The Garment of Fear.

By six o'clock, the hearts of most Florentines had settled into a weary relief that the arrival of the new Charlemagne was finally concluded. When the thunderous roll of drums, the piercing blast of trumpets, and the heavy tramp of hooves along the Pisan road had mingled with the frantic pealing of church bells, it was indeed a majestic spectacle for those perched upon turreted roofs. From their vantage points, they watched the long, winding procession of terrible pomp against the backdrop of green hills and valleys. Though the sky offered no sunshine to illuminate the banners, spears, and silken surcoats, the absence of a thick dust cloud allowed the details to stand out with stark clarity. Tall and tough Scottish archers, ponderous Swiss halberdiers, and nimble Gascons ready to scale walls marched alongside cavalrymen who resembled knight-errants with their indomitable spears and chargers. It was a comforting assurance that this array of strength would injure none but the enemies of God. With such confidence, viewing the nobles and knights, the youthful pages of noble lineage, the jewelled sword-hilts, the satin scarves embroidered with pious symbols, and the transcendent canopy borne above the Most Christian King became a less dubious pleasure. As an old diarist noted, simply halting behind the wonders of the royal visit: "It was great magnificence."

Yet for the Signoria, who had waited on their platform against the gates before marching out to meet the mighty guest, the grandeur of the scene had been obscured by unsettling sensations. Had Messer Luca Corsini's Latin welcome remained legible, it might have been less confusing when the rain began to fall, creating an impatience in both men and horses that shattered his well-studied periods. This forced the scholarly representatives to offer a makeshift greeting in impromptu French. However, this sudden chaos created a golden opportunity for Tito. Stationed behind the Signoria among the secretaries, he found himself thrust forward when the dignitaries were pressed by the approaching horses.

"Somebody step forward and say a few words in French," Soderini urged. But no high-ranking official wished to risk a second public failure. "You, Francesco Gaddi—you can speak." Gaddi, distrusting his own quickness, hesitated and pushed Tito forward, saying, "You, Melema."

Tito stepped forward instantly, speaking the necessary few words in French with an air of profound deference that came as naturally to him as breathing. He then withdrew gracefully, allowing the King to pass. The presence of mind that had deserted him during the terrifying crisis of the morning was now a ready instrument. It was the mark of an excellent livery servant to never forsake his master when danger was not visible. Yet, when complimented on his timely service, Tito laughed it off as trivial, ensuring that Gaddi received the credit for the improvised welcome. No wonder Tito was popular; the touchstone by which men judge us is most often their own vanity.

Other aspects of the day had proven even more disastrous than anticipated. The Florentine procession of clergy and laity had become so choked that they were forced to take a makeshift route through the back streets, meeting the King only at the Cathedral. Furthermore, the young monarch, seated beneath the canopy with his lance resting on his thigh, looked less like a Charlemagne and more like a hastily modeled grotesque. It might have been wished that the scourge of Italian wickedness and "Champion of the honour of women" possessed a less miserable leg, fewer toes, a mouth less like a reptile's slit, and a head of less exorbitant outline. Yet, the thin leg rested upon cloth of gold and pearls, and the face was merely a small interruption amidst black velvet, gold, blazing rubies, and the brilliant tints of the embroidered canopy—"It was great magnificence."

The people cried out "Francia, Francia!" with an enthusiasm proportional to the splendor of the canopy, which they subsequently tore to pieces as their spoil, following immemorial custom. Royal lips duly kissed the altar, and despite the mischances, the King and his retinue were lodged in the Palace of the Via Larga.
