The nobles were dispersed among the great houses of Florence, and the terrible soldiery were encamped in the Prato and other open quarters. The business of the day was ended.

However, the streets still presented a surprising aspect, unseen by Florentines under the November stars before. Instead of a gloom broken only by feeble lamps before saintly images or streams of redder light from open doorways, lamps were suspended at the windows of every house. Men could now walk along no less securely and commodiously than by day—"It was great magnificence."

Along these illuminated streets, Tito Melema walked at about eight o'clock in the evening on his way home. He had exerted himself throughout the day under the pressure of hidden anxieties and had finally escaped unnoticed from the midst of after-supper gaiety. Now, at leisure to face his circumstances, he hoped to adjust himself to all probabilities and shed his childish fear. If only he had not lacked the presence of mind necessary to recognize Baldassarre during that surprise! It would have been happier for him on all accounts; he still winced under the sense that he was deliberately inflicting suffering upon his father. He would have preferred Baldassarre to be prosperous and happy. But he had left himself no second path now; there could be no conflict anymore. The only thing remaining was to take care of himself.

As these thoughts occupied his mind, he advanced from the Piazza di Santa Croce along the Via dei Benci. As he neared the angle turning into the Borgo Santa Croce, his ear was struck by a music that was not the sound of evening revelry, but of vigorous labor—the music of the anvil. Tito gave a slight start and quickened his pace, for the sounds suggested a welcome thought. He knew they came from the workshop of Niccolò Caparra, the famous resort for all Florentines who cared for curious and beautiful iron-work.

"What makes the giant at work so late?" Tito thought. "But so much the better for me. I can do that little bit of business tonight instead of tomorrow morning."

Preoccupied as he was, he could not help pausing a moment in admiration as he approached the workshop. The wide doorway, standing at the truncated angle of a great block of houses, was surmounted by a loggia roofed with fluted tiles and supported by stone columns with roughly carved capitals. Against the red light framed by the outline of the tiles and columns stood in black relief the grand figure of Niccolò, his huge arms rising and falling rhythmically, first hiding and then disclosing the profile of his firm mouth and powerful brow. Two slighter ebony figures, one at the anvil and the other at the bellows, served to set off his superior massiveness.

Tito darkened the doorway with a very different outline, standing in silence, knowing it was useless to speak until Niccolò should deign to pause and notice him. This did not occur until the smith had beaten the head of an axe to the due sharpness of edge and dismissed it from his anvil. In the meantime, Tito had satisfied himself by a glance around the shop that the object of his search had not disappeared.

Niccolò gave an unceremonious but good-humored nod as he turned from the anvil and rested his hammer on his hip.

"What is it, Messer Tito? Business?"

"Assuredly, Niccolò; else I should not have ventured to interrupt you when you are working out of hours, since I take that as a sign that your work is pressing."

"I've been at the same work all day—making axes and spear-heads. And every fool that has passed my shop has put his pumpkin-head in to say, 'Niccolò, wilt thou not come and see the King of France and his soldiers?' and I've answered, 'No: I don't want to see their faces—I want to see their backs.'"

"Are you making arms for the citizens, then, Niccolò, that they may have something better than rusty scythes and spits in case of an uproar?"

"We shall see. Arms are good, and Florence is likely to want them. The Frate tells us we shall get Pisa again, and I hold with the Frate; but I should be glad to know how the promise is to be fulfilled if we don't get plenty of good weapons forged? The Frate sees a long way before him; that I believe. But he doesn't see birds caught with winking at them, as some of our people try to make out. He sees sense, and not nonsense. But you're a bit of a Medicean, Messer Tito Melema. Ebbene! so I've been myself in my time, before the cask began to run sour. What's your business?"

"Simply to know the price of that fine coat of mail I saw hanging up here the other day. I want to buy it for a certain personage who needs a protection of that sort under his doublet."

"Let him come and buy it himself, then," said Niccolò, bluntly. "I'm rather nice about what I sell, and whom I sell to. I like to know who's my customer."

"I know your scruples, Niccolò. But that is only defensive armour; it can hurt nobody."

"True: but it may make the man who wears it feel himself all the safer if he should want to hurt somebody. No, no; it's not my own work; but it's fine work of Maso of Brescia. I should be loth for it to cover the heart of a scoundrel. I must know who is to wear it."

"Well, then, to be plain with you, Niccolò mio, I want it myself," said Tito, knowing persuasion was useless. "The fact is, I am likely to have a journey to take—and you know what journeying is in these times. You don't suspect *me* of treason against the Republic?"

"No, I know no harm of you," said Niccolò, in his blunt way again. "But have you the money to pay for the coat? For you've passed my shop often enough to know my sign: you've seen the burning account-books. I trust nobody. The price is twenty florins, and that's because it's second-hand. You're not likely to have so much money with you. Let it be till tomorrow."

"I happen to have the money," said Tito, who had been winning at play the day before and had not emptied his purse. "I'll carry the armour home with me."

Niccolò reached down the finely-wrought coat, which fell together into little more than two handfuls.

"There, then," he said, when the florins had been counted out on his palm. "Take the coat. It's made to cheat sword, or poniard, or arrow. But, for my part, I would never put such a thing on. It's like carrying fear about with one."

Niccolò's words held an unpleasant intensity of meaning for Tito. But he smiled and replied,

"Ah, Niccolò, we scholars are all cowards. Handling the pen doesn't thicken the arm as your hammer-wielding does. Addio!"

He folded the armour under his mantle and hastened across the Ponte Rubaconte.

The Young Wife.

While Tito hurried across the bridge, the new steel armor hidden beneath his cloak, Romola paced the floor of the old library, her thoughts fixed on his return. Outside, the city had turned out to greet the French King and his nobility; almost every window had opened to witness the spectacle. Romola's had remained shut. It had been three months since her father's sudden death, a silence that had fallen over her household as abruptly as the event itself.

Earlier that evening, when the bell had tolled for the customary hour, her father Bardo had asked, "Is not Tito coming to write?" He had feared a refusal, yet Romola had seen the restless energy in his face. She had gently excused him, noting his obligation to a supper at the Cardinal's. Bardo, momentarily pacified by the hope of news regarding the library, had fallen silent. Then, flushing with a sudden urgency, he had commanded, "I must go on without him, Romola. Get the pen. He brings no new text, but I must speak of the New Platonists. I shall die, and nothing will be done."

Romola had stood ready with the pen, but the silence that followed was absolute. Her father never spoke again.

In the hours since, Romola had wrestled with a complex mixture of grief and a strange, quiet resignation. She told herself that her life with Tito would now be more perfect, a dream of undivided happiness finally realized. Yet, she acknowledged the subtle bitterness that had always lurked beneath the surface of their union. It was not Tito's fault; he remained gentle, tender, and endlessly accommodating. But the burden of her father's monotonous, exacting demands was a load only she could bear, and even she had found the patience required to sustain it eroding over the years. Tito, once unwearying in his novelty, now seemed to escape the suffocating atmosphere of their shared solitude with a charming gaiety that left Romola with a small, aching heartache. She convinced herself that their natures were simply different—his light and social, hers absorbing and domestic—and that her own inferiority lay in her inability to match his brilliance. To preserve her love, she had to suppress every rising impulse of pride or resentment, straining herself to subdue her nature to his.

Now, with her father gone, the weight of his legacy pressed heavily upon her. The library, the fruit of his lifetime of toil, was her sole sacramental obligation. It was safe from creditors thanks to Bernardo del Nero's advance, but the political winds had shifted violently. The Medici were expelled, and the famous collections in the Via Larga faced dispersal. The Florentine State, facing war and the need for French subsidies, would likely sell the manuscripts for cash. To Romola, these grave political upheavals mattered only insofar as they threatened the fulfillment of her father's wish. Her entire ardour, once directed toward her husband, was now concentrated on this singular duty.

Seeking to understand the forces shaping Florence, she had attended Fra Girolamo's sermon in the Duomo that morning. The fanatical preacher's voice had stirred something deep within her, a strange sympathy with the call for martyrdom that transcended her daily concerns. Yet, this emotion felt transient, lying outside the sanctuary of her true life. As she waited for Tito, she resolved to hide her growing interest in public affairs, fearing it might isolate her from him. She needed his return to restore the balance, to confirm that their union could still flow in one current.

When the great door finally creaked open, Romola hastened to the top of the stone steps. Tito appeared, and the sight of him brought a flush of joy to her face, making her seem more lovely than on the day of their wedding. He greeted her with a forced smile, attributing his lateness to fatigue. Inside, the fire crackled warmly, but Tito's demeanor was distant. He dismissed the library as too cold, preferring their private rooms, a remark that stung Romola deeply. She had expected solicitude, not indifference.

"I am not well, Romola," he admitted eventually, rubbing his face as if trying to shake off a shadow. "Do not be surprised if I am peevish."

Romola knelt beside him, caressing his hair, but her touch stopped abruptly when she felt something hard beneath his tunic. "What have you got under your clothes, Tito? Something as hard as iron?"

"It is chain-armour," he replied calmly. "I must wear it constantly."

"What threatens you?" she asked, terrified.

"Everyone is threatened who is not a rabid enemy of the Medici," Tito said, putting his hand on her neck to break the tension. "I am suspected of being an envoy. Men like Bernardo are protected by their families; I am a Greek with no one to avenge me."

Romola shuddered at the thought of him in danger, imagining the armor as a malignant shell replacing his sensitive humanity. "Will you not give it up?" she pleaded.

"If you do not mind my being stabbed or shot, why should I mind?" he teased, though a flicker of pain crossed his face.

Later, Tito recounted the day's events: the rain interrupting Luca Corsini's speech, the clumsy French welcome, and the chaotic display of armor and jewels at the Tornabuoni house. He spoke of the flattery and the food, eager to share the details of his day, yet his eyes remained distant.

Romola mentioned her visit to the Duomo and her reaction to Fra Girolamo's sermon. Tito reacted with playful warning, suggesting she had caught a touch of fanaticism. He described a wild-eyed prisoner who had run into the church to escape a French soldier, a man who had wept openly.

"I saw that man," Tito said suddenly, his voice dropping. "I was outside with Lorenzo Tornabuoni when he ran in. He escaped from a French soldier. Did you see him when you came out?"

"No," Romola answered softly. "Piero di Cosimo took him away. But you want rest, Tito? You feel ill?"

"Yes," he said, rising. The horrible sense that he must live in continual dread of what Baldassarre had said or done pressed upon him like a cold weight.

Four days hence, Romola set forth for the dwelling of Piero di Cosimo in the Via Gualfonda. The streets she traversed were thick with a tense, mutual scrutiny: Frenchmen gazing upon Florence with an air of arrogant discovery, and Florentines staring back with a mixture of resentment and wary observation. The atmosphere was far from cordial. Having initially played the gracious host, the city now found itself weary of its "too superior" guests. The French, having tasted the fruits of conquest elsewhere, now viewed Florence through the lens of imperial entitlement. They spoke of leaving a viceroy behind and restoring the Medici, treating the republic as a conquered prize despite the singular logic of their claim—that the only crime of Florence was the policy of a disowned man. The Florentines, however, were resolved not to bow. This defiance rippled through the Old Palace in heated councils and manifested in the defiant banners fluttering in the piazzas, turning the thoroughfares into a perilous promenade for ladies of birth. Yet, Romola, cloaked in her heavy black veil and mantle, accompanied by the steady presence of old Maso, felt a shield against the prying eyes of the crowd.

Her haste to reach the painter's studio was born of a specific, gnawing anxiety. A copy of her father's likeness, painted by Piero in the guise of Oedipus, remained unfinished. Piero was a man of fickle devotion; when the demand was not absolute, he would abandon a canvas for months, tucking it away in a corner or coffer until it risked total oblivion. Though Romola was his favorite and he inclined to grant her wishes, no general goodwill could guard against his sudden whims. He had promised completion within the week, and Romola's nerves frayed with the thought of losing the only existing image of her father in his days of blindness. To lose that image was to lose a vital thread of memory, and the guilt of such potential neglect drove her to vigilance. Love, she knew, demanded more than mere affection; it required perfect loyalty of heart, aiming for its own completeness through the preservation of the beloved's true form.

By special favor, she was permitted to enter without prior warning. She lifted the iron slide and called out in a flute-like tone, mimicking the little maiden with the eggs who once visited Titus. Piero answered quickly, though his explanation upon opening the door offered no compliment.

"Ah, Madonna Romola, is it you?" he exclaimed. "I thought my eggs had arrived; I wanted them."

"I have brought you something superior to hard eggs, Piero," Romola replied, smiling as she drew back her veil. She took the basket from Maso and stepped inside. "I know you appreciate these treats when they come without trouble. Confess, do you?"

"Yes, when they arrive as effortlessly as the light," Piero admitted, folding his arms and gazing down at the sweetmeats as Romola uncovered them. He then lifted his eyes to her face and hair, his gaze sharpening with a painter's admiration as her hood, weighed down by the veil, fell backward. "But I suspect you bring these sweets to stop my mouth while you scold me. Very well, proceed to the next room; you will find I have made progress on the picture, though it remains incomplete. I take care never to promise what I cannot guarantee:"

The door leading to the wild garden stood shut, and the painter was already at work. However, he was not attending to Romola's commission. That portrait stood propped against the wall, and Piero stooped to lift it, intending to move it into the proper light. In shifting the canvas, he inadvertently revealed another—a small oil sketch of Tito, upon which he had made significant additions only days prior. It was diminutive compared to the other works, standing far within the frame, and Piero, prone to forgetfulness regarding his own arrangements, was unaware of what he had exposed. As he peered at a detail in the painting he held, preparing to place it on an easel, Romola gasped, her face flushing with astonishment.

"That is Tito!"

Piero turned, offering a silent shrug, vexed by his own lapse in memory.

Still staring at the sketch in bewilderment, Romola turned to the painter, her voice tinged with puzzled alarm. "What a strange picture! When did you paint this? What does it signify?"

"A mere fancy," Piero said, removing his skull-cap and scratching his head, adopting the familiar grimace used to mask deeper emotions. "I required a handsome young face for it, and your husband's was the ideal choice."

He moved forward, stooping to the picture. With his back to Romola, he pretended to conduct a final inspection before setting it aside as unworthy of display. But Romola, recalling the fact of the chain-armour and struck by the eerie coincidence linking Tito to the imagery of fear, stepped to his elbow.

"Do not put it away; let me look again. That man with the rope around his neck—I saw him. I saw you approach him in the Duomo. What compelled you to place him in a picture with Tito?"

Seeing no better recourse than partial truth, Piero hesitated. "It was an accident. The man was fleeing—running up the steps—and caught hold of your husband; I suppose he stumbled. I happened to be there and saw it. I thought the savage-looking old fellow made a fitting subject. But it is worthless, merely a freakish daub of mine." He spoke with contempt, moving the sketch away with decisive air and placing it on a high shelf. "Come and view the Oedipus."

He had displayed too much anxiety in concealing the sketch, inadvertently confirming the very unease he sought to suppress—that there existed something unpleasant, something disadvantageous to Tito, in the circumstances surrounding the painting. This impression silenced Romola; her pride and delicacy recoiled from further questioning, fearing that inquiries might imply even a whisper of suspicion against her husband. In a quiet tone, she merely asked, "He was a strange, pitiful-looking prisoner. Do you know anything more of him?"

"No more," Piero replied. "I showed him the way to the hospital, that is all. Now, observe the face of Oedipus; it is nearly finished. Tell me your thoughts."

Romola gave her full attention to the portrait of her father, standing in long silence before it.

"Ah," she finally said, "you have achieved what I desired. You have captured the listening look." Turning to him with bright, moist eyes, she added, "My good Piero, I am deeply grateful."

"Now that is what I cannot bear in you women," Piero retorted impatiently, kicking aside the clutter on the floor. "You pour out feelings where there is no cause. Why should you be grateful for a picture you paid for, especially when I make you wait? If I paint, it is for my own pleasure and credit to execute it well. Are you to thank a man for not being a rogue or a fool? It suffices if he thanks Messer Domeneddio for granting him such a nature. But women imagine walls are held together with honey."

"You crusty Piero! I forgot how snappish you are. Here, take this crisp sweetmeat," Romola said, smiling through her tears and offering a treat from the basket.

Piero accepted it with the grace of a proverbial bear dreaming of pears—truly enjoying the gift, yet accustomed to concealing his pleasures and pains beneath a shaggy exterior. "It is good, Madonna Antigone," he remarked, reaching for another. He had subsisted on hard eggs for two weeks. Romola stood opposite, her fresh anxiety momentarily suspended by the sight of this naive enjoyment.

"Good-bye, Piero," she said eventually, setting down the basket. "I promise not to thank you if you finish the portrait soon and well; I shall simply tell you that you were bound to do it for your own credit."

"Good," Piero replied curtly, assisting her with deftness to fold her mantle and veil.

Once the door closed behind her, Piero thought, *I am glad she asked no more questions about that sketch. I should be sorry for her to guess that I considered her fine husband a good model for a coward. But I made light of it; she will not think of it again.*

Piero was too sanguine, a common failing of open-hearted men attempting clever simulation. Yet, the thought of the picture pressed increasingly upon Romola as she walked homeward. She could not help weaving together the two threads of the chain-armour and the encounter Piero described between her husband and the prisoner, both occurring on the morning the armor was adopted. Had Tito perceived that look of terror in the painter's eyes? What could it all signify?

"It means nothing," she tried to assure herself. "It was a mere coincidence. Shall I ask Tito about it?" Her mind concluded, *No: I will not question him about anything he did not reveal spontaneously. It is an offense against the trust I owe him.* Yet her heart whispered, *I dare not ask him.*

There was a terrible flaw in that trust; she feared any hasty movement, just as men who hold something precious and desperately wish to believe it remains unbroken often hesitate to test its integrity.

"The old fellow has vanished; he headed for Arezzo first thing the next morning. No doubt the stench of the French made him sick after being their prisoner. I stopped by the hospital to ask if those monkish cooks had managed to determine whether he was still in his right mind. They told me he showed no signs of madness—only a total refusal to engage, acting as though he were planting a vineyard fifty miles away. He was a mysterious old tiger. I would have liked to know more about him."

These words were spoken by Piero di Cosimo within the confines of Nello's shop on the twenty-fourth of November, a mere week since the French entered Florence. The air was thick with anticipation; a party of six or seven gathered at the unusual hour of three in the afternoon, driven by the city's feverish expectation of a decisive political turning point. Every public seat was occupied, and every shopkeeper without a wife or deputy stood at his threshold, thumbs hooked in his belt, watching the street. The thoroughfares were dotted with artisans pausing or drifting lazily like floating debris, poised to surge forward impetuously at the slightest provocation.

Nello had been strumming his lute while half-sitting on the shop counter, his gaze fixed on the piazza.

"Ah," he declared, setting down the instrument with emphasis, "I would not have missed that spectacle of the French soldiers waddling in their broad shoes after their fleeing prisoners for a gold florin! That is the price of leaving my shop to shave magnificent chins. It is always so: whenever I abandon this navel of the world, something dramatic happens in my piazza."

"Yes, you should have been there," Piero replied in his sharp, cutting tone, "just to witness your favorite Greek trembling as though Satanasso had seized him. I delight in seeing your ever-ready Messeri caught in a sudden gale, forced to reveal their linings despite themselves. What color do you suppose a man's liver turns when he resembles a bleached deer the moment a chance stranger grabs him?"

"Piero, keep your vinegar for your own eggs!" Nello retorted. "What is there against my *bel erudito* for looking startled when he felt claws upon him and saw an unchained madman at his side? Your scholar is not like those beastly Swiss and Germans, whose heads are built only for battering-rams and whose appetites are so voracious they consider eating a cannonball before breakfast acceptable. We Florentines value qualities in a man beyond that vulgar commodity called bravery, which is acquired by hiring dullards at a set price per dozen. Tell you what: once men realized they possessed more brains than oxen, they set the oxen to pull their plows; and when we Florentines discovered we had superior intellects to others, we set them to fight for us."

"Treason, Nello!" a voice cried from the inner room. "That is not the doctrine of the State. Florence is sharpening its weapons, and the last authenticated vision from the Frate depicted Mars standing atop the Palazzo Vecchio, his arm resting on the shoulder of San Giovanni Battista, who offered him a piece of honeycomb."

"It is well, Francesco," Nello said calmly. "Florence possesses a few thick skulls suitable for bombarding Pisa; there will remain the finer spirits at home to perform the thinking and the shaving. As for our Piero here, if he prizes valor so highly, let him take his largest brush as a weapon and his palette as a shield, and challenge the widest-mouthed Swiss he can find in the Prato to single combat."

"Go on, Nello," Piero grumbled. "Thy tongue runs on as usual, like a mill when the Arno is swollen—whether there is grain or not."

"Excellent grain, I tell thee. It would be as logical to expect a weathered painter like thee to enjoy having a javelin thrust into thy belly as to expect a man whose wits have been honed by the classics to relish having his handsome face clawed by a wild beast."

"There you go, assuming people will put their legs into a sack because you call it a pair of hose," Piero countered. "Who mentioned a wild beast, or an unarmed man charging into battle? Fighting is a trade, and it is not mine. I would be a fool to chase danger, but I could face it if it came to me."

"How is it you fear the thunder so, then, my Piero?" Nello pressed, determined to corner the accuser. "You ought to understand why one man is shaken by what seems trivial to another—you who hide with the rats the moment a storm breaks."

"That is because I possess a particular sensitivity to loud noises; it has nothing to do with my courage or my conscience."

"Well, and Tito Melema may have a peculiar sensitivity to being grabbed unexpectedly by prisoners who have fled from French soldiers. Men are born with antipathies; I myself cannot abide the smell of mint. Tito was born with an aversion to old prisoners who stumble and clutch. Ecco!"

A general laugh erupted at Nello's defense, revealing that Piero's dislike for Tito was not shared by the company. The painter, wearing an unreadable grimace, pulled tow from his *scarsella* and stuffed his ears in indignant contempt, while Nello continued triumphantly.

"No, my Piero, I cannot afford to have my *bel erudito* disparaged; nor can Florence, with her scholars molting off her at the tender age of forty. Our Phoenix Pico has gone straight to Paradise, as the Frate informs us; and the incomparable Poliziano, not two months ago, departed to—well, let us hope he is not among the eminent scholars in the Malebolge."

"By the way," interjected Francesco Cei, "have you heard that Camilla Rucellai has surpassed the Frate in prophecy? Two years ago she foretold that Pico would die in the time of lilies. He died in November. 'Not at all the time of lilies,' scoffed the critics. 'Go to!' replies Camilla; 'I meant the lilies of France, and they seem close enough under your nostrils.' I say, 'Euge, Camilla!' If the Frate can prove any of his visions fulfilled as accurately, I shall declare myself a Piagnone tomorrow."

"You are somewhat too flippant regarding the Frate, Francesco," observed Pietro Cennini, the scholar. "We are all indebted to him these past weeks for preaching peace and the cessation of party quarrels. Those of small discernment would gladly see the people slip the Frate's leash just now. And if the Most Christian King remains obstinate today and refuses to sign what is fair and honorable for Florence, Fra Girolamo is the man we must trust to bring him to reason."

"You speak truth, Messer Pietro," Nello agreed. "The Frate is one of the firmest nails Florence has to hang upon—at least, that is the opinion of the most respectable chins I have the honor of shaving. Yet young Messer Niccolò remarked the other morning—and doubtless Francesco means the same—there is as wonderful a power of stretching the meaning of visions as in Dido's bull's hide. It seems a dream may signify whatever follows it. As our Franco Sacchetti says, a woman dreams overnight of a serpent biting her, breaks a drinking cup the next day, and cries out, 'Look you, I thought something would happen—it is plain now what the serpent meant.'"

"But the Frate's visions are not of that sort," Cronaca insisted. "He does not merely predict what will happen—that the Church will be scourged and renovated, and the heathens converted—he declares it shall happen quickly. He is no slippery pretender who provides loopholes for himself; he is—"

"What is this? What is this?" Nello exclaimed, leaping from the counter and thrusting his head out the door. "People are streaming into the piazza, shouting. Something must have occurred in the Via Larga. Aha!" he burst forth with delighted astonishment, stepping out laughing and waving his cap.

The rest of the company hastened to the door. News from the Via Larga was precisely what they had awaited. Yet they were not little surprised by the manner of its arrival. Carried above the shoulders of the crowd on a bench apparently snatched from the street sat Tito Melema, smiling in amused resignation at the compulsion under which he labored. His cap had slipped from his head, hanging by the *becchetto* loosely wound around his neck. As he spotted the group at Nello's door, he raised his fingers in beckoning recognition. In the next instant, he leaped from the bench onto a cart laden with bales standing in the broad space between the Baptistery and the steps of the Duomo, while the people swarmed around him with the noisy eagerness of poultry awaiting feed.

Silence fell when he began to speak in his clear, mellow voice.

"Citizens of Florence! I have no warrant to tell the news except your will. But the news is good, and will harm no man in the telling. The Most Christian King is signing a treaty that is honorable to Florence. But you owe it to one of your citizens, who spoke a word worthy of the ancient Romans—you owe it to Piero Capponi!"

Immediately a roar of voices erupted. "Capponi! Capponi! What said our Piero?" "Ah! He would not stand being sent from Herod to Pilate!" "We knew Piero!" "Orsù! Tell us, what did he say?"

When the roar of insistence subsided slightly, Tito began again.

"The Most Christian King demanded a little too much—he was obstinate—and said at last, 'I shall order my trumpets to sound.' Then, Florentine citizens! Your Piero Capponi, speaking with the voice of a free city, replied, 'If you sound your trumpets, we will ring our bells!' He snatched the copy of the dishonorable conditions from the secretary's hands, tore it into pieces, and turned to leave the royal presence."

Again there were loud shouts, followed by impatient demands for more.

"Then, Florentines, the high majesty of France felt, perhaps for the first time, all the majesty of a free city. And the Most Christian King himself hastened from his place to call Piero Capponi back. The great spirit of your Florentine city accomplished its work by a great word, without need of the great actions that lay ready behind it. And the King has consented to sign the treaty, which preserves both the honor and the safety of Florence. The banner of France will float over every Florentine galley in sign of amity and common privilege, but above that banner will be written the word 'Liberty!'"

"That is all the news I have to tell; is it not enough?—since it is for the glory of every one of you, citizens of Florence, that you have a fellow-citizen who knows how to speak your will."

As the shouts rose again, Tito looked around with inward amusement at the various crowd, each elated by the notion that Piero Capponi had somehow represented them—that he was the mind whereof Capponi was the mouthpiece. He enjoyed the humor of the incident, which had suddenly transformed him, an alien and a friend of the Medici, into an orator tickling the ears of the people blatant for some unknown good they called liberty. He felt quite glad that he had been seized and hurried along by the crowd as he emerged from the palace in the Via Larga with a commission to the Signoria. It was very easy, very pleasant, this exercise of speaking to general satisfaction: a man who knew how to persuade need never be in danger from any party; he could convince each that he was feigning with all the others. The gestures and faces of weavers and dyers were certainly amusing when viewed from above in this manner.

Tito was beginning to feel at ease in his armor, and at this moment was entirely unconscious of it. He stood with one hand holding his recovered cap and the other at his belt, a light of complacent smile in his long, lustrous eyes, as he made a parting reverence to his audience before springing down from the bales—when suddenly his glance met that of a man who possessed none of the amusing aspect of the exulting weavers, dyers, and woolcarders. This man's face was clean-shaven, his hair close-clipped, and he wore a decent felt hat. A single glance would hardly have sufficed to assure anyone but Tito that this was the face of the escaped prisoner who had seized him on the steps. But to Tito, it came not simply as the face of the escaped prisoner, but as a face with which he had been familiar many years prior.

It seemed all compressed into a second—the sight of Baldassarre looking at him, the sensation shooting through him like a fiery arrow, and the act of leaping from the cart. He would have leaped down in the same instant, whether he had seen Baldassarre or not, for he was in a hurry to reach the Palazzo Vecchio; this time he had not betrayed himself by look or movement, and he said inwardly that he should not be taken by surprise again; he should be prepared to see this face rise up continually like the intermittent blotch that appears in diseased vision. But this reappearance of Baldassarre, so much more in his own likeness, tightened the pressure of dread: the idea of his madness lost its likelihood now that he was shaven and clad like a decent, though poor, citizen. Certainly, there was a great change in his face; but how could it be otherwise? And yet, if he were perfectly sane—in possession of all his powers and all his learning—why was he lingering in this way before making known his identity? It must be for the sake of making his scheme of vengeance more complete. But he did linger: that at least gave an opportunity for flight. And Tito began to think that flight was his only resource.

But while he, with his back turned on the Piazza del Duomo, had lost the recollection of the new part he had been playing, and was no longer thinking of the many things which a ready brain and tongue made easy, but of a few things which destiny had somehow made very difficult, the

The Avenger's Secret.

It was the first time Baldassarre had ventured into the Piazza del Duomo since his flight. A fierce longing pulled him toward the pulpit where the remarkable monk preached, yet he recoiled from returning to the very spot where he had stood half-naked, hair unkempt, a rope around his throat, branded a madman. The sting of Tito's words—"some madman, surely"—was not merely in their cruelty; it was in the sudden, bitter realization that he might indeed be powerless to prove them false. Alongside his burning desire for vengeance rose a cold, paralyzing doubt: could he actually achieve it? It felt as though Tito had been guided by a diabolical prompter who had whispered Baldassarre's deepest shame into the traitor's ear. He was not mad; he carried the pitiful mark of sanity, the clear awareness of his own shattered faculties. He measured his own feebleness. With the first surge of vindictive rage came a vague caution, like that of a wild beast fierce yet weak, or an insect pausing in a tremor of distrust because its footing had failed. This distrust, this refusal to take any step that might betray his condition, had driven him to reject Piero di Cosimo's friendly overtures.

He had exercised equal caution at the hospital, answering the brethren's questions only that he had been captured by the French on his way from Genoa. Yet his age, and the peculiar cadence of his speech, marked him as different from the ordinary mendicants and poor travelers sheltered there. The monks, moved by pity, offered him extra charity: a coarse woolen tunic against the chill, a pair of peasant shoes, and a few *danari*, the smallest Florentine coins, to aid his journey. He departed for the road to Arezzo early in the morning, but halted at the first small town. There, he spent a couple of coins to be shaved and to have his tangled hair cut short, restoring it to its former style. The barber possessed a small hand-mirror of bright steel. It had been years since Baldassarre had looked upon his reflection. Now, as his gaze fell upon the glass, a new thought pierced his mind. "Was he so altered that Tito truly did not know him?" The thought arrested his impetuous currents with a painful shock; his hand trembled like a leaf as he reached for the mirror. He needed to see himself before shaving. The barber, noting his shakiness, held the glass steady.

No, he was not so changed. He recognized the wrinkles he had known three years prior; they were merely deeper now. The same rough, clumsy skin formed little superficial bosses on his brow, like cipher-marks; it was only yellower, resembling a lifeless rind. That shaggy white beard was no disguise to eyes that had scrutinized him for sixteen years, eyes that should have searched for him with the expectation of finding him transformed, as one searches for a beloved among bodies cast up by the sea. There was a difference in his glance, but it was a difference that should only have made recognition more startling; for is not a known voice all the more thrilling when heard as a cry? Yet the doubt was folly; he felt Tito knew him. He pushed the mirror away. The strong currents rushed on again, and the energies of hatred and vengeance reawakened.

He retraced his steps toward Florence but refused to enter the city until dusk. Turning aside from the highroad, he sat by a small pool shadowed on one side by alder bushes still sprinkled with yellow leaves. It was a calm November day, and the sight of the pool's still surface suggested itself as a mirror. He wanted to contemplate himself slowly, something he had dared not do before the barber. Sitting on the pool's edge, he bent forward to study his image.

Was there something wandering and imbecile in his face—something mirroring the chaos within his mind?

Not now. When he examined himself with eager inquiry, there was an intense purpose in his eyes. But at other times? Yes, undoubtedly. In the long hours of vague aching for an unremembered past, when he sat in dark loneliness visited by whispers that died mockingly as he strained to catch them, and by forms that approached and floated away as he reached futilely for them, his glance must have been filled with frustration and amazement. More horribly, when the thick cloud parted for a moment and he sprang forward with hope, only to see it roll shut again, leaving him helpless as before, there must have been a blank confusion in his face, like a man suddenly struck blind.

Could he prove anything? Could he even begin to allege a case with the confidence that his train of thought would not break? Would anyone believe he had once possessed a mind filled with rare knowledge, busy with close thoughts, ready with varied speech? That laboriously gathered store had slipped away. Was it utterly and forever gone, like waters from an urn lost in the wide ocean? Or was it still within him, imprisoned by some obstruction that might one day shatter?

It might be so; he clung to this hope. Since the day he had first walked feebly from his straw couch and felt a new darkness within him under the sunlight, his mind had undergone changes, partly gradual and persistent, partly sudden and fleeting. As his bodily strength returned, so had his self-command and the energy of his will; he had recovered the memory of that part of his life closely enwrought with his emotions. But he felt more and more constantly and painfully the uneasy sense of lost knowledge. Yet, once or twice, when strongly excited, he had seemed momentarily to possess his past self entirely, as old men doze for an instant and regain the consciousness of their youth. He seemed again to see Greek pages and understand them, again to feel his mind moving unbenumbed among familiar ideas. It had been but a flash, and the closing darkness seemed the more horrible; but might not the same thing happen again, for longer periods? If it would only come and stay long enough for him to achieve revenge—devise an exquisite suffering that a mere right arm could never inflict!

Raising himself from his stooping posture, he folded his arms and attempted to concentrate all his mental force on the plan he must immediately pursue. He had to wait for knowledge and opportunity, and while waiting, he needed means of living without begging. What he dreaded above all was that anyone should think him a foolish, helpless old man. No one must know that half his memory was gone; the lost strength might return. If it were only for a little while, that might be enough.

He knew how to begin gathering information about Tito. He had repeated the words "Bratti Ferravecchi" so constantly after they were uttered to him that they never slipped from his mind for long. A man in Genoa, on whose finger he had seen Tito's ring, had told him that the ring was bought in Florence from a young Greek, well-dressed with a handsome dark face, in the shop of a *rigattiere* named Bratti Ferravecchi, located in the street also called Ferravecchi. This discovery caused a violent agitation in Baldassarre. Until then, he had clung with the tenacity of his fervent nature to his faith in Tito, never believing himself wilfully forsaken. At first, he had thought, "My bit of parchment has never reached him; that is why I am still toiling at Antioch. But he is searching; he knows where I was lost: he will trace me out and find me at last." Then, when taken to Corinth, he induced his owners, by assuring them he would be sought and ransomed, to secure against any inquiries regarding him at Antioch. At Corinth, he thought joyfully, "Here, at last, he must find me. Here he is sure to touch, whichever way he goes." But before another year had passed, the illness came, rising with body and mind so shattered that he was worse than worthless to his owners, except for the sake of the ransom that never arrived. Sitting helpless in the morning sunlight, he began to think, "Tito has been drowned, or they have made *him* a prisoner too. I shall see him no more. He set out after me, but misfortune overtook him. I shall see his face no more." Sitting in his new feebleness and despair, supporting his head between his hands with blank eyes and lips moving uncertainly, he looked so much like a hopelessly imbecile old man that his owners were content to be rid of him, allowing a Genoese merchant, who had compassion on him as an Italian, to take him aboard his galley. In a voyage of many months through the Archipelago and along the seaboard of Asia Minor, Baldassarre recovered his bodily strength, but upon landing at Genoa, he felt such a weary sense of desolation that he almost wished he had died of that illness at Corinth. There was just one possibility hindering this wish: that Tito might not be dead, but living in a state of imprisonment or destitution. If he lived, there was still a hope for Baldassarre—faint, perhaps, and likely to be long deferred, but still a hope, that he might find his child, his cherished son again; might yet again clasp hands and meet face to face with the one being who remembered him as he had been before his mind was broken. In this state of feeling, he had chanced to meet the stranger who wore Tito's onyx ring. Though Baldassarre would have been unable to describe the ring beforehand, the sight of it stirred the dormant fibers, and he recognized it. That Tito, nearly a year after his father had been parted from him, should be living in apparent prosperity at Florence, selling the gem which he ought not to have sold till the last extremity, was a fact Baldassarre shrank from trying to account for. He was glad to be stunned and bewildered by it, rather than to have any distinct thought; he tried to feel nothing but joy that he should behold Tito again. Perhaps Tito had thought his father dead; somehow the mystery would be explained. "But at least I shall meet eyes that will remember me. I am not alone in the world."

And now again Baldassarre said, "I am not alone in the world; I shall never be alone, for my revenge is with me."

It was as the instrument of that revenge, as something merely external and subservient to his true life, that he bent down again to examine himself with hard curiosity—not, he thought, because he cared for a withered, forsaken old man, whom nobody loved, whose soul was like a deserted home where the ashes were cold upon the hearth and the walls were bare of all but the marks of what had been. It is in the nature of all human passion, the lowest as well as the highest, that there is a point where it ceases to be properly egoistic, and becomes like a fire kindled within our being to which everything else in us is mere fuel.

He looked at the pale, black-browed image in the water until he identified it with that self from which his revenge seemed a thing apart; and he felt as if the image too heard the silent language of his thought.

"I was a loving fool—I worshipped a woman once, and believed she could care for me; and then I took a helpless child and fostered him; and I watched him as he grew, to see if he would care for me only a little—care for *me* over and above the good he got from me. I would have torn open my breast to warm him with my life-blood if I could only have seen him care a little for the pain of my wound. I have labored, I have strained to crush out of this hard life one drop of unselfish love. Fool! men love their own delights; there is no delight to be had in me. And yet I watched till I believed I saw what I watched for. When he was a child he lifted soft eyes towards me, and held my hand willingly: I thought, this boy will surely love me a little: because I give my life to him and strive that he shall know no sorrow, he will care a little when I am thirsty—the drop he lays on my parched lips will be a joy to him... Curses on him! I wish I may see him lie with those red lips white and dry as ashes, and when he looks for pity I wish he may see my face rejoicing in his pain. It is all a lie—this world is a lie—there is no goodness but in hate. Fool! not one drop of love came with all your striving: life has not given you one drop. But there are deep draughts in this world for hatred and revenge. I have memory left for that, and there is strength in my arm—there is strength in my will—and if I can do nothing but kill him—"

But Baldassarre's mind rejected the thought of that brief punishment. His whole soul had been thrilled into immediate, unreasoning belief in that eternity of vengeance where he, an undying hate, might clutch forever an undying traitor, and hear that fair, smiling hardness cry and moan with anguish. But the primary need and hope was to see a slow revenge under the same sky and on the same earth where he himself had been forsaken and had fainted with despair. And as soon as he tried to concentrate his mind on the means of attaining his end, the sense of his weakness pressed upon him like a frosty ache. This despised body, which was to be the instrument of a sublime vengeance, must be nourished and decently clad. If he had to wait, he must labor, and his labor must be of a humble sort, for he had no skill. He wondered whether the sight of written characters would so stimulate his faculties that he might venture to try finding work as a copyist; *that* might win him some credence for his past scholarship. But no! He dared trust neither hand nor brain. He must be content to do the work most like that of a beast of burden: in this mercantile city, many porters must be wanted, and he could at least carry weights. Thanks to the justice that struggled in this confused world in behalf of vengeance, his limbs had regained some of their old sturdiness. He was stripped of all else that men would give coin for.

But the new urgency of this habitual thought brought a new suggestion. There was something

"My Romola," Tito announced on the second morning following his harangue in the Piazza del Duomo, "grand visitors arrive today. The Milanese Count returns, accompanied by the Seneschal de Beaucaire, the great favorite of the Cristianissimo. I know you disdain the smiling ceremonies required by such rustling magnates, figures we shall scarcely behold again. Since they intend to inspect our antiquities and library, perhaps you should suspend your labors and visit your cousin Brigida."

Romola perceived the subtext in his invitation and immediately acquiesced. Yet, returning moments later, hooded and mantled, she remarked, "Oh, what a long breath Florence will draw when the gates fly open and the last Frenchman walks out! Even you, my Tito, seem weary of your patience; confess it. Ah, your brow burns."

He leaned over his desk, pen in hand, and she placed her palm upon his head, intending a parting caress. This posture was habitual; Tito was accustomed to lift his head, lean back slightly, and gaze upward when her hand touched him. But now, he felt as paralyzed as if her hand were a leaden cowl. Instead of rising, he spoke in a light tone, his ink continuing its steady flow.

"The French are as eager to depart Florence as wasps leaving a ripe pear once they have fastened upon it."

Romola, acutely sensitive to the absence of his customary warmth, withdrew her hand and declared, "I am going, Tito."

"Farewell, my sweet one. I must remain at home. Take Maso with you."

Tito did not lift his gaze. Romola departed without further word. In the quiet of marriage, trivialities often mark epochs; that morning, for the first time, she acknowledged not merely that Tito had changed, but that he had altered specifically toward her. Was the cause within herself? She might have suspected so had not the facts of the armor and the painting suggested an external catastrophe, a mystery entirely opaque to her.

The moment Romola vanished from the house, Tito laid down his pen and looked up, finding delightful security in the sight of parchment and broken marble alone. He was disgusted with himself for failing to meet her gaze and behave as usual. He would have chosen to be kinder than ever, yet he could not suddenly master an involuntary shrinking from her presence—a subtle recoil born of the very traits that drove him to desire her affection. He stood on the brink of a step he knew would provoke her deep indignation; he would face much unpleasantness before winning her forgiveness. Tito could never easily confront displeasure; his nature abhorred defiance, and every inclination leaned toward preserving her tenderness. He was not tormented by sentimental scruples, which he had quickly argued away as irrelevant to solid utility, yet his lack of scruples did not shield him from dread. Unscrupulousness removes many burdens, but not toothache, wounded vanity, or the profound loneliness for which the world offers no cure but a healthy jaw and a just, loving soul. At this moment, Tito felt that no device could save him from the pain of the impending collision with Romola; no bland persuasion could cushion the shock driving him forward like a timid animal urged to a desperate leap by the terror of teeth and claws closing in.

A secret conviction had long held that his tenacious adherence to Bardo's wishes regarding the library had become, under current difficulties, a piece of sentimental folly depriving both himself and Romola of substantial advantage. Perhaps this thought would never have crystallized into action but for the events of the past week, which introduced a new motive and a rare opportunity. Indeed, it was only after his dread intensified by seeing Baldassarre regain his sanity, and only when he began to feel compelled to flee Florence, that he resolved to exercise his legal right to sell the library before the chance presented by French and Milanese bidders slipped away. For if he were forced to leave Florence, he would not do so as a destitute wanderer. Accustomed to an agreeable existence, he wished to carry with him all means to retain those conditions. He desired, above all, to take Romola with him—and, if possible, to carry no infamy. Success had awakened in him an appetite for pleasures dependent on advantageous social standing; at no point could flight under dishonor appear as a temptation, but only as a hideous alternative, even with a bag of diamonds, leading to the life of an adventurer. He could not free himself from the regard of Florentines who treated him with polite distance, still less from Romola. She was the wife of his first love—he still loved her; she belonged to the furniture of life he shrank from parting with. He winced under her judgment, uncertain how far her revulsion might extend, and the sense of power a husband possesses, which makes him risk betrayals a lover never dares, proved insufficient to counteract Tito's unease. This was the leaden weight too strong for his will, preventing him from raising his head to meet her eyes. Her pure light brought too near the prospect of a coming struggle. Yet it could not be avoided; if they were to leave Florence, they needed money. Indeed, Tito could not arrange his life to his satisfaction without a considerable sum, and that problem of ordering his life had been the source of all his wrongdoing. He would have been equal to any sacrifice that was not unpleasant.

The rustling magnates arrived and departed, bargains were struck, and Romola returned home, though nothing grave was spoken that night. Tito was merely gay and chatty, pouring forth stories and descriptions of the French visit, a liveliness Romola interpreted as an effort. She attributed it to his consciousness that she had been wounded in the morning, accepting it as an act of penitence, while inwardly aching at the sign of growing distance—the offense neither dared to name.

The following day, Tito remained away until late night. It was a marked day for Romola, for Piero di Cosimo, spurred to greater industry by the fear that he might have caused her pain in the past week, had sent home her father's portrait. She had propped it against the back of his old chair and gazed at it when the door opened behind her and Bernardo del Nero entered.

"It is you, godfather! How I wish you had come sooner! It is already dusk," Romola said, moving toward him.

"I have just come to tell you good news, knowing Tito has not yet returned," Bernardo replied. "The French king moves off tomorrow; not before it is high time. There has been another clash between our people and his soldiers this morning. But there is now a chance for the city to regain order and trade to resume."

"That is joyful," Romola said. "But it is sudden, is it not? Tito seemed to think yesterday there was little prospect of the king's departure."

"He has been well barked at, that is the reason," Bernardo smiled. "His own generals opened their throats quite sufficiently, and finally our Signoria sent the mastiff of the city, Fra Girolamo. The Cristianissimo was frightened by that thunder and has ordered a move. I fear there will be little agreement among us when he is gone, but at least all parties are glad not to have Florence stifled with soldiery any longer, and the Friar barked this time to some purpose. Ah, what is this?" he added as Romola, clasping his arm, led him before the picture. "Let us see."

He began to unwind his long scarf while she arranged a seat for him.

"Do you not want your spectacles, godfather?" Romola asked anxiously, wishing he might see exactly what she saw.

"No, child, no," Bernardo said, uncovering his grey head as he sat with firm erectness. "For seeing at this distance, my old eyes are perhaps better than your young ones. Old men's eyes are like old men's memories; they are strongest for things far away."

"It is better than having no portrait," Romola said apologetically after a brief silence. "It is less like him now than the image I hold in my mind, but that might fade with the years." She rested her arm on the old man's shoulder, drawn strongly toward him by their shared interest in the dead.

"I do not know," Bernardo mused. "I almost think I see Bardo as he was when young better than this picture shows him when old. Your father possessed great fire in his eyes when young. It was what I could never understand—that he, with his fiery spirit, seemingly more impatient than mine, could hang over books and live with shadows all his life. However, he had put his heart into that."

Bernardo gave a slight shrug at the end, but Romola discerned in his voice a feeling that accorded with her own.

"And he was disappointed to the last," she said involuntarily. Immediately fearing her words might be taken as an accusation against Tito, she continued hurriedly, "If we could only see his longest, dearest wish fulfilled just to his mind!"

"Well, so we may," Bernardo said kindly, rising and putting on his cap. "The times are cloudy now, but fish are caught by waiting. Who knows? When the wheel has turned often enough, I may be Gonfaloniere yet before I die; and no creditor can touch these things." He glanced around the room. Turning to her, he patted her cheek. "And you need not fear my dying; my ghost will claim nothing. I have taken care of that in my will."

Romola seized the hand resting against her cheek and pressed it to her lips in silence.

"Haven't you been scolding your husband for keeping away from home so much lately? I see him everywhere but here," Bernardo said, eager to change the subject.

She felt the flush spread over her neck and face as she replied, "He has been very much wanted; you know he speaks so well. I am glad to know that his value is understood."

"You are contented then, Madonna Orgogliosa?" Bernardo asked, smiling as he moved to the door.

"Assuredly."

Poor Romola! There was one thing that would have made the pang of disappointment in her husband harder to bear: that anyone should know he gave her cause for disappointment. This might be a woman's weakness, but it is closely allied to a woman's nobleness. She who willingly lifts the veil of her married life has profaned it from a sanctuary into a vulgar place.

The following dawn brought with it the departure of the French, an event that stirred the entire city of Florence with a feverish excitement. For Romola, amidst the general rejoicing, there lingered a faint, almost superstitious hope that the departure of these foreign guests might also wash away the specific anxieties concerning Tito. Their presence had lasted barely eleven days, yet within that brief span, she had endured a depth of sorrow she had never known before. It was on the very day of their arrival that Tito had donned that hateful armor, a physical manifestation of his terror. Though logic offered her no clear reason why their exit should dismantle the cause of his fear—the terrifying image of a prisoner seizing him, as Piero had sketched it—she felt certain that removing the burden of their company would strip away the tangible association of her pain.

Clad in her mantle, Romola stood beneath the loggia at the house's summit, watching the distant glimpses of the troops and the royal retinue crossing the bridges toward the Porta San Piero, the gateway leading to Siena and Rome. Even after the gates had fallen shut, she lingered, eager to feel the vibration of the great bell peal that marked the finality of their departure. Dusk had settled when she finally descended into the library. Lighting her lamp, she resolved to quell the agitation that had paralyzed her all day and return to her work of copying the catalogue. Tito had left early; she did not expect him until later. Once he arrived, she planned to retreat to the charming salon adorned with dancing nymphs and birds—a space he had once deemed too chilly and gloomy for her comfort.

To her profound surprise, Tito entered before she had even begun her labor. Her initial thought was of the cheerlessness of the vast, dark room, illuminated only by a solitary oil lamp at the far end, with the fire nearly extinguished. She hastened toward him.

"Tito, dearest, I did not know you would return so soon," she said, her voice tinged with nervousness as she raised her white arms to loosen the *becchetto* from his neck.

"Am I not welcome, then?" he replied, offering one of his brightest smiles. He embraced her, yet playfully held his head back from her touch.

"Tito!" she exclaimed, the word vibrating with a tone of affectionate reproach. He kissed her fondly, stroking her hair in his customary manner, seemingly unconcerned with removing his mantle. Romola quivered with delight. All the turmoil of the day had sharpened her sensitivity to the return of this familiar tenderness. "It will come back," she whispered to herself. "The old happiness will perhaps return. He is like himself again."

Yet, Tito was striving with great effort to appear as his former self; his heart hammered with a secret, consuming anxiety.

"If I had anticipated your return so quickly," Romola said, helping him finally shed his wrappings, "I would have prepared a small festival to honor this joyful ringing of the bells. I did not intend to be here in the library when you came home."

"Never mind, sweet," he dismissed her concern casually. "Do not worry about the fire. Come—come and sit down."

A low stool stood beside his chair, the place where Romola habitually rested when conversing with him. She leaned her arm upon his knee, mimicking the gesture she had once shared with her father, and gazed upward at him. He had not yet noticed the portrait looming nearby, nor had she mentioned it, allowing the thought to fester in her mind.

"I have been listening to the clang of the bells for the first time, Tito," she began. "I enjoyed being shaken and deafened by them; I fancied myself a Bacchante possessed by a divine rage. Do not the people look very joyful tonight?"

"Joyful, indeed, in a sour and pious fashion," Tito replied, shrugging his shoulders. "But in truth, those left behind in Florence have little cause for joy. It seems to me the most reasonable grounds for gladness would be to have fled the city entirely."

Tito had struck the desired note without apparent premeditation. He spoke without emphasis, yet his grave demeanor prompted an anxious query from Romola.

"Why, Tito? Are there fresh troubles?"

"No need for fresh ones, my Romola. There are three powerful factions in the city, all poised to tear each other apart. And if the Frate's party proves strong enough to silence the others, as seems likely, life will be as pleasant and amusing as a funeral. They already simmer with plans for a Great Council; should they succeed, the man who sings sacred Lauds the loudest will be the most eligible for office. Furthermore, the city will be drained by the payment of this massive subsidy to the French king and the war to reclaim Pisa. The prospect would be dismal enough without the rule of fanatics. On the whole, Florence will be a delightful haunt for those worthies who entertain themselves by visiting crypts and lashing themselves, but for everything else, the exiles have the better of it. For my own part, I have been seriously considering that we would be wise to leave Florence, my Romola."

She started. "Tito, how could we leave Florence? Surely you do not think I could abandon it—at least, not yet, not for a long while." Her voice turned cold and trembling, finding difficulty in its articulation. Tito must know the reasons weighing heavily in her mind.

"That is merely a fabric of your own imagination, my sweet one. Your secluded life has led you to place undue stress on trivial matters. You know I told you before our marriage that I wished we were somewhere other than Florence. Had you seen more places and more people, you would understand what I mean when I say there is something in the Florentines that reminds me of their cutting spring winds. I prefer people who take life less eagerly; and it would be good for my Romola, too, to witness a new life. I should like to dip her slightly in the soft waters of forgetfulness."

He leaned forward, kissing her brow and resting his hand on her fair hair once more. Yet, she felt his caress no more than if he had kissed a mask. She was too agitated by the chasm widening between their minds to register the contact of his lips.

"Tito, it is not because I suppose Florence is the pleasantest place in the world that I refuse to quit it. It is because I—because we must fulfill my father's wish. My godfather is old; he is seventy-one; we cannot leave him alone."

"It is precisely those superstitions that cling to your mind like bedimming clouds, my Romola, which constitute a major reason why I wish we were two hundred leagues from Florence. I am compelled to take charge of you against your own will: if those dear eyes, so tender in their gaze, see falsely, I must see for them and save my wife from wasting her life on impracticable dreams."

Romola sat silent and motionless. She could not blind herself to the direction of his words: he sought to persuade her that they could deposit the library in a monastery or find other means to sever their ties to Florence. She was determined never to submit her mind to his judgment on this matter of duty to her father; she was inwardly prepared to endure any pain in resistance. Yet, this determination remained latent in these first moments, crushed by the heart-wrenching realization that she and Tito were now openly divided in their wishes. He welcomed her silence; despite his fears regarding the strength of her feeling, it was impossible for him, confined within the narrowness of a merely clever and unimpassioned man, not to overestimate the persuasiveness of his own arguments. His conduct did not appear ugly to himself, nor did his imagination suffice to reveal exactly how it appeared to Romola. He continued in the same gentle, remonstrating tone.

"You know, dearest—and your own clear judgment always showed you—that the notion of isolating a collection of books and antiquities, attaching a single name to them forever, holds no valid, substantial good for its object. Moreover, it is liable to be defeated in a thousand ways. Witness what has become of the Medici collections! For my part, I consider it even blameworthy to entertain such petty views of appropriation. Why should anyone reasonably rejoice that Florence should possess the benefits of learned research and taste more than any other city? I understand your feeling regarding the wishes of the dead; but wisdom must place a limit on such sentiments, or lives might be continually wasted in futile devotion—like praising deaf gods forever. You gave your life to your father while he lived; why should you demand more of yourself?"

"Because it was a trust," Romola said in a low but distinct voice. "He trusted me, he trusted you, Tito. I did not expect you to feel anything else about it—to feel as I do—but I did expect you to feel that."

"Yes, dearest, of course I should feel it on a point where your father's real welfare or happiness was concerned; but there is no question of that now. If we believed in purgatory, I should be as anxious as you to have masses said; and if I believed it could now pain your father to see his library preserved and used in a rather different way from what he had set his mind on, I should share the strictness of your views. But a little philosophy should teach us to rid ourselves of those air-woven fetters that mortals hang round themselves, spending their lives in misery under the mere imagination of weight. Your mind, which seizes ideas so readily, my Romola, is able to discriminate between substantial good and these brain-wrought fantasies. Ask yourself, dearest, what possible good can these books and antiquities do, stowed together under your father's name in Florence, more than they would do if they were divided or carried elsewhere? Nay, is not the very dispersion of such things in hands that know how to value them one means of extending their usefulness? This rivalry of Italian cities is very petty and illiberal. The loss of Constantinople was the gain of the whole civilized world."

Romola was still under the painful pressure of the new revelation Tito was making of himself, her resistance finding no strong vent. As his fluent talk fell upon her ears, a rising contempt stirred within her, heightening her awareness of her bruised, despairing love for the Tito she had married and believed in. Her nature, possessed by the energies of strong emotion, recoiled from this hopelessly shallow readiness which professed to appropriate the widest sympathies yet lacked a pulse for the nearest. She still spoke like one restrained from showing all she felt. She had only drawn her arm away from his knee, sitting with her hands clasped before her, cold and motionless as locked waters.

"You talk of substantial good, Tito! Are faithfulness, and love, and sweet grateful memories no good? Is it no good that we should keep our silent promises on which others build because they believe in our love and truth? Is it no good that a just life should be justly honored? Or, is it good that we should harden our hearts against all the wants and hopes of those who have depended on us? What good can belong to men who have such souls? To talk cleverly, perhaps, and find soft couches for themselves, and live and die with their base selves as their best companions."

Her voice had gradually risen until there was a ring of scorn in the final words. She made a slight pause, but he saw other words quivering on her lips and chose to let them come.

"I know of no good for cities or the world if they are to be made up of such beings. But I am not thinking of other Italian cities and the whole civilized world—I am thinking of my father, and of my love and sorrow for him, and of his just claims on us. I would give up anything else, Tito, I would leave Florence—what else did I live for but for him and you? But I will not give up that duty. What have I to do with your arguments? It was a yearning of *his* heart, and therefore it is a yearning of mine."

Her voice, having been tremulous, became full and firm. She felt she had been urged to say all that was needful. She thought, poor thing, there was nothing harder to endure than this struggle against Tito's suggestions as against the meaner part of herself.

He began to see clearly that he could not persuade her into assent; he must take another course and show her that the time for resistance was past. That, at least, would end the struggle; and if the disclosure were not made by himself that night, it must be made another way. This necessity nerved his courage, and his experience of her affectionateness and unexpected submissiveness since their marriage encouraged him to hope that, at last, she would accommodate herself to his will.

"I am sorry to hear you speak in that spirit of blind persistence, my Romola," he said quietly, "because it obliges me to give you pain. But I partly foresaw your opposition, and as a prompt decision was necessary, I avoided that obstacle and decided without consulting you. The very care of a husband for his wife's interest compels him to that separate action sometimes—even when he has such a wife as you, my Romola."

She turned her eyes upon him in breathless inquiry.

"I mean," he answered her look, "that I have arranged for the transfer, both of the books and of the antiquities, where they will find the highest use and value. The books have been bought for the Duke of Milan; the marbles, bronzes, and the rest are going to France. Both will be protected by the stability of a great Power, instead of remaining in a city exposed to ruin."

Before he had finished speaking, Romola had started from her seat, standing up to look down at him with tightened hands falling before her, and, for the first time in her life, with a flash of fierceness in her scorn and anger.

"You have *sold* them?" she asked, as if she distrusted her own ears.

"I have," said Tito, shrinking back slightly. The scene was unpleasant; the descending scorn already scorched him.

"You are a treacherous man!" she said, with something grating in her voice as she looked down at him.

She was silent for a minute, and he sat still, feeling that ingenuity

Baldassarre wandered through Florence seeking the cheapest shelter, his feet drawn inevitably toward the only uneven patch of ground within the city walls. From this vantage point, elevated above the rooftops, one could see past the urban sprawl to the protective hills and the distant valley, visible only through the gap carved by the Arno. While part of this terrain was the famous stone-quarry of Bogoli, Baldassarre ascended the slope behind the Via de' Bardi, known locally as the hill of San Giorgio. Bratti had informed him that Tito resided on this street, and Baldassarre hoped to find a hiding place among the scattered dwellings here. Having grown accustomed to the vast, open sky over the years, the claustrophobic labyrinth of narrow Florentine alleys intensified his sense of isolation and the foggy decay of his memory.

The hill was largely garden land, save for a jagged section of rough ground scarred by a landslide that had destroyed houses centuries prior. Perched atop this broken earth stood a peculiar, truncated tower-like structure with fluted tiles, adjacent to a small outhouse wedged against a ruinous stone wall. Beneath a dying mulberry tree, its final leaves fluttering into the open doorways, a withered, hardy old woman untied a goat with two kids. Baldassarre noted that one section of the outbuilding housed livestock, while the other stood empty save for tools and straw—the perfect refuge he sought.

He approached the woman, but it took persistent shouting near her ear to penetrate her silence. Initially, she responded only with head shakes and muffled denials of "No lodging," her tone betraying a profound deafness. Undeterred, Baldassarre explained that he was a destitute stranger from overseas, unable to afford hostels, and offered a few quattrini weekly for the straw. The woman remained dubious, muttering to herself, until a new idea sparked. She retrieved a hatchet, gesturing to a log half-buried in litter, and proposed a bargain: if he chopped it up, he might sleep there for one night.

Agreeing, Baldassarre began to chop. Monna Lisa watched with arms akimbo, a smile of cunning satisfaction playing on her lips. *"It has lain there since my old man died,"* she thought, *"What then? I might as well have put a stone on the fire. He chops well, though he speaks a foreign tongue and looks odd. I couldn't have gotten it done cheaper. And if he only needs straw, I can make him run errands up and down the hill. Who needs to know? A hidden sin is half forgiven. He is a stranger; he won't notice *her*. I'll tell her to keep her tongue still."*

The object of her silent threat possessed a pair of blue eyes fixed on a large round hole in the shutter of the upper window. The shutter was closed not for punishment, but because the opposite window alone boasted glass; the chill weather made the four-inch aperture sufficient for spying. Though the hole was too high, forcing the small observer to stand on a rickety stool, Tessa endured the discomfort willingly. Drawn by the strange voice, she had darted across her room to peer out, remaining there until the wood was split and Baldassarre entered the outhouse as dusk settled, sinking onto the straw.

A powerful temptation seized Tessa. She desired to share her supper with the old man, to converse with him in a way she could not with the deaf Monna Lisa. Strangers were transient figures, living nowhere specific, and while disobedience weighed heavily on her conscience, the confession next Easter seemed manageable alongside her minor faults of dozing over beads and occasional petulance toward Monna Lisa. With an excited, pouting expression, she adjusted her dark-green serge gown, red girdle, and string of red glass beads, her brown hair neatly knotted with a silver pin. She wore a single new pride: a fine gold ring.

Sitting on the stool, her soul fluttered with the thrill of this forbidden transgression. She had been strictly commanded to make no acquaintances, warned that such friendships would turn her happiness into lead, yet she had obeyed so thoroughly that she kept her face shaded at church. Fear of her stepfather, Nofri, had aided her compliance, but this old man was merely a sleeping stranger, likely unaware of Nofri's existence. If she fed him, he would be pleased and silent. Monna Lisa's objections could be ignored; consequences mattered only after the deed was done.

Knowing supper was being prepared—a mountain of cheesy macaroni fragrant enough to tame any wanderer—Tessa descended the stairs with innocent inquiry, quickly knitting her brow into a peremptory frown like a kitten asserting dominance. She ordered the old woman upstairs, declaring her intention to eat below. Minutes later, lantern in one hand and a wooden bowl of macaroni in the other, she kicked gently at the outhouse door. Baldassarre, roused from a somber reverie, doubted his wakefulness as he opened the door to this surprising little handmaid, whose wide eyes broke into his dismal solitude with delight.

"I've brought you some supper," she shouted, leaning close to his ear as if addressing the deaf. "Sit down and eat it, while I stay with you."

Surprise and distrust overwhelmed Baldassarre, yet he felt no impulse to reject the visitor. He sank passively onto the straw as Tessa placed herself beside him, set the bowl on his lap, and positioned the lantern, crossing her hands and nodding significantly. *"Yes, you may really eat it,"* her gesture declared. In her excitement, she had forgotten he could hear, falling back on her habitual pantomime and shouting.

The invitation was welcome; he had been gnawing dry bread and craved warmth. Tessa watched him eat, initially wary of his fierce gaze, until she ventured closer again. "I like my supper, don't you?" she cried.

Baldassarre returned a look of mild kindness, resembling a dog touched by affection but unable to smile. "Yes," he said, "but I can hear well—I'm not deaf."

"It is true; I forgot," Tessa exclaimed, clasping her hands. "But Monna Lisa is deaf, and I live with her. She's a kind old woman, and I'm not frightened. We live very well; we have plenty of nice things. I can have nuts if I like. I used to work, but I didn't like it; I preferred feeding the mules. I'd love to see poor Giannetta again.
