We only have a goat and two kids now. I used to talk to the goat because there was no one else but Monna Lisa. But now I have something else—can you guess what it is?"

She pulled her head back, offering a challenging smile as if presenting a riddle.

"No," Baldassarre replied dreamily, setting aside his bowl. This young, prattling creature seemed a memory from his own youth.

"You like me to talk to you, don't you?" Tessa pressed. "But you must not tell anybody. Shall I fetch you some cold sausage?"

He shook his head, his expression now so mild that Tessa relaxed. "Well, then, I have a little baby. Such a pretty *bambinetto*, with tiny fingers and nails! Not old yet; born at the Nativita, Monna Lisa says. I was married one Nativita, a long time ago, and nobody knew. Oh, Santa Madonna! I didn't mean to tell you that!"

Tessa stiffened, biting her lip, watching Baldassarre as if expecting a dramatic reaction. But he seemed indifferent, perhaps due to the nature of strangers. "Yes," she continued aloud, "you are a stranger; you don't live anywhere or know anybody, do you?"

"No," Baldassarre murmured, thinking more than answering, "I only know one man."

"His name is not Nofri, is it?" Tessa asked anxiously.

"No," Baldassarre noticed her fear and clarified. "Is that your husband's name?"

The mistake amused Tessa. She laughed and clapped her hands. "No, indeed! But I must not tell you anything about my husband. You would never think what he is—not at all like Nofri!" She laughed again at the absurd contrast between the gruff name Nofri and her husband's image.

"But I don't see him very often," she said more seriously. "Sometimes I pray to the Holy Madonna to send him oftener, and once she did. But I must go back to my *bimbo* now. I'll bring it to show you tomorrow. You would like to see it. Sometimes it cries and makes a face, but only when it's hungry, Monna Lisa says. You wouldn't think it, but Monna Lisa had babies once, and they are all dead old men. My husband says she will never die now, because she's so well dried. I'm glad of that, for I'm fond of her. You would like to stay here tomorrow, shouldn't you?"

"I should like to have this place to come and rest in, that's all," Baldassarre said. "I would pay for it, and harm nobody."

"No, indeed; I think you are not a bad old man. But you look sorry about something. Tell me, is there anything you shall cry about when I leave you by yourself? *I* used to cry once."

"No, child; I think I shall cry no more."

"That's right; and I'll bring you some breakfast, and show you the *bimbo*. Good-night."

Tessa departed with her bowl and lantern, closing the door behind her. To Baldassarre, this loving apparition was no more than a faint rainbow against the blackness for a man wrestling in deep waters. He scarcely thought of her again until his dreamy waking faded into the vivid disturbances of sleep.

Tessa, however, dwelt deeply on him. Upon entering the house, she immediately confessed her actions to Monna Lisa, insisting the stranger be allowed to rest whenever he wished. The old woman, hoping to make him a useful tenant, feigned reluctance, shaking her head and claiming Messer Naldo would be angry. Tessa did not believe this; Naldo had never objected to strangers who lived nowhere, and this old man knew only one person who was not Nofri.

"Well," Monna Lisa finally conceded in her thick undertone, "if I let him stay for a while and carry things up the hill for me, thou must keep thy counsel and tell nobody."

"No," Tessa countered, "I'll only tell the *bimbo*."

"And then," Monna Lisa whispered, "God may love us well enough not to let Messer Naldo find out anything. For he never comes here but at dark; and as he was here two days ago, it's likely he'll never return until the old man is gone."

"Oh me! Monna," Tessa clasped her hands, "I wish Naldo had not to go such a long, long way sometimes before he comes back again."

"Ah, child! the world's big, they say. There are places behind the mountains, and if people go night and day, they get to Rome, and see the Holy Father."

Tessa looked submissive to this mystery, rocking her baby and singing vague, loving syllables that mimicked a triple chime.

The next morning, eager for more dialogue and the joy of showing her baby, Tessa was unusually industrious. However, before she could prepare his breakfast, she found Monna Lisa employing Baldassarre as a water carrier. She deferred her prayers, rushing down to insist he sit on his straw so she could sit beside him while he ate. This posture made the new companionship doubly delightful for Tessa, recalling her own days sitting on straw with goats and mules.

"I will not let Monna Lisa give you too much work," she said, bringing steaming broth and soft bread. "I don't like much work, and I daresay you don't. I like sitting in the sunshine and feeding things. Monna Lisa says work is good, but she does it all herself, so I don't mind. She's not a cross old woman; you needn't be afraid. Now, eat that, and I'll go fetch my baby to show you."

Soon she returned with the small mummy-case. The baby looked lively, possessing unusually large dark eyes, though lacking more than a hint of a future nose.

"This is my baby," Tessa said, seating herself close. "You didn't think it was so pretty, did you? It is like the little Gesu, and I should think the Santa Madonna would be kinder to me now, is it not true? But I have not much to ask for, because I have everything now—only that I should see my husband oftener. You may hold the *bambino* a little if you like, but I think you must not kiss him, because you might hurt him."

She spoke the prohibition with a tone of soothing excuse, and Baldassarre could not refuse to hold the small package. "Poor thing! poor thing!" he said in a deep voice that held a strangely threatening quality beneath its apparent pity. It did not seem to him that this guileless woman could reconcile him to the world; rather, she appeared to be with him against it, a creature who would need vengeance.

"Oh, don't you be sorry for me," she said. "Though I don't see him often, he is more beautiful and good than anybody else in the world. I say prayers to him when he's away. You couldn't think what he is!"

She looked at Baldassarre with a wide, mysterious glance, taking the baby back and almost wishing he would question her as if desperate to know more.

"Yes, I could," Baldassarre said, rather bitterly.

"No, I'm sure you never could," Tessa insisted earnestly. "You thought he might be Nofri," she added with triumphant conclusiveness. "But never mind; you couldn't know. What is your name?"

He rubbed his hand over his knitted brow, then looked at her blankly. "Ah, child, what is it

No Place for Repentance.

Messer Naldo returned sooner than anyone anticipated, arriving on the evening of the twenty-eighth of November, merely eleven days after his previous visit. This brevity proved he had not ventured far beyond the mountains, and the scene that unfolded in the Via de' Bardi that night serves to illuminate the impulse that drew his steps toward the hill of San Giorgio.

When Tito first secured this dwelling for Tessa upon his return from Rome, more than a year and a half prior, he convinced himself he acted solely under the constraint of his own benevolence following the unfortunate incident that led the innocent Tessa to believe he was her husband. While it was true that his kindness flowed naturally toward such a trusting, pretty creature, it was equally true that Tito harbored feelings of affection for her distinct from any calculated gain. Though he might have preferred freedom from her company had he not been captivated by her charm, he was not in love with Tessa. His heart belonged, for the first time, to an entirely different woman—Romola. Her presence possessed him with such intensity that the mere sweep of her long tresses across his cheek seemed to vibrate through the hours. All the youthful, ideal passion within him had been awakened by her. His fiber was too refined, his intellect too bright, to succumb to the habits of a gross pleasure-seeker. Yet, he had spun a web around himself and Tessa that he felt incapable of breaking. In the initial moments after the mock marriage, he had been prompted to leave her under an illusion by a distinct calculation of his own potential needs; but since that critical moment, it seemed the web had spun itself in spite of him, growing like a vine over which he held no power. The elements of kindness and self-indulgence were difficult to distinguish in a soft nature like Tito's. The annoyance he had felt under Tessa's persistent pursuit on the day of his betrothal, coupled with his thorough intention to reveal the truth when he fulfilled his promise to see her again, served as sufficient proof to him that he had ultimately left Tessa under her delusion and provided her a home because he was overcome by his own kindness. In these days of his first devotion to Romola, he required a self-justifying argument. He had learned to find solace in the fact that she was deceived about certain matters. Yet, every strong feeling constructs its own conscience, possesses its own piety—much like the bond between a son and a mother, which can sometimes endure even amidst the foulest corruption. Tito could not yet find peace committing a secret offense against his wedded love.

Consequently, he exercised extreme caution to preserve the secrecy of this transgression. Monna Lisa, who, like many of her class, rarely left her habitation except to visit a few specific shops or attend confession once a year, knew nothing of his true name or whereabouts. She only knew that he paid her handsomely to ensure her comfort and cared little for the rest, save that she had grown fond of Tessa and found pleasure in the duties for which she was compensated. There was clearly some mystery at play, given that Tessa was a peasant girl (*contadina*) while Messer Naldo was a gentleman; but for all Monna Lisa knew, he might have been a real husband. Tito had thoroughly frightened Tessa into silence regarding the circumstances of their union by warning her that if she broke that silence, she would never see him again. Furthermore, Monna Lisa's deafness, which made it impossible to communicate with her without significant premeditation, had saved Tessa from any incautious revelation to her, such as the slip of the tongue she had made while speaking with Baldassarre. For a long while, Tito's visits were so infrequent that it seemed plausible he undertook journeys between them. These were prompted chiefly by a desire to ensure all was well with Tessa. Although he always found the visit more pleasant than the prospect of it—always renewing his appreciation for that pretty, ignorant lovingness and trust—he had not yet felt a pressing need for it. However, he was determined, if possible, to preserve the simplicity upon which this charm depended; to keep Tessa a genuine peasant and not place the small field-flower among conditions that would rob it of its grace. He would have been horrified to see her dressed in the attire of any rank higher than her own; the piquancy of her conversation would vanish if things began to acquire new relations for her, if her world expanded and her pleasures became less childish. The squirrel-like enjoyment of nuts at her discretion marked the standard of the luxuries he had provided. By this means, Tito saved Tessa's charm from being sullied, and he also, through a convenient coincidence, spared himself from aggravating expenses that were already becoming burdensome to a man whose funds were entirely consumed by his avowed habits of life.

Briefly, this was the history of Tito's relationship with Tessa up until a very recent date. It is true that once or twice before Bardo's death, the knowledge that Tessa resided up the hill, with whom it was possible to spend an agreeable hour, had served as an inducement for him to escape the slight weariness of the old man, when, lacking any positive engagement, he might otherwise have borne the weariness patiently and shared Romola's burden. But the moment when he first felt a genuine hunger for Tessa's ignorant lovingness and her belief in *him* had arrived only quite recently, distinctly marked by circumstances as unforgettable as the onset of a malady that permanently vitiated sight and hearing. It was the day he first saw Baldassarre and purchased the armor. Returning across the bridge that night with the coat of mail in his hands, he had felt an unconquerable shrinking from an immediate encounter with Romola. She, too, knew little of the actual world; she, too, trusted him. Yet, he possessed an uneasy consciousness that behind her frank eyes lay a nature capable of judging him, and that any ill-founded trust of hers sprang not from a pretty, brute-like incapacity, but from a nobleness that might prove an alarming touchstone. He desired a little ease, a little repose from self-control, after the agitation and exertions of the day; he wished to be where he could adjust his mind to the morrow without caring how he behaved in the present moment. And there was a sweet, adoring creature within reach whose presence was as safe and unconstraining as that of her own kids—one who would believe any fable and remain utterly unimpressed by public opinion. Thus, on that evening, when Romola waited and listened for him, he turned his steps up the hill.

No wonder, then, that his steps took the same course on this evening, eleven days later, when he had been forced to recoil under Romola's first outburst of scorn. He could not wish Tessa in his wife's place, nor refrain from wishing that his wife should be thoroughly reconciled to him; for it was Romola, and not Tessa, who belonged to the world where all the larger desires of a man possessing ambition and effective faculties must necessarily lie. Yet, he sought a refuge from a standard disagreeably rigorous, from which he could not make himself independent simply by dismissing it as folly; and Tessa's little soul was that inviting refuge.

It was not much past eight o'clock when he ascended the stone steps to the door of Tessa's room. Usually, she heard his entrance and ran to meet him, but not that night. When he opened the door, he saw the reason. A single dim light burned above the dying fire, illuminating Tessa in a kneeling attitude by the head of the bed where the baby lay. Her head had fallen aside onto the pillow, and her brown rosary, which usually hung above the pillow over the picture of the Madonna and the golden palm-branches, lay in the loose grasp of her right hand. She had fallen fast asleep over her beads. Tito stepped lightly across the small room and sat down close to her. She had probably heard the opening of the door as part of her dream, for she had not been looking at him two moments before she opened her eyes. She opened them without any start and remained quite motionless, gazing at him as if the sense of his presence smiling at her shut out any impulse that could disturb that happy passiveness. But when he placed his hand under her chin and stooped to kiss her, she said—

"I dreamed it, and then I said it was dreaming—and then I awoke, and it was true."

"Little sinner!" said Tito, pinching her chin, "you have not said half your prayers. I will punish you by not looking at your baby; he is ugly."

Tessa did not like those words, even though Tito was smiling. A pouting distress clouded her face as she said, bending anxiously over the infant—

"Oh, it is not true! He is prettier than anything. You do not think he is ugly. You will look at him. He is even prettier than when you saw him before—only he is asleep, and you cannot see his eyes or his tongue, and I cannot show you his hair—and it grows—isn't that wonderful? Look at him! It's true his face is very much alike when he is asleep; there is not so much to see as when he is awake. If you kiss him very gently, he won't wake: you want to kiss him, is it not true?"

He satisfied her by giving the small mummy a butterfly kiss, then placing his hand on her shoulder and turning her face toward him, he said, "You like looking at the baby better than looking at your husband, you false one!"

She was still kneeling, and now rested her hands on his knee, looking up at him like one of Fra Lippo Lippi's round-cheeked adoring angels.

"No," she said, shaking her head; "I love you always best, only I want you to look at the bambino and love him; I used only to want you to love me."

"And did you expect me to come again so soon?" said Tito, inclined to make her prattle. He still felt the effects of the agitation he had undergone—still felt like a man who has been violently jarred; and this was the easiest relief from silence and solitude.

"Oh, no," said Tessa. "I have counted the days—to-day I began at my right thumb again—since you put on the beautiful chain-coat that Messer San Michele gave you to take care of you on your journey. And you have got it on now," she said, peering through the opening in the breast of his tunic. "Perhaps it made you come back sooner."

"Perhaps it did, Tessa," he said. "But don't mind the coat now. Tell me what has happened since I was here. Did you see the tents in the Prato, and the soldiers and horsemen when they passed the bridges—did you hear the drums and trumpets?"

"Yes, and I was rather frightened, because I thought the soldiers might come up here. And Monna Lisa was a little afraid too, for she said they might carry our kids off; she said it was their business to do mischief. But the Holy Madonna took care of us, for we never saw one of them up here. But something has happened, only I hardly dare tell you, and that is what I was saying more Aves for."

"What do you mean, Tessa?" said Tito, rather anxiously. "Make haste and tell me."

"Yes, but will you let me sit on your knee? Because then I think I shall not be so frightened."

He took her on his knee and put his arm round her, but looked grave; it seemed that something unpleasant must pursue him even here.

"At first I didn't mean to tell you," said Tessa, speaking almost in a whisper, as if that would mitigate the offense; "because we thought the old man would be gone away before you came again, and it would be as if it had not been. But now he is there, and you are come, and I never did anything you told me not to do before. And I want to tell you, and then you will perhaps forgive me, for it is a long while before I go to confession."

"Yes, tell me everything, my Tessa." He began to hope it was after all a trivial matter.

"Oh, you will be sorry for him: I'm afraid he cries about something when I don't see him. But that was not the reason I went to him first; it was because I wanted to talk to him and show him my baby, and he was a stranger that lived nowhere, and I thought you wouldn't care so much about my talking to him. And I think he is not a bad old man, and he wanted to come and sleep on the straw next to the goats, and I made Monna Lisa say, 'Yes, he might,' and he's away all the day almost, but when he comes back I talk to him, and take him something to eat."

"Some beggar, I suppose. It was naughty of you, Tessa, and I am angry with Monna Lisa. I must have him sent away."

"No, I think he is not a beggar, for he wanted to pay Monna Lisa, only she asked him to do work for her instead. And he gets himself shaved, and his clothes are tidy: Monna Lisa says he is a decent man. But sometimes I think he is not in his right mind: Lupo, at Peretola, was not in his right mind, and he looks a little like Lupo sometimes, as if he didn't know where he was."

"What sort of face has he?" said Tito, his heart beginning to beat strangely. He was so haunted by the thought of Baldassarre that it was already him whom he saw in imagination sitting on the straw not many yards from him. "Fetch your stool, my Tessa, and sit on it."

"Shall you not forgive me?" she said, timidly, moving from his knee.

"Yes, I will not be angry—only sit down, and tell me what sort of old man this is."

"I can't think how to tell you: he is not like my stepfather Nofri, or anybody. His face is yellow, and he has deep marks in it; and his hair is white, but there is none on the top of his head: and his eyebrows are black, and he looks from under them at

For several days, Tito saw little of Romola. The next morning, he approached her with a gentle demeanor, suggesting it would be prudent for her to clear away the smaller personal items from the library, as agents were arriving to pack the antiquities. Leaning in to kiss her forehead, he advised her to retreat to her own chamber, where the small painted tabernacle stood, keeping herself away from the clamor of unfamiliar footsteps. Romola assented quietly, offering no sign of emotion. The night had been a prolonged vigil for her; despite her robust constitution, sensation had dulled into a continuous, dull ache, as though she had been stunned and bruised. Tito sensed her illness but dared not speak of it. Seeing her hand and brow turned to ice, he merely fetched a fur-lined mantle and draped it lightly over her shoulders. In every brief interval he returned, the scene remained unchanged: he attempted to soothe her with unobtrusive acts of tenderness, yet she seemed to have lost the capacity to speak to him or even to look at him.

"Patience!" he murmured to himself. "She will recover, and forgive at last. The bond between us must remain the strongest." When a struck person lies slow to rise and appears untouched, the striker often slides effortlessly into the role of the aggrieved party, feeling no pain himself while acutely aware of his own benevolence since delivering the blow. Yet Tito was not naturally inclined to feel aggrieved; his mind bent constantly toward reconciliation. He would have endured much just to feel Romola's hand rest upon his head again, as it had that morning when he first recoiled from gazing at her.

He found it easier to wait patiently for the return of domestic happiness because his life outside the walls grew increasingly captivating. While a course of action is often the slow outgrowth of an entire character, it usually traces back to a single impression as its apparent origin. Since the moment in the Piazza del Duomo, when Tito, mounted on bales, had tasted the keen pleasure of pleasing the ears of men with any phrase that suited them, his imagination had drifted toward a form of political activity that Florence's turbulent public life might soon demand. However, a fresh dread of Baldassarre, awakened at that same instant, had lain like an immovable rock across that path, urging him to sell the library as preparation for fleeing Florence at the very moment he began to feel a new attraction to the city. That dread was nearly lifted now. He must still wear his armor, preparing himself for demands on his coolness and ingenuity, but he no longer felt compelled to take the inconvenient step of leaving Florence to seek new fortunes. His father had refused the offered atonement, forcing him into defiance; an old man in a strange land, with his memory gone, was too weak to defy such resolve.

Tito's implicit desires were now unfolding into explicit thoughts. As the freshness of young passion faded, life took on for him the aspect of a game blending skill and chance. The game Florence promised was rapid and exciting—a struggle of revolution and party, certain to include ample unavowed action where brilliant ingenuity, discarding all inconvenient beliefs save that "ginger is hot in the mouth," sees the path of superior wisdom.

No sooner had the French guests departed than Florence became as agitated as a colony of ants when an alarming shadow is removed and the camp must be hastily repaired. "How shall we raise funds for the French king? How manage the war with the obstinate Pisan rebels? Above all, how mend our plan of government to ensure magistrates are chosen and laws voted correctly?" Until these questions were answered, trade faced stagnation, and the large body of working men, excluded from citizenship and lacking even a vote to soothe their stomachs, were likely to grow impatient. Something had to be done.

First, the great bell rang, summoning the citizens to a parliament in the Piazza de' Signori. When the crowd was wedged tight and hemmed in by armed men at every outlet, the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and the eight Priors—emerged and stood by the stone lion on the platform before the Old Palace. They proposed that twenty chief men of the city be granted dictatorial authority for one year, empowered to choose all magistrates and set the government in order. The people shouted their assent, feeling themselves the electors of the Twenty. This kind of "parliament" was an old Florentine fashion, designed to make the will of the few appear as the choice of the many.

The shouting in the square soon ceased, but the debate inside the palace continued. Was Florence to adopt a Great Council in the Venetian mode, where all officers and laws were elected by a wide number of qualified citizens regardless of rank or party? Or was it to be governed by a narrower scheme, preserving the hereditary influence of good families against the dilution of shopkeepers' votes? Doctors of law disputed day after day, far into the night. Messer Pagolantonio Soderini argued excellently for the popular scheme; Messer Guidantonio Vespucci countered with equally excellent reasons for a more aristocratic form. It was a question of boiled or roast meat, prejudged by the palates of the disputants; their excellent arguing might have been prolonged indefinitely without more result than deferring the cooking. The majority inside the palace, holding power already, sided with Vespucci, believing change should be moderate. The majority outside, conscious of little power and many grievances, feared change less.

Outside the palace, a force was gradually giving the vague desires of that majority the character of a determinate will: the preaching of Savonarola. Impelled partly by a spiritual necessity to guide the people and partly by public men who could carry no measures without his aid, he rapidly shifted in his daily sermons from the general to the specific. He moved from urging hearers to postpone private passions for the public good, to telling them precisely what government they must have to achieve it—from "Choose whatever is best for all" to "Choose the Great Council," and "The Great Council is the will of God."

To Savonarola, these were identical propositions. The Great Council was the only practical plan to express a public will large enough to counteract the corrupting influence of party interests; it was a plan that could make honest, impartial public action possible. The purer Florence's government became—the more secure from those who profited from the moral debasement of their fellows—the closer the Florentine people would approach the character of a pure community, worthy to lead the renovation of the Church and the world. Fra Girolamo's mind never stopped short of that sublimest end; the objects toward which he worked always possessed the same moral magnificence. He harbored no private malice and sought no petty gratification. Even in the last terrible days, when ignominy, torture, and the fear of torture laid bare every hidden weakness of his soul, he could tell his importunate judges: "Do not wonder if it seems to you that I have told but few things; for my purposes were few and great."

More than three weeks passed before the library's contents were finally packed and removed. Romola did not shut her eyes or ears to the process; instead, she watched it unfold. The exhaustion born of violent emotion often breeds a dreamy disbelief in the reality of its cause, yet here, in the evenings when the workmen had departed, Romola took her hand-lamp and wandered slowly through the chaos of straw and wooden cases. She paused at every vacant pedestal, every familiar object laid prostrate, driven by a bitter desire to confirm that there was a sufficient reason why her love had vanished and why the world had grown barren for her. As the nights wore on, however, her purpose shifted. She returned no longer merely to assure herself, but because this vivification of pain and despair regarding her father's memory constituted the strongest life remaining in her affections. By December 23rd, she knew the final packages were to leave. She ran to the loggia atop the house, refusing to miss the last pang of watching the slow wheels cross the bridge.

It was a cloudy day, nearing dusk. The Arno ran dark and shivering; the hills were mournful. Florence, viewed from above with its girdling stone towers, possessed a silent, tomb-like quality that unbroken shadow casts upon a city. Santa Croce, where her father lay, was dark amidst that darkness. Slowly crawling over the bridge and vanishing up the narrow street was the white load, appearing like a cruel, deliberate Fate carrying away her father's lifelong hope to bury it in an unmarked grave. Romola felt less that she was witnessing this herself than that her father was conscious of it as he lay helpless beneath the imprisoning stones, where her hand could not reach his to tell him he was not alone.

She stood still even after the load had disappeared, heedless of the cold, soothed by the gloom which seemed to cover her like a mourning garment, shutting out the discord of joy. Suddenly, the great bell in the palace-tower rang out a mighty peal—not the hammer-sound of alarm, but an agitated peal of triumph. One after another, every other bell in every other tower seemed to catch the vibration and join the chorus. As the chorus swelled until the air seemed made of sound, little flames, vibrating as if the sound had caught fire, burst out between the turrets of the palace and on the girdling towers.

That sudden clang and leaping light fell upon Romola like sharp wounds. They were the triumph of demons at the success of her husband's treachery and the desolation of her life. Less than three weeks prior, she had been intoxicated by the sound of those very bells; in the gladness of Florence, she had heard a prophecy of her own joy. Now, the general joy seemed cruel to her. She stood aloof from that common life—that Florence flinging out its loud exultation to stun the ears of sorrow and loneliness. She could never join hands with gladness again, only with those whom the hard nature of gladness chose to forget. In her bitterness, she felt all rejoicing was mockery. Men shouted paeans with their souls full of heaviness, then looked into their neighbors' faces to see if there was truly such a thing as joy. Romola had lost her belief in the happiness she once thirsted for; it was a hateful, smiling, soft-handed thing with a narrow, selfish heart.

She ran down from the loggia, pressing her hands against her ears, and hurried across the antechamber when she was startled by unexpectedly meeting her husband, who was coming to seek her. His step was elastic, and there was a radiance of satisfaction about him not quite usual.

"What! The noise was a little too much for you?" he said, for Romola, starting at the sight of him, had pressed her hands closer to her ears. He took her gently by the wrist, drew her arm within his, and led her into the saloon surrounded by dancing nymphs and fauns. "Florence is gone quite mad at getting its Great Council, which is to put an end to all the evils under the sun, especially to the vice of merriment. You may well look stunned, my Romola, and you are cold. You must not stay so late under that windy loggia without wrappings."

"I was coming to tell you," he continued, "that I am suddenly called to Rome about some learned business for Bernardo Rucellai. I am going away immediately, for I am to join my party at San Gaggio tonight, that we may start early in the morning. I need give you no trouble; I have had my packages made already. It will not be very long before I am back again."

He knew he could expect from her nothing but quiet endurance of what he said and did. He could not even venture to kiss her brow that evening, but merely pressed her hand to his lips and left her. Tito felt that Romola was a more unforgiving woman than he had imagined; her love was not that sweet, clinging instinct, stronger than all judgments, which he now began to see was the great charm of a wife. Still, this petrified coldness was preferable to a passionate, futile opposition. Her pride and her capability of seeing where resistance was useless had their convenience.

But when the door closed on Tito, Romola lost the look of cold immobility which came over her like an inevitable frost whenever he approached. Inwardly, she was very far from being in a state of quiet endurance. The days that had passed since the scene dividing her from Tito had been days of active planning and preparation for the fulfillment of a purpose.

The first thing she did was to call old Maso to her.

"Maso," she said in a decided tone, "we take our journey tomorrow morning. We shall be able now to overtake that first convoy of cloth while they are waiting at San Piero. See about the two mules tonight, and be ready to set off with them at break of day, and wait for me at Trespiano."

She meant to take Maso with her as far as Bologna, and then send him back with letters to her godfather and Tito, telling them she was gone and never meant to return. She had planned her departure so that its secrecy might be perfect, her broken love and life hidden away unscanned by vulgar eyes. Bernardo del Nero had been absent at his villa, willing to escape political suspicions to his favorite occupation of attending to his land, and she had paid him the debt without a personal interview. He did not even know the library was sold, left to conjecture that some sudden piece of good fortune had enabled Tito to raise this sum of money. Maso had been taken into her confidence only so far that he knew her intended journey was a secret; and to do just what she told him was the thing he cared most for in his withered, wintry age.

Romola did not mean to go to bed that night. When she had fastened the door, she took her taper to the carved and painted chest containing her wedding clothes. The white silk and gold lay there, the long white veil and the circlet of pearls. A great sob rose as she looked at them; they seemed the shroud of her dead happiness. In a tiny gold loop of the circlet, a sugar-plum had lodged—a pink hailstone from the shower of sweets. Tito had detected it first and had said it should always remain there. At certain moments—and this was one of them—Romola was carried, by a sudden wave of memory, back again into the time of perfect trust. She felt again the presence of the husband whose love made the world as fresh and wonderful to her as to a little child sitting in stillness among sunny flowers; she heard the gentle tones, saw the soft eyes without any lie in them, and breathed again that large freedom of the soul which comes from the faith that the being nearest to us is greater than ourselves. And in those brief moments, the tears always rose: the woman's lovingness felt something akin to what the bereaved mother feels when the tiny fingers seem to lie warm on her bosom, yet are marble to her lips as she bends over the silent bed.

But there was something else lying in the chest besides the wedding clothes: it was something dark and coarse, rolled up in a close bundle. She turned her eyes away from the white and gold to the dark bundle, and as her hands touched the serge, her tears began to be checked. That coarse roughness recalled her fully to the present, from which love and delight were gone. She unfastened the thick white cord and spread the bundle out on the table. It was the grey serge dress of a sister belonging to the third order of Saint Francis, living in the world but especially devoted to deeds of piety—a personage the Florentines were accustomed to call a Pinzochera. Romola was going to put on this dress as a disguise, and she determined to put it on at once, so that, if she needed sleep before the morning, she might wake up in perfect readiness to be gone.

She put off her black garment, and as she thrust her soft white arms into the harsh sleeves of the serge mantle and felt the hard girdle of rope hurt her fingers as she tied it, she courted those rude sensations. They were in keeping with her new scorn of that thing called pleasure which made men base—that dexterous contrivance for selfish ease, that shrinking from endurance and strain when others were bowing beneath burdens too heavy for them, which now made one image with her husband. Then she gathered her long hair together, drew it tight away from her face, bound it in a great hard knot at the back of her head, and taking a square piece of black silk, tied it in the fashion of a kerchief close across her head and under her chin; and over that she drew the cowl. She lifted the candle to the mirror. Surely her disguise would be complete to anyone who had not lived very near to her. To herself, she looked strangely like her brother Dino: the full oval of the cheek had only to be wasted; the eyes, already sad, had only to become a little sunken. Was she getting more like him in anything else? Only in this, that she understood now how men could be prompted to rush away forever from earthly delights, how they could be prompted to dwell on images of sorrow rather than of beauty and joy.

But she did not linger at the mirror; she set about collecting and packing all the relics of her father and mother that were too large to be carried in her small traveling wallet. They were all to be put in the chest along with her wedding clothes, and the chest was to be committed to her godfather when she was safely gone. First she laid in the portraits; then one by one every little thing that had a sacred memory clinging to it was put into her wallet or into the chest.

She paused. There was still something else to be stripped away from her, belonging to that past on which she was going to turn her back forever. She put her thumb and her forefinger to her betrothal ring, but they rested there, without drawing it off. Romola's mind had been rushing with an impetuous current toward this act, for which she was preparing: the act of quitting a husband who had disappointed all her trust, the act of breaking an outward tie that no longer represented the inward bond of love. But that force of outward symbols by which our active life is knit together so as to make an inexorable external identity for us, not to be shaken by our wavering consciousness, gave a strange effect to this simple movement toward taking off her ring—a movement which was but a small sequence of her energetic resolution. It brought a vague but arresting sense that she was somehow violently rending her life in two: a presentiment that the strong impulse which had seemed to exclude doubt and make her path clear might after all be blindness, and that there was something in human bonds which must prevent them from being broken with the breaking of illusions.

If that beloved Tito who had placed the betrothal ring on her finger was not in any valid sense the same Tito whom she had ceased to love, why should she return to him the sign of their union, and not rather retain it as a memorial? And this act, which came as a palpable demonstration of her own and his identity, had a power unexplained to herself of shaking Romola. It is the way with half the truth amidst which we live that it only haunts us and makes dull pulsations that are never born into sound. But there was a passionate voice speaking within her that presently nullified all such muffled murmurs.

"It cannot be! I cannot be subject to him. He is false. I shrink from him. I despise him!"

She snatched the ring from her finger and laid it on the table against the pen with which she meant to write. Again she felt that there could be no law for her but the law of her affections. That tenderness and keen fellow-feeling for the near and the loved, which are the main outgrowth of the affections, had made the religion of her life; they had made her patient in spite of natural impetuosity; they would have sufficed to make her heroic. But now all that strength was gone, or, rather, it was converted into the strength of repulsion. She had recoiled from Tito in proportion to the energy of that young belief and love which he had disappointed, of that lifelong devotion to her father against which he had committed an irredeemable offense. And it seemed as if all motive had slipped away from her, except the indignation and scorn that made her tear herself asunder from him.

She was not acting after any precedent, or obeying any adopted maxims. The grand severity of the stoical philosophy in which her father had taken care to instruct her was familiar enough to her ears and lips, and its lofty spirit had raised certain echoes within her; but she had never used it, never needed it as a rule of life. She had endured and forbore because she loved; maxims which told her to feel less, and not to cling close lest the onward course of great Nature should jar her, had been as powerless on her tenderness as they had been on her father's yearning for just fame. She had appropriated no theories; she had simply felt strong in the strength of affection, and life without that energy came to her as an entirely new problem.

She was going to solve the problem in a way that seemed to her very simple. Her mind had never yet

The first sound of the morning was a tap at the door, sharp and intrusive against the silence of the room. Romola woke to find Maso standing there, his face illuminated by the pale, hard light of dawn. He had come for the travelling-wallet. When she opened the door, the old man started, expecting the familiar, graceful silhouette crowned with the brightness of her hair. Instead, he saw only the heavy folds of a grey mantle and a pale face obscured by a dark cowl.

"It is well, Maso," Romola said, forcing her voice to remain steady and calm. "The wallet is ready. Go on quietly; I shall not be far behind. Once you pass the gates, you may walk slower, for I hope to join you before you reach Trespiano."

She closed the door behind him, locking the silence back into the house. Then, her hand went to the key she had taken from the casket the night before. It was the original key to the little painted tabernacle. Tito had failed to drown it in the Arno; instead, it had lodged in the corner of the embroidered *scarsella* he wore beneath his purple tunic. Years later, Romola had found it there, setting it aside without use, yet feeling a quiet satisfaction that it remained within reach.

The cabinet holding the tabernacle had been moved near a window, where the morning light now struck it, rendering the painted figures distinct enough for Romola to see them clearly—the triumphant Bacchus with his vine-clad spear embracing the crowned Ariadne; the Loves scattering roses; the wreathed vessels and the cunning-eyed dolphins amidst the rippling sea, all framed by a flowery border like a paradise bower. On this chill morning, however, the familiar images offered no comfort. They seemed a more pitiful mockery than ever, a lying screen rather than a tomb of sorrow. Foolish Ariadne, with her gaze of love, believing that bright face held the secret of her life!

"Ariadne is wonderfully transformed," Romola thought bitterly. "She would look strange among these vines and roses now."

She picked up the mirror and gazed at her reflection. The sight was startling in the harsh morning light, and she laid it down again, feeling a shrinking almost as intense as when she had turned away from the joyous painting. To recognize her own face, veiled in the cowl, brought back the dread of being drawn into the fellowship of wretched superstition, of joining the howling fanatics and weeping nuns whom she had despised since childhood. With trembling fingers, she thrust the key into the tabernacle, opened it hurriedly, and withdrew the crucifix without looking at it. She passed a cord through the small ring, hung the cross around her neck, and hid it deep within the bosom of her mantle. "For Dino's sake," she whispered to herself.

Still, there were matters to attend to. The letters for Maso to carry back to Bologna were brief. The first was addressed to Tito:

"Tito, my love for you is dead; and therefore, so far as I was yours, I too am dead. Do not attempt to enforce any laws to fetch me back; it would bring you no happiness. The Romola you married can never return. I need explain nothing further after the words I spoke to you during our last long conversation. If you believed them to be words of transient anger, you will now know they marked an irreversible change.

"I believe you will fulfill my wish that my bridal chest be sent to my godfather, who gave it to me. It contains my wedding clothes and the portraits and other relics of my father and mother."

She folded the ring inside this letter and wrote Tito's name on the outside. The second letter was to Bernardo del Nero:

"Dearest Godfather,—If I could have been of any good to your life by staying, I would not have gone so far away. But now I am gone. Do not ask the reason; and if you love my father, try to prevent anyone from seeking me. I could not bear my life in Florence. I cannot bear to tell anyone why. Help to cover my lot in silence. I have requested that my bridal chest be sent to you: when you open it, you will understand the reason. Please give all the things that belonged to my mother to my cousin Brigida, and ask her to forgive me for not saying words of parting to her.

"Farewell, my second father. The best thing I have in life remains the memory of your goodness and my gratitude.

"Romola."

Romola placed the letters and the crucifix within the bosom of her mantle. Everything was done. She was ready to depart.

The house was empty. She descended the stairs and stepped into the silent street almost as quietly as a grey phantom. Her heart hammered violently, yet she felt a strange enjoyment in the firm tread of her feet on the broad flags, in the swift movement that felt like a chained resolution finally set free. The anxiety to complete her act and the dread of any obstacle kept sorrow at bay. As she reached the Ponte Rubaconte, she felt less that Santa Croce was visible before her than that the yellow streak of morning light, parting the grey, was widening. Unless she hastened, she would soon encounter faces.

Her direct path led straight to the Borgo Pinti, then along the walls to the Porta San Gallo, the exit from the city, a route that took her past the Piazza di Santa Croce. Yet she walked steadily and rapidly through the square, refusing to look toward the church. The thought that eyes might turn upon her with curiosity or recognition, that indifferent minds might speculate on her private sorrows, made her shrink physically as from imagined torture. She felt degraded even by the act of her husband from which she suffered helplessly. Yet there was no sign that any eyes looked forth from the windows to notice this tall grey sister, with her firm step and the proud, hidden attitude of her cowl. Her road lay clear of the early traffic's stir. Reaching the Porta San Gallo, she slipped through easily while a dispute raged over the toll for panniers of eggs and market produce entering the gate.

Out! Once past the houses of the Borgo, she would be beyond Florence's last fringe. The sky would be broad above her, and she would enter a new life—one of loneliness and endurance, but of freedom. She had been strong enough to snap the bonds she had accepted in blind faith. Whatever befell her, she would no longer feel the breath of soft, hated lips warming her cheek, nor the stifling breath of an odious mind crushing her own. The bare wintry morning, the chill air, were welcome in their severity. The leafless trees and sombre hills were not haunted by the gods of beauty and joy, whose worship she had forever forsaken.

Presently, the light burst forth with sudden strength, casting long shadows across the road. It seemed the sun intended to chase away the greyness. Perhaps light is never felt more strongly as a divine presence stirring the inarticulate sensibilities of our deepest lives than in these moments when it instantly awakens the shadows. A certain awe, inevitable in this most momentous act of her life, became a conscious element in Romola's feeling as she found herself in the sudden presence of the impalpable golden glory and the long shadow of herself that could not be escaped.

Thus far, she had met only occasional countrymen with mules, and the many turns of the level road had prevented her from seeing that Maso was not far ahead. But when she passed Pietra and climbed onto rising ground, she lifted the hanging roof of her cowl and looked eagerly before her.

The cowl was dropped immediately. She had seen not Maso, but two monks approaching within a few yards. The edge of her cowl had acted as a penthouse over her brow, shutting out anything above eye level; for the last few moments, she had seen only the brightness of the path and her own shadow, tall and shrouded like a dread spectre.

She wished now that she had not looked up. Her disguise made her especially dislike encountering monks; they might expect pious passwords of which she knew nothing. She walked along with a careful appearance of unconsciousness until she saw the skirts of their black mantles pass her. The encounter made her heart beat unpleasantly. Romola harbored an uneasiness in her religious disguise, a shame at this studied concealment, made more distinct by her special effort to appear unaware under actual glances.

But the black skirts would vanish the faster, for they were going downhill. Seeing a great flat stone against a cypress that rose from a projecting green bank, she yielded to the desire the slight shock had given her to sit and rest.

She turned her back on Florence, intending not to look at it until the monks were quite out of sight. Raising the edge of her cowl again once seated, she discerned Maso and the mules at a distance where it was not hopeless for her to overtake them, for the old man would likely linger in expectation.

Meanwhile, she might pause a little. She was free and alone.

The Black Marks Become Magical.

The journey Tito Melema undertook to Rome, a move that cleared the path for Romola's own departure, had been decided upon with sudden urgency during a supper the previous evening. Tito had left for that meal buoyed by pleasant anticipations: delicate meats, choice wines, and a company of distinction awaited him. The venue was the Selva, or Orto de' Rucellai—the Rucellai Gardens—and the host, Bernardo Rucellai, epitomized the Florentine grandee. His very name carried a symbolic weight; derived from the *roccella*, a lichen growing on Greek isles and the Canaries that yields a rich purple dye under specific conditions, the surname reflected a lineage enriched by the importation of this secret, the *oricello*, from the Levant.

Bernardo stood out vividly against this purple heritage. He had married the sister of Lorenzo de' Medici, hosting a wedding of unparalleled splendor. Sent on embassies to France and Venice, elected Gonfaloniere, and a patron of the arts, he had constructed a magnificent palace and completed the black-and-white marble façade of Santa Maria Novella. He had cultivated a garden of rare trees, transforming it into classic ground for the Platonic Academy following Lorenzo's death, authored a learned treatise on ancient Rome, and possessed a pure Latinity. To summarize him was to write a laudatory epitaph so exhaustive that the Greek and Ausonian Muses might weep at the impossibility of nature combining such virtues within a single vessel.

Lorenzo Tornabuoni had conveyed Bernardo's invitation to Tito, with an emphasis suggesting political intent, though the public questions of the age were already consuming all attention. Tito, aware that Bernardo was one of the elect Twenty currently holding the reins of Florence, felt certain that the gathering served party purposes, enhanced by the flavors of stewed fish and aged Greek wine. This assurance lifted his spirits as he approached the Via della Scala. Without it, he might have feared the company would be as dull as the philosophical suppers he had endured in the gardens before, where he was often forced to accept and endure an exposition of the universe from its origins to its final ripeness.

It was a dark evening. Only when Tito crossed the intermittent light of a lamp suspended before an image of the Virgin did his tall, graceful figure, mantled and moving, become discernible. To any observer watching his passage from light to light, it would have appeared that he was constantly followed by a starkly different form: a thickset, elderly man in a serge tunic and felt hat. This conjunction might have seemed accidental amidst the street traffic, but when Tito halted at the gate of the Rucellai gardens, the figure behind him stopped as well. The *sportello*, the smaller door, stood ajar, held open by a servant distracted by another task. Tito turned in rapidly, giving his name, and passed between the evergreen bushes, which glowed like metal in the torchlight. The follower entered immediately after.

"Your name?" the servant asked.

"Baldassarre Calvo," came the immediate reply.

"You are not a guest; the guests have all passed."

"I belong to Tito Melema, who has just gone in. I am to wait in the gardens."

The servant hesitated. "I had orders to admit only guests. Are you a servant of Messer Tito?"

"No, friend, I am not a servant; I am a scholar."

There are those to whom one need only say, "I am a buffalo," in a tone of quiet confidence, and they will yield. The porter gave way instantly. Baldassarre entered, hearing the door close and chain behind him as he vanished among the shining bushes.

Those firm, ready answers signaled a profound transformation in Baldassarre since their last meeting, when the dagger had snapped in two. The change had manifested in a startling manner.

As the shadow of Tito passed before the hovel on his way home, Baldassarre sat in that peculiar state of after-tremor known to those prone to great emotional outbursts—a condition where physical paralysis is accompanied by an exceptional lucidity of thought, as if the dissipation of excited passion clears a fire-mist to reveal clarity beneath. He felt unable to rise; his limbs were benumbed, his hands shaking, his body cold. Yet, in this helplessness, he was surrounded not by the usual dimness and vanishing shadows, but by the crystal-clear images of the past. He was reliving a life that had seemed a long preparation for bitterness.

For several minutes, he was so absorbed by these visions that he failed to reflect on the fact that he was seeing them, noting the shift only when the sudden clearness traveled through the distance and rested upon the scene just concluded. He was fully present: he remembered Monna Lisa and Tessa. Ah! *He* was the mysterious husband; the one with another wife in the Via de' Bardi. It was time to retrieve the broken dagger and depart, leaving no trace. To conceal his feebleness was the only semblance of power remaining to him. He leaned to gather the fragments, then turned toward the book lying open beside him. It was a fine, large manuscript, an odd volume of Pausanias. Moonlight fell upon it, illuminating the large letters at the head of the page:

In days past, he had known Pausanias intimately; yet an hour or two prior, he had stared at that page in hopelessness, the letters appearing no more meaningful than black weather-marks on a wall. Now, however, they were the magical signs conjuring a world. The moonbeam striking the letters resurrected Messenia before him, its struggle against Spartan oppression.

He snatched up the book, though the light was too pale for further reading. It mattered not; he knew the chapter. He read inwardly. He visualized the stoning of the traitor Aristocrates—cast out by a whole people to lie unburied, with a pillar erected bearing verses declaring how Time had restored justice to the unjust. The words arose within him, stirring innumerable vibrations of memory. He forgot his age; he could almost have shouted. The light had returned, the mother of knowledge and joy! In that exultation, his limbs regained their strength. He sprang up with his broken dagger and his book, stepping out under the broad moonlight.

The air was nipping with frost, yet Baldassarre felt no chill, only the glow of conscious power. He paced the open spaces of the high ground, gazing down upon the domed and towered city sleeping darkly beneath its mountain guardians, upon the pale gleam of the river, and the valley vanishing toward snow-capped peaks. He felt himself master of them all.

That sense of mental empire available to all in moments of exceptional clarity was intensified for Baldassarre by the long days and nights when memory had been little more than the awareness of something lost. The city, once a weary labyrinth, was now material he could subdue to his purposes. His mind scanned its affairs with flashing conjecture; he was once again a man who knew cities, whose vision was instructed by vast experience, savoring the keen delight of holding all things within the grasp of language. Names! Images! His mind rushed through this wealth without pause, like an heir entering a great inheritance.

Yet, amidst this rushing eagerness, one End presided in Baldassarre's consciousness—a dark deity in the innermost cell, seemingly forgotten only while his hecatomb was being prepared. Once the first triumph of recovered certainty had run its course, his thoughts centered on Tito. That fair, slippery viper could not escape him now. Thanks to struggling justice, the heart that had never quivered with tenderness for another possessed sensitive, selfish fibers that could be pierced by the sharp point of anguish. The soul that bowed to no right now bowed to the great lord of mortals, Pain.

He could now penetrate every secret of Tito's life. He knew some already, and the failure of the broken dagger, which had seemed like frustration, was merely the beginning of achievement. That sudden rage had shaken away the obstruction stifling his soul. Twice before, when his memory had partially returned, it had been due to sudden excitation: once defending himself from an enraged dog, once scrambling up a rock to escape the waves.

Yes, but if this time, as then, the light were to die out and the dreary conscious blank return! This time the light was stronger and steadier, yet what security existed that before morning the dark fog would not envelop him again? Even the fear seemed like the onset of feebleness. He thought with alarm that he might sink faster from this excited vigil on the hill, expending his vital force. Seeking anxiously for a sheltered corner, he finally nestled against a heap of warm garden straw and fell asleep.

When he opened his eyes, it was daylight. The first moments were filled with strange bewilderment: he was a man with a double identity. To which life had he awakened? To the existence of dim-sighted sensibilities, like the sad heirship of fallen greatness, or to the life of recovered power? Surely the latter, for the events of the night returned to him: the recognition of the page in Pausanias, the crowding resurgence of facts and names, the sudden wide prospect that had given him such a moment as the Maenad's glorious awakening on the mountain top.

He took up the book again, reading and remembering simultaneously. He saw a name, and images of deeds rose with it; he saw a deed mentioned and linked it to a name. There were stories of inexpiable crimes, but also tales of guilt that seemed successful. There were sanctuaries for swift-footed miscreants; baseness had its armor, and the weapons of justice sometimes broke against it. So what then? If baseness triumphed everywhere else, if it could heap upon itself all the goods of the world and even hold the keys of hell, it would never triumph over the hatred it had itself awakened. It could devise no torture greater than the torture of submitting to its smile. Baldassarre felt the indestructible, independent force of a supreme emotion that knew no terror, asked for no motive, and was itself an ever-burning motive consuming all other desire. And now, in this morning light, when the assurance came that the fine fibers of association were still active and his recovered self had not departed, all his gladness was but the hope of vengeance.

From that time until the evening he entered the Rucellai gardens, he had been incessantly, yet cautiously, inquiring into Tito's position and circumstances. Hardly a day passed without him contriving to follow his movements. He wished to arouse no alarm in Tito; he sought a moment when the hated favorite of blind fortune was at the summit of confident ease, surrounded by chief men on whose favor he depended. It was not retributive payment or personal recognition that bent his whole soul; it was to find the sharpest edge of disgrace and shame by which a selfish smiler could be pierced, to send through his marrow the most sudden shock of dread. He was content to lie hard and live stintedly—he had spent the greater part of his remaining money on another poniard. His hunger and thirst were for nothing exquisite but an exquisite vengeance. He had avoided addressing anyone suspected of intimacy with Tito, lest an alarm in Tito's mind urge him to flight or some counteracting measure devised by hard-pressed ingenuity. For this reason, he had never entered Nello's shop, which he knew Tito frequented, and had turned aside to avoid meeting Piero di Cosimo.

The possibility of frustration added eagerness to his desire that the great opportunity he sought should not be deferred. His desire was eager on another ground as well; he trembled lest his memory fail again. Whether from the agitating presence of that fear or other causes, he had twice felt a sort of mental dizziness, where the inward sense or imagination seemed to lose the distinct forms of things. Once he had attempted to enter the Palazzo Vecchio and make his way into a council chamber where Tito was, but had failed. But now, on this evening, he felt his occasion had come.

A Supper in the Rucellai Gardens.

The heavy doors of the pavilion swung open, admitting the cool night air and the scent of jasmine to the warm interior. Tito's eyes, sharp and assessing, scanned the assembly instantly. His initial suspicion was confirmed: this was not merely a feast, but a political gathering. Yet, the nature of the politics was subtle, woven into the very fabric of the celebration. In Florence, it was widely believed that good wine and sumptuous food sharpened the mind for political discourse, that the "inspired ease" of the post-prandial hour revealed truths inaccessible to the sober, uninvited mind. The Rucellai, a family of immense wealth, maintained a table of such splendor that it seemed a testament to their power. Here, amidst the bust of Plato watching from a corner and the frescoes of the cardinal virtues adorning the walls, high philosophy was left to the shadows. Tonight, the agenda was simpler: the strengthening of party lines through fellowship and the shared pleasure of the senses.

Plato's bust, brought from Lorenzo's villa after his death, had long served as a silent sentinel over these gatherings. On the thirteenth of November, the anniversary of the philosopher's passing, it looked down upon a select circle of scholars. Among them were the ghosts of an era: Pico della Mirandola, once a Quixotic genius who had astonished Rome with heresy before retreating into a humble quest for inner perfection; Marsilio Ficino, the diligent feeder of Platonism whose mind had grown soft from such exclusive nourishment; Angelo Poliziano, the vibrant poet whose phrases still seemed to pulse with life; and the towering Leon Battista Alberti, a universal mind of practical and theoretical grandeur. These were the valiant workers whose names are often forgotten, yet whose labors form the unseen foundation of our inheritance.

Bernardo Rucellai, the host, stood in the prime of life, barely forty, possessing a haughty and cautious dignity. He was a man of pure Latinity, though, as Erasmus noted, one could not extract a single word of Latin from him by force. He received Tito with marked favor, seating him between Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, both accomplished young members of the Medicean faction.

As the brass bowl of scented water circulated, allowing the company to wash their hands, the atmosphere lightened. Rings flashed on white fingers under the wax candles, mingling with the fragrance of fresh French damask. The conversation drifted to the habits of posterity. Someone mused what Dante would think if he could see silver forks on the table; the consensus was that future generations would seem equally surprising to their ancestors. While silver forks dangled delicately over morsels of liver cooked to melt in the mouth, the guests admired the enamelled centers of the service and discussed the silver dish for confetti, a masterpiece by Antonio Pollajuolo, seduced by papal patronage from Florence to Rome.

"I recall," said Niccolò Ridolfi, his manner negligently commanding, "how our Antonio grew bitter about chiseling metal, declaring that 'the artist who puts his work into gold and silver puts his brains into the melting-pot.' He took to painting in a fury."

"And that was a true prophecy," Giannozzo Pucci observed. "If this war with Pisa continues, spreading to our other towns, it will not only be our silver dishes that vanish. I doubt Antonio's silver saints in San Giovanni will not soon be worshipped more devoutly as coin."

"The Frate prepares us for this already," Lorenzo Tornabuoni added dryly. "He tells the people God will not have silver crucifixes and starving stomachs; that the church is adorned best with the gems of holiness and the gold of brotherly love."

"A useful doctrine of war-finance," Bernardo Rucellai replied. "But politics come after the confetti, Lorenzo, when we have drunk enough wine to wash them down. They are too solid for roast and boiled meat."

"Our Luigi Pulci would say this boiled kid requires an impartial mind," Niccolò Ridolfi continued, recounting a story of Pulci's. "He claimed that opinion corrupts the saliva—that is why men took to pepper. Scepticism is the only philosophy that leaves the mouth clean. Yet, poor Lorenzo de' Medici disagreed, citing Matteo Franco, who wanted hotter sauce because he believed so strongly in his own verses. Pulci said his mind was like sharpest steel, cutting everything it touched, yet he was a gentle-hearted creature, temperate as a butterfly."

The light talk persisted through the roast and boiled meats, leading inevitably to the capon and game. The crowning glory was a peacock cooked according to Apicius, served with its feathers intact to resemble a living bird taking repose. It required the skill of a master carver to turn the classical bird on its back, exposing the plucked breast for the honorables, unless one possessed the independent mind to decline the expense and choose the vulgar digestibility of capon. Hardly anyone dared. Tito quoted Horace and scattered his slice in small particles; Bernardo Rucellai made a learned observation on the price of peacock eggs but did not eat; Niccolò Ridolfi held a mouthful while telling a tale of a man who boiled a wild goose in its feathers to pass it off as a pea-hen.

Very little peacock was consumed, but the satisfaction lay in the spectacle of such luxury, a caprice accessible only to the wealthiest. It would have been rash to criticize such venerable institutions when Fra Girolamo was preaching that the rich had no duty to be luxurious for the sake of the poor.

Outside, in the chill obscurity surrounding the warmth of the pavilion, a lonely figure walked in narrowing circles. Baldassarre paused among the trees, looking into the windows where the guests appeared as brilliant pictures against the gloom. He could hear the laughter, see Tito gesticulating with careless grace, and hear his voice mingling in the merry confusion. Baldassarre's mind was highly strung, preparing for the moment of his entrance. He derived a savage satisfaction from Tito's easy gaiety, viewing it as the unconscious victim preparing itself for more effective torture. But the men seated among the tapers and flashing cups knew nothing of the pale, fierce face watching them from without. Light can be a curtain as well as darkness.

As the conversation deepened, the sense of citizenship forced itself upon even the indifferent minds. The disturbing words of Fra Girolamo were uppermost in everyone's thoughts. Before the stewed fish was removed, the talk turned political, despite Rucellai's earlier prohibition. Gossip spread of the voting in the Palazzo, of Francesco Valori's domineering temper, and of Soderini's speeches, which seemed to share the same dough as the Frate's sermons.

"My opinion is," Niccolò Ridolfi stated, "that the Frate has a longer head for public matters than Soderini or any Piagnone. Soderini is his mouthpiece."

"No, Niccolò," Bernardo Rucellai corrected. "The Frate has an acute mind serving his own ends, but Pagolantonio Soderini, with his experience of the Venetian Council, owes little to a monk. Soderini loads the cannon; the Frate brings the powder and lights the match. He is master of the people, and the people are getting master of us."

Lorenzo Tornabuoni spoke up as the servants cleared the room. "Whether Soderini is indebted or not, *we* are indebted to the Frate for the general amnesty accompanying the Council scheme. We might have faced the fear of God and moral reform, but the proposition that our Medicean heads remain on our shoulders and our property is not confiscated has my warm approval. Only the Frate's predominance could have secured that."

A murmur of surprise rippled through the table, but Rucellai nodded, encouraging Tornabuoni to continue.

"I was favored with an interview in the Frate's own cell," Tornabuoni explained. "Even Francesco Valori rarely sees him in private. I see clearly that the only safe policy for us Mediceans is to throw our strength into the Frate's party. We are not strong enough to stand alone. If the Frate and the popular party were upset, the Arrabbiati—Nerli, Alberti, Pazzi—would be uppermost. Instead of amnesty, they would fly at our throats like mad dogs."

"There were strong interjections of assent," the narrative implies, as Tornabuoni paused. "A wise dissimulation is the only course for moderate men in times of violent party feeling. The Frate's theory of a popular government where every man strives for the general good is suitable for an island discovered by Columbus, but never for our quarrelsome Florence. A change must come, and with patience and caution, we can determine it in our favor. Meanwhile, we must keep the Frate's flag flying; any other flag raised now would be black for us."

"It is true," Niccolò Ridolfi declared curtly. "I am too old to change my feathers. There are men like our old Bernardo del Nero who would never borrow another's shield. But we can lie still like sleepy old dogs. Barking would be useless. As for the psalm-singing party, their day will not be long. After all the talk of scholars, there are but two sorts of government: one where men show their teeth, and one where men show their tongues and lick the feet of the strongest. Their Great Council will be voted tomorrow, and they will think they have found a new plan, but as sure as there is a human skin under every lucco in the Council, their new plan will end in snarling or licking. That is my view as a plain man. I shall be true to my old alliances; I have never seen a chalk-mark on political reasons to tell me which is true and which is false."

"If that is an appeal to me, Niccolò," Bernardo Rucellai replied with formal dignity, "I may say that while my wishes are partly determined by personal relations, I cannot enter into positive schemes with persons over whose actions I have no control. I might be content with a restoration of the old order, but with important modifications. The one point on which I concur with Lorenzo Tornabuoni is that the best policy is to throw the weight of our interest into the scale of the popular party. I condescend to no dissimulation; nor do I see the party or scheme that commands my full assent. In all alike there is crudity and confusion of ideas."

Niccolò Ridolfi shrugged and left the floor to others. As the wine circulated, the talk became more frank and lively, breaking into small knots. Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, foreseeing this shift, turned aside from the general conversation to engage Tito, who sat between them, gradually pushing their seats back and turning their attention fully to him.

"In truth, Melema," Tornabuoni said, crossing one hose-clad leg over the other, "I know of no man in Florence who can serve our party better than you. Most of our friends cannot hide their prejudices, or their ties are so notorious they invite suspicion. Giannozzo and I possess the power of concealment and finesse necessary for a rational man, without which one is at a disadvantage compared to a wild bull. But except yourself, I know of no one else on whom we could rely for the necessary discretion."

"Yes," Giannozzo Pucci added, placing his hand on Tito's shoulder. "You can help us better than if you were Ulysses himself, for Ulysses often made himself disagreeable. To manage men, one needs a sharp mind in a velvet sheath. There is no soul in Florence who could undertake a journey to Rome with the same safety as you. Your scholarship provides a pretext, and your talent is harder to match. Niccolò Machiavelli might have done for us if he had been on our side, but he is too much bitten with notions and lacks your power of fascination. He has lost a great chance; you have got it."

"You have only to play your game well, Melema," Tornabuoni said, lowering his voice significantly. "The future belongs to you. The Medici will keep a foot in Rome as well as Florence, and the time may not be far off when they can make a finer career for their adherents. Why shouldn't you take orders some day? There is a cardinal's hat at the end of that road, and you would not be the first Greek to wear it."

Tito laughed gaily. He measured Tornabuoni's exaggerated flattery but found it pleasant.

"My joints are not so stiff yet," he said, "that I cannot be induced to run without such a high prize."

"I was not joking," Tornabuoni replied with grave suavity. "We will talk of that another time. One object to bear in mind is that you must win the confidence of the men who hang about San Marco. Giannozzo and I shall do the groundwork, but you can carry it farther because you are less observed. You can gain a thorough knowledge of their doings and make a broader screen for your agency. Nothing can be done before you start for Rome, as the business between Piero de' Medici and the French nobles must be effected immediately. When you return, however, you could make yourself the pet votary of San Marco, though you are wise enough to know that effective dissimulation is never immoderate."

"If it were not that an adhesion to the popular side is necessary to your safety as an agent of our party, Tito mio," Giannozzo Pucci said, his manner more fraternal, "I could have wished your skill employed in another way. But now we must find someone to manage our sworn enemies, the Arrabbiati. We need to know their movements more than those of the Frate's party, who are strong enough to play above-board. It would have been difficult for you, given your known relations with the Medici and your wife's kinship with Bernardo del Nero. We need a man with no distinguished connections who has not yet taken a side."

Tito pushed his hair backward automatically, looking straight at Pucci with a scarcely perceptible smile.

"No need to look out

The stone beneath Romola's back offered a cool anchor against the heat of her flight. Here, beneath the swaying cypress, the world felt vast and unclaimed. The familiar grid of Florence's walls receded behind her, replaced by the widening silence of the Tuscan road. Tito rode toward the city, his path diverging sharply from hers; with every hour, the distance between them grew, a physical manifestation of the separation she had chosen. The morning air was still, heavy with the scent of dry earth, and the great dip of the roadside created a natural gulf, isolating her from the sombre, brooding mountains beyond. For the first time, Romola felt truly alone—not abandoned, but solitary in the presence of the earth and sky, unmediated by human law or expectation.

Then, a voice cut through the stillness, close and immediate.

"You are Romola de' Bardi, the wife of Tito Melema."

She knew that voice instantly. It had vibrated through her life more than once, carrying a weight she could not ignore. Because she recognized it, she did not turn. She remained seated, though her heart hammered against her ribs, a mixture of awe and fierce rebellion warring within her chest. It was one of the black-robed monks, a figure daring to intrude upon her sanctuary. Yet, as he spoke, she felt as though destiny itself—a sceptred deity made flesh—had reached out and seized her.

"You are fleeing from Florence in disguise," the voice continued, imperious and clear. "I have a command from God to stop you. You are not permitted to flee."

Anger, hot and sharp, rose in Romola's throat. She refused to turn, resenting the scrutiny of a gaze she deemed presumptuous. Sitting motionless, she challenged him.

"What right have you to speak to me, or to hinder me?"

"The right of a messenger," the monk replied. "You have donned religious garb, yet you wear it as a disguise. You sought to pass unseen, but you were discerned. It is declared to me that you seek to escape the lot God has laid upon you. You wish to hide your true name and your true place, to craft a new identity ruled only by your own will. I have a command to call you back. My daughter, you must return to your place."

With every sentence, Romola's resistance hardened. She feared that if she showed even a flicker of submission, she would lose her resolve entirely. Her voice sharpened with irritation.

"I will not return. I acknowledge no right of priests and monks to interfere with my actions. You have no power over me."

"I know," the monk said, his tone softening yet remaining firm. "I know you have been raised in scorn of obedience. But it is not the poor monk who claims authority over you; it is the Truth that commands you. You cannot escape it. Either you obey, and it shall lead you, or you disobey, and it shall hang upon you like a chain you will drag forever. But you will obey, my daughter. Your old servant returns with the mules; my companion goes to fetch him. You will go back to Florence."

Romola sprang to her feet, her eyes blazing with defiance. She faced the speaker directly. It was Fra Girolamo. She knew him well; their heights were nearly equal, their faces level.
