She had prepared words of rejection, but they died in her throat. Meeting Fra Girolamo's calm gaze, she felt a strange, unfamiliar impression wash over her. Her anger sank, ashamed and irrelevant.

There was no transcendent beauty in Savonarola's face, nor was it conventionally handsome. It was strong-featured, refined only by the rigors of mind and body. Yet, the impact of his glance was profound. It conveyed a deep, abiding interest in her, a care that existed apart from any personal desire. It was the first time Romola had encountered a look where simple human fellowship manifested as a binding force. Such a gaze was the essence of a spiritual guide, and suddenly, Romola found it impossible to question his right to address her. She stood silent, staring at him.

"You assert your freedom proudly, my daughter," Savonarola said. "But who is so base as the debtor who thinks himself free?"

A subtle pallor flashed across Romola's face. "And you are flying from your debts: the debt of a Florentine woman; the debt of a wife. You turn your back on the appointed lot to choose another. But can man or woman choose their duties? No more than they can choose their birthplace or their parents. My daughter, you are fleeing from the presence of God into the wilderness."

As the anger melted, it was replaced by a new presentiment. Perhaps this man, whom she began to view with a vague reverence, held a valid law she had missed. Yet, it was impossible; he could not know the depths of her turmoil. Still, she could not simply refuse guidance. Driven by a desperate need to be reverent even as she resisted, the title she had never used before slipped from her lips.

"My father, you cannot know the reasons which compel me to go. None can know them but myself. None can judge for me. I have been driven by great sorrow. I am resolved to go."

"I know enough, my daughter," Savonarola replied, his voice illuminating her situation. "My mind has been so far enlightened concerning you that I know enough. You are not happy in your married life; but I am not a confessor, and I seek nothing reserved for the seal of confession. I have a divine warrant to stop you, which does not depend on such knowledge. You were warned by a message from heaven, delivered in my presence—warned before marriage, when you might still have lawfully chosen freedom. But you chose the bond. In wilfully breaking it, I speak to you as a pagan if the holy mystery of matrimony is not sacred to you, you are breaking a pledge. Of what wrongs will you complain when you yourself commit one of the greatest wrongs a woman and citizen can commit—withdrawal in secrecy and disguise from a pledge given before God and your fellow-men? Of what wrongs will you complain when you break the simplest law at the foundation of trust between man and man: faithfulness to the spoken word? Is this the wisdom you gained by scorning the Church's mysteries? Not to see the bare duty of integrity, where the Church would teach you to see not only integrity, but religion?"

Blood rushed to Romola's face, and she shrank as if struck. "I would not have put on a disguise," she began, but her voice faltered. She could not continue; the suggestion in the friar's words—that her conduct mirrored Tito's—shook her to her core.

"And to break that pledge, you fly from Florence," Savonarola pressed. "Florence, where there are the only men and women in the world to whom you owe the debt of a fellow-citizen."

"I should never have quitted Florence," Romola whispered, trembling, "as long as there was any hope of fulfilling a duty to my father there."

"Do you own no tie but that of a child to her father in the flesh?" Savonarola asked. "Your life has been spent in blindness, my daughter. You have lived with those who sit on a hill aloof, looking down on the life of their fellow-men. I know their vain discourse, filled with fancied wisdom about the past while scorning God's work in the present. Doubtless you were taught that pagan women felt what it was to live for the Republic, yet you never felt that you, a Florentine woman, should live for Florence. If your own people wear a yoke, will you slip from under it instead of struggling to lighten it? There is hunger and misery in our streets, yet you say, 'I care not; I have my own sorrows; I will go away.' The servants of God struggle for a law of justice, peace, and charity so that the hundred thousand citizens among whom you were born may be governed righteously. But you think no more of this than a bird spreading its wings to fly wherever it finds food. You have scorned the teaching of the Church, my daughter. As if you, a wilful wanderer following your blind choice, were not below the humblest Florentine woman who stretches her hands with her own people, craves a blessing for them, and feels a close sisterhood with the neighbor who kneels beside her, not of her own blood. She thinks of the mighty purpose God has for Florence and waits and endures because the promised work is great, and she feels herself little."

"I was not going away to ease and self-indulgence," Romola insisted, raising her head with a desperate need to vindicate herself. "I was going away to hardship. I expect no joy: it is gone from my life."

"You are seeking your own will, my daughter. You seek some good other than the law you are bound to obey. But how will you find good? It is not a thing of choice; it is a river flowing from the foot of the Invisible Throne, flowing by the path of obedience. I say again, man cannot choose his duties. You may choose to forsake your duties and avoid the sorrow they bring, but you will go forth anyway. What will you find, my daughter? Sorrow without duty—bitter herbs, and no bread with them."

"But if you knew," Romola pleaded, clasping her hands tightly, "if you knew what it was to me—how impossible it seemed to me to bear it."

"My daughter," Savonarola said, pointing to the cord around her neck, "you carry something within your mantle. Draw it forth and look at it."

Romola gave a slight start, but her impulse was to obey. A stronger will than her own grappled with her self-doubt. She drew forth the crucifix. Still pointing toward it, he spoke.

"There, my daughter, is the image of a Supreme Offering, made by Supreme Love because the need of man was great."

He paused. Romola held the crucifix, trembling. A sudden realization struck her of the vast distance between her present self and the girl who had first taken this object from the friar's hands. How many secrets lay ahead, just as there had been then in her young blindness? This thought helped subdue her fears. At the sound of Fra Girolamo's voice again, Romola made a quick, involuntary movement, pressing the crucifix against her mantle and looking at him with more submission than before.

"Conform your life to that image, my daughter. Make your sorrow an offering. And when the fire of Divine charity burns within you, and you behold the need of your fellow-men by the light of that flame, you will not call your offering great. You have carried yourself proudly, as one who held herself not of common blood or common thoughts; but you have been as one unborn to the true life of man. What! You say your love for your father no longer tells you to stay in Florence? Then, since that tie is snapped, you are without a law, without religion: you are no better than a beast of the field when robbed of her young. If the yearning of fleshly love is gone, you are without love, without obligation. See, then, my daughter, how you are below the life of the believer who worships that image of the Supreme Offering, who feels the glow of a common life with the lost multitude for whom that offering was made, and beholds the history of the world as the history of a great redemption in which he is a fellow-worker in his own place and among his own people! If you held that faith, my beloved daughter, you would not be a wanderer flying from suffering, blindly seeking the good of a freedom which is lawlessness. You would feel that Florence was the home of your soul as well as your birthplace, because you would see the work given you to do there. If you forsake your place, who will fill it? You ought to be in your place now, helping in the great work by which God will purify Florence and raise it to be the guide of the nations. What! The earth is full of iniquity—full of groans—the light still struggles with mighty darkness—and you say, 'I cannot bear my bonds; I will burst them asunder; I will go where no man claims me'? My daughter, every bond of your life is a debt: the right lies in the payment of that debt; it can lie nowhere else. In vain will you wander over the earth; you will be wandering forever away from the right."

Romola struggled internally against immense forces: the overwhelming personal influence of Savonarola, born of the energy of his emotions and beliefs, and her own consciousness that his words implied a higher law than any she had yet obeyed. Yet, the resisting thoughts were not yet overborne.

"How, then, could Dino be right? He broke ties. He forsook his place."

"That was a special vocation," Savonarola answered. "He was constrained to depart, else he could not have attained the higher life. It would have been stifled within him."

"And I too," Romola said, raising her hands to her brow, her voice anguished as if dragged to torture. "Father, you may be wrong."

"Ask your conscience, my daughter. You have no vocation such as your brother had. You are a wife. You seek to break your ties in self-will and anger, not because the higher life calls upon you to renounce them. The higher life begins for us, my daughter, when we renounce our own will to bow before a Divine law. That seems hard to you. It is the portal of wisdom, freedom, and blessedness. And the symbol of it hangs before you. That wisdom is the religion of the Cross. And you stand aloof from it; you are a pagan. You have been taught to say, 'I am as the wise men who lived before the time when the Jew of Nazareth was crucified.' And that is your wisdom! To be as the dead whose eyes are closed and whose ears are deaf to the work of God done since their time. What has your dead wisdom done for you, my daughter? It has left you without a heart for the neighbors among whom you

"Rise, my daughter," Fra Girolamo finally commanded, his voice cutting through the heavy silence. "Your servant waits nearby with the mules. It is time for me to press on toward Florence."

Romola rose from her knees. That posture of stillness had become a sacred ritual for her, cementing the state of yearning passivity she had only just entered. With a single act of renouncing her plan to flee her husband, her will felt shattered, bruised beyond repair, leaving her desperate for direction even in the smallest matters. She lifted the edge of her cowl and saw Maso and the second Dominican standing with their backs turned on the hill's edge, some ten yards away. Yet, she did not look at them. Her gaze remained fixed on Savonarola, silent and pleading, as if the command to recall Maso must issue from his lips alone, not hers.

"I will go and fetch them," he said, answering her unspoken appeal. "And I commend you, my daughter, to the Brother who accompanies me. You seek guidance, seeking that wisdom which has hitherto appeared as folly to you. A primary gateway to this wisdom is the sacrament of confession. You require a confessor, my daughter, and I wish to place you under the care of Fra Salvestro, one of the brethren of San Marco, in whom I place my highest confidence."

"I would prefer no guidance but yours, Father," Romola replied, her voice trembling with anxiety.

"My daughter, I do not act as your confessor. My vocation withdraws me from offices that would force frequent contact with the laity and interfere with my specific duties."

"Then shall I never be able to speak to you in private? If I waver, if—" Romola broke off, seized by a sudden alarm. She feared that the fragile strength of her renunciation would evaporate the moment Savonarola's immediate personal influence faded.

"My daughter, if your soul requires the private word from my lips, you shall let me know through Fra Salvestro, and I will meet you in the sacristy or the choir of San Marco. I will not cease to watch over you. I shall instruct my brother regarding you, so that he may guide you into the path of labor for the suffering and the hungry to which you are called as a daughter of Florence in these times of hard need. I desire to behold you among the feebler and more ignorant sisters as the apple-tree among the trees of the forest, so that your fairness and all your natural gifts serve merely as a lamp through which the Divine light shines more purely. I must now go and call your servant."

Once Maso had been sent ahead, Fra Salvestro stepped forward, and Savonarola led Romola toward him. She had already felt an inward shrinking from a new guide who was a total stranger; yet to resist Savonarola's counsel would have been to assume an attitude of independence at a moment when all her strength must be drawn from the renunciation of that very independence. The entire bent of her mind was now toward doing what was painful rather than what was easy. She bowed reverently to Fra Salvestro before looking directly at him, but when she raised her head and saw him fully, her reluctance curdled into a palpitating doubt.

There are men whose presence naturally infuses trust and reverence; there are others to whom we must carry our trust and reverence ready-made. This distinction struck Romola as Savonarola receded from her sight and Fra Salvestro Maruffi stood in his place. It was not that Fra Salvestro possessed anything manifestly repulsive in his face or manner—no air of hypocrisy, no tinge of coarseness. His face was actually handsomer than Fra Girolamo's, his stature slightly taller. He was the long-accepted confessor of many among Florence's chief personages, possessing vast experience as a spiritual director. Yet, his face bore the vacillating expression of a mind unable to concentrate itself strongly within the channel of one great emotion or belief—an expression fatal to influencing an ardent nature like Romola's. Such a countenance is not the stamp of insincerity; it marks simply a shallow soul, one that may sincerely strive to fill a high vocation, sincerely composing its features to utter sublime formulas, only to find the muscles twitch or relax in spite of belief, as prose insists on intruding where poetry should reign. Fra Salvestro had a peculiar liability to visions, seemingly dependent on a constitution prone to somnambulism. Savonarola believed in the supernatural character of these visions, while Fra Salvestro himself had originally resisted such an interpretation, even rebuking Savonarola for his prophetic preaching: another proof, if one were needed, that the relative greatness of men cannot be gauged by their tendency to disbelieve the superstitions of their age. Between the two, there could be no question of which was the great man and which the small.

The difference between them was measured precisely by the shift in Romola's feelings as Fra Salvestro began to address her with words of exhortation and encouragement. After her initial angry resistance to Savonarola had passed, she had lost all memory of the old dread lest any influence drag her into the circle of fanaticism and sour monkish piety. But now, once again, the chill breath of that dread stole over her. It could have no decisive effect against the impetus her mind had just received; it was merely like the closing of grey clouds over a sunrise, rendering her returning path monotonous and sombre.

Perhaps of all sombre paths, the one we tread upon after returning with strong resolution is the one that most severely tests the fervor of renunciation. As they re-entered the city gates, light snowflakes fell around them. As the grey sister walked hastily homeward from the Piazza di San Marco, crossing the bridge again and turning into the large door of the Via de' Bardi, her footsteps were marked darkly on the thin carpet of snow, and her cowl fell laden and damp about her face.

She ascended to her room, threw off her serge habit, destroyed the parting letters, replaced all her precious trifles, unbound her hair, and donned her usual black dress. Instead of undertaking a long, exciting journey, she was to sit down in her usual place. The snow beat against the windows, and she was alone.

She felt the dreariness, yet her courage remained high, akin to that of a seeker who has discovered new signs of gold. She was about to thread life by a fresh clue. She had thrown all the energy of her will into renunciation. The empty tabernacle remained locked, and she placed Dino's crucifix outside it.

Nothing broke the outward monotony of her solitary home until night came like a white ghost at the windows. Yet, for Romola, this Christmas Eve of 1494 proved to be the most memorable of her life.

October 30, 1496. The morning air was crisp with the promise of autumn, yet the Florentines paid it little heed. Their minds were fixed not on the gentle land breeze, but on the violent gales tearing across the Ligurian Sea. These storms had scattered the fleet from Marseilles, ships laden with grain and soldiers, leaving Florence exposed to a dual terror: the gnawing hunger of Pale Famine and the looming threat of invasion.

Across the peninsula, the political landscape was a theater of shifting alliances and predatory ambition. The French King, hailed as a new Charlemagne, had swept through Italy with terrifying ease, conquering Naples before retreating, reportedly shattered by the loss of an infant son. In response, a Holy League had coalesced under the papal banner of Pope Borgia, ostensibly to expel the "barbarians" from Naples. Yet, beneath the pious rhetoric lay a grim reality: a pact among wolves to drive out rival wolves, only to devour the carcass of Florence itself. While Pisa, Venice, Milan, and the German Emperor Maximilian united against the French, Florence stood defiant, clinging to her foreign alliance at the peril of her existence. If the vital port of Leghorn fell, the city, hemmed in by papal hostility and regional jealousy, would be strangled.

The Signoria, guided by the fervent disciples of Savonarola, responded with a mixture of vigor and compassion. They raised funds and levied new troops, yet they refused the traditional cruelty of shutting out the starving masses. Instead, they opened their gates to the destitute, allowing the hungry *contadini* and alien mendicants to flock in like birds fleeing a blizzard. This magnanimity drew sharp criticism from the disaffected, who argued that Florence should join the League to secure peace rather than invite pestilence. Compounding the crisis, Fra Girolamo had been silenced by a papal mandate for over a month. Only when the news of the lost grain ships reached them did the Signoria defy Rome, summoning Savonarola back to the Duomo. Two days prior, he had preached a sermon of such audacious faith that he wagered his own habit on the city's survival, urging the people to wait steadfastly for divine intervention.

Yet, as the sun rose on the thirtieth, no miracle had yet arrived. Desperation hung heavy in the air, accompanied by the specter of pestilence. Hospitals overflowed, and private courtyards served as makeshift infirmaries, yet unsheltered want persisted. Early that morning, the silent procession of death continued; the confraternities bore away the bodies that had collapsed along the roadsides. Amidst this grim tableau moved Romola de' Bardi. Dressed in the austere simplicity of black serge, her head veiled to conceal her hair save for the gleaming bands of gold framing her brow, she walked from the Ponte Vecchio toward the Porta Santa Maria.

Her path was blocked by a bier carried by the brothers of San Jacopo del Popolo, searching for the unburied dead. At the head of the litter, the brethren paused to inspect a form, while a cluster of idle laborers, their faces hollowed by starvation, gathered around.

"He's dead," one declared. "Messere Domeneddio has taken him."

"It would be a mercy for us all to stretch out our legs and walk ahead with our heads," another muttered. "Standing upright against hunger is a burden no man should bear."

"He's an old man. Death has struck a poor bargain; life has won."

"And not a Florentine, ten to one!" cried a voice. "A beggar turned out of Siena! May San Giovanni protect us. They send us armies of starving men to fight us."

"No, no," corrected a brother lifting the bier. "This man is a prisoner from the Stinche. I know the grey patch where the prison badge was sewn."

"Quiet! Help him up. Perhaps he's merely unconscious from lack of food," a brother suggested.

Romola stepped forward, loosening the small flask at her belt. With practiced ease, she used a small ivory implement to pour a few drops of wine into the prostrate man's mouth. The liquid acted as a stimulant; the head twitched, and the old man's eyes fluttered open, meeting hers with a vague, returning consciousness.

In that instant, a shock of absolute recognition struck Romola. The wild, dark eyes, the sallow, deep-lined face, and the long white beard were unmistakable. This was Baldassarre, the escaped prisoner she had glimpsed in the Duomo the day Tito first donned armor—a man whose mere presence had terrified Tito in the sketches of Piero's studio. A cold tremor seized her. Was this the bitter secret she feared? An impulse to flee surged within her, followed immediately by a stronger, imperious need to stay beside the old man, whom her intuition knew her husband had wronged. Her hands trembled, yet the muscle memory of her charitable work guided them; her right hand remained poised to offer more wine, while her left supported his neck.

Baldassarre looked up at her for the first time. The seclusion of his imprisonment and her recent flight had kept them apart, erasing the vivid descriptions of the fair, golden-haired woman he had heard. Now, seeing her, those images dissolved like mist.

"Shall we carry him to the steps of San Stefano?" Romola asked gently. "We shall clear the street, and you may proceed with your bier."

They moved only thirty yards before reaching the church steps. By then, Baldassarre managed to sit up, propping himself against the doorway. The brethren departed, but the crowd of spectators swelled. The atmosphere shifted; knowing the man was alive, the crowd's tone grew less sympathetic, though Romola's presence imposed a hush.

"They fed him daily in the Stinche," someone remarked. "That's why he's so weak without it."

"Gnaffè!" scoffed another. "The Magnificent Eight released these thieves to make room for honest folk. But if every criminal is revived with wine and bread, the Ciompi might as well drink from the Arno while the waters are high."

Romola sat beside Baldassarre, offering him a piece of soft bread from her basket. "Can you eat a little? I must go to the hospital, but I will return if you wait. I will take you to shelter. Do you understand?"

He stared dreamily, repeating the words "come back." His mind was clouded by exhaustion, but she hoped he grasped her intent.

"Do you keep your bread for those who cannot swallow?" a rough voice in a red night-cap interrupted, elbowing his way into the tight circle.

"If anyone isn't hungry," another man replied, "let them mind their own business. He is better off than those with empty stomachs and no breakfast."

"Yes, if a man wishes to die, let him be encouraged. Dead men require no trencher."

"Oh, you misunderstand the Frate's charity," interjected a young man in fine cloth, his face untouched by want. "The Frate preaches to the birds like Saint Anthony, telling hawks they must feed sparrows. Every good citizen is bound to feed six starving beggars from Arezzo or Bologna. Madonna, you are a pious Piagnone; you won't waste your bread on honest citizens who have the Frate's prophecies to digest."

"Come, Madonna," urged the man in the red cap. "The old thief won't eat. Try us. We fast so much we are half-saints."

The circle tightened, the gaunt, starving men crowding Romola until there was no space to breathe. She held out a small horn-cup, moistening a piece of bread with wine, having ignored their taunts until now. Rising, she looked around, her hazel eyes burning with a direct, unyielding gaze. Instinctively, the men nearest her recoiled, as if their proximity were an offense.

"Hunger is hard to bear," Romola said, her voice steady despite her pale, pinched face. "You have the power to take this bread if you wish. It was saved for sick women and children. You are strong men; but if you choose not to suffer because of your strength, you have the power to take everything from the weak. You can take the bread from this basket, but I will watch by this old man. I will resist your taking the bread from *him*."

Silence fell upon the group. The man in the red cap looked foolish, backing away and shoving his neighbor with a gesture of moral condemnation. The retreat was general; everyone pretended to be pushed aside against their will, and the young man in fine cloth vanished.

Just then, the armed servitors of the Signoria, patrolling the route for the approaching procession, arrived to disperse the obstruction. The man known as Cecco retreated from the threat of a mace, turning to Romola with a respectful nod.

"Madonna, if you must go on your errands, I will take care of the old man."

Cecco was a wild figure: a ragged tunic matted with dust and wool, bare bony arms, a square jaw shaded by a bristly beard, and a narrow red rag tied over his ears. Romola hesitated, her doubt evident.

"Don't distrust me, Madonna," Cecco said, reading her expression perfectly. "I am not as beautiful as you, but I have an old mother who eats my porridge. There is a heart inside me, and I have bought candles for the Most Holy Virgin before. Besides, see there—the old fellow is eating his sop. He is hale enough; he will be on his feet as well as the best of us soon."

"Thank you for your offer, friend," Romola said, her doubt melting into penitence. Leaning toward Baldassarre, she whispered, "Wait for me until I return."

He assented with a slight movement of his head and hand. Romola turned and walked on toward the hospital of San Matteo in the Piazza di San Marco, leaving the old man in the care of the rough but honest Ciompo.

Returning from the hospital, an hour later, Romola chose a path that widened toward the river, reaching the banks far from the bustle of the Ponte Vecchio. Her destination was clear: she intended to hasten to San Stefano to find Baldassarre. Yet, a dread held her back; she feared the knowledge he might bring, yet felt that to leave him behind would be to sever a vital claim upon her soul.

As she neared the junction where the Por' Santa Maria lay to her right and the Ponte Vecchio to her left, she stumbled into a throng that suddenly sank to their knees. Without hesitation, Romola joined them. The Great Cross of the Duomo was passing, leading a solemn procession. Being late, she was forced to wait until the sacred burden had cleared the intersection.

When the Cross vanished and she rose, the sudden stillness and the weight of standing made her fatigue acute. Beside her, a shopkeeper spoke kindly, "Madonna Romola, you will tire standing here. Gian Fantoni would be glad to offer you a seat in his house; his door is right there. Let me open it. He loves God and the Frate as we do. His home is yours."

Romola, accustomed to such fraternal greetings from citizens whose faces she knew from the Duomo, felt a shift in her understanding of home. It was no longer the lonely chamber in the Via de' Bardi, but the very circuit of Florence itself, where every street corner seemed to offer a look of welcome. Gladly, she accepted the invitation, passing through the open door to be led by the hose-vendor to an upstairs window. There, a stout woman, dressed in the plain garb of the Piagnoni, made reverent space for her among bright draperies. From this vantage point, Romola watched the procession pour slowly between the houses on the bridge, while the river and the Lung' Arno stretched out toward the Santa Trinita.

The procession moved in sadness and silence. No wailing chant broke the air; only the tramp of feet and the faint sweep of woolen robes. First came a long train of Florentine youth, their faces fresh and beardless, bearing high the white image of the youthful Jesus, crowned with golden glory, standing beside the tall cross where thorns and nails awaited.

Following them were the Companies of Discipline, bound by secret vows of self-chastisement and piety. Their garments concealed all but their eyes, transforming sons, husbands, and fathers into anonymous symbols of a common vow. Each company bore its color and badge, yet all wore a shroud-like garb that expressed only fellowship.

In contrast, the multitude of monks appeared as distinct individuals despite their shared tonsure and frocks. First came a white stream of reformed Benedictines, followed by the vast host of the Frati Minori, or Franciscans, clad in grey with knotted cords and wooden sandals—perhaps the most numerous order in Florence, zealous in their love for mankind and their hatred of the Dominicans. After the grey came the black of the Augustinians of San Spirito, men who had inherited Boccaccio's library and formed Florence's most learned company. Then the white-over-dark of the Carmelites, and finally the unmixed black of the Servites, founded by seven merchants who renounced their gains to adore the Divine Mother.

The hearts of the onlookers quickened as a stream of black and white emerged from the bridge—black mantles over white scapularies. The Dominicans were coming. Those from Fiesole led the way, their mantles coarse and often threadbare, a testament to the Prior of San Marco's strict reduction of poverty and discipline. Yet, in that long line, one mantle stood out, slightly less worn than the rest, topped by a tonsured head recognizable to those who had seen it on bronze medals, guarded by armed men, or transfigured by the inward flame of the orator.

As Savonarola's approach became certain, no one dared break the stillness with sound, though a mingled hiss of curses and murmurs of blessing drifted through the air. Perhaps it was the sense that the hissing prevailed that caused two or three disciples in the foreground to fall to their knees as if something divine passed. This silent homage spread like a shockwave along the streets, bending the most stubborn heads, while hatred gathered an intense expression elsewhere. As Savonarola ascended the Por' Santa Maria, Romola saw someone at an upper window spit upon him.

Monks returned—the Frati Umiliati from Ognissanti, heirs to a glorious wool-trading tradition, followed by Vallombrosan and other Benedictines, their varied forms marking a change of spirit through a change of garb. Finally, the shaven crowns ended, giving way to the train of untonsured secular priests.

Then came the twenty-one incorporated Arts of Florence, arrayed in long lines with banners declaring their distinct functions, from bakers to judges. After them marched the secondary officers of State, ascending to the greater, until the line of secularities was broken by the Canons of the Duomo. They carried a sacred relic: the head of San Zenobio, immortal bishop of Florence, enclosed in silver, whose virtues were believed to have saved the city a millennium prior.

This was the nucleus of the procession. Behind the relic came the archbishop in a gorgeous cope, with a canopy held high above him. Following him was the mysterious, hidden Image. First veiled by rich brocade curtains enclosing an outer painted tabernacle, and within that, by an ancient shrine never opened in living memory, lay the image of the Pitying Mother, found ages ago in the soil of L'Impruneta, uttering a cry as the spade struck it. Usually, this unseen Image was preceded by rich gifts, lists of precious offerings, veils, and mantles woven by emulous communities. The richest, it was said, had been crafted by a poor abbess and her nuns, who wove a mantle of gold brocade with their prayers, embroidered it with devotion, and saw their work presented to the Blessed Virgin by two youths who spread white wings and vanished into the blue.

But today, no gifts were carried before the tabernacle; no donations were offered except to the poor. This was the counsel of Fra Girolamo, whose preaching insisted not on gifts to invisible powers, but on aid to visible need. Altars had been raised at various points for the oblations of the needy. Not even a torch was carried. Surely the hidden Mother cared less for torches and brocade than for the wail of the hungry. Florence was in extremity; she had done her utmost and could only wait for a divine intervention beyond her own power.

The Frate in the torn mantle had promised help would come, and many faint-hearted clung to his word rather than the virtues of the unseen Image. Yet, some fierce-hearted souls rejoiced secretly that the Frate's promise might prove false.

Slowly the tabernacle moved forward, knees bent in profound stillness. The train of priests and chaplains from L'Impruneta stirred no passion. The procession was about to close with the Priors and the Gonfaloniere; the long train of companies and symbols, with their silent music that stirred the mind like a chorus, was passing out of sight. A faint yearning hope struggled against the accustomed despondency.

Romola, whose heart had swelled with foreboding and an enthusiasm of fellowship made habitual by the last two years, gave a deep sigh as if releasing a long mental tension. She remained on her knees, overcome by languor. Suddenly, something bright-colored flashed from between the houses onto the distant bridge. Romola started up, stretching her arms and leaning from the window. The black drapery fell from her head, and the golden gleam of her hair and the flush in her face seemed the effect of a single illumination.

A shout arose instantly. The last troops of the procession paused, all faces turning toward the distant bridge. But the bridge was already passed. A horseman pressed at full gallop along the Arno; the sides of his bay horse, streaked with foam, looked white from swiftness. His cap flew loose from his red *becchetto*, and he waved an olive-branch in his hand. It was a messenger—a messenger of good tidings! The blessed olive-branch spoke afar off.

But the impatient people could not wait. They rushed to meet the oncomer, seizing the horse's reins, pushing and trampling. Now Romola saw that the horseman was her husband, Tito, sent to Pisa a few days prior on a private embassy. The recognition brought no new flash of joy to her eyes. She had checked her first impulsive attitude of expectation; her governing anxiety remained fixed on what news of relief had come for Florence.

"Good news!"
"Best news!"
"News to be paid with hose!" (*novelle da calze*)

These were the vague answers Tito met with as the crowd importuned him, until he pushed his horse to the meeting of the ways where the Gonfaloniere and the Priors awaited. There he paused, bowed low, and declared, "Magnificent Signori! I have to deliver to you the joyful news that the galleys from France, laden with corn and men, have arrived safely in the port of Leghorn, by favor of a strong wind which kept the enemy's fleet at a distance."

The words left Tito's lips and seemed to vibrate up the streets. A great shout rang through the air, rushing along the river, then another, and another, spreading along the line of the procession toward the Duomo. Fainter answering shouts followed, like the intermediate splash of distant waves in a great lake obeying one impulse.

For some minutes, no one attempted to speak further. The Signoria themselves lifted their caps, standing bare-headed in the presence of a rescue that had come from outside the limits of their own power—from that region of trust and resignation called divine.

Finally, as the signal was given to move forward, Tito said with a smile, "I ought to say that any hose to be bestowed by the Magnificent Signoria in reward of these tidings are due, not to me, but to another man who rode hard to bring them. He would have been here in my place if his horse had not broken down just before he reached Signa. Meo di Sasso will doubtless be here in an hour or two, and may all the more justly claim the glory of the messenger, because he has had the chief labor and has lost the chief delight."

It was a graceful way of stating a necessity. After a brief reply from the *Proposto*, the dignified extremity of the procession passed on, and Tito turned his horse's head to follow in its train. The great bell of the Palazzo Vecchio began to swing, giving a louder voice to the people's joy. At that moment, when Tito's attention was no longer imperatively directed, it might have been expected that he would look round and recognize Romola. Yet he appeared engaged with his cap, which, as the eager people led his horse, he seized and placed on his head, his right hand still encumbered by the olive-branch. He possessed a becoming air of lassitude after his exertions.

Romola, instead of making any effort to be recognized by him, threw her black drapery over her head again and remained perfectly quiet. Yet she felt almost sure that Tito had seen her; he had the power of seeing everything without seeming to see it.

As the throng surged past, Romola slipped away from the Piazza and hurried down to the street, her heart beating a frantic rhythm against her ribs. She sought the sanctuary of San Stefano, but found it empty of the faithful; instead, she discovered Baldassarre standing solitary against the church door. He held his horn-cup loosely, his posture rigid, waiting.

There was a terrifying transformation in him. The vacant, dreamy haze that had clouded his eyes moments ago had evaporated, replaced by a ferocity that seemed to ignite the very air around him. It was the gaze of a caged beast sensing freedom, or perhaps prey, just beyond the bars of its confinement.

Romola flinched as his eyes locked onto hers, but her mind raced instantly to a single possibility: he had seen Tito. A fragile thread of hope began to weave through her anxiety. If this man was the criminal, then her husband was innocent. If she could force a confrontation, if she could make him speak to this stranger, she might finally find peace.

"If you will come with me," she said, her voice steady despite the tremor in her hands, "I can offer you shelter and food until you are strong again. Will you come?"

"Yes," Baldassarre replied, his voice low and grinding. "I shall be glad to get my strength. I want to get my strength," he muttered, the words sounding less like a conversation and more like a prayer to himself.

"Come!" she urged, guiding him away from the public square and toward the quieter path along the Arno, leading to the Ponte Rubaconte.

"I think you are not a Florentine," she ventured as they crossed the bridge, the river flowing dark beneath their feet.

He turned his head slowly, his expression guarded. The fog of confusion that had once shrouded him was now thickened by physical weakness, yet a new vigilance had taken its place. He looked at her, and in her gentle, earnest eyes, he found a compulsion he could not resist.

"No," he said, his caution sharpening. "I am no Florentine; I am a lonely man."

She noted his reticence, fearing that further questioning would drive him away. Yet, as they walked in silence, her mind churned, extinguishing the faint hope that had lingered regarding Tito. If this old man harbored malice, what cause for dread and secrecy existed? Why the fear?

They reached the entrance to the Via de' Bardi. Suddenly, Baldassarre stopped, a violent shock passing through him as if struck by lightning. He turned to her, his eyes wide with a sudden, terrible realization.

"Ah!" he breathed, cutting her off before she could speak. "You are his wife."

"Whose wife?" Romola asked, the question slipping out before she could check it.

The name died in his throat. The image of Tito pressed against him with such force that it expelled any ability to form words. He said nothing, staring at her with a fixed, haunting intensity.

Romola opened the courtyard door. Inside, the space was strewn with straw, serving as a bed for four or five sick figures. Tiny, pale children crawled among them, biting at dry straws and letting out weak, gurgling sounds.

"If you will come in," she said, her voice trembling, "I will find you a comfortable place and bring you more food."

"No," Baldassarre refused, though he remained rooted to the spot, paralyzed by the weight of impressions his confused mind could not process.

"Can I do nothing for you?" she pleaded. "Let me give you money so you may buy food. It will be more plentiful soon."

She reached into her *scarsella*, pulling out several *grossi*. She deliberately offered more than she would to any other beggar. He stared at the coins for a long moment, then nodded.

"Yes, I will take them."

She poured the coins into his palm; he clutched them tightly, as if afraid they might vanish.

"Tell me," she begged, "what shall you—"

But Baldassarre turned away, resuming his walk toward the bridge. He did not stop there. Instead, he marched straight up the Via del Fosso to the shop of Niccolò Caparra, entering without hesitation. Niccolò was absent, attending a procession with the city's armorers, leaving only an apprentice behind. The shop was lined with weapons, and Baldassarre's eyes, hungry for something far more potent than bread, found exactly what he sought.

The apprentice, less scrupulous and observant than the master, sold a sharp hunting-knife for three *grossi* without a second thought. It was a small, deadly blade that Baldassarre easily concealed within the folds of his tunic. He walked on, feeling a surge of renewed power. That sharp edge could lend lethality to the thrust of an aged arm. Perhaps the armor he feared was not always present; perhaps, in those long months of imprisonment, baseness had grown complacent and forgetful.

The knife had been purchased with the traitor's own money. That was justice. Before accepting the coin, he had known exactly what he would do with it: buy a weapon. And if possible, food. Food to nourish the arm that would wield the steel, food to sustain the body that served as the temple of vengeance. Once he had eaten, he would think, and act. First, he would find a place to hide, lest Tito drag him away again.

With this resolve hardening his resolve, Baldassarre turned into the narrowest alleys, bought meat and bread, and sat beneath a loggia to eat. The bells of the city rang out in peals of joyous celebration, vibrating through the air. To him, they were not a song of salvation, but merely the noise of a strong world arrayed against him.

Romola watched him disappear around the corner into the Piazza de' Mozzi. She felt a strange mixture of relief and self-reproach. Relief that he was gone, yet regret that she had not pressed harder to uncover the truth, to determine if his suffering was guiltless misery she could alleviate. Yet, what could she do if the truth was a burden of pain concerning her husband? Was a wife not permitted to desire ignorance of her spouse's crimes, especially when she alone bore the duty of warning others against him? The thought tangled too many fibers of her heart to be untangled now in her exhaustion.

It was time to rejoice, for help had arrived for Florence. She turned back into the courtyard to share the good news with her patients lying on their straw beds.

Closing the door to muffle the ringing bells, she threw back the black drapery covering her head so the women might see her clearly. Standing amidst them, she announced that corn was coming and that the bells rang for gladness. The sick propped themselves up to listen; the children, impatient to be near her face, trotted and crawled toward her, tugging at her black skirts. Weary as she was, she yielded to them, sitting down on the straw. The pale faces peered into her basket, pulling at her hair, while the feeble voices around her chanted, "The Holy Virgin be praised!"

"It was the procession!"
"The Mother of God has had pity on us!"

Finally, Romola rose from the heap of straw, too drained to offer a smile. Turning up the stone steps, she said, "I will come by-and-by to bring you your dinner."

"Bless you, madonna! Bless you!" the faint chorus replied, echoing the tone they had used moments before to praise the unseen Madonna.

Romola cherished that music deeply. Unlike other women who found comfort in the simple acts of tending the sick and clothing the ragged, her early training had kept her aloof from such domestic labors. Without the inspiration of her deepest feelings, they would have been irksome to her. But they had become the one unshaken resting place of her mind, the narrow pathway where light fell clear.

The widening gulf between herself and Tito, which only deepened with her attempts to bridge it through submission, brought a gnawing doubt. Could the bond she had labored to uphold be false? Had she been misled by her confessor, Fra Salvestro, or by the disciples of Savonarola, whose narrowness she now felt with a sickening clarity? When she felt an impetuous reaction rising against their superstition, she found herself recovering a firm footing in her works of womanly sympathy.

Whatever doubts plagued her, the help she gave to her fellow citizens made her certain that Fra Girolamo had been right to call her back. According to his unforgettable words, her place had not been empty; it had been filled with her love and her labor. Florence needed her. The more her own sorrow pressed upon her, the greater her gladness in the memories of the past two years, the hours in which she had lightened the burden of life for others. All that ardor of her nature, which could no longer spend itself solely on the tenderness for father and husband, had transformed into an enthusiasm of sympathy with the general life. She had ceased to think of her own happiness; the sole end of her existence seemed to be the diminishing of sorrow.

Her enthusiasm was continually stoked by the influence of Savonarola. Though she recoiled in disgust from the wearisome visions and allegories when repeated by others, her affinity for his passionate sympathy and the splendor of his aims remained undiminished. His burning indignation against the abuses and oppressions of the Church and the State kindled a fire within her. His special care for the liberty and purity of the Florentine government, constantly linked to the wider goal of universal regeneration, created in her a new consciousness of the great drama of human existence in which her life was a part. Through her daily contact with the less fortunate, this consciousness grew from a vague sentiment into a definite motive of self-denying practice.

She thought little of dogmas and shrank from reflecting closely on the Friar's prophecies of immediate scourge and regeneration. She had submitted her mind to his and entered into communion with the Church because, in doing so, she found an immediate satisfaction for moral needs that her previous culture and experience had left hungering. Fra Girolamo's voice had awakened in her a reason for living, apart from personal enjoyment or affection. Yet, it was a reason that seemed to require forces greater than she possessed within herself. Her submissive use of all Church offices was simply a watching and waiting, hoping that fresh strength might come.

The pressing problem for Romola was not to settle questions of controversy, but to keep alive that flame of unselfish emotion by which a life of sadness might still be a life of active love.

Her trust in Savonarola's nature as greater than her own formed a large part of the strength she had found. This trust was not lightly shaken. It was not the force of intellect that caused a repulsion from the aberrations of greatness, nor was it the force of vision that allowed the eye to explore the warts on a face bright with human expression; it was simply the negation of high sensibilities. Romola was so deeply moved by the grand energies of Savonarola's nature that she found herself listening patiently to all his dogmas and prophecies when delivered in the vehicle of his ardent faith and believing utterance.

No soul is desolate as long as there is a human being for whom it can feel trust and reverence. Romola's trust in Savonarola was something like a rope suspended securely by her path, making her step elastic while she grasped it; if it were suddenly removed, no firmness of the ground she trod could save her from staggering, or perhaps from falling.

At the Barber's Shop.

Following the unexpected arrival of the messenger bearing the olive branch—a favor of fortune Tito had not anticipated—he was immediately burdened with commissions of a far more deliberate nature. He paused before the Palazzo Vecchio, waiting for the return of the Ten, the body that managed external and war affairs, to deliver the results of his private mission to Pisa. This report was merely a preliminary step to an open embassy led by Bernardo Rucellai, aimed at securing a peaceful understanding with Emperor Maximilian and the League.

Tito's aptitude for diplomatic work had been thoroughly proven. As he presented the findings of his inquiries and interviews with fullness and precision, Bernardo del Nero, then a member of the Ten, could not suppress his admiration. Yet, he would have suppressed it if he could. His original dislike of Tito had resurfaced and intensified since the sale of the library. Romola had never spoken to her godfather regarding the circumstances of that sale. Bernardo interpreted her silence as a prohibition against raising the subject, yet he remained convinced that violating her father's wishes had caused her profound grief. His observant eyes detected further signs that her married life was unhappy.

"Ah," he murmured inwardly, "that is surely why she has taken to listening to Fra Girolamo and associating with the Piagnoni, something I never expected of her. Women, if they are unhappy and childless, must either descend into folly or embrace an overzealous religion that convinces them they bear the entire weight of heaven's work on their shoulders. As for my poor child Romola, as I have always said, the cramming of Latin and Greek has left her as much a woman as if she had spent her entire day pricking her fingers with a needle. And this husband of hers, who is employed everywhere because he is a tool with a smooth handle, I pray that Tornabuoni and the others may not find their fingers cut. Well, well, *solco torto, sacco dritto*—many a full sack comes from a crooked furrow; and he who will be captain of no honest men will have little hire to pay."

Holding this long-standing conviction that the moral character of political agents could not be sifted, the old Florentine refrained from interfering in Tito's disfavor. Aside from what must remain sacred and private for Romola's sake, Bernardo had no direct accusation against the useful Greek except that he was a Greek and that Bernardo disliked him. The duplicity of feigning attachment to the popular government while harboring Medicean sympathies was common to Tito and more than half the Medicean party; Tito merely executed it with greater skill. Consequently, Bernardo remained cold to Tito, who returned the coldness with scrupulous, distant respect. In Florence, it was still believed that any service rendered to Romola's husband constituted an acceptable homage to her godfather.

Having discharged his duties at the Old Palace, Tito felt the official work of the day was complete. Exhausted and sunburned from long riding, he did not return home. There were certain matters in his satchel and on his mind from which he wished to be freed, but these opportunities had to be seized so skillfully that they appeared accidental rather than sought. He strolled from the Palazzo toward the Piazza del Duomo. The procession had ended, yet the bells still rang, and the populace moved restlessly through the streets, craving a more definite outlet for their joy. If the Friar could have stood in the great square to preach to them, they might have been satisfied; but now, despite the new discipline declaring Christ the special King of the Florentines and demanding that all pleasures be Christian, a secret longing persisted among the youths shouting "Viva Gesu!" for a vigorous display of stone-throwing as a sign of thanks.

As Tito passed, he could not avoid recognition as the bearer of the olive branch. To rid himself of the inconvenient ovation, primarily in the form of eager questions, he told those who pressed upon him that Meo di Sasso, the true messenger from Leghorn, must now be entering and could certainly be met near the Porta San Frediano. He could reveal far more than he actually knew.

Freeing himself from importunity in this adroit manner, he made his way to the Piazza del Duomo, casting long, careless-looking eyes around the space while secretly searching for a presence that might provide one of his desired opportunities. The termination of the procession at the Duomo meant a probable concentration of loungers and talkers in the square and around Nello's shop. It was exactly as he expected. A group leaned against the rails near the north gates of the Baptistery, precisely where he sought to be. He affected indifference even more than before, seeming to recognize the tallest member of the group entirely by chance as he half-past him, turning his head to offer a slight greeting while tossing the end of his *becchetto* over his left shoulder.

Yet the tall, broad-shouldered figure greeted in that perfunctory manner possessed considerable claims. He wore a richly embroidered tunic with an abundance of linen, following the newest French fashion, and at his belt hung a sword and poniard of fine workmanship. His hat, adorned with a red plume, seemed a scornful protest against the gravity of Florentine costume, which had been exaggerated to the utmost under the influence of the Piagnoni. Certain indefinable indications of youth made the breadth of his face and the large diameter of his waist appear all the more emphatic stamps of coarseness, and his eyes held that rude, desecrating stare at all men and things which is as intolerable to a refined mind as a bad odor or a flaring light.

He and his companions, also young men dressed expensively and wearing arms, exchanged jokes with that sort of ostentatious laughter which implies a desire to prove that the laughter is not mortified, though some might suspect it. There were good reasons for such suspicion; for this broad-shouldered man with the red feather was Dolfo Spini, leader of the Compagnacci, or Evil Companions—that is to say, of all the dissolute young men belonging to the old aristocratic party, enemies of the Mediceans, enemies of the popular government, but still more bitter enemies of Savonarola. Dolfo Spini, heir of the great house with the loggia over the bridge of the Santa Trinita, had organized these young men into an armed band as sworn champions of extravagant suppers and all the pleasant sins of the flesh, standing against reforming pietists who threatened to make the world so chaste and temperate that there would soon be no reason for living except the extreme unpleasantness of the alternative. Up to that very morning, he had loudly declared that Florence was given over to famine and ruin entirely through its blind adherence to the advice of the Friar, and that there could be no salvation for Florence but in joining the League and driving the Friar out of the city—sending him to Rome, in fact, whither he ought to have gone long ago in obedience to the summons of the Pope. It was suspected, therefore, that Messer Dolfo Spini's heart was not aglow with pure joy at the unexpected succors which had arrived in apparent fulfillment of the Friar's predictions. The laughter, which rang out afresh as Tito joined the group at Nello's door, did not serve to dissipate the suspicion. Leaning against the door-post in the center of the group was a close-shaven, keen-eyed personage named Niccolò Machiavelli, who, young as he was, had penetrated all the small secrets of egoism.

"Messer Dolfo's head," he was saying, "is more of a pumpkin than I thought. I measure men's dulness by the devices they trust in for deceiving others. Your dullest animal of all is he who grins and says he doesn't mind just after he has had his shins kicked. If I were a trifle duller, now," he continued, smiling as the circle opened to admit Tito, "I should pretend to be fond of this Melema, who has got a secretaryship that would exactly suit me—as if Latin ill-paid could love better Latin that's better paid! Melema, you are a pestiferously clever fellow, very much in my way, and I'm sorry to hear you've had another piece of good-luck today."

"Questionable luck, Niccolò," said Tito, touching him on the shoulder in a friendly way; "I have got nothing by it yet but being laid hold of and breathed upon by wool-beaters, when I am as soiled and battered with riding as a *tabellario* (letter-carrier) from Bologna."

"Ah! you want a touch of my art, Messer Oratore," said Nello, who had come forward at the sound of Tito's voice; "your chin, I perceive, has yesterday's crop upon it. Come, come—consign yourself to the priest of all the Muses. Sandro, quick with the lather!"

"In truth, Nello, that is just what I most desire at this moment," said Tito, seating himself; "and that was why I turned my steps towards thy shop, instead of going home at once, when I had done my business at the Palazzo."

"Yes, indeed, it is not fitting that you should present yourself to Madonna Romola with a rusty chin and a tangled *zazzera*. Nothing that is not dainty ought to approach the Florentine lily; though I see her constantly going about like a sunbeam amongst the rags that line our corners—if indeed she is not more like a moonbeam now, for I thought yesterday, when I met her, that she looked as pale and worn as that fainting Madonna of Fra Giovanni's. You must see to it, my bel erudito: she keeps too many fasts and vigils in your absence."

Tito gave a melancholy shrug. "It is too true, Nello. She has been depriving herself of half her proper food *every* day during this famine. But what can I do? Her mind has been set all aflame. A husband's influence is powerless against the Friar's."

"As every other influence is likely to be, that of the Holy Father included," said Domenico Cennini, one of the group at the door, who had turned in with Tito. "I don't know whether you have gathered anything at Pisa about the way the wind sits at Rome, Melema?"

"Secrets of the council-chamber, Messer Domenico!" said Tito, smiling and opening his palms in a deprecatory manner. "An envoy must be as dumb as a father confessor."

"Certainly, certainly," said Cennini. "I ask for no breach of that rule. Well, my belief is, that if his Holiness were to drive Fra Girolamo to extremity, the Frate would move heaven and earth to get a General Council of the Church—ay, and would get it too; and I, for one, should not be sorry, though I'm no Piagnone."

"With leave of your greater experience, Messer Domenico," said Machiavelli, "I must differ from you—not in your wish to see a General Council which might reform the Church, but in your belief that the Frate will checkmate his Holiness. The Frate's game is an impossible one. If he had contented himself with preaching against the vices of Rome, and with prophesying that in some way, not mentioned, Italy would be scourged, depend upon it Pope Alexander would have allowed him to spend his breath in that way as long as he could find hearers. Such spiritual blasts as those knock no walls down. But the Frate wants to be something more than a spiritual trumpet: he wants to be a lever, and what is more, he *is* a lever. He wants to spread the doctrine of Christ by maintaining a popular government in Florence, and the Pope, as I know, on the best authority, has private views to the contrary."

"Then Florence will stand by the Frate," Cennini broke in, with some fervor. "I myself should prefer that he would let his prophesying alone, but if our freedom to choose our own government is to be attacked—I am an obedient son of the Church, but I would vote for resisting Pope Alexander the Sixth, as our forefathers resisted Pope Gregory the Eleventh."

"But pardon me, Messer Domenico," said Machiavelli, sticking his thumbs into his belt, and speaking with that cool enjoyment of exposition which surmounts every other force in discussion. "Have you correctly seized the Frate's position? How is it that he has become a lever, and made himself worth attacking by an acute man like his Holiness? Because he has got the ear of the people: because he gives them threats and promises, which they believe come straight from God, not only about hell, purgatory, and paradise, but about Pisa and our Great Council. But let events go against him, so as to shake the people's faith, and the cause of his power will be the cause of his fall. He is accumulating three sorts of hatred on his head—the hatred of average mankind against everyone who wants to lay on them a strict yoke of virtue; the hatred of the stronger powers in Italy who want to farm Florence for their own purposes; and the hatred of the people, to whom he has ventured to promise good in this world, instead of confining his promises to the next. If a prophet is to keep his power, he must be a prophet like Mahomet, with an army at his back, that when the people's faith is fainting it may be frightened into life again."

"Rather sum up the three sorts of hatred in one," said Francesco Cei, impetuously, "and say he has won the hatred of all men who have sense and honesty, by inventing hypocritical lies. His proper place is among the false prophets in the Inferno, who walk with their heads turned hind-foremost."

"You are too angry, my Francesco," said Machiavelli, smiling; "you poets are apt to cut the clouds in your wrath. I am no votary of the Frate's, and would not lay down my little finger for his veracity. But veracity is a plant of paradise, and the seeds have never flourished beyond the walls. You, yourself, my Francesco, tell poetical lies only

The evening air was thick with the promise of rain, heavy and threatening, as Romola returned from the hospital of San Matteo. Beside her walked Maso, holding a lantern that cast a wavering pool of light upon the cobblestones. Just as they emerged from the shadows of the street, her husband, Tito, stepped forth from the monastery of San Marco. He had barely spent the day with her, having retreated into the solitude of the Via de' Bardi moments after his arrival. Seeing her, he immediately dismissed Maso, his impatience with the servant's slow gait evident, and offered to escort Romola home himself.

This was a performance Tito reserved for occasions when his role demanded it; an official courtesy rather than a sign of intimacy. Between them, there existed no friction, no heated debate. They were two souls drifting in parallel currents, hopelessly alienated in their inner lives, rendering any true contest for agreement impossible. Instead, they navigated their shared existence with a careful, mechanical adherence to a pre-arranged script. If Tito desired a supper prepared in the old library, now transformed into a banquet hall, Romola complied without question, ensuring every detail was perfect. In return, Tito granted her absolute autonomy over her daily rhythms, accepting her assistance with his manuscripts and her supplies for charity, all while maintaining a rigid distance. He avoided her gaze, feigning ignorance of her presence within the house, and playfully attributed her withdrawal to a preference for solitude.

In the early days of her return, after she had abandoned her plan to flee, Romola had made timid attempts to restore a frank relationship. She believed that only through open dialogue regarding their differences could they find a moral understanding. Tito, however, required something far more elusive: a return to her effusive tenderness, a love that would erase the memory of their discord. He desired no explanation; he deemed it impossible. He wished for her affection, but she found tenderness impossible to fabricate. She could be submissive, gentle, and suppress any sign of repulsion, but she could not pretend. Helpless before the reality of their estrangement, she watched as her husband drifted further away.

This distance made her acutely sensitive to secrets he would never willingly share. Regarding his political maneuvers, Tito assured her that the Medici cause was lost and that he served the popular government with genuine conviction—a sentiment she shared warmly. Yet, subtle impressions, like faint odors, gnawed at her peace of mind concerning his dealings with San Marco. She was torn between the terror of discovering evidence that would confirm her deepest suspicions and the urgent impulse to watch, lest some harm befall the city that she might have prevented.

As they walked, Tito spoke of unfinished business. "The day is not yet done for me," he said, hurrying her toward their door. "I must fulfill another commission, perhaps taking an hour, before I can return to rest, which I sorely need." He then chatted lightly of his visit to Pisa, guiding her until they reached a quiet loggia where a lamp burned before a picture of the Virgin. Suddenly, the street, previously empty, filled with the sound of many approaching footsteps and confused voices.

"We shall not reach home dry unless we take shelter here," Tito said, pulling Romola hastily up the steps of the loggia with a start.

"Surely it is futile to wait for such a drizzle," Romola remarked, surprised.

"No," Tito replied, his eyes scanning the gloom with the hyper-vigilance of a mind accustomed to caution. "The rain is growing heavier. Wait a moment." He had spotted the leader of the approaching group: a red feather and a glittering sword-hilt marked him as Dolfo Spini, the captain of the Compagnacci. It was the last person Tito wished to encounter with Romola beside him. Earlier that day, he had met Spini, and now he faced a second, more critical interview. Tito had been entrusted with forged letters—carefully crafted documents bearing the signature and seal of the Cardinal of Naples, a powerful ally of Savonarola. These letters claimed the Cardinal, unwilling to enter Florence directly, intended to meet the Frate at San Casciano the following morning, disguised as a simple priest. The plot was clear: Spini was to ambush the Frate on the lonely road, seize him, and hand him over to Milanese horsemen waiting near San Casciano to be taken into Roman territory.

Tito had already spoken with Savonarola, presenting the letters with consummate art. He had led the Frate to believe they referred to a rendezvous outside the gates, urging him to request an armed guard from the Signoria, offering his own services to carry the request privately. Savonarola had refused, stating that an armed guard contradicted the privacy required for such a meeting. His flashing eyes revealed his intent to accept the risk. Tito cared little for the outcome; his acute mind saw that regardless of whether the trap succeeded or failed, he would emerge richer and more powerful. If Savonarola fell, Tito gained papal favor and diamonds; if he survived, Tito's agency strengthened his position with both the Frate and Spini.

However, Spini was an unpredictable variable. Cunning enough to devise plots, he lacked the self-command to keep them secret. He often drank heavily, and despite their agreement to maintain a cool demeanor in public, there was always the danger that a drunken Spini might blur the lines of their partnership with sudden, affectionate familiarity. Tito's greatest fear was that Spini had already seen him. In the shadow of the loggia, Tito had been illuminated for an instant by the lamp's rays, marking him as clearly as Spini himself. Romola, cloaked in black, had gone unnoticed, standing behind her husband's shoulder.

"Ha! My carrier-pigeon!" Spini's harsh voice grated out, though he meant it as a whisper, his hand clamping onto Tito's shoulder. "Why did you run to hide? You didn't know it was comrades coming. It's good I caught you; it saves time. What of the chase tomorrow? Will the bald-headed game rise? Are the falcons ready?"

If Tito possessed a temper, rage would have flared against this bull-faced accomplice, unfit to lead or follow. His lips turned white, his excitement born of the desperate need to choose a safe path. To hush Spini would only deepen Romola's suspicion; she was a woman who, once alarmed, could not be silenced. To repel Spini angrily might provoke the wine-soaked Compagnaccio into savagery. Tito chose a third course, one that demonstrated Romola still held a terrifying power over him: the power of dread.

He pressed Romola's hand, feigning a signal to her, and spoke in a tone of jovial camaraderie. "Yes, my Dolfo, you may prepare in all security. But take no trumpets with you."

"Don't be afraid," Spini retorted, slightly piqued. "No need to play Ser Saccente with me. I know where the devil keeps his tail as well as you. What! He swallowed the bait whole? The prophetic nose didn't scent the hook?"

"The brute won't be satisfied until he has emptied the bag," Tito thought, but aloud he said, "Swallowed it as easily as you swallow a cup of Trebbiano. Ha! I see torches; there must be a dead body coming. The pestilence has been spreading, I hear."

"Santiddio! I hate the sight of those biers. Good-night," Spini muttered, hastening away.

The torches were indeed approaching, preceding a church dignitary returning home. Tito bowed with due reverence, but Romola saw none of the outward ceremony. If defeating this treachery—which she felt with the force of long prescience—required her to spring upon her husband and hurl them both down a precipice, she felt she could do it. Union with this man was intolerable. The self-discipline of two years evaporated; she felt only the chasm between them.

Darkness fell again, leaving them to see only each other's faces dimly.

"Tell me the truth, Tito—this time tell me the truth," Romola whispered, her voice trembling. "It will be safer for you."

"Why should I desire to tell you anything else, my angry saint?" Tito replied with a touch of contempt, venting his annoyance. "Since the truth is precisely what you have most reason to rejoice in: that my knowledge of Spini's plot enables me to secure the Frate from falling victim to it."

"What is the plot?"

"That I decline to tell," Tito said. "It is enough that the Frate's safety will be secured."

"It is a plot to draw him outside the gates so Spini may murder him."

"There has been no intention of murder," Tito corrected smoothly. "It is simply a plot to compel him to obey the Pope's summons to Rome. But since I serve the popular government and believe the Frate's presence here is vital to its maintenance, I choose to prevent his departure. You may go to sleep with an easy mind tonight."

For a moment, Romola was silent. Then, in a voice of anguish, she said, "Tito, it is of no use: I have no belief in you."

She could just discern his shrug, the spreading of his palms in silent dismissal. A cold dislike, the anger of unimpassioned beings, hardened within him.

"If the Frate leaves the city—if any harm comes to him," Romola said, her tone shifting to one of indignant resolve, "I will declare what I have heard to the Signoria, and you will be disgraced. What if I am your wife?" she continued, impetuous. "I will be disgraced with you. If we are united, I am that part of you that will save you from crime. Others shall not be betrayed."

"I am quite aware of what you would likely do, *anima mia*," Tito said in his coolest, liquid tones. "Therefore, if you possess a small amount of reasoning, consider that if you believe me in nothing else, you may believe me when I say I will take care of myself, and not put it in your power to ruin me."

"Then you assure me that the Frate is warned—he will not go beyond the gates?"

"He shall not go beyond the gates."

A moment of pause followed, but distrust remained unexpelled.

"I will go back to San Marco now and find out," Romola said, making a move forward.

"You shall not!" Tito whispered bitterly, seizing her wrists with all his masculine strength. "I am master of you. You shall not set yourself in opposition to me."

Passersby were approaching; Tito had heard them, which was why he spoke in a whisper. Romola, too conscious of being mastered to struggle, and aware that witnesses were near, yielded instantly to his movement to lead her from the loggia.

They walked on in silence under the small, drizzling rain. The initial rush of indignation and alarm in Romola began to give way to more complex feelings, rendering speech and action difficult. In that simpler state of vehemence, open opposition to a husband whose soul she felt revolting against had seemed the easiest course. But now, habits of self-questioning, memories of subdued impulses, and the proud reserve left by her discipline began to emerge from the flood of passion. The grasp of her wrists, asserting her husband's physical dominance, did not arouse a new fierceness in her. Instead, it gave her a momentary shuddering horror at this form of contest. It was the first time they had been in declared hostility since her flight and return, and the check given to her ardent resolution then retained the power to arrest her now.

In this altered state, her mind dwelt on probabilities that would save her from a desperate course. Tito would not risk betrayal by her; whatever his original intention, he must now be determined by the fact that she knew of the plot. She was not bound to do anything else than hang over him with the certainty that if he deceived her, her lips would not be closed. And then, it was possible—yes, she must cling to that possibility until disproved—that Tito had never meant to aid in the betrayal of the Frate.

Tito, meanwhile, was busy with his own thoughts, remaining silent until they were near home. Then he said, "Well, Romola, have you now had time to recover calmness? If so, you can supply your want of belief in me by a little rational inference: you can see, I presume, that if I had any intention of furthering Spini's plot, I should now be aware that the possession of a fair Piagnone for my wife, who knows the secret of the plot, would be a serious obstacle in my way."

Tito assumed the tone most comfortable to him, guessing that persuasive deprecation would fall on deaf ears in her current mood.

"Yes, Tito," she said in a low voice, "I think you believe that I would guard the Republic from further treachery. You are right to believe it: if the Frate is betrayed, I will denounce you." She paused, then added with an effort, "But it was not so. I have perhaps spoken too hastily—you never meant it. Only, why will you seem to be that man's comrade?"

"Such relations are inevitable to practical men, my Romola," Tito said, gratified by discerning the struggle within her. "You fair creatures live in the clouds. Pray go to rest with an easy heart," he added, opening the door for her.

Tito's intricate schemes had been thwarted not by grand designs, but by the petty, unpredictable accidents that no mastermind can foresee. It was rare for him to stroll with Romola in the evening, yet fate had conspired to place them together precisely when her presence was a liability. Life proved to be a labyrinthine game where calculated moves were constantly undone by chance, as fragile as falling dandelion seeds.

Tito did not resent the collapse of Spini's plot; rather, he grappled with the delicate art of deception required to navigate his warnings. To alert Savonarola without arousing suspicion among the populace was one challenge; to manage Dolfo Spini without triggering the fury of a cornered beast was another. If he rushed to the monastery to warn the friar immediately after delivering the forged letters, the proximity of the acts would invite dangerous speculation. Conversely, warning Spini instantly would require confessing the truth—that Savonarola had altered his course—and Tito knew Spini well enough to understand that such honesty would only confirm Tito's treachery, painting him as a man who had "turned round" and betrayed his allies. Yet, delaying the warning to Savonarola until morning risked losing the window to stop Spini's enraged band of Compagnacci from returning in defeat. Weighing these dangers, Tito chose the lesser evil: he would soothe Spini with lies, attributing the failure solely to the friar's caution, while accepting the risk of missing the critical moment to save Savonarola.

Annoyed and irritable, Tito found himself unable to maintain his usual composure. Determined to avoid further confrontation with Romola, he abandoned his home that night and vanished into the city's shadows.

Inside, Romola remained awake, her clothes untouched, as the rain intensified outside. She found solace in the storm, viewing the tempestuous heavens as a divine barrier against human malice, forcing men to stand still. Yet, her own mind was a battleground. Doubt gnawed at her regarding Tito's fidelity, compounded by a terrifying uncertainty about her own actions. What falsehoods might he have whispered to her? What hidden agendas might she remain ignorant of? Every person who trusted Tito now stood in peril, and her attempts to convince herself otherwise felt futile. Was she not acting out of pride when she hesitated to warn others against him? "If my husband is a criminal, my place is beside him in prison"—this thought offered a grim comfort, yet it left a burning question unanswered: Could a wife allow her spouse to commit atrocities when she possessed the power to intervene? Prayer felt impossible; her mind, agitated by frantic speculation, refused the stillness required for faith.

As the night wore on, the storm's violence waned, the clouds dispersed, and a clear morning broke over Florence. Paradoxically, the clarity only deepened her torment. Wrapping her mantle tightly, she rushed to the loggia, scanning the vast landscape as if some external sign could dictate her next move, though she saw only rooftops concealing the streets where Savonarola might walk toward ruin.

Could she summon her godfather to block the gates without revealing specific details? It seemed too late. Distress tightened her chest; she realized she had gleaned no crucial information from Tito, and the hour had long passed seven. There was no alternative but to proceed to San Marco.

She descended the stairs with urgency, ignoring the sick who awaited her care, and hurried along the Via de' Bardi toward the Ponte Vecchio. She chose the path through the city's heart, the most direct route, hoping that in the great Piazza she might intercept her husband. Perhaps, by some miracle, he could reassure her of the friar's safety, rendering her journey to San Marco unnecessary.
