Upon reaching the courtyard of the Palazzo Vecchio, she peered eagerly into the pillared space and scanned the square, but the figure she had once loved and now feared was absent. Undeterred, she pressed on toward the Piazza del Duomo.

The square buzzed with life: worshippers ascending the marble steps, men pausing for conversation, vendors laden with goods. Amidst this throng, Romola spotted her husband. He had diverted from San Marco to Nello's shop and now leaned casually against the doorpost. As she drew near, she observed him with a relaxed demeanor, holding his cap, shaking back his freshly combed hair. The stark contrast between his effortless ease and the agony he had inflicted upon her convulsed her with indignation; the vision of his cold hardness intensified her dread. She recognized Cronaca and two other regulars of San Marco standing nearby. A sudden resolve flashed through her mind: "I will force him to speak before these witnesses."

Her light step carried her swiftly toward him before he could retreat, just as Cronaca announced, "Here comes Madonna Romola."

A jolt ran through Tito as he faced his wife. She appeared haggard from sleepless vigilance, yet her eyes burned with a intensity that went beyond mere worry.

"Has the Frate passed beyond the gates?" she demanded.

"No," Tito replied, feeling utterly powerless before her gaze, straining every ounce of his self-control to mask his fear with feigned surprise.

"And are you certain he is not departing?" she pressed.

"I am certain he is not going."

"That is sufficient," Romola declared, turning up the steps to seek refuge within the Duomo, needing the sanctuary of the church to steady her trembling hands.

Tito watched her retreat, a sensation of hatred rising within him that bordered on the abyss. Among the crowd gathered in the square were not only devoted followers of the friar but also Ser Ceccone, the notary. Like Tito, Ceccone was secretly an agent of the Mediceans.

Ser Francesco di Ser Barone, known infamously as Ser Ceccone, was neither learned nor handsome, nor successful, and possessed no generosity. He was a traitor devoid of charm, a fact that ensured he held no affection for Tito Melema.

The afternoon sun had begun to wane, casting long, grey shadows across the floor when Tito returned to his home. In the narrow chamber opposite the cabinet, Romola sat over her documents, her hand pausing mid-copy as the light failed. Her husband entered not with the casual ease of a spouse, but with a singular, heavy purpose. He did not remove his cap or mantle; instead, he leaned an elbow upon the cabinet and stood before her, a dark silhouette against the dimming room.

Romola felt the sudden, sharp recoil of her own conscience. Throughout the day, the news of the Friar's safety had settled in her mind, washing away the immediate sting of her public confrontation. She realized now how foolish her impetuousness had been. The duplicity inherent in Florentine politics, a truth even her godfather had whispered, could easily explain Tito's connection to Spini without implying any treason on his part. She had acted in anger, driven by a distrust that she now saw as a mistake born of isolation. For hours, she had rehearsed a confession, hoping to break the two-year silence that had locked them together like a banquet hall where death had once shattered the feast. She had fled, and she had returned, yet neither had ever truly acknowledged the past that lay between them.

She looked up at him, her gaze softened by self-reproach, but the subtle shift in his demeanor halted her words. A crisis had arrived, invisible until now, altering the landscape of their marriage. Beneath his usual blandness and beseeching manner, Tito's determination to master his wife had surfaced, hardening his features as if a hidden muscle were strangling a dangerous, feeble thing within him.

"Romola," he began, his voice a cool, liquid tone that sent a shiver through her, "it is time that we should understand each other."

"That is what I most desire, Tito," she replied faintly. Her pale face, stripped of its anger and filled only with the timidity of doubt, seemed to highlight the predominance of his dark strength.

"You took a step this morning," Tito continued, "which you must now perceive to be useless—a step that exposed you to remark and may involve me in serious practical difficulties."

"I acknowledge that I was too hasty," Romola said, her voice finding a firmer register. She hoped this admission of regret would soften his gaze, preparing the ground for the words she truly needed to speak.

"I wish you to understand once and for all," Tito said, his voice unchanging, "that such collisions are incompatible with our position as husband and wife. Reflect on the manner in which you were led to that step, lest it be repeated."

"That depends chiefly on you, Tito," Romola retorted, a spark of resentment igniting her cheeks. It was not what she had intended to say, for the path of mutual speech was narrow and obscured.

"You would say, I suppose," Tito answered, "that nothing in the future should excite your unreasonable suspicions. You were frank enough last night to declare you hold no belief in me. I am not surprised by the exaggerated conclusions you draw from slight premises, but I wish to point out the fruit of making such conclusions a basis for interfering in affairs of which you are ignorant. Are you paying attention?"

He waited for a response.

"Yes," Romola flushed, unable to suppress her resentment at his superior coldness.

"Well, then," Tito pressed, "it may not be long before another chance word or incident sets your imagination to devising crimes against me. You might rush to the Palazzo Vecchio to alarm the Signoria and set the city ablaze. Shall I tell you the result? Not merely the disgrace of your husband, to which you look forward with such courage, but the arrest and ruin of many chief men in Florence, including Messer Bernardo del Nero."

Tito had made his move, decisive and absolute. The flush drained from Romola's face, leaving her lips pale—a rare sight, for she was little given to fear. Tito perceived his success.

"You might flatter yourself," he went on, "that you are performing a heroic deed of deliverance. You might as well try to turn locks with fine words as apply such notions to the politics of Florence. The question is no longer whether you can believe in me, but whether, now that you have been warned, you will dare to rush, like a blind man with a torch, into intricate affairs you know nothing of."

Romola felt her mind gripped in a vice by Tito's intellect. The possibilities he outlined rose before her with terrible clarity.

"I am too rash," she admitted. "I will try not to be rash."

"Remember," Tito insisted, his tone unsparing, "that your act of distrust this morning might have had far more fatal effects than the sacrifice of your husband, a fate you have learned to contemplate without flinching."

"Tito, it is not so!" Romola burst forth, rising and moving closer to him with a desperate resolve to speak the truth. "It is false that I would willingly sacrifice you. It has been the greatest effort of my life to cling to you. I went away in my anger two years ago, and I returned because I was bound to you more than to anything on earth. But it is useless. You shut me out from your mind. You pretend to think of me as too unreasonable to share in your affairs. You will be open with me about nothing."

She looked like a good angel pleading with a sinner, bending her face toward him with dilated eyes and laying her hand upon his arm. Yet, Romola's touch and glance stirred no fiber of tenderness in her husband. The good-humoured, tolerant Tito, capable of little hatred or impatience, felt himself growing strangely hard toward this wife who had once been his strongest influence. With all his softness of disposition, he possessed a masculine effectiveness of intellect and purpose, an energy sharp as an edge, working its way without great momentum. Romola possessed her own energy, one that thwarted his, and no man, unless exceptionally weak, endures being thwarted by his wife. Marriage must be either a relation of sympathy or of conquest.

No emotion crossed Tito's face as he heard Romola speak of having left him. His lips merely hardened slightly as he smiled faintly.

"My Romola, when certain conditions are ascertained, we must make up our minds to them. No amount of wishing will fill the Arno, as your people say, nor turn a plum into an orange. I have not observed that prayers have much efficacy in that regard. You are constituted to have strong impressions inaccessible to reason; I cannot share those impressions, and you have withdrawn all trust from me in consequence. You have changed toward me; therefore, I have changed toward you. It is useless to look backward. We must simply adapt ourselves to altered conditions."

"Tito, it would not be useless for us to speak openly," Romola said, her voice trembling with the exasperation of striking against an insurmountable, lifeless resistance. "It was the sense of deception in you that changed me, and that has kept us apart. And it is not true that I changed first. You changed toward me the night you first wore that chain-armour. You had some secret from me—it was about that old man—and I saw him again yesterday. Tito," she continued in a tone of agonized entreaty, "if you would once tell me everything, let it be what it may—I would not mind pain—that there might be no wall between us! Is it not possible that we could begin a new life?"

For the first time, a flash of emotion crossed Tito's face. He stood perfectly still, the flash seeming to whiten him. He ignored Romola's appeal. After a moment's pause, he said quietly, "Your impetuosity about trifles, Romola, has a freezing influence that would cool the baths of Nero."

At these cutting words, Romola shrank back, drawing herself up into her usual self-sustained posture. Tito continued, "If by 'that old man' you mean the mad Jacopo di Nola who attempted my life and made a strange accusation against me, of which I told you nothing because it would have alarmed you to no purpose, he, poor wretch, has died in prison. I saw his name in the list of the dead."

"I know nothing about his accusation," Romola said. "But I know he is the man I saw with a rope around his neck in the Duomo—the man whose portrait Piero di Cosimo painted, grasping your arm as he saw him grasp it the day the French entered, the day you first wore the armour."

"And where is he now, pray?" Tito asked, still pale but governing himself.

"He was lying lifeless in the street from starvation," Romola replied. "I revived him with bread and wine. I brought him to our door, but he refused to come in. Then I gave him some money, and he went away without telling me anything. But he had found out that I was your wife. Who is he?"

"A man, half mad, half imbecile, who was once my father's servant in Greece, and who bears a rancorous hatred toward me because I dismissed him for theft. Now you have the whole mystery, and the further satisfaction of knowing that I am again in danger of assassination. The fact of my wearing the armour, about which you seem to have thought so much, must have led you to infer that I was in danger from this man. Was that the reason you chose to cultivate his acquaintance and invite him into the house?"

Romola fell silent. To speak now would be like rushing with a bare breast against a shield.

Tito moved from his leaning posture, slowly removing his cap and mantle, and pushing back his hair. He was collecting himself for final words. Romola stood upright, looking at him as one might regard an oncoming deadly force, to be met only by silent endurance.

"We need not refer to these matters again, Romola," he said, in precisely the same tone as at the beginning. "It is enough if you will remember that the next time your generous ardour leads you to interfere in political affairs, you are likely, not to save anyone from danger, but to be raising scaffolds and setting houses on fire. You are not yet a sufficiently ardent Piagnone to believe that Messer Bernardo del Nero is the prince of darkness, and Messer Francesco Valori the archangel Michael. I think I need demand no promise from you?"

"I have understood you too well, Tito."

"It is enough," he said, and left the room.

Romola turned with despair written across her face and sank into her seat. "O God, I have tried—I cannot help it. We shall always be divided." These words passed silently through her mind. "Unless," she said aloud, as if a sudden vision had startled her into speech—"unless misery should come and join us!"

Tito, too, carried a new thought in his mind after closing the door behind him. Alongside his project of leaving Florence as soon as his life there became a sufficient stepping-stone to a new existence, perhaps in Rome or Milan, there arose for the first time a desire to be free from Romola, to leave her behind. She had ceased to belong to the desirable furniture of his life; there was no possibility of an easy relation between them without genuineness on his part. Genuineness implied a confession of the past, and confession involved a change of purpose. But Tito had as little bent that way as a leopard has to lap milk when its teeth are grown. From all relations that were not easy and agreeable, Tito shrank; why should he cling to them?

Moreover, Romola had made his relations difficult with others besides herself. He had had a troublesome interview with Dolfo Spini, who had returned in a rage after an ineffectual soak in the rain and a long wait in ambush. That scene between Romola and himself at Nello's door, once reported to Spini's ear, might be a seed of something more unmanageable than mere suspicion. But now, at least, he believed he had mastered Romola through a terror that appealed to the strongest forces of her nature. He had alarmed her affection and conscience with the shadowy image of consequences; he had arrested her intellect by hanging before it the idea of a hopeless complexity in affairs that defied moral judgment.

Yet Tito was not at ease. The world was not yet quite cushioned with velvet, and even if it had been, he could not have abandoned himself to that softness with thorough enjoyment. For before he went out again that evening, he put on his coat of chain-armour.

The winter days drifted past Romola with the silent, uniform tread of white ships passing a lone watcher on the shore. Each vessel carried a hidden cargo of impending change, though none announced its arrival. Tito's whispered warnings had woven a thread of dread into her curiosity regarding public affairs, leading her to prefer ignorance to knowledge. The threatening German Emperor had vanished, and Florence's precarious position seemed momentarily eased; yet the underlying distress remained so profound that Romola's active duties showed little diminution. In these relentless tasks, her mind found its only sanctuary from the gnawing doubts that plagued her.

She dared not allow herself the joy that the relief arrived in extremity, seemingly validating the Frate's party, had brought to Francesco Valori. The hot-tempered chieftain of the Piagnoni had been elected Gonfaloniere at the start of the year, eager to imprint his liberal will upon the city during his brief two-month tenure. On the surface, this appeared as a triumph for the faction most devoted to liberty and a shield for Savonarola. But Romola's spirit was now attuned to every shadow that suggested the present was merely an unconscious incubation of mixed germs, destined one day to bloom into tragedy. By Carnival time, shortly after mid-February, her foreboding crystallized into certainty: the Medici had ceased their passive stance and were actively maneuvering to secure the election of Bernardo del Nero as the new Gonfaloniere.

On the final day of the Carnival, between the tenth and eleventh hour, Romola walked toward the Corso degli Albizzi, fulfilling her promise to meet her cousin Brigida. They intended to depart from the Via de' Bardi in the early afternoon, securing their places at a window Tito had reserved in the Piazza della Signoria. A spectacle of unprecedented strangeness awaited them there, drawing the gaze of every Florentine eye. The Piagnoni were exercising their newfound authority over the manner of celebrating the festival. Despite the efforts of Dolfo Spini and his companions to revive the traditional masques and practical jokes—seasoned with the indecency of the past—such frivolities were deemed incompatible with a city that had declared Christ its King.

Romola set forth with a languid detachment, the mood of one entering a long day of sightseeing solely to gratify a child or a cherished friend. The day marked an epoch in the keeping of Carnival, yet this phase of reform failed to ignite her enthusiasm. She remained unaware that it was also an epoch in her own life, when another destiny would cease to be secretly entwined with hers and become visibly so.

Choosing to traverse the great Piazza alone, she sought a preliminary survey of the unparalleled sight. Entering from the south, she beheld a monstrous, multi-colored structure resembling a pyramid or a colossal fir-tree, sixty feet high. Its shelves widened toward the base until they spanned a circumference of eighty yards. The square teemed with life: slender youths in white garments, crowned with olive wreaths, moved to and fro around the base, carrying baskets brimming with bright objects. Maturer figures, clad in monastic robes or the loose tunics and dark-red caps of artists, assisted, examined, or retreated to vantage points to survey the wondrous whole. Among a considerable group standing on the marble steps of Orgagna's Loggia, Romola recognized Piero di Cosimo, who seemed to stand aloof in a posture of discontent and scorn.

Drawing nearer, she paused to inspect the multifarious objects ranged in gradation from the base to the summit. There were tapestries and brocades of immodest design, pictures and sculptures likely to incite vice. Boards and tables for every game lay alongside printing blocks, dice, and other apparatus for gambling. Worldly music-books and instruments of all varieties—lutes, drums, cymbals, trumpets—filled the air. Masks and costumes from old Carnival shows were displayed, alongside handsome copies of Ovid, Boccaccio, Petrarch, Pulci, and other works of vanity or impurity. Implements of feminine vanity abounded: rouge-pots, false hair, mirrors, perfumes, powders, and transparent veils designed to provoke inquisitive glances. At the very apex stood the unflattering effigy of a mythical Venetian merchant, rumored to have offered a heavy sum for this collection of marketable abominations. Soaring above him in surpassing ugliness was the symbolic figure of the old, debauched Carnival.

This was the preparation for a new kind of bonfire—the Burning of Vanities. Hidden within the pyramid's interior was a plentiful store of dry fuel and gunpowder. On the evening of this last festival day, the pile was to be ignited to the sound of trumpets, sending the ugly old Carnival tumbling into the flames amidst songs of reforming triumph.

Such a crowning act could hardly have been prepared without a peculiar organization initiated by Savonarola two years prior. The mass of Florentine boyhood and youth was no longer left to its own promptings toward street mischief and crude dissoluteness. Under the training of Fra Domenico, Savonarola's lieutenant, lads and striplings—the hope of Florence—were to speak only pure words, possess a zeal for the Unseen Good that would shame the lukewarmness of their elders, and know no pleasures save those of an angelic sort: singing divine praises and walking in white robes. It was for them that the ranges of seats had been raised high against the walls of the Duomo, where they had learned to hear Savonarola appeal to them as the future glory of a city specially appointed to execute God's work.

These fresh-cheeked troops were the chief agents in the regenerated merriment of the new Carnival, a sacred parody of the old. Had there been bonfires in times past? Now, there would be a bonfire to consume impurity from the earth. Had there been symbolic processions? Now, there were processions bearing white robes, red crosses, and olive wreaths—emblems of peace and innocent gladness—while banners and images proclaimed the triumphs of goodness. Had there been dancing in a ring under the open sky to the sound of choral voices chanting loose songs? Now, there was dancing of monks and laity in fraternal love and divine joy, accompanied by the music of hymns. The collections from street passengers would be greater than ever, not for gross suppers, but for the hungry and needy, alongside the gathering of the *Anathema*, the vanities destined for the great pyramidal bonfire.

Troops of young inquisitors went from house to house on this exciting business, demanding the surrender of the Anathema. Perhaps, after the more avowed vanities were yielded, the Madonna of the household still possessed little reddened balls brought from the Levant, intended to produce a sudden, ingenuous falsity upon a sallow cheek? If so, she was to bring them down and cast them into the basket of doom. Or perhaps she had ringlets and coils of "dead hair"? Then she was to bring them to the street door, not upon her head, but in her hands, publicly renouncing the Anathema that hid the respectable signs of age under a ghastly mockery of youth. In reward, she would hear fresh young voices pronounce a blessing upon her and her house.

The beardless inquisitors, organized into little regiments, undoubtedly took to their work with willingness. To coerce people by shame or spiritual pelting into parting with things they would otherwise cherish was a form of piety to which the boyish mind was readily converted. If some obstinately wicked men grew enraged and threatened the whip or the cudgel, this too added to the excitement. Savonarola himself evidently felt the difficulty weighing on all minds with noble yearnings toward great ends, yet possessing that imperfect perception of means which forces a resort to supernatural constraining influence as the only sure hope. The Florentine youth had harbored very evil habits and foul tongues; it seemed at first an unmixed blessing when they shouted "Viva Gesù!" But Savonarola was eventually forced to declare from the pulpit, "There is a little too much shouting of 'Viva Gesù!' This constant utterance of sacred words brings them into contempt. Let me have no more of that shouting till the next Festa."

Nevertheless, as the long stream of white-robed youthfulness, adorned with little red crosses and olive wreaths, had gone to the Duomo at dawn to receive communion from Savonarola's hands, it was a sight of beauty. Doubtless, many of those young souls were laying up memories of hope and awe that might save them from ever resting in a merely vulgar view of their work as men and citizens. There is no kind of conscious obedience that does not represent an advance on lawlessness, and these boys became the generation of men who fought greatly and endured greatly in the Republic's final struggle. Now, in the intermediate hours between early communion and dinner, they made their last perambulations to collect alms and vanities, which was why Romola saw the slim white figures moving to and fro about the base of the great pyramid.

"What think you of this folly, Madonna Romola?" a brusque voice asked close to her ear. "Your Piagnoni will make *l'inferno* a pleasant prospect to us if they are to carry things their own way on earth. It is enough to fetch a cudgel over the mountains to see painters, like Lorenzo di Credi and young Baccio there, helping to burn color out of life in this fashion."

"My good Piero," Romola replied, looking up and smiling at the grim man, "even you must be glad to see some of these things burnt. Look at those gewgaws, wigs, and rouge-pots. I have heard you talk as indignantly against them as Fra Girolamo himself."

"What then?" Piero turned on her sharply. "I never said a woman should make a black patch of herself against the background. Va! Madonna Antigone, it is a shame for a woman with your hair and shoulders to run into such nonsense—leave it to women who are not worth painting. What! The most holy Virgin herself has always been dressed well; that is the doctrine of the Church: talk of heresy, indeed! And I should like to know what the excellent Messer Bardo would have said to the burning of the divine poets by these Frati, who are no better an imitation of men than if they were onions with the bulbs uppermost. Look at that Petrarch sticking up beside a rouge-pot: do the idiots pretend that the heavenly Laura was a painted harridan? And Boccaccio, now: do you mean to say, Madonna Romola—you who are fit to be a model for a wise Saint Catherine of Egypt—that you have never read the stories of the immortal Messer Giovanni?"

"It is true I have read them, Piero," Romola said. "Some of them a great many times over, when I was a little girl. I used to get the book down when my father was asleep so that I could read to myself."

"Ebbene?" Piero asked in a fiercely challenging tone.

"There are some things in them I do not want ever to forget," Romola replied. "But you must confess, Piero, that a great many of those stories are only about low deceit for the lowest ends. Men do not want books to make them think lightly of vice, as if life were a vulgar joke. And I cannot blame Fra Girolamo for teaching that we owe our time to something better."

"Yes, yes, it is very well to say so now you've read them," Piero said bitterly, turning on his heel and walking away.

Romola, too, walked on, smiling at Piero's innuendo with a sort of tenderness toward the old painter's anger, knowing that her father would have felt something similar. For herself, she was conscious of no inward collision with the strict and sombre view of pleasure that tended to repress poetry in the attempt to repress vice. Sorrow and joy each possess their peculiar narrowness; and a religious enthusiasm like Savonarola's, which ultimately blesses mankind by giving the soul a strong propulsion toward sympathy with pain, indignation against wrong, and the subjugation of sensual desire, must always incur the reproach of a great negation. Romola's life had granted her an affinity for sadness that inevitably made her unjust toward merriment. That subtle result of culture we call Taste was subdued by the need for a deeper motive; just as the nicer demands of the palate are annihilated by urgent hunger. Moving habitually among scenes of suffering and carrying woman's heaviest disappointment in her heart, the severity that allied itself with self-renouncing beneficent strength found no dissonance within her.

Tessa Abroad and at Home.

Another figure, easily recognized by those who knew the city's shifting tides, approached the Piazza that morning. She wore not the somber black of mourning, but the vibrant red, green, and white of the carnival season. She came from a different direction now; Tessa no longer dwelt on the hill of San Giorgio. Following the turmoil with Baldassarre, Tito had deemed it prudent to relocate her, seeking a new sanctuary within a quiet, airy quarter. Her new home bordered the expansive gardens north of the Porta Santa Croce, a place of relative safety and seclusion.

Tessa had not ventured out for sightseeing without explicit permission. The evening before, Tito had accompanied her, and she had held back a plea swelling in her throat until she saw him in a state of radiant ease. With one arm around the sturdy Lillo and the other resting gently on her shoulder as she steadied the tiny Ninna on her legs, Tito appeared transformed. The weariness that had previously weighed upon his brow and lips had completely melted away. Tessa, quick to learn the subtle languages of her household, had mastered small stratagems to avoid vexing Naldo while securing her own desires. She could read little else, but she had learned to decipher the landscape of her husband's face with surprising accuracy.

Indeed, the charm of that bright, gentle-humored Tito who had awakened beneath the Loggia de' Cerchi five years prior—untainted by deceit—had never returned so fully as in the person of Naldo, seated in the straight-backed, carved armchair provided for his comfort. Tito himself was surprised by the growing sense of relief these moments brought him. No guile was required toward Tessa; she was too innocent and ignorant to suspect him of anything. The little voices calling him "Babbo" were sweet in his ears for the brief duration he heard them. When he considered leaving Florence, he never imagined leaving Tessa and the children behind. He was deeply fond of these round-cheeked, wide-eyed creatures who clung to him, knowing no evil. Wherever affection springs, it is like a green leaf or a blossom—pure and breathing purity, regardless of the soil in which it grows. Poor Romola, with all her self-sacrificing efforts, was inadvertently hardening Tito's nature, chilling it with a positive dislike that had once seemed impossible. Yet, Tessa kept the fountains of kindness flowing.

"Ninna is very good without me now," Tessa began, feeling her request rising high in her throat, and allowed Ninna to sit on the floor. "I can leave her with Monna Lisa anytime, and if she cries in the cradle, Lillo is as sensible as can be—he goes and thumps Monna Lisa."

Lillo, whose great dark eyes appeared even darker against his light-brown curls, jumped off Babbo's knee and immediately demonstrated his intelligence by thumping Monna Lisa, who shook her head slowly over her spinning at the far end of the room. "A wonderful boy!" Tito laughed. "Isn't he?" Tessa asked eagerly, moving slightly closer. "And might I go to see the Carnival tomorrow, just for an hour or two, mightn't I?"

"Oh, you wicked pigeon!" Tito said, pinching her cheek. "Those are your longings, are they? What have you to do with carnivals now that you are an old woman with two children?"

"But old women like to see things," Tessa replied, her lower lip trembling slightly. "Monna Lisa said she would like to go, only she is so deaf she cannot hear what is behind her, and she thinks we could not take care of both the children."

"No, indeed, Tessa," Tito said, his expression turning grave. "You must not think of taking the children into the crowded streets, or I shall be angry."

"But I have never been into the Piazza without leave," Tessa pleaded in a frightened tone, "since the Holy Saturday, and I think Nofri is dead, for you know the poor madre died; and I shall never forget the Carnival I saw once; it was so pretty—all roses and a king and queen under them—and singing. I liked it better than the San Giovanni."

"But there is nothing like that now, my Tessa. They are going to make a bonfire in the Piazza—that is all. But I cannot let you go out by yourself in the evening."

"Oh no, no! I do not want to go in the evening. I only want to see the procession by daylight. There *will* be a procession, is it not true?"

"Yes, after a sort," Tito said. "As lively as a flight of cranes. You must not expect roses and glittering kings and queens, my Tessa. However, I suppose any string of people to be called a procession will please your blue eyes. And there is a thing they have raised in the Piazza de' Signori for the bonfire. You may like to see that. But come home early, and look like a grave little old woman; and if you see any men with feathers and swords, keep out of their way: they are very fierce, and like to cut old women's heads off."

"Santa Madonna! where do they come from? Ah! you are laughing; it is not so bad. But I will keep away from them. Only," Tessa whispered, leaning her lips near Naldo's ear, "if I might take Lillo with me! He is very sensible."

"But who will thump Monna Lisa then, if she doesn't hear?" Tito said, finding it difficult not to laugh, though he maintained a serious demeanor. "No, Tessa, you could not take care of Lillo if you got into a crowd, and he is too heavy for you to carry."

"It is true," Tessa said, rather sadly, "and he likes to run away. I forgot that. Then I will go alone. But now look at Ninna—you have not looked at her enough."

Ninna was a blue-eyed thing at the tottering, tumbling age—a fair solid object that, like a loaded die, found its base with a constancy that warranted prediction. Tessa snatched her up, and when Babbo paid due attention to the recent teeth and other marvels, she whispered, "And shall I buy some confetti for the children?"

Tito drew some small coins from his scarsella and poured them into her palm.

"That will buy no end," Tessa said, delighted at this abundance. "I shall not mind going without Lillo so much, if I bring him something."

Thus, Tessa set out in the morning toward the great Piazza where the bonfire was to be. She did not consider the February breeze cold enough to demand more than her green woolen dress. A mantle would have been oppressive, for it would have hidden a new necklace and a new clasp, mounted with silver—the only ornamental presents Tito had ever made her. Tessa did not think of showing her figure, for no one had ever told her it was pretty; but she was quite sure her necklace and clasp were of the finest sort ever worn by the richest contadina. She arranged her white hood over her head so that the front of her necklace might be well displayed. These ornaments, she considered, must inspire respect for her as the wife of someone who could afford to buy them.

She tripped along cheerily in the February sunshine, thinking much of the purchases for the little ones to fill her small basket, and thinking not at all of anyone who might be observing her. Yet her descent from her upper storey into the street had been watched, and she was kept in sight by a person who had often waited in vain to see if it were Tessa who lived in that house to which he had more than once dogged Tito. Baldassarre was carrying a package of yarn; he was constantly employed in that way as a means of earning his scanty bread and keeping the sacred fire of vengeance alive. He had come out of his way this morning, as he had often done before, to pass by the house to which he had followed Tito in the evening. His long imprisonment had intensified his timid suspicion and his belief in some diabolic fortune favoring Tito, so much so that he had not dared to pursue him except under the cover of a crowd or darkness. He felt, with instinctive horror, that if Tito's eyes fell upon him, he would again be held up to obloquy, again be dragged away; his weapon would be taken, and he cast helpless into a prison cell. His fierce purpose had become as stealthy as a serpent's, depending for its prey on one dart of the fang. Justice was weak and unfriended; he could not hear again the voice that pealed the promise of vengeance in the Duomo. He had been there again and again, but that voice, too, had apparently been stifled by cunning, strong-armed wickedness. For a long while, Baldassarre's ruling thought was to ascertain whether Tito still wore the armor, for now at last his fainting hope would have been contented with a successful stab on this side the grave; but he would never risk his precious knife again. It was a weary time he had had to wait for the chance of answering this question by touching Tito's back in the press of the street. Since then, the knowledge that the sharp steel was useless, and that he had no hope but in some new device, had fallen with leaden weight on his enfeebled mind. A dim vision of winning one of those two wives to aid him came before him continually, and continually slid away. The wife who had lived on the hill was no longer there. If he could find her again, he might grasp some thread of a project and work his way to more clearness.

And this morning he had succeeded. He was quite certain now where this wife lived, and as he walked, bent a little under his burden of yarn, yet keeping the green and white figure in sight, his mind dwelt upon her and her circumstances as feeble eyes dwell on lines and colors, trying to interpret them into consistent significance.

Tessa had to pass through various long streets without seeing any other sign of the Carnival than unusual groups of country people in their best garments, and that disposition in everybody to chat and loiter which marks the early hours of a holiday before the spectacle begins. Presently, in her disappointed search for remarkable objects, her eyes fell on a man with a pedlar's basket before him, who seemed to be selling nothing but little red crosses to all the passengers. A little red cross would be pretty to hang up over her bed; it would also help to keep off harm, and would perhaps make Ninna stronger. Tessa went to the other side of the street to ask the pedlar the price of the crosses, fearing they would cost a little too much for her to spare from her purchase of sweets. The pedlar's back had been turned toward her hitherto, but when she came near him she recognized an old acquaintance of the Mercato, Bratti Ferravecchi. Accustomed to feel that she was to avoid old acquaintances, she turned away again and passed to the other side of the street. But Bratti's eye was too well practiced in looking out at the corner after possible customers for her movement to have escaped him, and she was presently arrested by a tap on the arm from one of the red crosses.

"Young woman," Bratti said as she unwillingly turned her head, "you come from some castello a good way off, it seems to me, else you'd never think of walking about, this blessed Carnival, without a red cross in your hand. Santa Madonna! Four white quattrini is a small price to pay for your soul—prices rise in purgatory, let me tell you."

"Oh, I should like one," Tessa said hastily, "but I couldn't spare four white quattrini."

Bratti had at first regarded Tessa too abstractedly as a mere customer to look at her with any scrutiny, but when she began to speak he exclaimed, "By the head of San Giovanni, it must be the little Tessa, and looking as fresh as a ripe apple! What! you've done none the worse, then, for running away from father Nofri? You were in the right of it, for he goes on crutches now, and a crabbed fellow with crutches is dangerous; he can reach across the house and beat a woman as he sits."

"I'm married," Tessa said rather demurely, remembering Naldo's command that she should behave with gravity; "and my husband takes great care of me."

"Ah, then, you've fallen on your feet! Nofri said you were good-for-nothing vermin; but what then? An ass may bray a good while before he shakes the stars down. I always said you did well to run away, and it isn't often Bratti's in the wrong. Well, and so you've got a husband and plenty of money? Then you'll never think much of giving four white quattrini for a red cross. I get no profit; but what with the famine and the new religion, all other merchandise is gone down. You live in the country where the chestnuts are plenty, eh? You've never wanted for polenta, I can see."

"No, I've never wanted anything," Tessa said, still on her guard.

"Then you can afford to buy a cross. I got a Padre to bless them, and you get blessing and all for four quattrini. It isn't for the profit; I hardly get a danaro by the whole lot. But then they're holy wares, and it's getting harder and harder work to see your way to Paradise: the very Carnival is like Holy Week, and the least you can do to keep the Devil from getting the upper hand is to buy a cross. God guard you! think what the Devil's tooth is! You've seen him biting the man in San Giovanni, I should hope?"

Tessa felt much teased and frightened. "Oh, Bratti," she said with a discomposed face, "I want to buy a great many confetti: I've got little Lillo and Ninna at home. And nice colored sweet things cost a great deal. And they will not like the cross so well, though I know it would be good to have it."

"Come, then," Bratti said, fond of laying up a store of merits by imagining possible extortions and

As Romola spoke of someone expecting her, she referred to her cousin Brigida, yet she could not have guessed how desperately that kindred spirit needed her presence. Walking back toward the Piazza together, they spotted a gathering of youths halting before Tessa. When Romola stepped closer, seeing the simple country girl's distress, she called out, "Wait a moment, cousin." But Monna Brigida, her composure fracturing, replied hastily, "No, I cannot go on. Come with me to Boni's shop; I shall return there."

The truth lay deeper than a mere desire to flee. Monna Brigida was torn between the vanity she wore on her person and a dawning terror that the Piagnoni were correct in their belief that rouge, false hair, and pearl embroidery corrupted the soul. The serious atmosphere of the sect hung over the city like a pervasive odor; nothing tasted or felt as it once had. Here stood her dear cousin Romola, youthful and beautiful, living a life that seemed to demand rigorous sacrifice from a woman. For a widow of fifty-five whose self-worth had long been anchored in her appearance and the admiration of others, maintaining buoyant spirits required immense imagination. Monna Brigida had begun to fight battles at her toilette. Was it truly worth the effort to apply rouge and braid her hair if her soul required such austerity to flourish? Yet, when she gazed into the hand-mirror, she saw a sallow face with sagging cheeks and crow's-feet that no coquettish lip-curl could hide. When she parted her grey hair to lie flat against her temples in the Piagnone style, her courage evaporated. Monna Berta, though only fifty-two, possessed a sharp wit that would surely laugh her to death, calling her an old hag. Every non-Piagnone woman would shrug at her sight, and every man would address her as if she were his grandmother. At fifty-five, she reasoned, she was not ancient—she merely needed a touch of makeup. Thus, the rouge, the braids, and the embroidered berretta remained, and Monna Brigida was content with her familiar reflection. However, today, while preparing for the Piagnone Carnival, the internal war between fear and longing proved too great. She turned back to seek refuge in the druggist's shop rather than face the collectors of the Anathema without Romola beside her. But her retreat was not swift enough. Even before she turned, the white-robed boys trailing behind the crowd that had wheeled toward Tessa had spotted her. Perhaps their eagerness to seize her was fueled by the fact that some of their number had already cornered another figure bearing the heavier burden of the Anathema. Several of these youths were at the tenderest age, undergoing peculiar training. Among them was a small boy of ten, an olive wreath resting above cherubic cheeks and wide brown eyes. His imagination was seized by a hovering awe of existence, sensing that great consequences hinged on every action, yet his longings ran toward mischief and mastery. He was the first to reach Monna Brigida, placing himself squarely in her path.

She felt a surge of anger and scanned for an open door, finding none. To attempt escape now would only worsen her plight. Yet, it was not the cherubic boy who first addressed her, but a youth of fifteen gripping the handle of a wide basket.

"Venerable mother!" he began, his voice ringing with conviction. "The blessed Jesus commands you to surrender the Anathema you carry upon you. That cap embroidered with pearls, those jewels fastening your false hair—give them up and sell them for the poor. Cast the hair itself away from you, for it is a lie fit only for the fire. Doubtless, beneath your silk mantle, you harbor other treasures."

"Yes, lady," chimed in the youth at the opposite handle, who had memorized many of Fra Girolamo's phrases. "They are too heavy for you. They weigh more than a millstone, dragging you toward perdition. Will you adorn yourself with the hunger of the poor and boast of carrying God's curse upon your head?"

"In truth, you are old, *buona madre*," the little boy added in a sweet soprano. "You look frightfully ugly with the red on your cheeks and that black, glistening hair, and those fine things. Only Satan could find pleasure in seeing you. Your Angel is sorrowful; He wishes you to rub away the red."

The small fellow snatched a soft silk scarf from the basket and held it out to Monna Brigida, urging her to use it as her guardian angel desired. Her anger and mortification were rapidly yielding to spiritual alarm. Monna Berta and the cloud of high-society witnesses were nowhere to be seen. Instead, she was surrounded by young monitors whose white robes, wreaths, red crosses, and terrifying candor possessed an awful, unusual power. Her Franciscan confessor, Fra Cristoforo, was absent to bolster her distrust of Dominican teachings, leaving her helplessly shaken by a vague sense that a supreme warning had arrived. With no other course seeming open to her, she accepted the offered scarf and rubbed her cheeks with trembling submission.

"It is well, madonna," said the second youth. "It is a holy beginning. Once you remove those vanities from your head, the dew of heavenly grace will descend upon it." As the infusion of mischief grew stronger, the boy placed his hand on one of the jeweled pins securing her braids to the berretta and drew it out. The heavy black plait fell forward, obscuring Monna Brigida's face and pulling the rest of her headgear down. This became a new reason for haste; she raised her hands quickly, undone the remaining fastenings, and flung her beloved crimson-velvet berretta, with its unsurpassed embroidery of seed-pearls, into the basket of doom. She stood revealed as an unrouged woman, her grey hair pushed back from a face where deep lines of age had triumphed over her former fullness.

Yet, the berretta was not permitted to rest in the basket. With impish zeal, the youngsters lifted it and held it up pitilessly, the false hair dangling limply.

"See, venerable mother," the taller youth declared, "what ugly lies you have delivered yourself from! Now you resemble the blessed Saint Anna, mother of the Holy Virgin."

Thoughts of entering a convent immediately, never again to show her face in the world, rushed through Monna Brigida's mind. There was nothing left for her but to tend to her soul.

Of course, spectators laughed; she did not need to look around to confirm it. Very well, perhaps it would be better to be forced to think more of Paradise. But at the realization that the dear, accustomed world was no longer within her choice, hard tears gathered in her elderly eyes, blurring her vision. She could barely discern a large, rough hand holding a red cross, suddenly thrust before her over the shoulders of the boys, accompanied by a strong, guttural voice.

"Only four quattrini, madonna, blessing and all! Buy it. You will find comfort in it now that your wig is gone. *Deh!* What are we sinners doing all our lives? Making soup in a basket and receiving only the scum for our stomachs. Better buy a blessing, madonna! Only four quattrini; the profit is not even the smell of a coin, and it goes to the poor."

Monna Brigida, her eyes dim with confusion, moved toward the further submission of reaching into her embroidered *scarsella*, currently hidden by her silk mantle, when the group surrounding her—which she had not yet considered escaping—parted before a figure as welcome as an angel releasing prison bolts.

"Romola, look at me!" Monna Brigida cried in a piteous tone, extending both hands.

The white troop was already moving away, with a slight awareness that their zeal regarding the headgear had been superabundant, affording them a dispensation from demanding further penitential offerings.

"Dear cousin, do not be distressed," Romola said, smitten with pity yet hardly able to suppress a smile at the sudden apparition of her kinswoman in a genuine, natural guise, so strangely contrasted with all her memories. She removed the black drapery from her own head and draped it over Monna Brigida's. "There," she continued soothingly, "no one will notice you now. We will turn down the Via del Palagio and go straight home."

They hurried away, Monna Brigida clutching Romola's hand tightly, as if seeking a stronger assurance that her cousin was truly there.

"Ah, my Romola, my dear child!" the short, stout woman cried, hurrying with frequent steps to keep pace with the majestic young figure beside her. "What an old scarecrow I am! I must be good—I mean to be good!"

"Yes, yes; buy a cross!" the guttural voice called out, as the rough hand was thrust once more before Monna Brigida. Bratti was not to be abashed by Romola's presence into renouncing a probable customer; he had quietly followed their retreat. "Only four quattrini, blessing and all—and if there were any profit, it would all go to the poor."

Monna Brigida would have been compelled to pause, even in a less submissive mood. She raised one hand deprecatingly to arrest Romola's remonstrance, while with the other she reached out a *grosso*, worth many white quattrini, saying in an entreating tone, "Take it, good man, and begone."

"You are in the right, madonna," Bratti said, snatching the coin quickly and thrusting the cross into her hand. "I will not offer you change, for I might as well rob you of a mass. What! We must all be scorched a little, but you will come off the easier; better fall from the window than the roof. A good Easter and a good year to you!"

"Well, Romola," Monna Brigida cried pathetically as Bratti left them, "if I am to be a Piagnone, it matters not how I look!"

"Dear cousin," Romola said, smiling at her affectionately, "you do not know how much better you look than you ever did before. I see now how good-natured your face is, like yourself. That red and finery seemed to thrust themselves forward and hide your expression. Ask our Piero or any other painter if he would not rather paint your portrait now than before. I think all lines of the human face possess something either touching or grand, unless they seem to spring from low passions. How fine old men are, like my godfather! Why should not old women look grand and simple?"

"Yes, when one reaches sixty, my Romola," Brigida replied, relapsing slightly. "But I am only fifty-five, and Monna Berta, and everyone else... but it is no use: I will be good, like you. Your mother, had she lived, would have been as old as I am; we were cousins together. One *must* either die or grow old. But it does not matter about being old, if one is a Piagnone."

To Romola, the chaotic whirlwind of the Carnival seemed to leave no lasting mark upon her life save a singular, heavy duty: the tender, exhausting care of her cousin Brigida, whose spirit wavered between resignation and the frailty of age. Yet, beneath this domestic burden, a Lenten season had begun, one that demanded of her a feverish intensity of mind and a relentless physical exertion.

Bernardo del Nero had been elected Gonfaloniere. Through immense effort, the Medicean faction had secured a victory that now cast a long shadow over Romola's soul. She felt a gnawing presentiment that this triumph was merely the prologue to a secret scheme, poised to either flourish into success or curdle into betrayal within the next two months of her godfather's authority. Each morning, as the pale dawn pierced her curtains, that dread returned, cold and insistent. It accompanied her as she navigated the crowded streets toward the early sermon in the Duomo, a chilling companion until she stepped inside the great cathedral. There, amidst the clash of voices and the fervor of the crowd, the fear dissolved, much as the terror of death vanishes in the heat of battle.

Within the Duomo, Romola felt herself swept into a struggle of passion that transcended the walls of Florence itself. Savonarola was preaching—the final series of Lenten sermons he would ever be permitted to deliver before the threat of exclamation loomed. He knew his end was near, yet he preached with a defiant confidence, holding up the rot of the Church in a mirror of unflinching truth. He spoke of renovation, of a coming revolt against corruption that would shake the very foundations of Christendom. His vision oscillated wildly: at times, he saw himself as the glorious voice of a new age, resurrecting the dead body of the Church; at others, he saw only the path to martyrdom, viewing this life as a mere vigil before the true dawn.

Such a crisis possessed a terrifying gravity for all but the dull-minded, even for Machiavelli, who viewed the Friar through a lens of cynical pragmatism. But for Romola, whose own ardour mirrored Savonarola's, the stakes felt personal. The crisis blended with her daily labours, demanding not just perseverance but a new, fierce courage. Famine still stalked Florence, and the long endurance of distress had hardened the hearts of the people; disease festered in the crowded quarters, and the Plague was whispered to be at the gates. As Romola walked, weary and heavy-hearted, among the sick, the hungry, and the murmuring masses, she found solace not in pity alone, but in the belief that she served a heroic struggle for sublime ends. Her pity was but the dew that prepares the soil for a future harvest, however unseen.

Yet, the mighty music that stirred her in the Duomo was not without dissonance. Since the early days of glowing hope, when the Frate had preached peace and the coming of the French deliverer, the atmosphere had shifted. Political intrigue proved too stubborn for oblivion; the dream of French salvation had crumbled as the King turned his back on his mission; and hostility, both petty and grand, now assailed the Prophet with renewed ferocity.

Consequently, the spirit of contention seeped into his sermons. Urged by popular demand, Savonarola began to rely increasingly on visions and private revelations, adopting a tone of defiant challenge against his objectors. Having once denounced the desire for miracles, he now claimed that Divine power would soon attest his truth through a sign. In the rapid, excited transitions of his speech, as the vision of triumph receded behind the reality of evil, his threats against vicious tyrants and corrupt priests gathered momentum, fueled by personal exasperation as much as indignant zeal.

In the career of a great orator yielding to the moment's inspiration, the hidden conflict of selfish and unselfish emotion is laid bare in letters of fire. While these tones of exasperation jarred Romola, they resonated with a thrilling bass note for another member of the congregation: Baldassarre. He had discovered the Frate was preaching again and attended whenever possible, drinking in the threats of a voice that seemed to champion justice. He watched Romola closely, waiting for the right moment to seek an interview with her, noting how she too sought the Frate's gaze. Twice she had caught sight of his dark, fixed stare in the nave. She wished to avoid it, yet she looked for it, like a superstitious peasant awaiting a portent. Any revelation concerning this old man was now a secondary fear; her anxiety was consumed by the pressing present.

The stirring Lent passed, and April, the final month of her godfather's supreme authority, drew to a close. Nothing had occurred to fulfill her presentiment. Rumors from Rome spoke of menacing activity by Piero de' Medici, but soon the feared Bernardo would depart from power.

It was on the twenty-seventh, as Romola walked out on her afternoon errands of mercy, that a messenger from Camilla Rucellai intercepted her. Camilla, one of Florence's chief feminine seers, demanded Romola's presence immediately on matters of the highest moment. Romola shrank with unconquerable repulsion from the shrill volubility of such "illuminated" women, and her recent knowledge that Camilla had announced revelations hostile to Bernardo del Nero made her instinctively recoil. She was tempted to refuse flatly, to close her ears to knowledge that would only weigh down her spirit. But habit had trained her to reject impulsive cowardice; reproving herself for letting presentiments dictate her actions, she complied and went straight to Camilla.

She found the nervous, grey-haired woman in a chamber arranged like a convent cell. Thin fingers clutched at Romola as she sat, and the eager, loud whisper caused a physical shrinking that made it difficult for her to remain seated.

Camilla had a vision to communicate—a revelation from Romola's Angel stating that Romola knew certain secrets concerning her godfather, Bernardo del Nero, which, if disclosed, could save the Republic. Camilla's voice rose in pitch as she narrated, ending with an exhortation to obey the Angel's command and separate herself from the enemy of God.

Romola's impetuosity was that of a massive nature; usually calm and self-controlled, she possessed a constitutional disgust for the shallow excitability of women like Camilla, whose faculties seemed wrought entirely into fantasy. Before the exhortation concluded, Romola sprang up, attempting to wrench her arm from Camilla's tightening grasp. It was useless. The prophetess held on like a crab, only incited to greater fervor by the resistance, and launched into a shrill declaration of corroborating visions. Christ himself had appeared, ordering her to send commands to citizens in office to throw Bernardo del Nero from the window of the Palazzo Vecchio. Even Fra Girolamo knew of it and had dared not deny its divine authority this time.

"And since then," Camilla cried in her excited treble, straining upward with wild eyes toward Romola's face, "the Blessed Infant has come to me and laid a wafer of sweetness on my tongue in token of his pleasure that I had done his will."

"Let me go!" Romola said, her voice deep with anger. "God grant you are mad! Else you are detestably wicked!"

The violence of her effort finally broke Camilla's hold. Romola wrenched free and rushed out of the room, not pausing until she had hurried far down the street, finding herself close to the church of the Badia. She slipped behind the curtain under the old stone arch, entering a sanctuary shut in from the noise and hurry of the street, where every object suggested the thought of eternal peace amidst turmoil.

She sank down on the step of the altar before Filippino Lippi's serene Virgin appearing to Saint Bernard, waiting for the inward tumult to subside. The thought that pressed most acutely was that Camilla could allege Savonarola's countenance of her wicked folly. Romola did not for a moment believe he had sanctioned the throwing of Bernardo from the window as a Divine suggestion; she felt certain there was falsehood or mistake in that allegation. Savonarola had become more severe in his views on resisting malcontents, yet strict law and order were fundamental to his political teaching. Still, since he knew the fatal effects of such visions, and since he distrusted spirit-seeing women, why did he not publicly denounce these pretended revelations that brought darkness instead of light?

The answer came with painful clarity: he was fettered inwardly by the consciousness that such revelations were not distinctly separable from his own visions; he was fettered outwardly by the foreseen consequence of raising a cry against himself, even among his own party, as one who would suppress all Divine inspiration of which he himself was not the sole vehicle.

Kneeling with her face buried on the altar-step, Romola endured one of those sickening moments when the enthusiasm that had made life worthy seemed inevitably bound up with vain dreams and wilful eye-shutting. Her mind rushed back with new attraction toward the strong worldly sense, the dignified prudence, and the untheoretic virtues of her godfather. He was to be treated as a sort of Agag because he believed a restricted form of government was better than the Great Council, and because he would not pretend to forget old ties to the banished family.

With this thought rose the presentiment of a plot to restore the Medici, and then again she felt the popular party was half-justified in its fierce suspicion. She felt that keeping the Government of Florence pure was a sacred cause; the Frate was right there, and had carried her understanding irrevocably with him. But at this moment, the assent of her understanding went alone; it was given unwillingly. Her heart recoiled from a right allied to so much narrowness, a right entailing a hard, systematic judgment of men that measured them by superficial symbols rather than the manhood within. Her affection and respect clung with new tenacity to her godfather, and with him to those memories of her father, which stood in opposition to the division of men into sheep and goats by the easy mark of some political or religious symbol.

After all has been said about the widening influence of ideas, it remains true that they would hardly be such strong agents unless taken in a solvent of feeling. The great world-struggle of developing thought is continually foreshadowed in the struggle of the affections, seeking a justification for love and hope.

If Romola's intellect had been less capable of discerning the complexities in human things, all the early loving associations of her life would have forbidden her to accept implicitly the denunciatory exclusiveness of Savonarola. She had simply felt that his mind suggested deeper and more efficacious truth than any other, and the large breathing-room she found in his grand view of human duties had made her patient toward that part of his teaching she could not absorb, so long as its practical effect collided with no strong force in her. But now a sudden insurrection of feeling had brought about that collision. Her indignation, once roused by Camilla's visions, could not pause there; it ran like an illuminating fire over all the kindred facts in Savonarola's teaching, and for the moment she felt what was true in the scornful sarcasms flung against him more keenly than she felt what was false.

But it was an illumination that made all life look ghastly to her. Where were the beings to whom she could cling, with whom she could work and endure, believing she was working for the right? On the side from which moral energy came lay a fanaticism from which she was shrinking with newly-startled repulsion; on the side to which she was drawn by affection and memory, there was the presentiment of secret plotting, which her judgment told her would not be unfairly called crime. And surmounting every other thought was the dread inspired by Tito's hints, lest that presentiment should be converted into knowledge, tearing her by irreconcilable claims.

Calmness would not come even on the altar-steps; it would not come from looking at the serene picture where the saint, writing in rocky solitude, was visited by faces with celestial peace. Romola was in the hard press of human difficulties, and that solitude was too far off. She rose from her knees to hasten to her sick people in the courtyard, hoping to revive that sense of worth in life which, at this moment, was unfed by any wider faith. But when she turned round, she found herself face to face with a man standing only two yards away. The man was Baldassarre.

"I desire to speak with you," Baldassarre said, his voice cutting through the silence as Romola regarded him with a look of quiet, suspended expectation. It was evident he had trailed her, waiting patiently for this moment. She was finally prepared to confront the secret that hung over him.

"Yes," she replied, offering a submission that mirrored the tone of one accepting a penance imposed from above. "Yet you wish to withdraw where no ear can catch our words?"

"Where *he* will not stumble upon us," Baldassarre corrected, glancing nervously over his shoulder. "Out—into the open air—far from the noise of the streets."

"I occasionally visit San Miniato at this hour," Romola said. "If you will, I shall go forthwith, and you may follow. The distance is considerable, yet it guarantees solitude."

He nodded, and Romola set out. To lesser minds, venturing to such a secluded spot with a man bearing the outward marks of the madness Tito had attributed to him might have seemed a perilous gamble. But Romola possessed no such personal terrors; rather, she welcomed the separation, viewing it as a necessary buffer before the next blow could fall upon her spirit. The afternoon had deepened, the sun dipping low in the western sky, when she halted on rough terrain beneath the shadow of towering cypress trunks, scanning for Baldassarre. He was not distant, yet when he joined her, he collapsed onto the edge of a stony bank. His thickset frame, once robust and formidable when he first appeared in the Duomo with the rope around his neck, now lacked its former vigor. The exertion of climbing the hill had left his eyes with a helpless, vacant vagueness.

"The ascent is steep," Romola said, her voice soft with compassion as she sat beside him. "And I fear you have been sapped by hunger?"

He turned his head, fixing his gaze upon her in silence. When the moment demanded speech, he seemed unable to seize the words required to convey his thought. She remained as still as possible, lest he interpret her stillness as impatience. In this light, he appeared no higher than a common, neglected old man. Yet Romola was accustomed to the proximity of such suffering souls and pondered their troubles deeply. Slowly, a definite expression returned to his eyes, and with abrupt emphasis, he spoke:

"Ah! You would have been my daughter!"

A swift flush colored Romola's cheeks and faded just as quickly, leaving her lips parted in a pale, marble-like horror. The revelation struck her mind with the force of a physical blow. She divined the truths lying behind that single word, and in that first instant, the impulsive belief born of her keen experience with Tito's nature could not be checked. Her sensitive reaction served as a stimulus to Baldassarre; for the first time, his words achieved their intended effect. He proceeded with growing eagerness and firmness, laying his hand upon her arm.

"You are of proud blood—is it not so? You attend the preacher; you despise baseness—the baseness that smiles and triumphs. You hate your husband?"

"Oh God! Were you truly his father?" Romola whispered, her voice low, entirely consumed by the ghosts of the past and unable to register Baldassarre's question. "Or was it as he claimed? Did you take him when he was small?"

"Ah, you believe me—you know the truth of what he is!" Baldassarre exclaimed, tightening his grip on her arm as if the contact alone restored him power. "Will you aid me?"

"Yes," Romola answered, failing to interpret his words as he intended. She placed her palm gently over the rough hand clutching her arm, and tears welled in her eyes as she looked at him. "Oh, it is pitiful! Tell me—you were a great scholar; you taught him. How is this?"

She broke off. Tito's accusation of this man's insanity flashed through her mind, and where were the signs of past refinement? Yet she maintained her self-command, refusing to withdraw her hand. She sat perfectly still, waiting to listen with renewed caution.

"It is gone!—it is all gone!" Baldassarre cried. "They would not believe me because he lied, claiming I was mad, and they had me dragged to prison. I am old; my mind will not return. And the world stands against me."

He paused, his eyes sinking as if submerged under a wave of despondency. Then he looked up again, his voice renewing its eagerness. "But *you* are not against me. He made you love him, and he has proven false to you; you hate him. Yes, he made *me* love him: he was beautiful and gentle, while I was a lonely man. I took him when they were beating him. He slept in my bosom when he was little, and I watched him grow, giving him all my knowledge, everything I meant to be his. I possessed much—money, books, gems. He had my gems—he sold them; and he left me in slavery. He never sought me, and when I returned poor and in misery, he denied me. He called me a madman."

"He told us his father was dead—drowned," Romola murmured faintly. "Surely he must have believed it then. Oh! He could not have been so base *then*!"

A vision rose in her mind of what Tito represented to her in those early days when she thought no more of wrong in him than a child thinks of poison in flowers. The yearning regret inherent in that memory brought a fleeting relief from the tension of horror. With one great sob, tears rushed forth.

"Ah, you are young, and tears flow easily," Baldassarre said, with a hint of impatience. "But tears serve no purpose; they extinguish the fire within, and it is the fire that acts. Tears will hinder us. Listen to me."

Romola turned toward him with a slight start. Once more, the possibility of his madness darted through her mind, checking the rush of belief. If, after all, this man were merely a mad assassin? Yet her deep conviction in his story remained, and it was more out of sympathy than fear that she avoided risking pain to him by showing doubt.

"Tell me," she said as gently as possible, "how did you lose your memory—your scholarship?"

"I fell ill. I cannot say how long—it was a blank. I remember nothing, only that eventually I was sitting in the sun among the stones, and everything else was darkness. And slowly, by degrees, I felt something besides that: a longing for something—I knew not what—that never came. And when I was on the ship upon the waters, I began to know what I longed for; it was for the Boy to return—to find all my thoughts again, for I was locked away outside them all. And I am outside now. I feel nothing but a wall and darkness."

Baldassarre had become dreamy again, sinking into silence with his head resting between his hands. Once more, Romola's belief in him submerged all cautioning doubts. The pity with which she dwelt on his words seemed like the revival of an old pang. Had she not daily witnessed how her own father missed Dino and the future he had dreamed of in that son?

"It all returned once," Baldassarre continued presently. "I was master of everything. I saw the whole world again, my gems, my books; and I thought I held him in my power, so I went to expose him where—the lights shone and the trees stood. He lied again, saying I was mad, and they dragged me away to prison... Wickedness is strong; and he wears armor."

Fierceness flamed up within him again. He spoke with his former intensity, grasping Romola's arm once more.

"But you will help me? He has been false to you too. He has another wife, and she bears children. He makes her believe he is her husband, and she is a foolish, helpless creature. I will show you where she lives."

The first shock that passed through Romola was visibly one of anger. The woman's sense of indignity was inevitably paramount. Baldassarre instinctively felt her standing in sympathy with him.

"You hate him," he pressed on. "Is it not true? There is no love between you; I know that. I know women can hate; and you have proud blood. You hate falseness, and you can love revenge."

Romola sat paralyzed by the shock of conflicting emotions. She was unaware of the grasp that was bruising her tender arm.

"You shall contrive it," Baldassarre said presently, in an eager whisper. "I have committed to memory that you are his rightful wife. You are a noble woman. You go to hear the preacher of vengeance; you will aid justice. But you must think for me. My mind fails—everything goes sometimes—all but the fire. The fire is God: it is justice: it will not die. You believe that—is it not so? If they will not hang him for robbing me, you will take away his armor—you will make him go without it, and I will stab him. I possess a knife, and my arm is still strong enough."

He slid his hand beneath his tunic and drew out the hidden blade, feeling the edge abstractedly, as if he needed the sensation to keep his ideas alive.

To Romola, it seemed as if every fresh hour of her life was destined to become more difficult than the last. Her judgment was too vigorous and rapid for her to fall into the error of using futile deprecating words to a man in Baldassarre's state of mind. She chose not to answer his final speech. Instead, she would win time for his excitement to subside by asking something else she cared to know. She spoke rather tremulously—

"You say she is foolish and helpless—that other wife—and believes him to be her true husband. Perhaps he is: perhaps he married her before he married me."

"I cannot tell," Baldassarre replied, pausing in his action of feeling the knife, looking bewildered. "I remember no more. I only know where she lives. You shall see her. I will take you; but not now," he added hurriedly, "_he_ may be there. The night is approaching."

"It is true," Romola said, starting up with a sudden realization that the sun had set and the hills were darkening; "but you will come and take me—when?"

"In the morning," Baldassarre said, dreaming that she, too, wished to hasten to her vengeance.

"Come to me, then, where you came to me today, in the church. I will be there at ten; and if you are not there, I will go again towards mid-day. Can you remember?"

"Mid-day," Baldassarre repeated—"only mid-day. The same place, and mid-day. And, after that," he added, rising and grasping her arm again with his left hand while holding the knife in his right, "we will have our revenge. He shall feel the sharp edge of justice. The world is against me, but you will help me."

"I would help you in other ways," Romola said, making a first, timid effort to dispel his illusion regarding her. "I fear you are in want; you must labor and earn little. I should like to bring you comforts, and make you feel again that there is someone who cares for you."

"Speak no more of that," Baldassarre said fiercely. "I will accept nothing else. Help me to wring one drop of vengeance on this side of the grave. I have nothing but my knife. It is sharp; but there is a moment after the thrust when men see the face of death—and it shall be my face that he will see."

He released his hold and sank down again into a sitting posture. Romola felt helpless; she must defer all intentions until the morrow.

"Mid-day, then," she said in a distinct voice.

"Yes," he answered, with an air of exhaustion. "Go; I will rest here."

She hastened away. Turning at the last spot from whence he was likely to be visible, she saw him seated still.

A singular purpose drove Romola forward, a resolve that had coalesced from the scattered anxieties of the afternoon into a single, urgent necessity. Ignoring the encroaching dusk and refusing the slow, cautious protection of Maso, she crossed the bridge where the river churned black beneath the fading crimson of the sky. She took the shortest path to the Old Palace, accepting the risk of encountering her husband as a trivial price to pay for the need to speak. She did not calculate the odds; she simply had to unload the weight pressing upon her soul. Whether she passed unnoticed through the pillared court or climbed the broad stairs remained a blur; her only certainty was the desperate urgency of her request to an usher. She gave her name, demanding a private audience with the Gonfaloniere.

She was not long left alone amidst the silent frescoes and the flickering new candles. The door swung open, and Bernardo del Nero entered, his white hair held high and proud above his silk lucco.

"Romola, my child, what is this?" he asked, his voice tight with anxious surprise as he shut the door against the corridor.

She uncovered her head and moved toward him in silence. He placed a hand on her shoulder, holding her at a respectful distance to study her features. Her face was ravaged by exhaustion and prolonged turmoil, her hair tumbling in disarray, yet her eyes burned with a fierce excitement that seemed to have conquered her physical frailty.

"What has he done?" Bernardo demanded abruptly. "Speak, child; cast aside your pride. I am your father."

"It is not about myself—nothing concerns me personally," Romola replied quickly. "Dearest godfather, it concerns you. I have heard whispers—some I dare not utter. But you are in peril within these walls; you are in danger everywhere. Fanatics seek to harm you, and there are traitors among you. Trust no one. If you place your trust, you will be betrayed."

Bernardo smiled faintly.

"Have you driven yourself into such a frenzy, my poor child," he said, lifting a hand to stroke her hair gently, "to repeat such ancient truths to an old man like me?"

"Oh, no, no! These are not old truths," Romola insisted, clutching her hands together with painful force as if to stifle the words she could not yet speak. "These are fresh horrors I know, but cannot reveal. Dearest godfather, you know I am not foolish. I would not disturb you without cause. Is it too late to warn you against anyone, every single one who appears to serve your interests? Is it too late to say, 'Retreat to your villa and hide in the countryside once these final three days of office end'? Oh God! Perhaps it is too late! And if harm comes to you, it will feel as though I have caused it!"

The final words tore from her involuntarily, a long-suppressed emotion finding spasmodic release. Yet, she was startled by her own outburst and fell silent.

"I mean," she added, her voice trembling, "I know nothing definite. I only know what fills me with dread."

"Poor child!" Bernardo said, regarding her with quiet intensity for a moment. Then he spoke softly, "Go, Romola—return home and rest. These fears may merely be the monstrous shadows of something small and harmless. Even traitors require motive; the rats run only where they smell the cheese, and we do not yet know which direction the scent drifts."

He paused, turning his gaze away with an air of abstract resignation, until, with a slow shrug, he continued,

"As for warnings, they avail me nothing, child. I enter no conspiracies, yet I never abandon my colors. If I march alongside obstinate men who rush toward guns and pikes, I must share the consequences. Let us say no more of that. I have not many years left in my sack for them to steal. Go, child; return home and rest."

He touched her head again with affection, and she could not resist clinging to his arm, pressing her forehead against his shoulder. His caress felt like the final remnant of that youthful filial bond, which now appeared so bright to her even in its distress, for those troubles were untainted by malice.

"Is silence best, my Romola?" the old man asked.

"Yes, now; but I cannot tell if it will always be so," she answered hesitantly, lifting her head with a pleading gaze.

"Well, you have a father's ear while I draw breath," he said, lifting the black drapery to fold it tenderly around her head, adding, "and a father's home; remember that." Opening the door, he urged, "Hasten away. You look like a black ghost; you will find safety enough."

That night, when Romola finally succumbed to sleep, it was deep and profound. Agitation had reached its limit; she needed to gather strength before she could endure further suffering. In defiance of her rigid habits, she slept well past sunrise.

Her awakening came to the sound of gunfire. Piero de' Medici, backed by thirteen hundred men, stood before the gate facing Rome.
