Romola learned this from Maso, accompanied by many circumstantial details of dubious reliability. A countryman had arrived before dawn to alarm the Signoria; otherwise, the city would have fallen by surprise. His master was absent, having been summoned to the Palazzo long ago. Romola sent the old man out again to gather news while she ascended to the loggia repeatedly, straining to discern any sign of the dreaded entry or its repulsion. Maso reported that the great Piazza teemed with armed men and that many leading citizens, suspected of favoring the Medici, had been summoned and detained. Some of the populace seemed indifferent to Piero's arrival, with rumors even suggesting the Signoria itself had invited him; yet, regardless of the truth, they offered him a grim welcome, and soldiers from Pisa were moving against him.

In her recollection of those morning hours, few external events stood out clearly above the tumultuous waves of retrospect and anticipation. She knew she had walked to the Badia at the appointed time despite the street alarms, and she knew she had waited there in vain. However, the scene she witnessed upon leaving the church, standing on the steps while the doors closed behind her for the afternoon recess, returned to her with the vivid clarity of a remembered dream.

There was a distinct shift in the faces and tones of the armed and unarmed crowds pausing or hurrying through the streets. The guns fired again, but this time the sound provoked only laughter. She soon understood the cause of the change. Piero de' Medici and his horsemen had turned their backs on Florence, galloping as fast as possible along the road to Siena. She learned this from a substantial shopkeeper, a Piagnone who had not yet laid down his pike.

"It is true," he concluded, with a bitter emphasis. "Piero is gone, but those left behind who were privy to his coming remain—we all know that; and if the new Signoria fulfills its duty, we shall soon know who they are."

The words struck Romola like a sharp spasm, but the evil they foreshadowed was not yet immediate. As she entered her home once more, her most pressing anxiety shifted to the possibility that she had lost sight of Baldassarre for a long while.

The sun stretched its golden fingers across the days, elongating them without delivering the answer Romola craved or the terror she feared. There was no word from Baldassarre, and despite the Government's vigilant eyes, the shadow of the suspected conspiracy remained unbroken. Yet, in this suspended silence, other forces surged forward, pressing close against her heart and weaving a bridge between the phantom-laden corridors of her anxiety and the tangible trials of the present. These forces were the spreading Plague and the thunderous Excommunication of Savonarola.

Both calamities served to halt her drifting away from the Frate, anchoring her once more to the man who had unveiled for her a life of sacred duty. Now, as he appeared to falter in his struggle against the tide of profligacy, Romola found she could not carry the sublime, ecstatic joy of her own existence into the hallowed halls of pestilence and suffering without acknowledging the debt she owed to Fra Girolamo. How could she witness the silencing of a soul whose greatness lay not in heresy or superstition, but in the fierce energy with which he forged the Christian life into reality, without feeling pulled irresistibly to his defense?

Deep into the scorching days of June, the decree from Rome arrived and was solemnly proclaimed within the vast expanse of the Duomo. Romola attended, seeking to fortify the sympathy she felt for Savonarola, a wellspring of her own strength. The scene stood in stark, memorable contrast to the vibrant gatherings she had known before. Gone were the upturned faces of citizens, rising in amphitheater-like tiers toward the walls to form a garland of hope around the elders; gone was the mighty voice that thrilled the heart with visions of visible and invisible struggles. Instead, the evening air grew heavy, the bare walls darkened by the flickering glow of tapers. A black and grey flock of monks and secular clergy stood with bent, expectant faces, listening to the occasional tinkling of small bells interrupting a monotonous voice that read a sentence already hanging in the air for weeks. Finally, the lights died, leaving only the slow, shuffling tread of monkish feet retreating into a dim, suffocating silence.

Romola's fervor for the Frate was doubled by the gleeful triumph etched onto the hard, coarse faces of the crowd, and by the fear-stricken confusion visible in the expressions of many of his staunchest allies. The boundary between the duty of obedience and the right of resistance was never easy to define, but here it was rendered overwhelmingly difficult by a conviction that the Church was not merely a political compromise, but a living organism infused with divine power to bless and to curse. For most pious Florentines, this belief was not a chosen opinion but an inalienable impression, as undeniable as the curve of the sky. Thus, Savonarola's bold arguments—that the Excommunication was unjust and therefore void—did not reassure them; instead, they trembled more deeply, viewing his defiance as a sacrilege cast against a mystic image whose power knew no defense.

Romola, however, had never drawn her early nourishment from the traditional currents of the Christian community where her father had lived apart. Her relation to the Church flowed solely through Savonarola; his moral force was the only authority she had ever bowed to. In his excommunication, she saw only the menace of hostile vice. On one side stood a man devoted to public virtue and spiritual purity; on the other, the assault of alarmed selfishness, led by a lustful, greedy, lying, and murderous old man—Rodrigo Borgia, now elevated to infamy as Pope Alexander the Sixth. The finer shades of truth that soften such stark antitheses are rarely seen by those not distressed by the folly of martyrs or the prudence of their burners. But Romola needed a strength that neutrality could not provide. This Excommunication, by simplifying and ennobling Savonarola's resistance, seemed to offer her a rescue from the threatening isolation of doubt. The Frate was no longer trapped in a petty antagonism with Florentine enemies; he stood simply as a standard-bearer leaping into the breach against a vicious exercise of ecclesiastical power. Life seemed clearest when the heart beat faster at the sight of such generous self-sacrifice. In that moment, there was no doubt of the highest prize the soul could win, and a renewed current of enthusiasm carried her through these difficult summer days.

She had said nothing to Tito regarding her recent interview with Baldassarre or the revelation he had made. What agitating words could she possibly extract from him? Only a cool sarcasm about her sympathy with his assassin, or perhaps nothing at all. Baldassarre was evidently helpless; the true danger was not that he would harm Tito, but that Tito, tracking him down, might devise a new scheme to rid himself of the injured man who haunted his conscience. Romola felt she could take no decisive action until she saw Baldassarre again and learned the full truth about that "other wife"—whether she was the woman to whom Tito was first bound.

The possibilities surrounding that other wife, which struck the deepest wound to her hereditary pride, mingled with the earliest memories of her illusory love. These suspicions ate away at the lingering tenderness of her past with her husband, shrinking her from Tito with a horror so profound it might have spurred a passionate outburst had he not been unusually absent from home. Like many wealthy citizens during the plague, Tito spent his leisure hours in the countryside. The agreeable Melema was welcomed at many villas, and since Romola had refused to leave the city, he had no need to maintain a country residence of his own.

Finally, in the later days of July, the alleviation of these public troubles left Romola to face the raw weight of her personal lot. The Plague had nearly vanished, and Savonarola's position had grown hopeful thanks to a favorable magistracy writing urgent vindicatory letters to Rome, entreating the withdrawal of the Excommunication. Romola's healthy frame, exhausted by continuous excitement and over-exertion, began to succumb to the inevitable languor of reaction. Yet, her mental restlessness refused to let her remain idle. During the sultry hours, she stayed indoors, but in the cool of the mornings and evenings, she walked constantly, varying her direction with a vague, desperate hope. If Baldassarre still lived, perhaps she might encounter him. Maybe some illness had paralyzed his memory, causing him to forget where she lived, or even her existence entirely. That was her most sanguine explanation for his silence. But the explanation she felt was most probable was far sadder: that he had died of the Plague.

The morning sun had grown heavy and oppressive to Romola by the time she turned from the walls of San Marco back into the intersecting lanes near Santa Croce. The Borgo La Croce lay in a profound, sun-drenched silence, so still that she could hear the rhythm of her own footsteps on the pavement. As she rounded a bend, a small figure emerged from the glare—a toddler, no older than three, clad only in a white shirt. He paused mid-waddle, his back square and sturdy beneath a cloud of reddish-brown curls.

When he turned, Romola saw dark eyes wide with tears and a lower lip trembling as he clutched his shirt in helpless fists. A tall black figure casting a shadow over him seemed to trigger a climax of bewildered fear, sending a loud sob rolling from his throat.

Acting on a maternal instinct that stirred deep within her, Romola uncovered her head, stooped low, and wrapped her arms around the child, pressing her cheek against his. At first, his cries grew louder, but he made no attempt to flee. Soon, the outburst ceased with the strange abruptness characteristic of childhood emotions; his face smoothed out, fixed in an open-mouthed stare at her.

"You have lost yourself, little one," she said softly, kissing him. "Never mind! We will find the house again. Perhaps your mother will meet us."

She sensed he had escaped while his mother's attention was elsewhere and believed he would soon be pursued.

"Oh, what a heavy, heavy boy!" she murmured, attempting to lift him. "I cannot carry you. Come, then, you must toddle back by my side."

The parted lips remained motionless in awed silence, one brown fist still gripping the shirt with tenacity, while the other yielded willingly to her soft, strong hand.

"Do you have a mother?" Romola asked, looking down at the boy with a yearning she could not suppress. He offered no answer. A girl might have chirped, but this square-shouldered little man with his big cloud of curls remained mute.

He was, however, keenly aware of his destination. At the turn by San Ambrogio, he dragged Romola toward it, looking up at her.

"Ah, that is the way home, is it?" she smiled. He merely thrust his head forward and pulled, urging her to hurry.

After another turn, Romola found herself in a short street opening onto garden grounds. The boy paused before a house at the end, pulling her toward stone stairs. He showed no desire to release her hand, and she refused to leave him without ensuring he was delivered to his guardians. They ascended the stairs, emerging from the sunlight into dimness, until a stream of light spilled from an open doorway. Passing through a small lobby, they entered another room where Romola paused. No sound greeted her arrival.

At the far end of the room, opposite the light, sat Tessa with one hand on the edge of a cradle, her head drooping slightly as she slept. Near a window, with her back to the door, Monna Lisa sat preparing salad in a deaf, unconscious state. Romola had only an instant to take in the stillness before Lillo snatched his hand away and ran to his mother's side, leaning his head back against her arm to survey Romola seriously from a distance.

As Lillo pushed against her, Tessa opened her eyes in bewilderment. But her gaze rested on the figure at the doorway, and she started up, blushing deeply and trembling, unable to speak or move forward.

"Ah! We have seen each other before," Romola said, smiling and stepping forward. "I am glad it was your little boy. He was crying in the street; I suppose he had run away. So we walked together a little way, and then he knew where he was and brought me here. But you had not missed him? That is well, else you would have been frightened."

The shock of discovering Lillo had wandered away overwhelmed Tessa. Her color drained, and seizing the boy's arm, she ran to Monna Lisa, whispering loudly in the old woman's ear with a half-sob:

"Oh, Lisa, you are wicked! Why did you stand with your back to the door? Lillo ran away ever so far into the street."

"Holy Mother!" Monna Lisa exclaimed in her meek, thick tone, dropping her spoon. "Where were *you*, then? I thought you were there, watching him."

"But you *know* I go to sleep when I rock," Tessa retorted pettishly.

"Well, well, we must keep the outer door shut, or tie him up," Monna Lisa advised. "For he'll be as cunning as Satan before long, and that's the holy truth. But how did he come back?"

This question brought Tessa back to the present. Without answering, she turned toward Romola, blushing and timid again, while Monna Lisa's eyes followed her. The old woman made a low reverence and said, "Doubtless the most noble lady brought him back." Advancing slightly, she added, "It is my shame that he was found with only his shirt on; but he kicked and wouldn't wear his other clothes this morning, and the mother, poor thing, will never hear of him being beaten. But what can an old woman do without a stick when the lad's legs get so strong? Let your nobleness look at his legs."

Lillo, conscious his legs were being inspected, pulled his shirt up higher and looked down at their olive roundness with a dispassionate curiosity. Romola laughed, stooping to give him a caressing shake and a kiss. This action helped reassure Tessa, who had already begun to feel relief from Monna Lisa's respectful address. For when Naldo had been told of the adventure at the Carnival, and Tessa had asked who the heavenly lady who appeared just when needed might be—perhaps the Holy Madonna herself?—he had replied, "Not exactly, my Tessa; only one of the saints," choosing not to elaborate. Thus, in Tessa's dreamlike combination of small experiences, Romola remained confusedly associated with church pictures. When she reappeared, the grateful memory of her protection was slightly tinged with religious awe—not deeply, for Tessa's dread was chiefly of ugly and evil beings. It seemed unlikely that good beings would be angry and punish her, as Nofri and the devil were wont to do. Now that Monna Lisa spoke freely of Lillo's legs and Romola laughed, Tessa felt more at ease.

"Ninna is in the cradle," she said. "She's pretty too."

Romola went to look at the sleeping Ninna, and Monna Lisa, one of the exceptionally meek deaf who never expected to be spoken to, returned to her salad.

"Ah! She is waking; she has opened her blue eyes," Romola noted. "You must take her up, and I will sit down in this chair—may I?—and nurse Lillo. Come, Lillo!"

She sat in Tito's chair and extended her arms toward the lad, whose eyes had followed her. He hesitated, pointing his small fingers at her with a half-puzzled, half-angry expression. "That's Babbo's chair," he said, not seeing how to resolve the difficulty if Babbo returned to find Romola in his place.

"But Babbo is not here, and I shall go soon. Come, let me nurse you as he does," Romola said, wondering for the first time what sort of Babbo he was whose wife was dressed in contadina fashion yet possessed a certain daintiness indicating idleness and plenty. Lillo consented to be lifted, and finding the lap exceedingly comfortable, began to explore her dress and hands, searching for ornaments beside the rosary.

Tessa, having coaxed Ninna out of her waking peevishness, sat down in her low chair near Romola's knee, arranging Ninna's tiny person with jealousy that the strange lady seemed to notice the boy as much as Naldo did.

"Lillo was going to be angry with me because I sat in Babbo's chair," Romola said, bending to kiss Ninna's little foot. "Will he come soon and want it?"

"Ah, no!" Tessa replied. "You can sit in it a long while. I shall be sorry when you go. When you first came to take care of me at the Carnival, I thought it wonderful; you came and went away so fast. And Naldo said, perhaps you were a saint, and that made me tremble a little, though the saints are very good, I know; and you were good to me, and now you have taken care of Lillo. Perhaps you will always come and take care of me. That was how Naldo did a long while ago; he came and took care of me when I was frightened, one San Giovanni. I couldn't think where he came from—he was so beautiful and good. And so are you," Tessa concluded, looking up at Romola with devout admiration.

"Naldo is your husband. His eyes are like Lillo's," Romola said, examining the boy's darkly pencilled eyebrows, unusual for his age. She spoke not interrogatively, but with a quiet certainty of inference that was necessarily mysterious to Tessa.

"Ah! You know him!" Tessa said, pausing in wonder. "Perhaps you know Nofri and Peretola, and our house on the hill, and everything. Yes, like Lillo's; but not his hair. His hair is dark and long—" she continued, growing excited. "Ah! If you know it, ecco!"

She placed her hand on a thin red silk cord around her neck and drew from her bosom the tiny old parchment *Breve*, a horn of red coral, and a long dark curl carefully tied at one end and suspended with those mystic treasures. She held them toward Romola, keeping them away from Ninna's snatching hand.

"It is a fresh one. I cut it lately. See how bright it is!" she said, laying it against the white background of Romola's fingers. "They get dim, and then he lets me cut another when his hair grows; and I put it with the *Breve*, because sometimes he is away a long while, and then I think it helps to take care of me."

A slight shiver passed through Romola as the curl was laid across her fingers. At Tessa's first mention of her husband arriving mysteriously from unknown quarters, a possibility had risen before Romola that made her heart beat faster; for one anxiously searching for a certain object finds peculiar significance in the faintest suggestions. And when the curl was held toward her, it seemed for an instant like a mocking phantom of the lock she herself had cut five years ago. Yet she preserved her outward calmness, bent not only on knowing the truth but on arriving at that knowledge in a way that would not pain this poor, trusting, ignorant creature with the child's mind in a woman's body. "Foolish and helpless:" yes; so far she corresponded to Baldassarre's account.

"It is a beautiful curl," she said, resisting the impulse to withdraw her hand. "Lillo's curls will be like it, perhaps, for his cheek, too, is dark. And you never know where your husband goes when he leaves you?"

"No," Tessa said, putting back her treasures out of the children's way. "But I know Messer San Michele takes care of him, for he gave him a beautiful coat, all made of little chains; and if he puts that on, nobody can kill him. And perhaps, if—"

Tessa hesitated, under a recurrence of that original dreamy wonder about Romola which had been dispelled by chatting contact—"if you *were* a saint, you would take care of him, too, because you have taken care of me and Lillo."

An agitated flush came over Romola's face in the first moment of certainty, but she had bent her cheek against Lillo's head. The feeling that leaped out in that flush was something like exultation at the thought that the wife's burden might be about to slip from her overladen shoulders; that this little ignorant creature might prove to be Tito's lawful wife. A strange exultation for a proud and high-born woman to experience! But it seemed to Romola as if that were the only issue that would make duty anything other than an insoluble problem. Yet she was not deaf to Tessa's last appealing words; she raised her head and said, in her clearest tones—

"I will always take care of you if I see you need me. But that beautiful coat? Your husband did not wear it when you were first married? Perhaps he used not to be so long away from you then?"

"Ah, yes! He was. Much—much longer. So long, I thought he would never come back. I used to cry. Oh me! I was beaten then; a long, long while ago at Peretola, where we had the goats and mules."

"And how long had you been married before your husband had that chain-coat?" Romola asked, her heart beating faster and faster.

Tessa looked meditative and began counting on her fingers, while Romola watched them as if they would reveal the secret of her destiny.

"The chestnuts were ripe when we were married," Tessa said, marking off her thumb and fingers again as she spoke; "and then again they were ripe at Peretola before he came back, and then again, after that, on the hill. And soon the soldiers came, and we heard the trumpets, and then Naldo had the coat."

"You had been married more than two years. In which church were you married?" Romola asked, too entirely absorbed by one thought to pose any question less direct. Perhaps before the next morning she might go to her godfather and declare she was not Tito Melema's lawful wife—that the vows binding her to strive after an impossible union had been rendered void beforehand.

Tessa gave a slight start at Romola's new tone of inquiry and looked up with a hesitating expression. Hitherto she had prattled on without consciousness that she was making revelations, any more than when she repeated old things to Monna Lisa.

"Naldo said I was never to tell about that,"

Tito possessed ample justification for his claim of safety. Over the preceding three months, anticipating the inevitable exposure of the Medicean conspirators, he had meticulously fortified his position. He had expanded his influence in Rome and Milan, acting as a conduit for clandestine intelligence and executing subtle maneuvers against the Frate and the popular faction. Simultaneously, he had cultivated the favor of that very popular party with deceptive fervor, projecting an image of unwavering political alignment. Yet, paradoxically, he had deepened his reliance on the Mediceans, seeking their exclusive trust and active employment. This triple game was effortlessly maintained. The Mediceans, accustomed to duplicity in their own dealings, lacked the moral framework to judge a colleague devoid of hereditary ties and traditional prejudices. To them, deceiving the enemy was a legitimate strategy; deceiving their own was a baseness they did not entertain. In employing Tito's facile talents, they remained blind to the fact that his lack of ancestral attachment—their primary safeguard of honor—was precisely what rendered him a perfect instrument of betrayal. Similarly, the *Arrabbiati*, the aristocratic enemies of Savonarola, operated a network of espionage rooted in profound hypocrisy, demanding exorbitant fees for their services. Suspecting an agent merely because he played a role convincingly was, in their eyes, a logical fallacy. Conversely, the *Piagnoni* of the popular party, driven by the directness of energetic conviction, were inclined to believe Tito's professed loyalty, largely because he feigned no religious sympathy.

Under these conditions, the last quarter had been a period of flattering success for Tito. His primary ambition was to secure a permanent station in Rome or Milan. He harbored a growing resolve to abandon Florence for a great capital where life was less arduous and the rewards for talent more splendid. Currently, the scales tipped toward Milan; if he could render decisive services to Duke Ludovico Sforza within the year, the prospect of a court appointment outweighed the allure of Rome.

The revelation of the Medicean conspiracy was, therefore, a calculated risk Tito had anticipated, though not the specific manner of its occurrence. The arrest of Lamberto dell'Antella, found in possession of incriminating correspondence and fueled by bitter hatred for the Medici, was an unforeseen variable. Despite the careful pretexts guarding his agency, Tito could not escape implication in a broad discovery. However, his sharp mind quickly charted a course to ensure his safety with minimal collateral damage. While it is pleasant to remain unscathed, one must acknowledge that the skin remains sensitive to the atmosphere.

His calculations proved accurate. That night, before returning home, he secured the three outcomes he desired: immunity from prosecution regarding his Medicean complicity, the retention of his secretaryship for another year, and the strict confidentiality of the price exacted for these guarantees. A sum considered heavy by most, and one Tito himself would have preferred to avoid paying, it was nonetheless the currency of his survival.

His first move was to win over Francesco Valori, head of the *Piagnoni* and one of the Ten under whom Tito served. Valori, a man of fine qualities tempered by violent partisanship, an arrogant temper, and deep-seated animosity toward Bernardo del Nero, was also a member of the special council investigating the plot. In a brief, private interview sealed by a pledge of secrecy, Tito confessed his agency for the Mediceans, citing motives he presented with frankness. He declared his belief in the futility of their efforts and his sincere preference for the popular government. He feigned confidence in Valori, revealing his personal dislike for Bernardo del Nero, before delivering his crucial proposition: there existed another Medicean plot, and if granted certain conditions, he could travel to Siena and Romagna, where Piero de' Medici was gathering forces, to obtain documentary evidence for the council. This mission required that his reputation as a Medicean agent remain unblemished in the eyes of his former masters; thus, his role as a source of information to the authorities had to be buried in profound secrecy. Yet, fearing that even precautions might leak, he insisted on immediate assurances of immunity from prosecution and job security for the coming year.

These propositions did not resonate with Valori as they do with modern sensibilities. Valori's mind was not focused on weighing Tito's moral conduct; it was singularly obsessed with securing an extreme sentence against the five prisoners. He anticipated immense efforts to save them and believed that the strongest possible evidence was necessary to alarm the populace to the dangers of clemency. In the legal culture of the time, evidence was a commodity attainable only through foul means. Torturing suspects into confession was viewed as a step toward justice, and the appearance of a traitor was often welcomed to validate the guilt of others. Lamberto dell'Antella had been tortured to extract knowledge he barely possessed, yet stronger facts were deemed essential, particularly against Bernardo del Nero, who had thus far refrained from betraying the plot despite previous attempts to discourage it. For Valori, the welfare of Florence demanded that Bernardo's guilt be illuminated in the starkest light. Driven by zeal, perhaps unconsciously fueled by his own hatred, Valori accepted Tito's proposal. He presented it to his colleagues anonymously and secured their agreement. Late that afternoon, Tito was summoned before the Special Council. By revealing a second plot designed to install Piero de' Medici as master of Florence, scheduled for execution in mid-August, he caused a sensation. He argued that documentary evidence would clarify the right course of action. He was commissioned to depart for Siena at dawn and, departing from the council chamber, carried with him a written guarantee of his immunity and the retention of his office.

Among the twenty Florentines who regarded Tito with grave eyes as he stood before them, speaking of startling matters with easy circumlocution and an apparent lack of higher motive—a mask often worn by the most intense affectation—some began to reassess him. They silently concluded that this ingenious and serviceable Greek was now to be utilized for public necessity rather than private intimacy. Unprincipled men were useful tools, allowing those with scruples to keep their hands relatively clean in a world requiring dirty work. Indeed, it was unclear to the respectable Florentine mind, unless one adhered to the Frate's extravagant belief in earthly purity, how any department of life could function without instruments whom it was proper to kick or spit upon while paying their wages. Some of these very men, soon to be engaged in a memorable transaction requiring unscrupulousness as resolute as Tito's, reflected on the dichotomy of their nature. As their own poet Pulci had noted: "Betrayal pleases many, but no one likes the traitor." Society had erected gibbets for both murderers and martyrs, offering execrations for both dastardly acts and words of generous truth. This mixed condition signaled not hopelessness, but a struggling order.

For Tito himself, he was acutely aware that he had slightly diminished in the esteem of those who had accepted his services. Possessing the self-contemplation inherent in acting on well-considered reasons, he would have preferred to avoid their disapproval. He had never intended to be judged poorly; he aimed to conduct himself so ably that opposition to societal standards went unnoticed. The barrier between himself and Romola had been raised by the impossibility of such concealment with her. He shrank from condemnatory judgments as one shrinks from a climate to which he cannot adapt. Yet, reality was not as malleable as cleverness wished, and events had turned inconveniently. He bore no rancor against Messer Bernardo del Nero; he held personal likings for Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci. He had served them ably, meriting high reward had their party prevailed. Was he to relinquish the agreeable fruits of life solely because their party had failed? His offer of additional proof against them would likely alter their fate little; he was convinced they would escape extreme consequences. Had he withheld it, however, his own promising fortunes would have been utterly ruined. What motive could any man possess but self-interest? Florentines engaged in petty political schemes might derive self-interest from family pride and ancient hatreds; a modern simpleton swallowing an old philosophy might mistake indigestion for divine influx; fanatics believing in the coming Scourge might find their interest in a future palm-branch. But no man of clear intellect allowed his course to be determined by such puerile impulses or questionable inward fumes. Did not Pontanus, poet and philosopher of unrivalled Latinity, deliver the finest oration at Naples welcoming the French king, who came to dethrone his royal friend and patron? And yet, Pontanus held his head high and prospered. Men did not truly care about such things unless their personal spleen was touched. Weakness alone was despised; power carried immunity. No man could mount high without incurring unpleasant necessities that laid him open to enmity, and perhaps to hissing, when enmity sought a pretext.

It was a faint prognostic of that hissing, gathered by Tito from indications during his audience, that gave his present conduct the character of an epoch. It was not that he was taking a deeper step in wrongdoing, for he felt no tie to the Mediceans stronger than that to his father. His conduct toward his father had been hidden by successful lying; his present act, however, admitted of no total concealment—it was, in its very nature, a revelation. Tito winced under this new liability to disesteem.

Very well. A little patience, and in another year, or perhaps half a year, he might turn his back on these hard, eager Florentines with their futile quarrels and sinking fortunes. His brilliant success in Florence had indeed possessed ugly flaws: he had fallen in love with the wrong woman, and Baldassarre had returned under incalculable circumstances. But as Tito galloped with a loose rein toward Siena, he saw a future ahead where he would no longer be haunted by those mistakes. He had much money safely out of Florence; he was in the fresh ripeness of twenty-eight years; he was conscious of well-tried skill. Could he not strip himself of the past, as of rehearsal clothing, and discard the old bundle to robe himself for the real scene?

It did not enter Tito's meditations on the future that, as he issued from the council-chamber and descended the stairs, he had brushed against a man whose face he had not paused to recognize in the lamplight. The man was Ser Ceccone—also willing to serve the State by providing information against unsuccessful employers.

Tito returned from Siena with swift efficiency, yet his departure marked the beginning of a longer absence; he did not return until the seventeenth of August. Nearly two weeks had elapsed since the arrest of the accused, and the shadow of uncertainty still clung to them within the prison walls. For Romola, these days had felt like a suspension of existence, her entire world narrowed to the precarious balance of probabilities regarding their fate. The mood in the city was a volatile mixture of fear and calculation. On one hand, the clamor grew louder that the Republic teetered on the brink of ruin, and that mercy shown to the prisoners would invite immediate attack from their enemies. On the other, the certainty loomed that condemning five citizens of such distinguished standing to death and confiscating their estates would breed a rancorous hatred from their families that no authority could withstand.

The power to deliver the final judgment rested with the Eight, the magistrates overseeing criminal justice, whose sentence required a majority of six votes. Yet, the Eight recoiled from this heavy burden, requesting that the responsibility be shared by the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and the eight Priors. The Signoria, in turn, shrugged off the weight, proposing an appeal to the Great Council. According to a law championed by Savonarola three years prior, any citizen condemned to death by the "six beans" (*set fave*) possessed the right to appeal to the Great Council. However, at this critical juncture, the friends of the accused resisted the formal appeal, driven primarily by a desperate need for delay. Since strict legality demanded a sentence be passed before an appeal could be lodged, their resistance forced a compromise: the case was referred to a massive assembly convened on the seventeenth, comprising all higher magistracies, the Senate of Eighty, and a select body of citizens.

On this fateful day, Romola, her anxiety sharpened by the fear that her godfather's destiny might be sealed before the proceedings concluded, had secured leave to visit him for the second time, though only in the presence of witnesses. She returned to the Via de' Bardi accompanied by her cousin, Monna Brigida, who remained unaware of whether the council had reached a decisive verdict. Brigida departed again to await news at a friend's house within one of the magistracies, promising to return with authentic tidings the moment they were available.

Exhausted and sick at heart, Romola collapsed onto the first seat in the bright saloon, too agitated to care for her surroundings or notice the discordant arrangement of the room. She sat with her back to the door, resting her head in her hands, waiting for Brigida's return. When the door finally opened, she knew instantly that it was not her cousin who entered.

Since her parting from Tito on that memorable night, Romola had possessed no external proof that he had secured his safety through treachery. To the contrary, she had evidence that he was still trusted by the Mediceans, believed to be carrying out their errands in Romagna under the guise of a government commission. The obscurity surrounding the conspirators allowed the comforting illusion that Tito had escaped any implication. Yet, Romola's suspicion was insurmountable. Her horror at his conduct toward Baldassarre projected itself over every interpretation of his actions; it was as if she had witnessed a murder and carried a permanent, diseased impression that his hands were stained with fresh blood.

As she heard his step on the stone floor, a chill shudder ran through her. She could not turn, could not rise to offer greeting. He did not speak but, after a brief pause, took a seat opposite her across the table. When she finally raised her eyes to meet his gaze, she found herself mute. He showed no irritation, only a cool composure.

"This meeting corresponds with our parting, Romola," he said softly. "But I understand it is a moment of terrible suspense. I have come, if you will listen, to bring you the relief of hope."

Romola started, shifting her position but regarding him with deep doubt.

"It will not be unwelcome to hear—even though it is I who tell it—that the council is prorogued until the twenty-first. The Eight have finally been frightened into passing a sentence of condemnation, but a demand has now been made on behalf of the condemned for an Appeal to the Great Council."

Romola's doubtful expression vanished; she asked eagerly, "And when is it to be made?"

"It has not yet been granted, but it *may* be. The Special Council meets again on the twenty-first to deliberate whether the Appeal shall be allowed. In the meantime, there is an interval of three days—a window in which chances may arise in favor of the prisoners, where interest may be used on their behalf."

Romola sprang from her seat. Color rushed to her face like a visible thought, and her hands trembled. In that instant, her feelings toward Tito were forgotten.

"Possibly," Tito said, rising as well, "your own intention anticipated what I was about to say. You are thinking of the Frate."

"I am," Romola replied, looking at him with surprise. "Has he done something? Is there anything to tell me?"

"Only this: it was Messer Francesco Valori's bitterness and violence that chiefly determined the council's course today. Half the men who voted against the prisoners were frightened into it. There are numerous friends of Fra Girolamo, both inside and outside this Special Council, who strongly oppose the death sentence—Piero Guicciardini, for instance, a member of the Signoria who offered stoutest resistance; and Giovan Battista Ridolfi, who, as a Piagnone, will not lightly forgive the death of his brother Niccolò."

"But how can the Appeal be denied?" Romola cried indignantly. "When it is the law! Was it not one of the chief glories of our popular government to enact such a law?"

"They call this an exceptional case. Of course, there are ingenious arguments, but there is much more loud bluster about the danger to the Republic. Yet, no opposition could prevent the assembly from being prorogued. A certain powerful influence, rightly applied during these next three days, might determine the wavering courage of those who wish the Appeal granted, and perhaps check the headlong enmity of Francesco Valori. It has come to my knowledge that the Frate has already interfered by sending a message to Valori in favor of Lorenzo Tornabuoni. I know you sometimes have access to the Frate; it would certainly be worthwhile to use your privilege now."

"It is true," Romola said, her voice abstracted. "I cannot believe the Frate would approve of denying the Appeal."

"I heard it said by many in the Palazzo court before I left that it would be to Fra Girolamo's everlasting discredit to allow a government composed almost entirely of his party to deny the Appeal without protest, especially when he boasts in his books and sermons that he secured the law's passage. But between ourselves, with all respect for your Frate's ability, my Romola, he has fallen into the habit of preaching that form of human sacrifice called killing tyrants and wicked malcontents—a stance some of his followers might find inconsistent with lenity in this specific case."

*I know, I know,* Romola thought, pain etching her features. *He is driven into those excesses of speech. It used to be different.* "I *will* ask for an interview. I cannot rest without it. I trust in the greatness of his heart."

She did not look at Tito; her eyes were bent in a vague gaze toward the ground, lacking distinct consciousness of the words issuing from her husband.

"Better lose no time, then," Tito said, his tone unmixt with suavity, moving his cap in his hands as if preparing to depart. "And now, Romola, you will perhaps be able to see, despite prejudice, that my wishes align with yours in this matter. You will not regard the misfortune of my safety as an offense."

An electric shock passed through Romola: the full, crushing consciousness of her husband's presence returned to her. She looked at him without speaking.

"At least," he added, his tone hardening slightly, "you will endeavor to base our intercourse on reasonings other than the fact that an evil deed is possible, *I* have done it. Am I alone to be cast beyond the pale of your extensive charity?"

The feeling that had been suppressed from Romola's lips two weeks prior rose again with the gathered force of a tidal wave. She spoke with a decision that signaled her disregard for consequences.

"It is too late, Tito. There is no killing the suspicion that deceit has once begotten. And now I know everything. I know who that old man was: he was your father, to whom you owe everything—to whom you owe more than if you had been his own child. By comparison, it is a small thing that you broke my trust and my father's. As long as you deny the truth about that old man, a horror rises between us; the law that should make us one can never be obeyed. I too am a human being. I have a soul of my own that abhors your actions. Our union is a pretense—as if a perpetual lie could be a sacred marriage."

Tito did not answer immediately. When he finally spoke, it was with calculated caution, stimulated by alarm.

"And you mean to carry out that independence by quitting me, I presume?"

"I desire to quit you," Romola said impetuously.

"And suppose I do not submit to part with what the law gives me some security for retaining? You will then, of course, proclaim your reasons in the ear of all Florence. You will bring forward your mad assassin, who is doubtless ready to obey your call, and tell the world that you believe his testimony because he is so rational as to desire to assassinate me. You will first inform the Signoria that I am a Medicean conspirator, and then inform the Mediceans that I have betrayed them. In both cases, you will offer the excellent proof that you believe me capable in general of everything bad. It will certainly be a striking position for a wife to adopt. And if, on such evidence, you succeed in holding me up to infamy, you will have surpassed all the heroines of the Greek drama."

He paused, but she stood mute. He continued with a sense of mastery.

"I believe you have no other grievance against me—except that I failed in fulfilling some lofty, indefinite conditions on which you gave me your wifely affection, so that, by withdrawing it, you have gradually reduced me to the careful supply of your wants as a fair Piagnone of high condition and liberal charities. I think your success in gibbeting me is not certain. But doubtless you would begin by winning the ear of Messer Bernardo del Nero?"

"Why do I speak of anything?" Romola cried in anguish, sinking back onto her chair. "It is hateful in me to be thinking of myself."

She did not notice when Tito left the room, nor did she know how long it was before the door opened to admit Monna Brigida. But in that instant, she sprang up and declared, "Cousin, we must go to San Marco directly. I must see my confessor, Fra Salvestro."

The morning broke with a pale, earnest light as Romola set out for San Marco. She had secured, the night before through Fra Salvestro, a rare audience with Fra Girolamo in the chapter-house. Such meetings were exceptional; Savonarola guarded his solitude with a rigidity that brooked no pretext for slander, ensuring that when he did grant access, it was stripped of all mystery. Thus, she arrived during the bustling hours of the outer cloisters, when the air was thick with the chatter of lay visitors.

She chose to traverse the heart of the city first, seeking to read the pulse of public sentiment. Every loggia and corner of the piazza buzzed with the feverish energy of gratuitous debate. The languor of trade had given way to the vigor of politics; the factions supporting and opposing the death of the conspirators were desperate to gauge the popular mood during these three days of suspension. Handbills fluttered through the streets like black birds. Some proclaimed in massive, bold letters the stark choice: justice for the conspirators or ruin for the Republic. Others, equally shouting, demanded the observance of the law and the granting of the Appeal. Around these islands of giant type swarmed smaller print, offering arguments deemed unnecessary to read aloud, for in the fresh flush of the printing press's triumph, it was believed that no phrase could convince the masses quite like a question-begging headline.

Romola, however, cared little for the shouting headlines. She sought the quiet, complex arguments in the smaller type. Though she hurried, her eyes scanned the crowds anxiously for copies. For a long stretch, she found only sheets clutched by eager readers or torn from walls by the *sbirri*. Finally, passing behind San Giovanni, she spotted Bratti, a familiar face of the humble Florentine life, exchanging pamphlets for small coins.

"Have you both sorts, Bratti?" she asked, her voice cutting through his slow movements. "Let me have them quickly."

"Two sorts," Bratti replied, separating the damp sheets with a deliberate slowness that tested her patience. "'Law,' and 'Justice.'"

"Which sells faster?"

"'Justice.' It flies off the shelves, so I raised the price to two danari. But then I thought, 'Law' is just as worthy a commodity. People scorn cheap things; if I sold 'Law' for one danaro, I'd be wronging it. I'm a fair trader. Both are good wares, bought for nothing and sold for profit. You want more than one of each?"

"No," Romola said, pressing a white quattrino into his hand and snatching the bills before he could elaborate further. She folded them tightly and hastened toward the convent.

In the outer cloisters, Fra Salvestro awaited her, lost in conversation with lay visitors. Under the current government, the fear that had gripped San Marco following the Excommunication was slowly receding. Among the listeners was Andrea Cambini, a known satellite of Francesco Valori, gesturing emphatically as he recounted news. Fra Salvestro listened with that trivial curiosity that signals a mind interested in gossip rather than truth. Romola recognized the topic immediately from the disjointed fragments reaching her ear: the fate of the conspirators. She avoided the group, waiting until Fra Salvestro's attention shifted before turning toward the chapter-house door. A lay brother opened it for her, closing it softly behind her.

Inside, the sad frescoed figures seemed to mourn anew with her the death of her brother Dino. Yet, the intensity of her present dread rendered the memory less a clear picture than a blur of impressions, blending with her agitating fears. She felt her current anxiety as a revival of a past yearning, once again repelled by the cold rigidity of marble. Without pausing to dwell on the past, she unfolded the handbills, hoping to read them before Fra Girolamo's arrival. By the time she finished the pamphlet urging legal observance, the door swung open, and she stood expectant, papers doubled in her hands.

When the Frate entered, she knelt, adhering to the custom of those who sought private audiences. But as soon as he offered a benedictory greeting, she rose and stood opposite him at a respectful distance. His seclusion since the Excommunication meant this was their first meeting in months, and the years had etched deeper lines of mental strain and physical austerity onto his face. Yet, Romola felt less the weight of age than a subtle shift in his essence. Was the serene elevation and pure fellowship that had once moved her diminished? Or was it the growing sense of division regarding her godfather that clouded her vision? Perhaps both currents ran together. Our relations with others are often decided by such converging tides; the final blow rarely comes until affection has been eroded by repeated excuses.

Savonarola's gaze held a hardness born of self-preoccupation. He sensed the purpose of her visit—the fate of the conspirators—and knew he had already silenced inner voices that might grow loud if encouraged from without. Seated in his cell, correcting the sheets of his *Triumph of the Cross*, he found it easier to maintain a posture of neutrality.

"It is a matter of great moment, doubtless, on which you wish to see me, my daughter," he began, his tone gentle, though controlled rather than inclined. "I know you do not usually stress trifles."

"Father, you know what it is before I speak," Romola said, pouring forth her plea instantly. "You know what I care for—it is the life of the old man I love best in the world. The thought of him has accompanied the thought of my father since I can remember daylight. That is my warrant for coming to you, even if my coming proves needless. Perhaps you have already decided that your power over hearts will be used to deny Florentines a right you yourselves helped secure."

"I meddle not with the functions of the State, my daughter," Fra Girolamo replied, disinclined to reopen a debate he had already wrestled with internally. "I have preached and labored that Florence should have a good government, for it is needful to the perfecting of the Christian life; but I keep my hands from particular affairs which it is the office of experienced citizens to administer."

"Surely, father—" Romola broke off. The word escaped her impetuously, but she was halted by the counter-agitation of feeling herself in an attitude of remonstrance toward the man who had been her guide and strength. In the act of rebelling, she bruised her own reverence.

Savonarola was too keen not to divine the conflict arresting her—too noble, deliberately, to assume the calm, self-justifying evasiveness he often displayed in public.

"Say what is in your heart; speak on, my daughter," he said, standing with arms crossed, looking at her with quiet expectation.

"I was going to say, father, that this matter is surely of higher moment than many about which I have heard you preach fervidly. If it belonged to you to urge that men condemned for offenses against the State should have the right to appeal to the Great Council—if—Romola grew eager—"if you count it a glory to have won that right for them, can it less belong to you to declare yourself against the right being denied to almost the first men who need it? Surely that touches the Christian life more closely than whether you knew beforehand that the Dauphin would die, or whether Pisa will be conquered."

A subtle movement, like a subdued sign of pain, crossed Savonarola's strong lips before he spoke.

"My daughter, I speak as it is given me to speak. I am not master of the times when I may become the vehicle of knowledge beyond the common lights of men. In this case, I have no illumination beyond what wisdom may give to those charged with the safety of the State. As to the law of Appeal against the Six Votes, I labored to have it passed so that no Florentine should suffer loss of life or goods through the private hatred of a few in power. But these five men, who desired to overthrow a free government and restore a corrupt tyrant, have been condemned with the assent of a large assembly of their fellow-citizens. They refused at first to bring their cause before the Great Council. They have lost the right to the appeal."

"How can they have lost it?" Romola cried. "It is the right to appeal against condemnation, and they have never been condemned till now! And forgive me, father, it *is* private hatred that would deny them the appeal; it *is* the violence of the few that frightens others. Else why was the assembly divided again directly after it seemed to agree? And if anything weighs against the observance of the law, let this weigh for it: that you used to preach more earnestly than all else that there should be no place for hatred and bloodshed born of party strife, so that private ill-will should not find opportunity in public acts. Father, you know there is private hatred here. Will it not dishonor you not to interpose on the side of mercy, when many hold it is also the side of law and justice?"

"My daughter," Savonarolo said, with more visible emotion than before, "there is a mercy which is weakness, and even treason against the common good. The safety of Florence, which means even more than the welfare of Florentines, now demands severity, as it once demanded mercy. It is not only for a past plot that these men are condemned, but also for a plot which has not yet been executed. The devices leading to its execution remain active: the tyrant still gathers his forces in Romagna, and the enemies of Florence, who sit in the highest places of Italy, are ready to hurl any stone that will crush her."

"What plot?" Romola asked, reddening and trembling with alarmed surprise.

"You carry papers in your hand, I see," Savonarola pointed out, indicating the handbills. "One of them will, perhaps, tell you the government has new information."

Romola hastily opened the unread pamphlet and saw that the government now possessed positive evidence of a second plot, scheduled for this very August. To her mind, it felt like reading a confirmation that Tito had won his safety by foul means; his pretense of wanting the Frate to act on behalf of the condemned only deepened her wretched conviction. She crushed the paper in her hand and turned to Savonarola with new passion.

"Father, what safety can there be for Florence when the worst man can always escape? And," she continued, a sudden flash of remembrance striking her regarding her husband, "have not you yourself encouraged this deception which corrupts the life of Florence, by wanting more favor shown to Lorenzo Tornabuoni, who wears two faces and flatters you with a show of affection, when my godfather has always been honest? Ask all Florence who of those five men has the truest heart, and there will not be many who will name any other than Bernardo del Nero. You did interpose with Francesco Valori for the sake of one prisoner; you have *not* then been neutral. And you know that your word will be powerful."

"I do not desire the death of Bernardo," Savonarola said, coloring deeply. "It would be enough if he were sent out of the city."

"Then why do you not speak to save an old man of seventy-five from dying a death of ignominy—to give him at least the fair chances of the law?" Romola burst out, her nature so roused by indignation that she forgot everything else. "It is not that you feel bound to be neutral; else why did you speak for Lorenzo Tornabuoni? You spoke for him because he is more friendly to San Marco; my godfather feigns no friendship. It is not, then, as a Medicean that my godfather is to die; it is as a man you have no love for!"

When Romola paused, cheeks glowing and lips quivering, there was a dead silence. As she saw Fra Girolamo standing motionless before her, she seemed to hear her own words echoing in strange, painful dissonance with the memories that made part of his presence to her. The moments of silence expanded with gathering compunction and self-doubt. She had committed sacrilege in her passion. Even the sense that she could retract nothing of her plea, that her mind could not submit to Savonarola's negative, made it more needful to satisfy those reverential memories. With a sudden movement toward him, she said:

"Forgive me, father; it is pain to me to have spoken those words—yet I cannot help speaking. I am little and feeble compared with you; you brought me light and strength. But I submitted because I felt the proffered strength—because I saw the light. *Now* I cannot see it. Father, you yourself declare that there comes a moment when the soul must have no guide but the voice within it, to tell whether the consecrated thing has sacred virtue. And therefore I must speak."

Savonarola possessed a readily-roused resentment toward opposition, hardly separable from a power-loving nature accustomed to seeking great ends that cast a reflected grandeur on the means. His sermons burned with that red flame. If he had been a meaner man, his susceptibility might have shown as irritation at Romola's accusatory freedom, which contrasted sharply with the deference he habitually received. But at this moment, such feelings were nullified by the hard struggle that made half the tragedy of his life: the struggle of a mind possessed by a never-silent hunger after purity and simplicity, yet caught in a tangle of egoistic demands, false ideas, and difficult outward conditions that made simplicity impossible. Keenly alive to the suggestions of Romola's remonstrance, he rapidly surveyed the courses of action open to him and their probable results. But it was a question on which arguments seemed decisive only in proportion as they were charged with feeling, and he had received no impulse that could alter his bias. He looked at Romola and said:

"You have full pardon for your frankness, my daughter. You speak, I know, out of the fulness of your family affections. But these affections must give way to the needs of the Republic. If those men who have a close acquaintance with the affairs of the State believe, as I understand they do, that the public safety requires the extreme punishment of the

The moon, rising just above the rooftops of the Piazza della Signora past the stroke of midnight, failed to cast its usual patterns of light and shadow upon the cobblestones. There was no pavement visible, no empty space for the moon to illuminate; there was only the sea of heads, a churning multitude pressing forward. The silence that usually accompanied the nocturnal wanderers of Florence—the soft patter of feet, the hum of conversation, the strumming of lutes—had been swallowed by a deafening roar. Shouts, curses, the trampling of boots, and the accidental clash of weapons formed a chaotic backdrop against which only a piercing shriek or the heavy, rhythmic tolling of a bell could be distinguished.

Every citizen of Florence who could stir himself was awake, either trapped within the square or fighting desperately to reach it. Inside the Old Palace, the Council Chamber remained a theater of fierce contention. The chief magistrates, the eighty senators, and the select citizens had debated through the daylight and torchlight whether to grant an appeal or execute the prisoners immediately to prevent the dangers of delay. The debate had degenerated into a quarrel so violent that its echoes reached the crowd outside. Only in the final hour had a decision been reached. The Signoria had been split, with four members stubbornly voting for an appeal despite the threat that their homes would be sacked if they refused. It was Francesco Valori who broke the deadlock. In a brief, furious speech, he declared that if the Signoria would not defend the liberties of the Florentine people by executing the five conspirators, others would seize the cause and endanger everyone who opposed them. The Florentine Cato had triumphed. When the votes were recounted, the four obstinate white beans vanished; the entire nine cast fatal black ballots, sentencing the five prisoners to immediate death—a verdict that also, though tacitly and with greater delay, sentenced Francesco Valori himself.

As the judicial Eight departed for the Bargello to prepare the scaffold, the five condemned men were led barefoot and in irons through the center of the council chamber. This humiliation had been orchestrated by their friends: would the Florentines not be moved by the sight of such cruel ignominy visited upon two venerable elders, Bernardo del Nero and Niccolò Ridolfi, whose loyalty predated the current political shift? Would they not mourn the brilliant young men, Tornabuoni and Pucci, whose absence would leave a haunting void in every gathering of the elite? It was futile. Any pity stirred now was of a hopeless variety, leading not to rescue but to the slower, darker action of revenge.

While this scene unfolded above, Romola stood below, leaning against a massive pillar in the palace courtyard. She waited for the moment her godfather would appear on his way to execution. Through powerful intervention, she had secured permission to visit him that evening and remain until the council's decision was final. Now, she waited with his confessor to follow the guard leading him to the Bargello. Her heart was resolved to cling to the presence of this childless old man until the very last moment, just as her father would have done; she had silenced all remonstrances. Giovan Battista Ridolfi, a disciple of Savonarola who went in bitterness to witness the death of his elder brother Niccolò, had promised to guard her and now stood beside her.

Tito was also in the palace, though Romola had not seen him. Since the evening of the seventeenth, they had avoided one another. Tito knew only by inference from the friar's neutral report that Romola's pleas had failed. Surrounded by official and foreign dignitaries awaiting the council's conclusion, he maintained a subdued air and grave silence, caught in a painful strife between public duty and private feeling. When allusions were made to his wife regarding these events, he implied that her violent mental excitement caused her to view his continued office under a government condemning her godfather with diseased hostility. For her sake, he felt it best not to approach her.

"Ah, the old Bardi blood!" Cennini remarked with a shrug. "I shall not be surprised if this business shakes her loose from the Frate, as well as some others I could name."

"It is excusable in a woman, who is doubtless beautiful, since she is the wife of Messer Tito," a young French envoy smiled, bowing to Tito. "To think that her affections must overrule the good of the State, and that nobody is to be beheaded who is anybody's cousin; but such a view is not to be encouraged in the male population. It seems to me your Florentine polity is much weakened by it."

"That is true," Niccolò Machiavelli replied. "But where personal ties are strong, the hostilities they raise must be taken into account. Many of these half-measures are merely hot-headed blunders. The only safe blows to be inflicted on men and parties are those too heavy to be avenged."

"Niccolò," Cennini said, "there is a clever wickedness in thy talk sometimes that makes me mistrust thy pleasant young face as if it were a mask of Satan."

"Not at all, my good Domenico," Machiavelli smiled, laying his hand on the elder's shoulder. "Satan was a blunderer, an introducer of novelty, who made a stupendous failure. If he had succeeded, we should all be worshipping him, and his portrait would be far more flattering."

"Well, well," Cennini conceded. "I say not thy doctrine is not too clever for Satan: I only say it is wicked enough for him."

"I tell you," Machiavelli insisted, "my doctrine is the doctrine of all men who seek an end a little farther off than their own noses. Ask our Frate, our prophet, how his universal renovation is to be brought about: he will tell you, first, by securing a free and pure government; and since this cannot be achieved by making all Florentines love each other, it must be done by cutting off every head that happens to be obstinately in the way. Only if a man incurs odium by sanctioning a severity that is not thorough enough to be final, he commits a blunder. And something like that blunder, I suspect, the Frate has committed. It was an occasion on which he might have won lustre by exerting himself to maintain the appeal; instead, he has lost lustre and gained no strength."

Before anyone else could speak, the announcement came that the prisoners were leaving the council chamber. The majority of those present hurried toward the door, intent on securing the clearest path to the Bargello behind the prisoners' guard, for the scene of the execution drew alike those moved by deep passion and those driven by cold curiosity.

Tito remained behind. He possessed a native repugnance to sights of death and pain. Five days prior, whenever he had considered this execution a possibility, he had hoped it would not happen, that the utmost sentence would be exile; his own safety demanded no more. But now, he felt that the death of Bernardo del Nero would be a welcome guarantee of his security. The new knowledge and new attitude revealed to him by Romola on the day of his return had instilled in him a fresh dread of the power she possessed to make his position insecure. If any act of hers succeeded in making him an object of suspicion and odium, he foresaw not only frustration but frustration under unpleasant circumstances. Her belief in Baldassarre had clearly determined her wavering feelings against further submission, and if her godfather lived, she would win him to share her belief without much trouble. Romola seemed more than ever an unmanageable fact in his destiny. But if Bernardo del Nero were dead, the difficulties that would beset her in placing herself in opposition to her husband would probably be insurmountable to her shrinking pride. Therefore, Tito felt easier knowing that the Eight had gone to the Bargello to order the instant erection of the scaffold. Four other men—his intimates and confederates—were to die besides Bernardo del Nero. Yet a man's own safety is a god that sometimes makes very grim demands. Tito felt them to be grim: even in the pursuit of what was agreeable, this paradoxical life forced upon him the desire for what was disagreeable. As he heard through the open doorway the shuffle of many feet and the clanking of metal on the stairs, he answered the questions of the young French envoy without showing signs of any feeling other than sad resignation to state necessities.

Those sounds fell upon Romola as if her power of hearing had been exalted along with every other sensibility of her nature. She needed no arm to support her; she shed no tears. She felt that intensity of life which seems to transcend both grief and joy—in which the mind seems to itself akin to elder forces that wrought out existence before the birth of pleasure and pain. Since her godfather's fate had been decided, the previous struggle of feeling within her had given way to an identification of herself with him in these supreme moments. She was inwardly asserting for him that, if he suffered the punishment of treason, he did not deserve the name of traitor; he was the victim of a collision between two kinds of faithfulness. It was not given him to die for the noblest cause, and yet he died because of his nobleness. He might have been a meaner man and found it easier not to incur this guilt. Romola was feeling the full force of that sympathy with the individual lot that is continually opposing itself to the formulae by which actions and parties are judged. She was treading the way with her second father to the scaffold, nerving herself to defy ignominy by the consciousness that it was not deserved.

The path was fenced in by three hundred armed men, placed as a guard by the orders of Francesco Valori. Among the apparent contradictions belonging to this event, not the least striking was the alleged alarm on one hand at the popular rage against the conspirators, and the alleged alarm on the other lest there should be an attempt to rescue them in the midst of a hostile crowd. When they arrived within the court of the Bargello, Romola was allowed to approach Bernardo with his confessor for a moment of farewell. Many eyes were bent on them even in that struggle of an agitated throng as the aged man, forgetting that his hands were bound with irons, lifted them toward the golden head bent toward him, and then, checking that movement, leaned to kiss her. She seized the fettered hands that hung down again and kissed them as if they had been sacred things.

"My poor Romola," Bernardo said in a low voice, "I have only to die, but thou hast to live—and I shall not be there to help thee."

"Yes," Romola said hurriedly, "you *will* help me—always—because I shall remember you."

She was taken away and conducted up the flight of steps leading to the loggia surrounding the grand old court. She took her place there, determined to watch until the moment when her godfather laid his head on the block. Now, while the prisoners were allowed a brief interval with their confessor, the spectators pressed into the court until the crowd became dense around the black scaffold. Torches fixed in iron rings against the pillars threw a varying, startling light at one moment on passionless stone carvings, at another on pale faces agitated with suppressed rage or suppressed grief—the faces of many near relatives of the condemned, who were presently to receive their dead and carry them home.

Romola's face looked like a marble image against the dark arch as she stood watching for the moment when her godfather would appear at the foot of the scaffold. He was to suffer first, and Battista Ridolfi, who stood by her side, had promised to take her away through a door behind them once she had seen the last look of the man who alone in all the world had shared her pitying love for her father. And still, in the background of her thought, there was the possibility striving to become a hope, that some rescue might yet come, something that would keep that scaffold unstained by blood.

For a long while there was constant movement, lights flickering, heads swaying to and fro, confused voices within the court, rushing waves of sound through the entrance from without. It seemed to Romola as if she were in the midst of a storm-troubled sea, caring nothing about the storm, caring only to hold out a signal till the eyes that looked for it could seek it no more.

Suddenly there was stillness, and the very tapers seemed to tremble into quiet. The executioner was ready on the scaffold, and Bernardo del Nero was seen ascending it with a slow, firm step. Romola made no visible movement, uttered not even a suppressed sound; she stood more firmly, caring for *his* firmness. She saw him pause, saw the white head kept erect while he said, in a voice distinctly audible:

"It is but a short space of life that my fellow-citizens have taken from me."

She perceived that he was gazing slowly around him as he spoke. She felt that his eyes were resting on her, and that she was stretching out her arms toward him. Then she saw no more till—a long while after, as it seemed—a voice said, "My daughter, all is peace now. I can conduct you to your house."

She uncovered her head and saw her godfather's confessor standing by her, in a room where there were other grave men talking in subdued tones.

"I am ready," she said, starting up. "Let us lose no time."

She thought all clinging was at an end for her: all her strength now should be given to escape from a grasp under which she shuddered.

On the eighth day following that unforgettable night, Romola stood upon the precipice of the Mediterranean, gazing at the gentle, rhythmic pulse of the summer sea just above the tiny fishing hamlet of Viareggio. Once more, she had fled from Florence, and this time, no commanding voice had summoned her return. Clad once again in the grey habit of the religious order, she felt no shame in the disguise; indeed, in the depths of her heart-sickness, she cared nothing for the label she bore. A fresh rebellion had ignited within her, fueled by a profound despair. Why should she cling to one title over another, or insist on being addressed by her own name? She had lost faith in the existence of any consistent duty attached to that identity. What power remained to forge within her that sacred motive men call duty, a force that exists only through the vessel of believing love?

Every bond of strong affection had been severed. In her marriage, the highest of all ties, she could no longer perceive the mystical union that guarantees indissolubility, nor even the obligation of a voluntary pledge. Had she not demonstrated that the things to which she had bound herself were impossible? The urge to liberate herself surged with overwhelming force, yet she knew this freedom would merely be an exchange of one calamity for another. There is no recompense for the woman who realizes that the central relationship of her life was nothing more than a mistake. She had lost her crown. The deepest secret of human blessedness had half-whispered its truth to her, only to slip past her forever.

Now, Romola's greatest support in her supreme sorrow had vanished. The vision of any grand purpose, any end to existence capable of ennobling endurance and elevating the mundane dust of daily life with divine ardor, was utterly eclipsed by a sense of confusion in human affairs. Every effort felt like dragging at tangled threads; every fellowship, whether for resistance or advocacy, appeared as mere unfairness and exclusivity. Who, after all, was the man who had embodied for her the highest heroism—the heroism not of stoic, self-contained endurance, but of willing, self-offering love? What cause was he truly fighting for? Romola had lost her trust in Savonarola, extinguishing the fervor of admiration that had previously made her blind to his aberrations, allowing her to focus solely on the grand arc of his destiny. Now that her deep affection for her godfather had cast her into opposition with the Friar, she viewed the repulsive and inconsistent details of his teachings with a painful, exaggerated clarity. In the bitterness of her disappointment, she declared that his striving to renovate the Church and the world was a pursuit of a mere name—a title that signified nothing more than the measures designed to strengthen his own position in Florence, or perhaps questionable deeds performed merely to shield his influence from the consequences of his own errors. The political reform that had once ignited a new interest in her life now seemed reduced to narrow schemes for the safety of Florence, a contemptible contradiction to his alternating professions of blind trust in Divine care.

It was inevitable that she should judge the Friar unfairly regarding a question of individual suffering, viewing it through the lens of personal tenderness, while he viewed it through the eyes of theoretic conviction. In his declaration that the cause of his party was the cause of God's kingdom, she heard only the ring of egoism. Perhaps such words are rarely spoken without that meaner undertone; yet they remain the implicit formula of all energetic belief. If such belief, pursuing a grand and remote end, often risks becoming a form of demon-worship where the votary sends his children through the fire with a readiness that hardly resembles sacrifice, then tender fellow-feeling for the near and dear has its own dangers too. It is apt to become timid and skeptical toward the larger aims without which life cannot rise into religion. Thus, poor Romola was being blinded by her tears.

No one who has ever known the agony of losing faith in a fellow-man whom he has profoundly loved and revered will lightly claim that such a shock can leave the faith in the Invisible Goodness unshaken. With the sinking of high human trust, the dignity of life sinks as well; we cease to believe in our own better selves, for that too is part of the common nature degraded in our thoughts, and all the finer impulses of the soul grow dull. Romola felt even the springs of her once active pity drying up, leaving her to barren, egoistic complaining. Had not she suffered her own sorrows? And while she had cared for many, few had cared for her. She had done enough; she had striven for the impossible and was weary of this stifling, crowded existence. She longed for that repose in mere sensation, a dream she had sometimes entertained during the sultry afternoons of her early girlhood, when she had fancied herself floating naively in the waters.

The clear waves seemed to beckon her; she wished she could lie down upon them and pass from sleep into death. Yet Romola could not directly seek death; the fullness of young life within her forbade such a direct act. She could only wish that death would come to her.

At the spot where she paused, the shore curved deeply, and a small boat with a sail was moored there. In her longing to glide over the waters turning golden under the level sun-rays, she recalled a story from Boccaccio that she had loved to dwell upon when her father slept and she crept from her stool to the floor to read the *Decamerone*. It was the tale of the fair Gostanza, who, in her lovesick despair, desired no longer to live but lacked the courage to attack her young life. She had placed herself in a boat, pushed off to sea, and lain down, wrapping her mantle around her head, hoping to be wrecked so that her fear would be helpless to flee from death. The memory had remained a mere thought in Romola's mind, without blossoming into a distinct wish; but now, as she paused again in her pacing to and fro, she saw another boat gliding black against the red gold, carrying a single man toward the bend where the first, smaller boat was moored. Walking on again, she finally saw the man land, pull his boat ashore, and begin to unload cargo. He was perhaps the owner of the smaller boat as well; he would be departing soon, and her opportunity would vanish with him—her chance to purchase that small vessel. She had not yet admitted to herself that she intended to use it, but she felt a sudden eagerness to secure the possibility of using it, revealing the half-unconscious growth of a thought into a desire.

"Is that little boat yours also?" she asked the fisherman, who had looked up, slightly startled by the tall grey figure, and made a reverence to this holy Sister wandering thus mysteriously in the evening solitude.

It was indeed his boat; an old one, hardly seaworthy, yet worth repairing to any man who would buy it. By the blessing of San Antonio, whose chapel stood in the village yonder, his fishing had prospered, and he now possessed a better boat, once belonging to Gianni, who had died. But he had not yet sold the old one. Romola asked him its value, and then, while he was busy, thrust the price into a small satchel lying on the ground, which contained the remnants of his dinner. Afterward, she watched him furl his sail and asked him how he should set it if he wished to go out to sea. Pacing up and down again, she waited to see him depart.

The imagination of herself gliding away in that boat upon the darkening waters grew into a longing, as the thought of a cool brook in sultriness becomes a painful thirst.
