To be freed from the burden of choice when all motive was bruised, to commit herself, sleeping, to destiny which would either bring death or else new necessities that might rouse a new life in her—it was a thought that beckoned her the more because the soft evening air made her long to rest in the still solitude, rather than return to the noise and heat of the village.

At last, the slow fisherman had gathered up all his belongings and was walking away. Soon the gold was shrinking and growing dusky in the sea and sky, and there was no living thing in sight, no sound but the lulling monotony of the lapping waves. In this sea, there was no tide that would carry her away if she waited for its ebb; but Romola thought the breeze from the land was rising slightly. She stepped into the boat, unfurled the sail, and fastened it as she had learned in that first brief lesson. She saw that it caught the light breeze, and this was all she cared for. Then she loosed the boat from its moorings and tried to urge it with an oar until she was far out from the land, until the sea was dark even to the west, and the stars were disclosing themselves like a palpitating life over the wide heavens. Resting at last, she threw back her cowl and, removing the kerchief underneath that confined her hair, doubled them both under her head for a pillow on one of the boat's ribs. Her fair head was still very young and could bear a hard pillow.

Thus she lay, with the soft night air breathing upon her as she glided on the water, watching the deepening quiet of the sky. She was alone now; she had freed herself from all claims, and she had freed herself even from that burden of choice which presses with heavier and heavier weight when claims have loosened their guiding hold.

Had she found anything like the dream of her girlhood? No. Memories hung upon her like the weight of broken wings that could never be lifted—memories of human sympathy which, even in its pains, leaves a thirst that the Great Mother has no milk to still. Romola felt orphaned in those vast spaces of sea and sky. She read no message of love for her in that far-off symbolic writing of the heavens, and with a great sob, she wished that she might be gliding into death.

She drew the cowl over her head again and covered her face, choosing darkness rather than the light of the stars, which seemed to her like the hard light of eyes looking at her without seeing her. Presently, she felt that she was in the grave, yet not resting there; she was touching the hands of the beloved dead beside her, trying to wake them.

February 27th dawned with a peculiar gravity, the air thick with the final breath of Carnival. By ten o'clock, the usual currents of Florentine life had shifted decisively toward San Marco. The populace knew the second Bonfire of Vanities awaited them before the Old Palace, yet the true magnet drew them all to the square. The Piazza di San Marco was a sea of humanity, a surging tide of new arrivals pressing against the rigid front ranks that had already formed a dense, immovable wall around the semicircular barrier guarding the church entrance. Within this enclosure, the Dominican Brethren assembled in silence, their black mantles absorbing the morning light.

Yet, the temporary wooden pulpit erected above the church door remained starkly empty. It was destined for the man the Pope had exiled from the Duomo, the cleric shunned by the ecclesiastical hierarchy and forbidden to the citizens under threat of excommunication. This man, Savonarola, had dared declare that a wicked, bribed Pontiff was no Vicar of Christ, that his curses were broken swords and his commands antithetical to the Christian life. He had proclaimed it lawful not merely to disobey, but to refuse obedience entirely. Despite the Pope's terrifying threats of interdict and ruin, the people still flocked to hear him, still accepted Communion from his hands. They wondered: if this friar truly held sway over divine judgment, why wait for a sign? If God had chosen him to rebuke the High Priest, surely the choice would be attested by unmistakable power.

For months, the demand for a miracle had swelled in Savonarola's ear, a fatal question gnawing at his resolve. As long as his preaching offered hope without immediate peril, no sign was needed; his words were a music to which the city marched willingly. But now, with the threat of commercial collapse and political isolation looming, the crowd demanded proof. "What miracle showest thou?" the question echoed in his mind. His faith wavered, though his speech remained unbroken—a necessary performance for any man speaking to a hungry multitude, relying on yesterday's conviction to fuel tomorrow's hope.

The wooden pulpit had been raised specifically to answer this popular impatience for a supernatural guarantee. As the ordinary friars in black entered, arranging themselves and chanting, the multitude's gaze did not yet fix upon the empty platform. They watched the monks, noting the familiar faces of high-born families mingling with the sons of artisans and shopkeepers who formed the bulk of the crowd. Only when the choir was complete, their voices rising in psalmody, did a hush fall, heavy with anticipation. "Fra Girolamo must be coming now," someone whispered, and the silence deepened, spreading like a pale solemn light over thousands of upturned faces.

In an instant, the pulpit was no longer empty. A figure, cloaked entirely in black from head to foot, stepped inside and knelt, head bowed, face turned away. To the eager crowd, the time seemed interminable as the black figure knelt and the monks continued their chant. Yet the stillness held; the friar's communion with Heaven carried an electric awe that unnerved even those ready to stone him.

Finally, a vibration rippled through the multitude, a silent passing of hands like a shared breath as the expected presence revealed itself. Savonarola rose, turned toward the people, and pushed back his cowl. The monotonous wail of the choir ceased, merging instantly into the force of his flashing glance as he looked out upon the silence. He stretched forth his hands—exquisite, delicate instruments transformed from tools of grasping into vessels of acute sensibility. They seemed to speak from the soul masked by his strong, passionate face, now etched with deeper lines than forty-four years of ordinary life could produce.

At the first extension of his hands, some in the front ranks fell to their knees, and scattered devout disciples followed suit. But the majority stood firm. Some resisted the impulse to kneel before an excommunicated man, fearing judgment upon the act itself; others viewed it with scorn, seeing the ambitious deceiver staging a new comedy, a fashion they felt powerless to resist.

Then came the voice, clear and low, uttering the words of absolution: "*Misereatur vestri*." More fell to their knees. As the voice rose, clearer and stronger, the number of erect heads dwindled until, at the climax "*Benedicat vos omnipotens Deus*," it erupted into a masculine cry, a trumpet blast protesting the demon's clutch that sought to stifle it. The sound rang to the extremities of the Piazza, and under its resonance, every head bowed.

After the blessing, Savonarola himself fell to his knees, hiding his face in temporary exhaustion. Such great jets of emotion were necessary to his existence; he had told the people long ago, "Without preaching I cannot live." But it was a life that shattered him.

Minutes passed. Some rose, but a larger number remained kneeling, all faces fixed intently on him. He held a crystal vessel containing the consecrated Host and addressed the crowd.

"You remember," he said, his voice carrying in the vast stillness, "three days ago I besought you. When I held this Sacrament in my hand before you all, I asked you to pray fervently to the Most High: if this work of mine does not come from Him, let Him send a fire to consume me, that I may vanish into eternal darkness, away from His light which I have hidden with my falsity. Again I beseech you to make that prayer, and to make it *now*."

It was a breathless moment. Perhaps no one truly prayed, save those in devout obedience making the effort. Every consciousness was dominated by the sense that Savonarola was praying, his voice distinct and audible in the wide silence.

"Lord, if I have not wrought in sincerity of soul, if my word cometh not from Thee, strike me in this moment with Thy thunder, and let the fires of Thy wrath enclose me."

He ceased speaking, standing motionless with the consecrated Mystery in his hand, eyes uplifted, his whole aspect quivering with excitement. All else was motionless and silent. Sunlight, which had pierced the greyness in fitful streaks for the last quarter-hour, suddenly parted wider. With a gentle quickness, like a smile, a stream of brightness poured onto the crystal vase and spread over Savonarola's face with mild glorification.

An instantaneous shout tore through the Piazza: "Behold the answer!"

The warm radiance thrilled through Savonarola's frame, and so did the shout. It was his last moment of untroubled triumph. In rapturous confidence, he felt carried to a grander scene, before an audience representing all Christendom, where he would be sealed as the messenger of supreme righteousness, charged with Divine strength. It was but a moment that expanded into a vision. While the shout still rang in his ears, he turned away within the church, feeling the strain too great to bear longer.

When the friar disappeared and the sunlight returned to its impartial, ordinary spreading over clean and unclean alike, a confusion of voices arose alongside the crowd's movement. Strong discords and varying scales of laughter revealed that the previous silence and universal kneeling had been a submission to a momentary spell, hostility and scorn merely holding their ground.

"It seems to me the plaudits are giving way to criticism," observed Tito, watching from an upper loggia opposite the church. "Nevertheless, it was a striking moment, was it not, Messer Pietro? Fra Girolamo is a man to make one understand that there was a time when the monk's frock was a symbol of power over men's minds rather than over the keys of women's cupboards."

"Assuredly," replied Pietro Cennini. "And until I have seen proof that Fra Girolamo has much less faith in God's judgments than the common run of men, instead of having considerably more, I shall not believe that he would brave Heaven in this way if his soul were laden with a conscious lie."

March had arrived, bringing with it the heavy, expectant air of a city holding its breath. A month after the Carnival's chaotic revelry, Tito descended the marble steps of the Old Palace, his destination San Marco, yet his path was deliberately indirect. He chose to loop through the Piazza di Santa Croce, drawn by the swelling tide of people emerging from the great church following the early Lenten sermon.

Inside Santa Croce, the atmosphere had grown feverish. Fra Girolamo Savonarola's voice, once the thunder of the city, had been silenced in his own church of San Marco by a hostile Signoria obeying a papal threat of interdict. Yet, in Santa Croce, the hunger for his words had only intensified, now fueled by the spectacle of his vilification. The crowd's excitement peaked at a daring proposal: a Franciscan preacher, Fra Francesco di Puglia, had challenged Savonarola to walk through fire. The logic was simple and terrifying; if the prophet emerged unscathed, his divine mandate was proven; if consumed, his falsehood exposed. To ensure the test could not be evaded, the Franciscan offered himself as the necessary counterweight, willing to be burned alongside the prophet.

Savonarola, as usual, ignored the pulpit attacks. But the challenge had ignited a spark in Fra Domenico, a fervent follower preaching to women in the Via del Cocomero. He had seized the gauntlet, declaring his readiness to share the flames for his master. The populace, eager for a shortcut to conviction, watched with bated breath. Yet, Savonarola, keenly aware that mere discussion could be dangerous, commanded Fra Domenico to withdraw. The Franciscan accepted, noting he had challenged the prophet directly, not a subordinate.

By the time Tito reached the piazza, the crowd was thick, converging not on the church doors but near the Franciscan monastery. There, pinned to a wall by order of the Signoria, was a large placard covered in official Latin script. Curiosity gnawed at the onlookers; while they knew the gist of the decree, the written word remained a barrier of learned tongues. They whispered among themselves, trying to decipher the government's intent.

"It is the Frate's doctrines he must prove by burning," declared Goro, a prominent figure in the throng. "The Signoria has made it plain."

"Nay, Goro," corrected a sleek shopkeeper with pity. "The Frate must prove his doctrines by *not* being burned. He walks through the fire and emerges whole."

"Yes, but Fra Girolamo objects," interjected a young sculptor, tipping his white-streaked cap. "Being whole already, he sees no need to walk through fire to remain so. He leaves such odds and ends to Fra Domenico."

"Then he flinches like a coward!" Goro wheezed. "Remember the Carnival? He had us watching lightning strike him, and nothing happened."

"Silence your bleating," snapped a tall shoemaker, his shoulders laden with slippers. "You are as wise as a calf with water on its brain. The Frate will flinch from nothing. Perhaps he says nothing beforehand, but when the moment comes, he walks through without asking a grey-frock to accompany him. Though, I would give a shoestring to know what this Latin actually says."

"There is so much of it," sighed the shopkeeper. "Is there no scholar here?"

The heads turned in unison as Tito approached, his black mantle and gold fibula catching the light, his velvet cap marking him as a man of high station.

"Here is one," the sculptor smiled, raising his cap.

"He is the secretary of the Ten," the shopkeeper announced, bowing deeply—a rare honor for a Florentine. "Make way."

Tito acknowledged the deference with a graceful nod. "Thanks, my friend. I merely wished, as you did, to see what is at the foot of this placard. Ah, as I expected. The government permits anyone to subscribe their name as a candidate to enter the fire—an act of liberality worthy of the magnificent Signoria—reserving, of course, their right to select. Surely many believers are eager to sign."

He paused, lifting his shoulders with an oratorical flourish. "What is it to enter the fire to one whose faith is firm? A man fears fire because he believes it burns him; but if he believes the contrary?" He scanned the faces around him. "For which reason, I have never disbelieved the Frate when he said he would enter the fire to prove his doctrine. In his place, if you believed the fire would not burn you, which of you would not step in as readily as you would walk along the dry bed of the Mugnone?"

The crowd shifted, their eyes brightening like dogs scenting something pungent. The abstract debate was becoming practical.

"Then, Messer Segretario," the sculptor pressed, "it seems Fra Francesco is the greater hero, offering to enter for the truth, certain the fire will burn him."

"I do not deny it," Tito replied blandly. "But if Fra Francesco is mistaken, he will be burned for the wrong side, and the Church has never counted such victims as martyrs. We must suspend judgment until the trial truly takes place."

"True enough," the shopkeeper murmured with impatience. "But will you interpret the Latin for us?"

"Assuredly," Tito smiled. "It expresses the doctrines the Frate teaches, which the trial will prove true or false. First, that Florence—"

"Bit by bit, then, and the meaning," the shoemaker insisted.

"Willingly," Tito agreed. "_Ecclesia Dei indiget renovatione_: The Church of God needs purifying or regenerating. That means the priests must lead better lives; no miracle is needed for that. _Flagellabitur_: It will be scourged. _Renovabitur_: It will be purified. _Florentia quoque post flagellam renovabitur et prosperabitur_: Florence also, after the scourging, shall be purified and shall prosper."

"That means we get Pisa again," the shopkeeper declared.

"And the wool from England as we used to," added an elderly man in a berretta. "There has been enough scourging with the sinking of our trade."

Just then, a tall figure topped with a red feather emerged from the convent door, exchanging a casual glance with Tito before the bystanders timidly parted to make way for Messer Dolfo Spini.

"_Infideles convertentur ad Christum_: The infidels shall be converted to Christ," Tito continued. "Those are the Turks and Moors. Well, I have nothing against that."

"_Haec autem omnia erunt temporibus nostris_: And all these things shall happen in our times."

"Why, what use would they be else?" Goro asked.

"_Excommunicato nuper lata contra Reverendum Patrem nostrum Fratrem Hieronymum nulla est_: The excommunication lately pronounced against our reverend father, Fra Girolamo, is null. _Non observantes eam non peccant_: Those who disregard it are not committing a sin."

"I shall know better what to say to that when we have the Trial by Fire," the shopkeeper noted.

"Which doubtless will clear up everything," Tito said. "That is all the Latin—the conclusions to be tested. The rest is simply a proclamation in good Tuscan, calling on those eager to walk through the fire to come to the Palazzo and subscribe their names."

With an easy, natural bow, Tito turned and quickened his pace. After a few turns, he paused before a quiet door, knocking with a light, peculiar rhythm. A young woman opened it, and he chucked her under the chin before passing through an ajar inner door into a handsome, untidy room.

Dolfo Spini sat there, playing with a fine stag-hound that alternately sniffed a basket of pups and licked his hands with affectionate disregard for her master's morals. Spini looked up briefly as Tito entered, then resumed his play, lost in the persistence of an irrelevant action.

"A handsome *bracca* that," Tito said quietly, standing with his thumbs in his belt. "When you have finished such caresses as cannot possibly be deferred, my Dolfo, we will talk of business. My time, which I could wish to be eternity at your service, is not entirely my own this morning."

"Down, Mischief, down!" Spini snapped, pushing the dog aside with sudden roughness. "Malediction!" He stood close, placing a hand on Tito's shoulder. "I hope your sharp wits see all the ins and outs of this business, my fine necromancer, for it seems to me no clearer than the bottom of a sack."

"These accursed Frati Minori at Santa Croce are drawing back," Spini complained. "Fra Francesco himself seems afraid of sticking to the challenge. He talks of the Prophet using magic to create a false miracle, thinks he might be burned while the Prophet emerges whole by sorcery, leaving the Church no better. And after all our talking, there isn't even a blessed lay brother willing to pair with that pious sheep, Fra Domenico."

"It is the peculiar stupidity of the tonsured skull that prevents them from seeing how little consequence it is whether they are burned or not," Tito said calmly. "Have you sworn them no danger of entering the fire?"

"No," Spini looked puzzled. "Because one of them will be obliged to go in with Fra Domenico, who thinks it a thousand years till the fagots are ready."

"Not at all. Fra Domenico himself is unlikely to go in. I have told you, my Dolfo, that now you have the Signoria taking up this affair to prevent Fra Girolamo from hushing it up, nothing is required but that on a given day the fuel be prepared in the Piazza and the people gathered expecting a prodigy. If, after that, the Prophet quits the Piazza without a miracle, he is ruined. The people will pelt him out of the city; the Signoria will banish him; and His Holiness may do as he pleases. Therefore, my Alcibiades, swear to the Franciscans that their grey-frocks shall not come within singeing distance of the fire."

Spini rubbed the back of his head and tapped his sword against his leg, stimulating his mind to grasp the intangible. "But unless we fall on him in the Piazza when the people are in a rage and make an end of him and his lies then and there, Valori and the Salviati and the Albizzi will take up arms for him. I know this was talked of on Ascension Sunday. The people may turn again; there may be a story of the French king returning, or some other cursed chance in the hypocrite's favor. The city will never be safe till he is out of it."

"He *will* be out of it before long, without your giving yourself further trouble than this little comedy of the Trial by Fire," Tito assured him. "The wine and the sun will make vinegar without any shouting to help them, as your Florentine sages would say. You will have the satisfaction of delivering your city from an incubus by an able stratagem, instead of risking blunders with sword-thrusts."

"But suppose he *did* get magic and the devil to help him and walk through the fire after all?" Spini grimaced, hiding a shyness in treading this speculative ground. "How do you know there is nothing in those things? Plenty of scholars believe in them, and this Frate is bad enough for anything."

"Oh, of course there are such things," Tito shrugged. "But I have particular reasons for knowing the Frate is not on such terms with the devil as can give him confidence in this affair. The only magic he relies on is his own ability."

"Ability!" Spini scoffed. "Do you call it ability to set Florence at loggerheads with the Pope and all the powers of Italy—all to keep beckoning at the French king who never comes? You may call him able, but I call him a hypocrite who wants to be master of everybody and get himself made Pope."

"You judge with your usual penetration, my captain, but our opinions do not clash," Tito said with bland gravity. "The Frate, wanting to be master and carrying out his projects against the Pope, requires the lever of a foreign power and Florence as a fulcrum. I used to think him a narrow-minded bigot, but now I think him a shrewd ambitious man who knows what he aims at and directs his aim as skilfully as you direct a ball when playing at *maglio*."

"Yes, yes," Spini nodded cordially. "I can aim a ball."

"It is true," Tito said. "And I should not have troubled you with my trivial remark on the Frate's ability, but that you may see how this will heighten the credit of your success against him at Rome and Milan, which is sure to serve you in good stead when the city changes its policy."

"Well, thou art a good little demon, and shalt have good pay," Spini said patronizingly. The Greek adventurer smiled with gratification. "Of course, any advantage to me depends entirely on your—"

"We shall have our supper at my palace tonight," Spini interrupted, nodding significantly and patting Tito's shoulder affectionately. "And I shall expound the new scheme to them all."

"Pardon, my magnificent patron," Tito said. "The scheme has been the same from the first—it has never varied except in your memory. Are you sure you have a fast hold of it now?"

Spini rehearsed the plan.

"One thing more," he said as Tito hastened away. "There is that sharp-nosed notary, Ser Ceccone; he has been handy of late. Tell me, you who can see a man wink when you are behind him, do you think I may go on making use of him?"

Tito dared not say "No." He knew his companion too well to trust him with advice when all Spini's vanity and self-interest were not engaged in concealing the adviser.

"Doubtless," he answered promptly. "I have nothing to say against Ceccone."

The suggestion of the notary's intimate access to Spini caused Tito a passing twinge, interrupting

The Prophet in His Cell.

The announcement of Tito's arrival had preceded him, and Fra Niccolò, Savonarola's secretary, ushered him immediately up the spiraling stairs into the monastery's long, echoing corridors. These halls, lined with cells, bore witness to the delicate artistry of Fra Angelico; his frescoes, soft as rainbows on melting clouds, broke the austerity of the stone, casting sudden, ethereal reflections of the Madonna in radiant glory and the Divine Infant with perpetual promise upon the unaccustomed eye.

It was a hour of quietude within the monastery, and most cells stood empty. Light filtered through narrow windows to illuminate nothing but bare walls, hard pallets, and crucifixes. Even at the far end of a long corridor, in the inner cell accessible only through an antechamber where the Prior typically sat at his desk, a single shaft of light fell upon a solitary figure. It was a man in the long white Dominican tunic and scapulary, kneeling with bowed head before a crucifix. To the casual observer, he might have been any ordinary Fra Girolamo, confessing only trivial sins—thoughts of impropriety during choir service or a fleeting spite when a fellow monk dropped ink upon his illuminated breviary—a soul content merely to climb the narrow ladder of prayer, fasting, and obedience toward Paradise. Yet beneath this plain white garment beat a heart pulsing with a consciousness alien to the average monk, perhaps incomprehensible to any man who has not traversed a tumultuous inner life. Here, irrevocable errors and lapses from truth were inextricably entwined with noble purposes and sincere beliefs; self-justifying expediency was woven into the very fabric of a great work that the whole being seemed unable to abandon, just as the body cannot cease trembling before the objects of hope and fear. Consequently, no matter the course chosen, perfect repose for the conscience remained elusive.

Savonarola was not merely in the posture of prayer; Latin supplications flowed from his lips, yet he was not truly praying. He had collapsed to his knees seeking a calmness that would serve as a warrant against the resolutions urged upon him by crowding thoughts and passions, fearing he was being wrested from Divine support. But the previsions and impulses working within him for the last hour were too imperious. While his hands pressed against his face and his lips uttered *"Cor mundum crea in me,"* his mind remained cluttered with images of the snares his enemies had prepared, busy constructing arguments to justify himself against their taunts.

His defense was required not only against his opponents. That morning, he had received fresh proof that his own friends and followers were as eager to press for the Trial by Fire as his adversaries. They desired and tacitly expected that he would finally accept the challenge, evoking the long-awaited miracle to dissipate doubt and triumph over malignity. Had he not declared that God would reveal Himself at the fitting time? To the plain Florentines, eager to settle party questions, no time seemed more fitting than the present. Certainly, if Fra Domenico walked through the flames unhurt, *that* would be a miracle, and the fervor of that good brother served as a cheering augury. Yet Savonarola was acutely conscious that his reasons for refusal had failed to quell the secret longing of his followers to see him accept the challenge.

It was impossible for him to satisfy them. With bitter distress, he realized he could no longer resist the prosecution of the trial in Fra Domenico's case. He had not spoken a falsehood when declaring his belief in a future supernatural attestation; however, his constitution was such that while it was easy for him to believe in a distant, undefined miracle screened by strong reasons, and even easier to believe in inward miracles like his own prophetic inspiration, it was insurmountably difficult for him to believe in the probability of a miracle pressing all its details upon his imagination—a miracle demanding not just belief but exceptional action.

Savonarola's nature held opposing tendencies in almost equal strength: a passionate sensibility, impatient of definite thought and flooding every idea with emotion, alternating with a keen perception of outward facts and a vigorous practical judgment. In this case, the latter characteristics were stimulated by an acute physical sensitiveness that gave overpowering force to the conception of pain and destruction as a necessary sequence of past experiences. The promptitude with which men consent to touch red-hot iron with a wet finger is not measured by their theoretical acceptance of the impossibility of burning; practical belief depends on what is most strongly represented in the mind at a given moment. With the Frate's constitution, when the Trial by Fire was urged upon his imagination as an immediate demand, it was impossible for him to believe that he or any other man could walk through the flames unhurt—impossible for him to believe that even if he resolved to offer himself, he would not shrink at the last moment.

But the Florentines were unlikely to make such fine distinctions. To the common run of mankind, finding moral questions easy and judging conduct by concise alternatives is a proof of mental vigor. Nothing seemed plainer than that a man who declared God would not leave him without a miraculous guarantee, yet drew back when the test was proposed, had said what he did not believe. Were not Fra Domenico and Fra Mariano, along with scores of Piagnoni, ready to enter the fire? What caused their superior courage if not superior faith? Savonarola could not explain his conduct satisfactorily to his friends, nor thoroughly to himself. Our naked feelings hasten to clothe themselves in propositions lying at hand among our store of opinions; to give a true account of what passes within us requires something besides sincerity, even when that sincerity is unmixed. In these very moments, as Savonarola knelt in audible prayer, he had ceased to hear the words on his lips. They were drowned by argumentative voices within him shaping reasons for an outward audience.

"To appeal to heaven for a miracle by a rash acceptance of a challenge, which is a mere snare prepared by ignoble foes, would be a tempting of God, and the appeal would not be responded to. Let the Pope's legate come, let the ambassadors of all great Powers come and promise that the calling of a General Council and the reform of the Church shall hang on the miracle, and I will enter the flames, trusting that God will not withhold His seal from that great work. Until then, I reserve myself for higher duties directly laid upon me: it is not permitted to me to leap from the chariot for the sake of wrestling with every loud vaunter. But Fra Domenico's invincible zeal to enter the trial may be the sign of a Divine vocation, may be a pledge that the miracle—"

No! When Savonarola brought his mind close to the threatened scene in the Piazza, imagining a human body entering the fire, his belief recoiled. It was not an event his imagination could simply see; he felt it with shuddering vibrations traveling to the extremities of his sensitive fingers. The miracle could not be. Nay, the trial itself was not to happen; he was warranted in doing all in his power to hinder it. The fuel might be ready in the Piazza, the people assembled, the formalities gone through—all this was perhaps inevitable now, and he could no longer resist it without bringing dishonor upon himself, yes, and therefore upon the cause of God. But it was not really intended that the Franciscan should enter the fire; while *he* hung back, there would be means to prevent Fra Domenico's entrance. At the very worst, if Fra Domenico were compelled to enter, he should carry the consecrated Host with him, and with that Mystery in his hand, there might be a warrant for expecting that the ordinary effects of fire would be stayed; or, more probably, this demand would be resisted, becoming a final obstacle to the trial.

These intentions could not be avowed; he must appear frankly to await the trial and trust in its issue. This dissidence between inward reality and outward seeming was not the Christian simplicity after which he had striven through years of youth and prime, which he had preached as a chief fruit of the Divine life. In the stress and heat of the day, with cheeks burning and shouts ringing in the ears, who is so blest as to remember the yearnings of the cool, silent morning and know that he has not belied them?

"O God, it is for the sake of the people—because they are blind—because their faith depends on me. If I put on sackcloth and cast myself among the ashes, who will take up the standard and head the battle? Have I not been led by a way I knew not to the work that lies before me?"

The conflict could not end. In the effort at prayerful pleading, the uneasy mind laved its smart continually in thoughts of the greatness of that task which there was no other man to fulfill if he forsook it. It was not a thing of everyday that a man should be inspired with the vision and daring that made a sacred rebel.

Even the words of prayer had died away. He continued to kneel, but his mind was filled with images of results to be felt through all Europe; the sense of immediate difficulties was being lost in the glow of that vision, when the knocking at the door announced the expected visit.

Savonarola drew on his mantle before leaving his cell, as was his custom when receiving visitors. With that immediate response to any appeal from without which belongs to a power-loving nature accustomed to make its power felt by speech, he met Tito with a glance as self-possessed and strong as if he had risen from resolution instead of conflict.

Tito did not kneel but simply offered a greeting of profound deference, which Savonarola received quietly without sacerdotal words. Desiring him to be seated, he asked at once: "Your business is something of weight, my son, that could not be conveyed through others?"

"Assuredly, father, else I should not have presumed to ask it. I will not trespass on your time by any proem. I gathered from a remark of Messer Domenico Mazzinghi that you might be glad to make use of the next special courier sent to France with despatches from the Ten. I must entreat you to pardon me if I have been too officious; but inasmuch as Messer Domenico is at this moment away at his villa, I wished to apprise you that a courier carrying important letters is about to depart for Lyons at daybreak tomorrow."

The muscles of Fra Girolamo's face were eminently under command, as must be the case with all men whose personality is powerful, and in deliberate speech he was habitually cautious, confiding his intentions to none without necessity. But under any strong mental stimulus, his eyes were liable to a dilatation and added brilliancy that no strength of will could control. He looked steadily at Tito and did not answer immediately, as if considering whether the information he had just heard met any purpose of his.

Tito, whose glance never seemed observant but rarely let anything escape it, had expected precisely that dilatation and flash of Savonarola's eyes which he had noted on other occasions. He saw it, and then immediately busied himself in adjusting his gold fibula, which had become misaligned, seemingly implying that he awaited an answer patiently.

The fact was that Savonarola had expected to receive this intimation from Domenico Mazzinghi, one of the Ten, an ardent disciple whom he had already employed to write a private letter to the Florentine ambassador in France, preparing the way for a letter to the French king himself in Savonarola's handwriting, which now lay ready in the desk at his side. It was a letter calling on the king to assist in summoning a General Council that might reform the abuses of the Church and begin by deposing Pope Alexander, who was not rightfully Pope, being a vicious unbeliever elected by corruption and governing by simony.

This fact was not what Tito knew, but what his constructive talent, guided by subtle indications, had led him to guess and hope.

"It is true, my son," said Savonarola quietly, "it is true I have letters which I would gladly send by safe conveyance under cover to our ambassador. Our community of San Marco, as you know, has affairs in France, being, amongst other things, responsible for a debt to that singularly wise and experienced Frenchman, Signor Philippe de Comines, on the library of the Medici, which we purchased; but I apprehend that Domenico Mazzinghi himself may return to the city before evening, and I should gain more time for preparation of the letters if I waited to deposit them in his hands."

"Assuredly, reverend father, that might be better on all grounds, except one, namely, that if anything occurred to hinder Messer Domenico's return, the despatch of the letters would require either that I should come to San Marco again at a late hour, or that you should send them to me by your secretary; and I am aware that you wish to guard against the false inferences which might be drawn from a too frequent communication between yourself and any officer of the government." In throwing out this difficulty, Tito felt that the more unwillingness the Frate showed to trust him, the more certain he would be of his conjecture.

Savonarola was silent; but while he kept his mouth firm, a slight glow rose in his face with the suppressed excitement growing within him. It would be a critical moment—that in which he delivered the letter out of his own hands.

"It is most probable that Messer Domenico will return in time," said Tito, affecting to consider the Frate's determination settled, and rising from his chair as he spoke. "With your permission, I will take my leave, father, not to trespass on your time when my errand is done; but as I may not be favoured with another interview, I venture to confide to you—what is not yet known to others, except to the magnificent Ten—that I contemplate resigning my secretaryship and leaving Florence shortly. Am I presuming too much on your interest in stating what relates chiefly to myself?"

"Speak on, my son," said the Frate; "I desire to know your prospects."

"I find, then, that I have mistaken my real vocation in forsaking the career of pure letters, for which I was brought up. The politics of Florence, father, are worthy to occupy the greatest mind—to occupy yours—when a man is in a position to execute his own ideas; but when, like me, he can only hope to be the mere instrument of changing schemes,

The Piazza della Signoria, usually a stage for civic pride, transformed into a theater of suspense on the seventh of April. A crowd, denser than any gathered for the Feast of San Giovanni, pressed against every available inch of the square. Men hung precariously from iron rods jutting from walls, wedged themselves between rough stone pillars, and balanced atop the necks of statues, their limbs cramped and skin chafed, yet utterly unmindful of discomfort. They had come to witness a miracle. While humanity often hears of wonders and accepts them with varying degrees of skepticism, the Florentines were about to see one unfold before their eyes. At the very least, they would witness the descent; if the monk emerged unscathed, it would be a divine sign; if he perished, the verdict would be equally clear.

Before them stood the long platform, eight feet wide and stretching twenty yards across the marble terrace near the Old Palace, flanked by the frowning gaze of the stone lion, the Marzocco. Upon it lay a terrifying grove of fuel: dry oak branches, crackling thorns, and tow soaked in oil, prepared to roar into a consuming flame. To ensure the spectacle's gravity, the Signoria had deployed five hundred hired soldiers before the palace, while five hundred Compagnacci under Dolfo Spini waited on the opposite side. Between them, three hundred armed citizens loyal to Savonarola, led by Marco Salviati, stood guard near Orgagna's Loggia, where the Franciscans and Dominicans were to face each other.

The air buzzed with anticipation as the Franciscans took their silent, grey-robed places on one side of the loggia. From the other side, a deafening chant rose from two hundred voices: "Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered." The leader, Fra Domenico, wore a cope of flame-colored velvet, his simple mind exalted by faith and his genuine intent to walk into the fire for the glory of God and his rival, Fra Girolamo. Behind him walked Savonarola in white vestments, carrying the consecrated Host. He, too, projected an aura of unshakeable confidence, his face flashing with energy under the scrutiny of thousands.

Yet, beneath this public performance, a profound internal struggle tore at Savonarola. He was not merely acting the part of a prophet facing martyrdom; he was performing a desperate charade to avoid impending ruin. The weight of foreboding pressed upon his heart, heavier than the leaden sky above. Two evenings prior, the courier of the Ten had been arrested, his dispatches stolen. Savonarola's private letters were now in the hands of the Duke of Milan and soon the Pope, providing the very justification for the extreme measures currently being plotted against him.

Watching this drama from a window of the Old Palace was Tito Melema. His satisfaction was not born of malice, but of the mild self-gratification of a gambler who had finally won. To Tito, the complex web of human nature, molded into the figure of a plain-faced friar, was merely a puzzle solved. Fra Girolamo was an immensely clever man, capable of keeping secrets and mixing absurd superstition with shrewd political notions, but he was also a tool Tito had used to climb the ladder. Now, with the trap sprung, Tito intended to leave Florence entirely. The "Trial by Fire" was merely the final act of a comedy written by Tito, one that the thick-headed Dolfo Spini could never have orchestrated alone.

As the chanting swelled into a roar, joined by the Piagnoni waving their red crosses, the tension in the square reached a fever pitch. The Franciscans, however, remained silent, their grey robes a stark contrast to the fiery colors of their opponents. They had two champions: Fra Giuliano paired with Fra Domenico, while the original challenger, Fra Francesco, reserved his dispute solely for Savonarola.

The crowd grew restless. "All must be ready now," they thought, watching the flame-colored cope. But the Dominicans did not move. Instead, figures passed to and fro between the loggia and the palace. Dignitaries debated with the Franciscans, and eventually, Fra Domenico was summoned inside. The crowd assumed the fire had been lit and the miracle was imminent. But the cope vanished, only to return moments later with another Dominican, guarded by Franciscans lest he be "enchanted."

The realization dawned slowly: Fra Domenico was not entering the fire. The focus shifted to Fra Girolamo. He stood firm, delivering a bold, ironic speech demanding the cessation of delays, reminiscent of Elijah challenging the priests of Baal. But words were useless to a crowd hungry for action and trapped in uncomfortable positions. If the miracle did not begin, the blame would fall squarely on Fra Girolamo for failing to offer himself when the fuel was ready.

Time slipped away as clouds gathered, darkening the light and sending a chill through the hungry, impatient multitude. Suddenly, the dynamic shifted. Fra Domenico insisted on carrying a crucifix into the fire, an object the Franciscans deemed too sacred to profane. Reluctantly yielding, Savonarola handed him the vessel containing the Host instead. A theological debate erupted instantly. Was it impious to burn the Sacrament? Could the "Accidents" of the Eucharist survive the flames while the "Substance" remained? The question was elastic, impossible to settle by fire, yet both sides dug in their heels.

The sky darkened further, and the air grew colder. The chanting faded into confused murmurs and rising shouts of scorn. "Light the fire!" "Drive them in!" "What is your last vision?" The crowd's patience evaporated, replaced by a chaotic uproar of clashing metal and trampling feet. Dolfo Spini's Compagnacci surged forward, eager to seize the "arch-hypocrite," pushing the civilians aside and threatening the loggia.

But the guard under Marco Salviati held firm, repulsing the rush. In that moment of utter confusion, the heavens opened. Rain began to fall, initially in scattered drops, then with violent force. It soaked the dry fuel on the platform, turning the potential inferno into a damp mess. Streams of water ran off the stones, drenching the weary, hungry crowd. The rain washed away the dust of the square and extinguished the possibility of the trial before it could truly begin.

The Signoria, relieved to have an excuse to disperse the dangerous gathering, declared the event over. Savonarola stepped away from the loggia, the rain plastering his white robes to his frame. He knew the design of his enemies was fulfilled; his honor was not saved. He would have to face the enraged mob again, knowing that friends who once defended him with their lives would now turn against him.

He asked for a guard from the Signoria, which was granted. He had been willing to die for his cause, but to die in dishonor, branded a false prophet? "O God! That is not martyrdom," he thought, his courage mounting despite the moral assault. "It is the blotting out of a life that has been a protest against wrong. Let me die because of the worth that is in me, not because of my weakness."

As the rain ceased and the breaking clouds cast a pale, impartial light upon him, Savonarola walked away with the Sacrament in his hand. There was no glory in the light, no smile from heaven. Only the cold, steady illumination that justifies or condemns by simply revealing the truth of things. He heard no blessings, only taunts and threats. He knew this was a foretaste of the bitterness to come, yet his spirit remained unbroken.

"Well parried, Frate!" Tito Melema called out as Savonarola descended the steps. "But I fear your career at Florence is ended. What say you, my Niccolò?"

"It is a pity his falsehoods were not all of a wise sort," Machiavelli replied, shaking his head with a melancholy shrug. "With the times so much on his side as they are about Church affairs, he might have done something great."

The following day was Palm Sunday, known in the olive-rich valleys of the Valdarno simply as Olive Sunday. The morning sun broke with a crystalline clarity, washing away the lingering damp of the previous night's rain. Once more, Savonarola ascended the pulpit of San Marco, gazing upon a congregation whose faith remained unshaken. In a tone of calm, sorrowful sincerity, he proclaimed his readiness to die, seeing his own doom clearly before him, undisturbed by any other vision. He offered the benediction, and beneath his gaze rose the faces of men and women lifted in veneration and love. Then, having descended the steps, he turned his back upon that sight forever.

Before the sun had set, Florence erupted into chaos. The passions stirred the day prior had smoldered through the quiet morning, now bursting forth with a fury aided by design and sanctioned by official complicity. The disturbance began at the Duomo, where certain Compagnacci attempted to disrupt the evening sermon the Piagnoni had gathered to hear. But once blood was drawn and the gathering turned to affray, the cry rang out: "To San Marco! Fire to San Marco!"

Long before darkness fully claimed the sky, the church and convent were besieged by an enraged, swelling multitude. Resistance was fierce. The monks, long aware of the hostility brewing outside, kept arms within their walls; some fought with such vigor in their long white tunics that they resembled Knights Templar. Even Savonarola's command could not quell the instinct for self-defense that surged beneath the Dominican serge. Laymen who had refused to flee also fought desperately. From the high altar near the great crucifix came the crack of gunfire; from the convent roof, stones and burning embers rained down; within the cloisters, swords clashed in close combat. Despite the overwhelming force of the attackers, the assault raged until the depths of night.

The government's demonstrations had targeted the convent directly. Early in the attack, guards were summoned not to disperse the mob, but to order everyone inside to lay down arms, to command all laymen to depart, and to demand that Savonarola himself leave Florentine territory within twelve hours. Had Savonarola obeyed then, he likely would have been torn apart; he was willing to go, but his friends restrained him. It was deemed too perilous even for men of high standing to exit via the garden wall, yet among those who chose to flee was Francesco Valori, hoping to rally rescue forces from without.

Now, as the night grew deep—and the struggle could hardly endure much longer, with the Compagnacci poised to breach the library where Savonarola prayed with the brethren who had laid down their arms—a second body of guards arrived. Commissioned by the Signoria, they demanded the surrender of Fra Girolamo and his two coadjutors, Fra Domenico and Fra Salvestro.

A roar of triumphant hate echoed as the lantern-light revealed the friar emerging from the convent door, escorted by a guard offering no safety save that of the prison. The struggle shifted instantly to a race: who should reach the stream rushing up the narrow street first to witness the Prophet carried back in ignominy to the Piazza, where he had dared them the day before? Who should stand closest to hurl insults, or worse, to strike and trample him? This was easy for some armed Compagnacci, who mingled freely with the guards.

As Savonarola felt himself dragged and shoved through the jeering crowd, as lanterns were raised to reveal mocking faces, and as he endured spittle, blows, and kicks accompanied by the grossest insults, it seemed to him that the bitterest cup of life had been drained. If men judged him guilty and thirsted for his blood, death alone awaited him. Yet the deepest bitterness cannot be wrung from the lips by external force; the lowest depth of resignation is not found in martyrdom, but only when one covers one's head in silence and feels, "I am not worthy to be a martyr; the Truth shall prosper, but not by me."

That brief, imperfect triumph of humiliating the friar, who soon vanished beneath the doorway of the Old Palace, was merely like the taste of blood to a tiger. Were there not the houses of the hypocrite's friends to be sacked? Already, half the armed multitude, too far in the rear to share heavily in the siege, had turned to the more profitable work of attacking wealthy homes. They did not plunder with planless greed, but with discriminating selection, striking only the residences of chief Piagnoni. This showed the riot was guided, the rabble with clubs and staves well officered by sword-girt Compagnacci. Next in rank of criminality stood the ambitious Francesco Valori, suspected of plotting with the Frate to make himself a Doge or Gonfaloniere for life. And there was the grey-haired man who, eight months prior, had raised his voice in ferocious demands for justice against five fellow citizens. He had barely escaped San Marco to experience what *others* called justice: his house surrounded by an angry, greedy throng, his wife struck dead by an arrow, and himself murdered on his way to answer a summons to the Palazzo by the swords of men named Ridolfi and Tornabuoni.

Thus, this Masque of the Furies, called Riot, was enacted in Florence through the hours of night and into the early morning.

Yet the chief director remained invisible, issuing orders from a private retreat, for his name was too high to let his red feather wave amidst the work done before dawn. The retreat was the same house and room in a quiet street between Santa Croce and San Marco where we had seen Tito pay a secret visit to Dolfo Spini. Here the Captain of the Compagnacci sat through this memorable night, receiving visitors who came and went, some disguised as armed Compagnacci, others dressed obscurely without visible arms. Abundant wine lay on the table, cups ready for chance comers. Though Spini guarded against excess, he drank enough to heighten the excitement produced by the ceaseless news brought to him.

Among the obscurely dressed visitors, Ser Ceccone was one of the most frequent. As the hours advanced toward the morning twilight, he remained Spini's constant companion, joined by Francesco Cei, who was then in careless hiding in Florence, expecting his banishment revoked once the Frate's fall was accomplished.

The tapers had burned themselves into low, shapeless masses, and holes in the shutters were marked by a sombre outward light, when Spini, having risen and paced with an angry flush on his face after some talk with those two unmilitary companions, burst out:

"The devil spit him! He shall pay for it, though. Ha, ha! The claws shall be down on him when he little thinks of them. So *he* was to be the great man after all! He has been pretending to chuck everything towards my cap, as if I were a blind beggar, all the while winking and filling his own *scarella*. I should like to hang skins about him and set my hounds on him! And he has that fine ruby of mine, which I was a fool enough to give him yesterday. Malediction! And he was laughing at me in his sleeve two years ago, spoiling the best plan that ever was laid. I was a fool for trusting myself with a rascal who had long-twisted contrivances that nobody could see to the end but himself."

"A Greek, too," said Francesco Cei, a slight smile of amusement on his face at Spini's fuming, "who dropped into Florence with gems packed about him. You did not choose your confidant very wisely, my Dolfo."

"He is a cursed deal cleverer than you, Francesco, and handsomer too," Spini retorted, turning on his associate with a general desire to worry anything that presented itself.

"I humbly conceive," said Ser Ceccone, "that Messer Francesco's poetic genius will outweigh—"

"Yes, yes, rub your hands! I hate that notary's trick of yours," interrupted Spini, whose patronage consisted largely in this sort of frankness. "But here comes Taddeo, or somebody: now is the time! What news, eh?" he continued as two Compagnacci entered with heated looks.

"Bad!" said one. "The people have made up their minds they were going to sack Soderini's house, and now that they have been balked, we shall have them turning on us if we do not take care. I suspect there are some Mediceans buzzing about among them, and we may see them attacking your palace over the bridge before long, unless we can find another bait for them."

"I have it!" cried Spini. Seizing Taddeo by the belt, he drew him aside to give directions while the other went on telling Cei how the Signoria had interfered regarding Soderini's house.

"Ecco!" exclaimed Spini, presently giving Taddeo a slight push toward the door. "Go, and make quick work."

On the far bank of the Arno, where the twilight had settled into a chill, slate-grey hue, another figure emerged from the shadows, moving with a purpose entirely disconnected from the armed bands crossing the river nearby. This man sought not the city's heart, but its neglected margins. He made his way to a stretch of the riverbank hidden from view by towering warehouses and granaries, a place where the silence was heavy and the air damp with the scent of rotting rushes and stagnant gutters. Here, amidst the long, sodden grass, he sat down, bending his weary frame over the swift, dark current.

He had come for the river's bounty. In times past, a kind-hearted beggar had dropped a crust of bread, a raw carrot, or apple parings into his lap. These small, anonymous gifts were the only reason he endured the cold vigil. To wait here, unseen, was preferable to the silent, humiliating supplication required on the church steps beyond the Porta San Frediano.

Baldassarre loathed begging with a visceral hatred; he would have preferred death to the act of asking for charity. Yet, he lived for a single, consuming reason. It was no longer a hope, but a delusion that had taken root deep in his mind—a possibility so persistent it defied logic. It was the kind of obsession that drives a woman to watch a headland for a ship long known to be a wreck, refusing to accept the neighbors' certainty of doom. Since fleeing the convent hospital of San Miniato, where the monks had tended him only until he became helpless, Baldassarre had searched in vain for the Wife who was to be his salvation. She had vanished, likely claimed by the pestilence that seemed to swallow Florence whole. Without her, the idea of sacred vengeance through his own hand had crumbled. His knife was lost, and his body and mind were too shattered to forge another weapon or devise a plan. He was a broken, bewildered old man, yet he clung to life, waiting for something formless and dim that pulsed within him with the intensity of a waking dream. He crept out into the grey light, seated himself in the dank grass, and watched the waters, searching for a faint, elusive promise.

While Baldassarre waited in solitude, the Compagnacci were already in motion. The formidable bands of armed men, left largely unchecked by a distracted Signoria, had split into two streams, both converging on the Arno. One mass headed for the Ponte Rubaconte, the other for the Ponte Vecchio, yet their destination was identical: the Via de' Bardi. A whispered command had spread through the rabble, promising plunder and the sack of a specific house. The leaders understood the true intent: to ambush the master of that house in his morning slumber.

But the master, Tito, was neither asleep nor in bed. The events of the previous day had driven him to flee Florence. Investigations were imminent, and delay meant danger. Fear, once a tangible armor he had worn, had died, only to be replaced by its ghost—the undying habit of dread. He felt unsafe within the city's fierce, turbid walls. His house was being handed over to a new tenant, his horses awaited in San Gallo, and his family, Tessa and the children, were lodged safely in the Borgo. Tito descended the stone steps into his courtyard, passing through the great doorway. He was no longer the man who had once fallen in love with Romola; that life was cast off, and he was ready to depart.

He turned swiftly toward the Piazza dei Mozzi, intending to cross the Ponte Rubaconte, but sounds caught his ear, forcing him to reverse course. Was the mob approaching from the Oltrarno? He hurried toward the Ponte Vecchio, drawing his mantle tight around him. Before he could reach the end of the Via de' Bardi, the sounds grew louder, unmistakable now. The mob was coming over the bridge. Driven by an impulse stronger than reason, he turned to confirm it, only to find the street filled with the throng entering from the opposite end. He did not retreat to his house; he reasoned they would not attack him personally, yet he carried valuables, and chaos made all things possible. Necessity forged his courage. He marched toward the bridge, the roar of trampling feet and confused shouts drowning out everything else.

At the end of the street, the crowd pouring from the bridge hemmed him in. Before he could process the sudden shout rising around him, he felt himself surrounded not by unarmed rioters, but by armed Compagnacci. His cap was knocked from his head, and he was shoved violently forward into the crush of the mob along the narrow bridge passage. The cries erupted instantly: "Piagnone! Medicean! Throw him over the bridge!"

Strong hands tore at his mantle, threatening to strangle him until his belt buckle gave way. They snatched at his money pouch, the *scarsella*, but he was already being dragged and hustled. Shouts of execration rang in his ears, spreading panic even to those who had not yet recognized him. Tito's horror was that he would be trampled or struck down before reaching the open arches of the bridge. His only hope was that they would throw him into the river before inflicting fatal wounds. His entire soul focused on this desperate gamble.

They reached the central arches. In that moment of extremity, Tito summoned a final reserve of strength. With a frantic effort, he unclasped his belt and flung the pouch forward onto a patch of clear space near the parapet, shouting in a ringing voice, "There are diamonds! There is gold!"

In the instant the mob's attention shifted to the pouch, Tito seized his chance. He threw himself onto the parapet and leaped with all his might, plunging into the dark river below with a great splash. It was his salvation. He had survived drowning before, and as he broke the surface, gasping for air, he felt a surge of deliverance. He struck out with the energy of his youth, aided by the current. If he could reach the Ponte alla Carrara, he might land in a remote district and make his way to San Gallo. Life was still possible. The idiot mob on the bridge, shouting and bellowing, would assume he was drowned.

They believed him. Peering over the parapet into the darkness, they saw nothing but the moving blackness of floating hair and velvet sleeves.

From the other side, however, a pale olive face emerged from the dark water, white and strained against the current. It was a face unforgettable in its features: a square forehead, long low arches of eyebrows, and lustrous, agate-like eyes. The face drifted onward, nostrils inflated, blue veins distended on the temples. He passed the bridge of Santa Trinita. Should he risk landing now? No. He heard, or imagined he heard, cries pursuing him. Terror pressed hardest from the side of his fellow men; he was less afraid of fate than of humanity, and he swam on, panting and straining, though exhaustion began to cloud his senses.

The last bridge passed. He felt vaguely that he was safe, but the current carried him blindly, exhaustion creeping into a dreamy state bordering on unconsciousness.

Then, eyes discerned him—aged, strong eyes fixed on the distance. Baldassarre, looking up blankly from his vigil by the runlet, saw a white object drifting along the broader stream. Could this be fortune? He watched until the object took form, then leaned forward with a start, his eyes filling with a new, fierce light. Yet he remained motionless. Something was being brought to him.

The next instant, a man's body was cast violently onto the grass just two yards from him. Baldassarre sprang forward like a panther, clutching the velvet tunic as he fell upon the body, flashing a look into the man's face.

Dead? The eyes were rigid. But no, it could not be. Justice often brought men back from the brink. Baldassarre did not feel feeble now; he knew exactly what he could do. He inserted his large fingers into the neck of the tunic, holding them tight, kneeling on one knee beside the body and watching the face. A fierce hope burned in his heart, mixed with trembling. In his eyes, there was only ferocity; the slow-burning remnant of life within him seemed to leap into flame.

Rigid. Still rigid. Those eyes with half-fallen lids were locked against vengeance. Could he truly be dead? Time was hard to measure, but it seemed long enough for hope to freeze into despair.

Surely now the eyelids were quivering. The eyes were no longer rigid. A vibrating light flickered within them; they opened wide.

"Ah, yes! You see me—you know me!"

Tito knew him, but he did not know if it was life or death that had brought him into the presence of his injured father. It might be death—and death might mean this chill gloom with the hideous past hanging over him forever.

Now, Baldassarre's only dread was that the young limbs should escape him. He pressed his knuckles against the round throat and knelt upon the chest with all the force of his aged frame. Let death come now!

He kept his watch on the face. When the eyes went rigid again, he dared not trust them. He would never release his hold until someone came. Justice would send a witness, and then he, Baldassarre, would declare that he had killed this traitor, to whom he had once been a father. They would perhaps believe him now, and then he would be content with the struggle of justice on earth—then he would desire to die with his hold on this body, following the traitor to hell to clutch him there.

So he knelt, pressing his knuckles against the throat, refusing to trust the seeming death, until the light grew strong and he could kneel no longer. Then he sat on the body, still clutching the neck of the tunic. But the hours passed, and no witness came. No eyes descried the two human bodies lying among the tall grass by the riverside. Florence was busy with greater affairs, preparing for a deeper tragedy.

Not long after those two bodies lay in the grass, Savonarola was being tortured, crying out in agony, "I will confess!"

It was not until the sun had sunk westward that a wagon drawn by a mild grey ox arrived at the edge of the grassy margin. As the driver leaned to gather the round stones heaped nearby, ready for removal, he detected a startling object in the grass. The aged man had fallen forward, his dead clutch still gripping the garment of the other. It was impossible to separate them; indeed, it was better to place them in the wagon and carry them as they were into the great Piazza, so notice might be given to the Eight.

As the wagon entered the frequented streets, a growing crowd escorted it with its strange burden. No one recognized the bodies for a long while, for the aged face had fallen forward, half hiding the younger. But before they were moved out of sight, recognition came.

"I know that old man," Piero di Cosimo testified. "I painted his likeness once. He is the prisoner who clutched Melema on the steps of the Duomo."

"He is perhaps the same old man who appeared at supper in my gardens," said Bernardo Rucellai, one of the Eight. "I had forgotten him. I thought he had died in prison. But there is no knowing the truth now."

Who can point to the work of justice and say, "It is there"? Justice is like the Kingdom of God—it is not merely a fact without us, but a great yearning within us.

Romola drifted from the turbulence of her dreams into a profound, heavy slumber, only to rise again into a restless, busy dreaming until the final image solidified: she stretched her arms wide within the cold, flickering court of the Bargello, where the shadows of the tapers burned brighter and brighter until the darkness was utterly consumed by light. Her eyes snapped open to the pale, insistent gray of morning. Her boat lay motionless in a secluded cove; to her right stretched the unblemished, sapphire expanse of the Mediterranean, and to her left unfolded a landscape of recurring, rhythmic beauty, a scene etched into the shores of that loveliest sea.

Deep within a curve of the mountains, a swath of green land lay sheltered, draped in the soft shadows of trees leaning against rocky heights. Here and there, gleaming above the canopy, a path wound upward to a cluster of buildings that seemed to have scrambled hastily up the slope, securing a precarious perch solely to display its tall belfry as a beacon to the scattered village below. The sun, newly risen, cast oblique rays upon the western horn of this crescent-shaped bay; the rest of the valley remained bathed in dewy shadow. No sound disturbed the stillness; the waters themselves seemed to have curved inward to rest.

The golden rays touched Romola, thrilling her limbs with a gentle caress. She lay motionless, hardly watching the scene, but simply absorbing the presence of peace and beauty. In the early hours of youth, passive existence can sometimes become a form of Lethe, where the exquisite, indefinite sensation of the present creates a bliss devoid of memory or desire. As the warmth seeped into her young limbs and her eyes rested on this secluded luxuriance, the agitating past seemed to glide away like the dark specter of the Bargello, and the afternoon dreams of her girlhood returned. For a minute or two, the oblivion was untroubled; she did not even conceive that she could rest here forever, she merely felt that she rested.

Then, consciousness sharpened. She realized she was lying in a boat that had borne her over the waters all night. Instead of delivering her to death, it had served as the gently lulling cradle of a new life. Despite her evening despair, she was glad the morning had come, glad to rest in the familiar sunlight rather than in the unknown regions of death. *Could* she not rest here? No sound from Florence could reach her. Already, however, the oblivion was troubled; from behind the golden haze pierced the silhouettes of domes, towers, and walls, parted by a river and enclosed by green hills.

She rose from her reclining posture and sat up in the boat, willing herself to resist the rush of thoughts conjured by the conjecture of how far the boat had carried her. Why should she mind? This was a sheltered nook where simple villagers would not harm her. For a little while, at least, she might rest and resolve on nothing. Soon she would procure bread and milk, then nestle in the green quiet to feel a pause in her life. She turned to watch the crescent-shaped valley, seeking to reclaim the soothing sense of peace she had felt upon waking.

She had not been in this contemplative state more than a few minutes when a piercing cry cut across the stillness. It was not a brief cry, but continuous, growing more intense with every passing second. Romola felt certain it was the cry of a child in distress, unanswered. She started up, placing one foot on the side of the boat, ready to leap onto the beach, but she paused to listen. The mother must be near; the cry should soon cease. But it continued, drawing Romola irresistibly, seeming all the more pitiful for the preceding peace, until she jumped onto the sand and walked many paces before knowing which direction to take.

The cry appeared to emanate from a rough garden growth yards to her right, where she spotted a half-ruined hovel. Climbing a low, broken stone fence, she moved across patches of weedy green crops and ripe, neglected corn. The cry grew plainer, confirming her suspicion, yet even in this hurried walk, she felt an oppressive change in the air as she left the sea behind. Was there some taint lurking among the green luxuriance that had seemed such an inviting shelter? She could now see the opening to the hovel, and the cry darted through her like a physical pain. The next moment her foot was within the doorway, but the sight that met her in the sombre light arrested her with a shock of awe and horror.

On the straw-strewn floor lay three dead bodies: a tall man, a girl of about eight years old, and a young woman whose long black hair was being clutched and pulled by a living child—the child sending forth the piercing cry. Romola's experience in the haunts of death and disease made her thought and action prompt. She lifted the little living child, and while trying to soothe it against her bosom, she glanced back at the bodies to confirm they were truly dead. The strongly marked features and peculiar garb led her to conjecture they were Spanish or Portuguese Jews, perhaps put ashore and abandoned by rapacious sailors who claimed their property. Such things happened continually to Jews compelled to flee their homes by the Inquisition; the cruelty of greed thrust them from the sea, and the cruelty of superstition thrust them back to it.

"But surely," thought Romola, "I shall find some woman in the village whose mother's heart will not let her refuse to tend this helpless child—if the real mother is indeed dead."

This doubt lingered because while the man and the girl looked emaciated and showed signs of having been dead for some time, the woman seemed harder, retaining the robustness of her form. Kneeling, Romola was about to lay her hand on the heart, but as she lifted the piece of yellow woollen drapery across the bosom, she saw the purple spots marking the familiar pestilence. It struck her then that if the villagers knew of this, she might face greater difficulty in gaining their help; they might shrink from her with the child in her arms. But she had money to offer, and they would not refuse goat's milk in exchange.

She set out toward the village, her mind filled with the effort to soothe the little dark creature and wondering how she might win a woman's compassion. She could not help hoping, with a certain awe she had observed in herself, that her appearance as an unknown stranger in religious dress would inspire trust. As she crossed a breadth of cultivated ground, she noticed with wonder that patches of corn had been left to over-ripeness, untouched by the sickle, and that golden apples and dark figs lay rotting on the weedy earth. There were grassy spaces, but no cows, sheep, or goats. The stillness began to have something fearful in it to Romola; she hurried toward the thickest cluster of houses, where the most life would appeal on behalf of the helpless life she carried. She had picked up two figs, biting little pieces from the sweet pulp to still the child.

She entered between two lines of dwellings. It was time for villagers to be stirring, yet not a soul was in sight. The air became increasingly oppressive, laden with some horrible impurity. One door stood open, revealing grim emptiness. Another opened to show a man lying dead, fully clothed, his head resting athwart a spade handle, an earthenware cruse in his hand as if he had fallen suddenly.

Horror took possession of Romola. Was she in a village of the unburied dead? She wanted to listen for any faint sound, but the child cried out afresh when she ceased to feed it, filling her ears. Finally, she saw a figure crawling slowly out of a house, soon sinking back into a sitting posture against the wall. She hastened toward it; it was a young woman in fevered anguish, holding a pitcher. As Romola approached, the woman did not start; the one need was too absorbing for any other idea.

"Water! Get me water!" she moaned.

Romola stooped to take the pitcher and said gently in her ear, "You shall have water; can you point toward the well?"

The hand lifted toward the distant end of the little street, and Romola set off with all the speed she could manage while carrying the pitcher and feeding the child. The little one grew more content as the morsels of sweet pulp were repeated, ceasing to distress her with its cry, allowing Romola to pay less distracted attention to her surroundings.

The well lay twenty yards or more beyond the end of the street. As Romola approached it, her eyes were drawn to the opposite green slope below the church. High up, on a patch of grass between the trees, she had descried a cow and a couple of goats, and she tried to trace a line of path that would lead her close to that cheering sight once her errand was done. Occupied thus, she was unaware that she was very near the well, and that someone approaching it from the other side had fixed astonished eyes upon her.

Romola certainly presented a sight that could hardly be seen without causing pause and palpitation. With her gaze fixed intently on the distant slope, the long lines of her thick grey garment giving a gliding character to her rapid walk, her hair rolling backward and illuminated on the left side by the sun-rays, and the little olive baby on her right arm looking out with jet-black eyes, she might well startle the youth of fifteen accustomed to swinging the censer before a Madonna less fair and marvellous than this.

"She carries a pitcher in her hand—to fetch water for the sick. It is the Holy Mother, come to take care of the people who have the pestilence."

It was a sight of awe; he would perhaps be angry with those who fetched water for themselves alone. The youth flung down his vessel in terror, and Romola, aware now of someone near her, saw the black-and-white figure fly as if for dear life toward the slope she had just contemplated. But remembering the parched sufferer, she half-filled her pitcher quickly and hastened back.

Entering the house to look for a small cup, she saw salt meat and meal; there were no signs of want in the dwelling. With nimble movement, she seated the baby on the ground and lifted a cup of water to the sufferer, who drank eagerly and then closed her eyes, leaning her head backward as if giving herself up to the sense of relief. Presently she opened her eyes and, looking at Romola, said languidly, "Who are you?"

"I came over the sea," said Romola. "I only came this morning. Are all the people dead in these houses?"

"I think they are all ill now—all that are not dead. My father and my sister lie dead upstairs, and there is no one to bury them: and soon I shall die."

"Not so, I hope," said Romola. "I am come to take care of you. I am used to the pestilence; I am not afraid. But there must be some left who are not ill. I saw a youth running towards the mountain when I went to the well."

"I cannot tell. When the pestilence came, a great many people went away, and drove off the cows and goats. Give me more water!"

Suspecting that if she followed the direction of the youth's flight, she would find men and women still healthy and able, Romola determined to seek them out at once, hoping to win them to take care of the child so she could return to attend to others. She trusted her powers of persuasion to conquer the aid of the timorous once they knew what was to be done.

Promising the sick woman she would return, Romola lifted the dark bantling again and set off toward the slope. She felt no burden of choice now, no longing for death. She was thinking how she would go to the other sufferers as she had gone to that fevered woman.

But with the child on her arm, it was not so easy to walk up a slope, and it seemed a long while before the winding path took her near the cow and the goats. She was beginning to feel faint from heat, hunger, and thirst. As she reached a double turning, she paused to consider whether she would wait near the cow, which someone was likely to milk soon, rather than toil up to the church before resting. Raising her eyes to measure the steep distance, she saw peeping between the boughs, not more than five yards off, a broad round face watching her attentively, and lower down the black skirt of a priest's garment and a hand grasping a bucket. She stood mutely observing, and the face remained motionless. Romola had often witnessed the overpowering force of dread in cases of pestilence, and she was cautious.

Raising her voice in a tone of gentle pleading, she said, "I came over the sea. I am hungry, and so is the child. Will you not give us some milk?"

Romola had divined part of the truth, but she had not divined the preoccupation of the priest's mind which charged her words with strange significance. Only a little while ago, the young acolyte had brought word to the Padre that he had seen the Holy Mother with the Babe, fetching water for the sick: she was as tall as the cypresses, with a light about her head, looking up at the church. The parish priest had not listened with entire belief; he had been more than fifty years in the world without having any vision of the Madonna, and he thought the boy might have misinterpreted the unexpected appearance of a villager. But he had been made uneasy, and before venturing to come down and milk his cow, he had repeated many *Aves*.
