The priest's conscience tormented him; he trembled at the pestilence but also trembled at the thought of the mild-faced Mother, conscious that that Invisible Mercy might demand something more of him than prayers and "Hails." In this state of mind—unable to banish the image the boy had raised of the Mother with glory tending the sick—the priest had come down to milk his cow and had suddenly caught sight of Romola pausing at the parted way. Her pleading words, with their strange refinement of tone and accent, had a preternatural sound for him. Yet he did not quite believe

In the hushed, pale hours of winter, as Romola lay spent by her labors, her mind drifted backward through the days and forward into the misty unknown of tomorrow. The world shifted beneath her gaze, viewed from a vantage point entirely new. Since she had awakened in the boat, her experience had struck her with the force of a fresh seal pressing upon dissolving wax. She had felt untethered, motiveless, sinking into a selfish despair that life offered no sustenance, whispering the fatal wish, "I am weary of life; I wish to die." That cry had accompanied her into sleep, yet from the moment she awoke to the sound of the weeping, reflection had ceased. Unlike in Florence, where she had once rationalized her survival as a duty to alleviate sorrow, she now simply lived. An impulsive, fierce energy drove her to share the burdens of others, to answer the urgent cries of need, and to perform the work that demanded to be done, rendering the arguments for endurance unnecessary.

This transformation felt like a second baptism. In Florence, the simple bonds of humanity had been tangled with the heavy chains of marriage, statecraft, and religious devotion; when these structures failed her, the blow had severed her from life and numbed her compassion. Now, she declared, "It was a base thing in me to crave death. If all else is uncertain, this suffering I can alleviate is certain; if the glory of the cross is a phantom, then sorrow is the only truth. While strength remains in my arm, I will extend it to the fainting; while light visits my eyes, let them seek out the forsaken."

Then, the past surged forth with renewed urgency. Her labor in this green valley was concluded, and the emotions released from the people surrounding her flowed back into the deep, familiar channels of use and affection. This rare solitude, born of total separation from her accustomed life, allowed her to judge herself with a severity unknown before. The compunction inherent in a sympathetic soul, keenly aware of the suffering of others, stirred within her with growing intensity. She questioned the justice of her own judgments and actions: she had been rash, arrogant, perpetually dissatisfied that others were not virtuous enough, while she herself had betrayed the highest ideals her soul once recognized. She began to condemn her flight as cowardly self-preservation, realizing that the reasons Savonarola had given for taking her back were truer and deeper than the excuses for her second departure. How could she claim to feel the needs of strangers while ignoring the needs of those nearest to her?

Yet, a reaction against this self-reproach soon arose. The memory of her life with Tito, of the conditions that rendered their true union impossible while forcing her into a role of false duty—a role that concealed and sanctioned what her mind revolted against—told her that flight had been her only recourse. All minds, save those dulled by insensibility, must endure this recurring conflict where life's twisted conditions forbid the fulfillment of a bond. Strictly speaking, no relation can be replaced; the presence of the new does not erase the breach of the old. Life had lost its perfection; it had been maimed. Until the wounds were fully scarred, conscience would continually cast backward, casting doubtful glances.

Romola shrank with dread at the prospect of returning to Tito, yet unease gnawed at her that she had placed herself beyond his reach, fearing a day when he might suffer and require her aid. A thread of pain remained within her, echoing Fra Girolamo's words: she could not cease to be a wife. Could anything truly end for her that had once mingled with the very current of her heart's blood?

Florence, and every moment of her existence there, returned to her like a ravenous hunger. Her feelings could not wander aimlessly after the possible and the vague; their living fiber was fed by the memory of familiar things. The thought that she had severed herself from them forever became increasingly importunate during these idle hours. What if Fra Girolamo had been mistaken? What if the life of Florence was merely a web of inconsistencies? Was she, then, something superior, entitled to shake the dust from her feet and declare, "This world is not good enough for me"? If she were truly higher, she would not have so easily lost all her trust.

Her indignant grief for her godfather no longer held complete sway, and her sense of debt to Savonarola began to regain predominance. Nothing that had occurred or would occur could erase the fact that there had been a great inspiration in him, one that had awakened a new life within her. Who, in all her experience, could demand greater gratitude from her than he? Yet, his errors—could they not bring calamity?

She could not rest. She scarcely knew whether it was her strength returning with the budding leaves that made her active again, or whether it was an eager longing to draw nearer to Florence. She did not imagine herself daring to enter the city, but the desire to be close enough to learn what transpired there urged itself with a power that excluded all other purposes.

On one March morning, the people of the valley gathered to witness the departure of the Blessed Lady. Jacopo had procured a mule for her and was to accompany her over the mountains. The Padre also went with her to the nearest town, intending to assist her in finding the safest route to Pistoja. Her store of trinkets and money, untouched since arriving in the valley, was ample for her needs.

Had Romola been less drawn by the longing pulling her away, it would have been a difficult moment as she walked the village street for the final time, while the Padre and Jacopo awaited her near the well with the mule. Her steps were hindered by the wailing crowd, who knelt to kiss her hands, then clung to her skirts, kissing the grey folds of her habit and crying, "Ah, why will you go, when the good season begins and the crops will be plentiful? Why will you go?"

"Do not be sorry," said Romola. "You are well now, and I shall remember you. I must go to see if my own people need me."

"Ah, yes, if they have the pestilence!"

"Look at us again, Madonna!"

"Yes, yes, we will be good to little Benedetto!"

Finally, Romola mounted her mule, but a vigorous scream from Benedetto as he saw her turn away in this new capacity served as an excuse for the entire populace to follow, insisting that he must ride on the mule's neck to the foot of the slope. The parting must eventually come, but as Romola turned repeatedly before passing out of sight, she saw the little flock lingering to catch the last wave of her hand.

April 14th marked Romola's return to the walls of Florence, a city that had swallowed her hopes and now demanded her presence. She had not found peace in Pistoia, where rumors of the Trial by Fire clashed like storm clouds, nor in Prato, where dread had held her in a paralyzed state. It was only the encounter with an Augustinian monk from San Spirito—a former confessor to her father—that broke her hesitation. He carried the weight of the city's collective grief, having walked beside the wagon bearing the terrible burden into the Piazza. From him, she learned the brutal finality of her husband's fate: Tito had leaped from the mob's assault into the Arno, only to be slain on the riverbank by an old enemy. Yet, the monk spoke of Savonarola with the bitter prejudice of the Black Friars, labeling the friar a deceiver, a man who had confessed his duplicity under the lash.

Romola absorbed these truths not as news, but as the heavy stones of a tomb she had already entered. That evening, she sat in a silent agony amidst the cacophony of Monna Brigida's house. The old woman, having briefly succumbed to vanity with false hair, now reverted to her piety, declaring she would rather be grey than lose her "dear child." But Romola remained unmoved by Brigida's gossipy details; the shadow of Tito's death and Savonarola's fall cast such a long darkness that minor circumstances dissolved into insignificance. Her mind, burdened by awe-stricken grief, focused on two desperate imperatives: to see Savonarola and to locate Tessa and the children.

"Tell me, cousin," Romola interrupted Brigida's rambling plans for their shared future, "has anything been seen or said of a young woman with two little children?"

Brigida recoiled, her hands rising in horror. "Cristo! No! Was he so bad as that?" she cried, her voice trembling. "Ah, then, that explains your flight. I thought you strange, flighty... 'She's got her father's temper,' I said to myself. But if you had told me the truth..."

"No, dear cousin," Romola insisted, her tone urgent. "Do not speak thus. I must find that woman and her children. They are helpless. That is my first duty."

"If that is being a Piagnone," Brigida muttered, lowering her voice, "I have been counting peas instead of prayers. Fra Girolamo says widows should not remarry; step inside the door and it is sin, but come down the chimney and you are welcome. Two children—Santiddio!"

"Cousin," Romola pleaded gently, "she has done no conscious wrong. She is ignorant. I will tell you later."

The next morning, Romola sought the house beyond San Ambrogio where she had last seen Tessa. It was empty. Old Lisa had departed a week prior with her belongings, leaving no trace of the young woman. Romola feared the worst: that Tito had sent Tessa away to a hiding place, perhaps intending to join her there. The thought was a knife twist; if Tessa were outside Florence, the chances of reunion were slim, and the image of the child waiting in helpless misery at a roadside haunted her. With no leads and a noble reserve preventing her from broadcasting Tessa's connection to Tito, days passed in anxious stillness. Every pair of round brown legs or short figure in peasant dress made her heart race.

She did not view this search as heroic charity, but as a sacred obligation. She needed to care for them, to clasp the children and make them love her, a sweet redemption for her own sorrows. Though she possessed a claim to Tito's property, she distrusted the purity of that wealth and resolved to surrender it to the State, keeping only enough to buy her father's library for Tessa's support. Brigida, however, clamored that Romola must stay with her, insisting that if Romola wished to raise others' children, she must do so alongside her own household.

Driven by a superstition of hope, Romola turned her gaze to the gate of San Gallo. She knew Tito had arranged horses there to escape Florence. Clad in her long widow's veil, she walked the Via San Gallo, scanning the crowd until she spotted Bratti haggling with a customer. A sudden movement caught her eye: something glinting from the corner of his basket. Her heart leaped.

"Bratti, my friend," she asked abruptly, "where did you get that necklace?"

"Your servant, madonna," Bratti replied, his face unreadable. "It is worth money, but my heart is too tender to trade it freely. I have pledged it."

"From a little woman named Tessa, is it not true?"

"Ah! If you know her," Bratti said, his eyes softening, "and would redeem it for her, you would be doing a great charity. She wept when she parted with it, as if running into a brook. I will charge you only a florin; I dislike to be hard-hearted."

Romola handed over the coin and retrieved the necklace, her fingers trembling with joy. "Where is she?"

"Outside the gate, at the other end of the Borgo, at old Sibilla Manetti's. Anybody knows the house."

With wings of purpose, Romola hastened to the address. Inside, the young woman and the children had been there for weeks, destitute, paying a poor widow merely with their clothes. But when Romola opened the door, Tessa did not turn. Seated on a low bed, she stared blankly at her children, who played in a corner—Lillo roaring at Ninna, then peering out with wide, fearful eyes. Expectation had long died in Tessa's heart.

Romola stepped forward, removing her veil and holding the necklace aloft. In the pure voice that once cheered her father, she called out, "Tessa!"

The woman sprang to her feet, her eyes widening in shock. "See," Romola said, fastening the beads around Tessa's neck. "God has sent me to you again."

Tessa screamed, a sound of pure release, and clung to the arms holding the necklace, sobbing uncontrollably. The children rushed from their corner, grabbing their mother's skirts and looking up at the stranger with wide, trusting eyes.

That afternoon, they returned to Monna Brigida's house in the Borgo degli Albizzi. Romola gently explained to Tessa that Naldo could never return—not out of cruelty, but because he was dead. "Be comforted, my Tessa," Romola assured her. "I am here to care for you always. And we have found Lillo and Ninna."

Brigida's face twisted in a struggle between reverence for Romola and the urge to assert her authority. *A thousand years,* she thought, *until I tell this simple country girl who Romola truly is.* Yet, before retiring, she confessed to Romola that it would be shameful not to care for such cherubs, though she requested Romola yield some control over their feeding, citing her own tragic experience with twin infants who had died at birth.

The spring had nearly faded when Romola returned home with Tessa and the children, her heart heavy with a fresh, crushing weight. The publication of Fra Girolamo's trial—the confessions extracted by the sixteen Florentine citizens commissioned to interrogate him—had ignited a storm of public suspicion and rage. The Signoria, alarmed by the uproar, ordered the document recalled, yet copies had slipped into the hands of eager readers, and a second, unauthorized edition circulated swiftly through the city.

Romola, having begun to despair of ever seeing the friar again, devoured the printed evidence. She sought a clearer light than the contradictory assertions swirling through the mouths of partisans and enemies alike. To the devout followers of Savonarola, his lack of constancy under torture and his retraction of prophetic claims caused a consternation too deep to be easily healed. They clung to the hope that any words contradicting their faith were not spoken by the prophet but forged by Ser Ceccone, the notary of evil repute who had compiled the digest. Yet, glaring flaws undermined the document's credibility. Was it not true that half the panel of sixteen examiners were bitter enemies of the prophet? Was not Dolfo Spini, a notorious figure, among the newly elected Eight, seemingly placed there to load the dice against a man whose ruin was already decided by the ruling party? It appeared to be a slow, formalized murder within the Old Palace. The Signoria had resolved to strike a bargain with the Pope and the Duke of Milan by extinguishing a man who was a nuisance to vicious citizens, greedy foreign tyrants, and a corrupt clergy alike. The Frate was doomed beforehand; the only debate remaining was whether the Republic should deliver him alive to the Pope in exchange for taxing ecclesiastical property, or if the Pope would grant the Republic the dignity of hanging and burning its own prophet on its own piazza.

Under such circumstances, who could give full credit to this so-called confession? If the Frate had denied his prophetic gift, it had been wrenched from him by the agony of torture—an agony that, in his sensitive frame, must quickly induce raving. What if the wicked examiners claimed he suffered only three times on a single day, and confessed only when free from bodily coercion? Could that be believed? He had been tortured far more; he had been broken in proportion to the distress his confessions caused in the hearts of those who loved him.

Other friends of Savonarola, less ardent in their partisanship, doubted the confession's genuineness only insofar as it had been colored by the notary's transpositions. They argued indignantly that nothing warranted a death sentence or even grave punishment. To any impartial observer, it was clear that if this examination were the sole evidence, the Frate died not for a crime, but because he had become inconvenient to the Pope, to the rapacious Italian states seeking to dismember Tuscany, and to unworthy citizens chasing private ambition against the common weal. No shadow of political crime had been proved against him. No stain marred his private conduct. Even his fellow monks, including a former secretary who possessed more culture than his companions and a tendency to critique Fra Girolamo's rule as Prior, testified to an unimpeachable purity and consistency that had commanded their veneration, even after the shock of his retraction. The Pope himself could raise no charge of heresy except for disobedience to a mandate and disregard of excommunication. While difficult to justify by argument, this breach of discipline sparked a moral insurgence in the minds of grave men, blurring the line between the Church and its men and softening the scandal of disobedience.

Men of ordinary morality and public spirit felt that the triumph of the Frate's enemies was truly the triumph of gross license. Shrewd Florentines like Soderini and Piero Guicciardini likely wore an angry smile at a severity that dispensed with all law to hang and burn a man whose zeal had warped the strictness of his veracity. They might have noted that if the Frate had mixed deeper fraud with a zeal less inconvenient to high personages, that fraud would have served as excellent oil for ecclesiastical and political wheels.

Yet, even these shrewd men were forced to admit that, however clumsily the Florentine government pretended to a judicial warrant for what was actually a predetermined act of policy, the Pope's measures against Savonarola were necessary acts of self-defense. To fail to rid himself of a man stirring the Powers of Europe to summon a General Council and depose him would have been adding ineptitude to iniquity. There was no denying that toward Alexander the Sixth, Savonarola was a rebel, and a dangerous one. Florence had heard him say, and understood, that he would not obey the devil. It was inevitably a life-and-death struggle between the Frate and the Pope; but it was less inevitable that Florence should make itself the Pope's executioner.

Romola's ears were filled with the suggestions of a faith still ardent under its wounds and the judgments of worldly discernment operating on a moderate standard of human nature. She could find satisfaction in neither. Her long meditations over the printed document were haunted by painful observations, registered consciously or unconsciously through years of discipleship, whispering a presentiment that Savonarola's retraction was not merely a spasmodic effort to escape torture. On the other hand, her soul cried out for an explanation that would allow her to believe the main striving of his life had remained pure and grand. The recent memory of selfish discontent, which had swept over her like a blighting wind alongside her loss of trust in this incarnation of the highest motives, had produced a reaction familiar to many: a faith springing from the very depths of despair. It was impossible, she told herself, that the negative, disbelieving thoughts which had made her soul arid could be founded in the truth of things. Impossible that it had not been a living spirit, not a hollow pretence, which once breathed in the Frate's words and kindled a new life in her. Whatever falsehood existed in him, it had been a fall, not a purpose; a gradual entanglement in which he struggled, not a contrivance encouraged by success.

Reading the printed confessions, she saw many sentences bearing the stamp of bungling fabrication: the emphasis and repetition of self-accusation used only by low hypocrites. Yet, the fact that these sentences stood in striking opposition to Savonarola's character and the general tone of the document strengthened her impression that the rest of the text represented what had truly fallen from his lips. Hardly a word was dishonorable to him except regarding his prophetic annunciations. He was unvarying in stating the ends he pursued for Florence, the Church, and the world. Apart from the mixture of falsity in his claim to special inspiration, there was no admission of using unworthy means. Even here, without expurgating the notary's malign phrases, Fra Girolamo shone as a man who sought his own glory indeed, but sought it by laboring for the highest end—the moral welfare of men—not through vague exhortations, but by striving to turn beliefs into energies working in the details of life.

"Everything that I have done," read one memorable passage, perhaps bearing erasures and interpolations, "I have done with the design of being for ever famous in the present and in future ages; and that I might win credit in Florence; and that nothing of great import should be done without my sanction. And when I had thus established my position in Florence, I had it in my mind to do great things in Italy and beyond Italy, by means of those chief personages with whom I had contracted friendship and consulted on high matters, such as this of the General Council. And in proportion as my first efforts succeeded, I should have adopted further measures. Above all, when the General Council had once been brought about, I intended to rouse the princes of Christendom, and especially those beyond the borders of Italy, to subdue the infidels. It was not much in my thoughts to get myself made a Cardinal or Pope, for when I should have achieved the work I had in view, I should, without being Pope, have been the first man in the world in the authority I should have possessed, and the reverence that would have been paid me. If I had been made Pope, I would not have refused the office: but it seemed to me that to be the head of that work was a greater thing than to be Pope, because a man without virtue may be Pope; but *such a work as I contemplated demanded a man of excellent virtues*."

That blending of ambition with belief in the supremacy of goodness held no new tone for Romola, who had heard it ring through the Duomo. It was the habit of Savonarola's mind to conceive great things and feel himself the man to accomplish them. Iniquity should be brought low; the cause of justice, purity, and love should triumph, and it should triumph by his voice, his work, and his blood. In moments of ecstatic contemplation, doubtless, the sense of self melted into the sense of the Unspeakable, containing the elements of genuine self-abasement; but in the presence of his fellow-men for whom he acted, pre-eminence seemed a necessary condition of his life.

Perhaps this confession, even describing a doubleness that was conscious and deliberate, implied no more than the wavering of belief concerning one's own impressions and motives to which most human beings are liable under a marked change of external conditions. In a life so tumultuously mixed as the Frate's, what possibility was opened for a change of self-judgment? Instead of eyes that venerated and knees that knelt, instead of a great work nearing accomplishment and prosperity stamping the agent as a chosen instrument, there came the hooting and spitting of the crowd, the hard faces of enemies acting as judges, and then the horrible torture, accompanied by the irrepressible cry, "It is true, what you would have me say: let me go: do not torture me again: yes, yes, I am guilty. O God! Thy stroke has reached me!"

As Romola thought of the anguish following the confession—whether conscience retracted or confirmed the self-taxing words in the subsequent solitude of the prison—that anguish pressed upon her own heart, urging slow, bitter tears. Every vulgar, self-ignorant person in Florence glibly pronounced on the man's demerits, while *he* knew a depth of sorrow known only to the soul that has loved and sought the most perfect thing, and beholds itself fallen.

She had not yet seen—what she would see later—the evidence of the Frate's mental state after laying his mouth in the dust. As days passed, reports of new, unpublished examinations eliciting no change in his confessions ceased. Savonarola was left alone in his prison, allowed pen and ink for a while so that, if he wished, he might use his poor, bruised, and strained right arm to write. He wrote; but what he wrote was no vindication of innocence, no protest against the proceedings used toward him. It was a continued colloquy with that divine purity with which he sought complete reunion; an outpouring of self-abasement; one long cry for inward renovation. There were no lingering echoes of the old vehement self-assertion, "Look at my work, for it is good, and those who set their faces against it are the children of the devil!" The Voice of Sadness told him, "God placed thee in the midst of the people even as if thou hadst been one of the excellent. In this way thou hast taught others, and hast failed to learn thyself. Thou hast cured others: and thou thyself hast been still diseased. Thy heart was lifted up at the beauty of thy own deeds, and through this thou hast lost thy wisdom and art become, and shalt be to all eternity, nothing... After so many benefits with which God has honoured thee, thou art fallen into the depths of the sea; and after so many gifts bestowed on thee, thou, by thy pride and vainglory, hast scandalised all the world." And when Hope spoke and argued that divine love had not forsaken him, it said nothing now of a great work to be done, but only, "Thou art not forsaken, else why is thy heart bowed in penitence? That too is a gift."

There was no jot of worthy evidence that from the time of his imprisonment to the supreme moment, Savonarola thought or spoke of himself as a martyr. The idea of martyrdom had been to him a passion dividing the dream of the future with the triumph of beholding his work achieved. And now, in place of both, had come a resignation which he called by no glorifying name.

*But therefore he may the more fitly be called a martyr by his fellow-men to all time.* For power rose against him not because of his sins, but because of his greatness—not because he sought to deceive the world, but because he sought to make it noble. And through that greatness he endured a double agony: not only the reviling, the torture, and the death-throe, but the agony of sinking from the vision of glorious achievement into that deep shadow where he could only say, "I count as nothing: darkness encompasses me: yet the light I saw was the true light."

The words echoed in Romola's mind, not as a whisper, but as a collective cry rising from the lips of many. They were the final utterances of Savonarola, spoken as he signed his confession before the brethren of San Marco: "Pray for me, for God has withdrawn from me the spirit of prophecy." These words struck her with a sudden, paralyzing doubt, casting a shadow over the memory of his earlier composure. Her uncertainty deepened when more pitiful tidings reached her ears.

May nineteenth had arrived, bringing with it the bright sunshine that marked the entry of the two Papal Commissaries into Florence. They came charged with concluding Savonarola's trial, welcomed by the populace with acclamations demanding the friar's death. The common cry was now a roar of vengeance: "It is the Frate's deception that has brought all our misfortunes; let him be burned, and all shall be right."

The following day, the records confirm, the tortured frame of the friar was subjected to fresh agony. At the mere sight of the horrific instruments, Savonarola, convulsed with agitation, fell to his knees. In brief, passionate words, he retracted his confession, declaring that he had lied in denying his prophetic gift and that if he suffered, it would be for the truth—"The things that I have spoken, I had them from God."

Yet the torture continued. When questioned under the lash why he had spoken those retracting words, the answer came, broken and desperate: "I said it that I might seem good; tear me no more, I will tell you the truth."

Florentine assessors witnessed this second retraction, and the news spread quickly through the city, filling Romola with dismayed uncertainty. Then, a thought flashed across her mind: *There will come a moment when he may speak.* A moment when the dread of falsehood would be the only thing left, when he stood before death itself, lifted above the mob, looking upon them for the last time. No one could hinder him from speaking a final, decisive word. *I will be there.*

Three days later, on May twenty-third, 1498, a long, narrow platform stretched across the great piazza, leading from the Palazzo Vecchio toward the Tetta de' Pisani. Unlike previous preparations, there was no grove of fuel scattered about; instead, a single great heap of combustible material stood on the circular area at the platform's end. Rising above this pyre was a gibbet with three halters. Though one arm had been truncated to avoid the appearance of a cross, the structure still bore a cruciform shape that made some observers deeply uneasy.

On the marble terrace of the Palazzo stood three tribunals. One, near the door, was reserved for the Bishop to perform the ceremony of degradation upon Fra Girolamo and his two brethren, who were to suffer as accomplices. Another was set aside for the Papal Commissaries to pronounce them heretics and schismatics, handing them over to the secular arm. The third, positioned close to the Marzocco statue at the corner where the platform began, was for the Gonfaloniere and the Eight, who would pronounce the sentence of death.

Once again, the Piazza was thronged with expectant faces, awaiting the great fire. For the majority of the crowd pressing around the gibbet, the anticipation was fueled by ferocious hatred or a morbid curiosity to witness a barbarous spectacle. Yet, on the wide pavement, on the rooftops, and at the windows, others remained. Amidst their bitter grief and their endurance of insults hurled at the hypocritical Piagnoni, they harbored a lingering hope. Even at this eleventh hour, they prayed that God might interpose, manifesting their beloved prophet as His servant. And there were still more, like Romola, who trembled with eagerness for that final moment when Savonarola might declare, "O people, I was innocent of deceit."

Romola stood at a window on the north side of the Piazza, far removed from the marble terrace where the tribunals stood. Near her, also gazing out with painful doubt at the man who had once commanded her reverence, was a young Florentine of twenty-two named Jacopo Nardi. Later, he would earn honor as one of the few to write about Fra Girolamo with a simple desire for veracity. Seeing the struggle in Romola's face—the shuddering horror of the scene warring with her yearning to witness the end—Jacopo had gently said, "Madonna, there is no need for you to look at these cruel things. I will tell you when he comes out of the Palazzo. Trust to me; I know what you would see."

Romola covered her face, but the hootings that seemed to make the hideous scene visible could not be shut out. Finally, her arm was touched, and she heard the whispered words, "He comes." She looked toward the Palace and saw Savonarola led out in his Dominican garb. She watched him stand before the Bishop, stripped of the black mantle, the white scapulary, and the long white tunic, until he stood in a close woollen under-tunic that revealed no sacred office, no rank. He had been degraded, cut off from the Church Militant.

The baser elements of the multitude delighted in degradations, understanding them as the ultimate satire. A fresh hoot of triumph rose as the three degraded brethren passed on to the tribunal of the Papal Commissaries, who pronounced them schismatics and heretics. Could the prophet not now be seen as a schismatic and a heretic? It was easy to believe in the damnable state of a man standing stripped and humiliated.

They passed the third tribunal, that of the Florentine officials who would pronounce the sentence. Among them, even from her distance, Romola discerned the odious figure of Dolfo Spini, clad in the grave black lucco as one of the Eight. The three figures, in their close white raiment, trod their way along the platform amidst yells and grating tones of insult.

"Cover your eyes, Madonna," Jacopo Nardi urged. "Fra Girolamo will be the last."

It was not long before she had to uncover them again. Savonarola was there. He was no longer far off; he had mounted the steps, and she could see him looking round upon the multitude.

But in that same moment, expectation died. She saw only what he saw: torches waving to kindle the fuel beneath his dead body, faces glaring with a light far worse than any before. She heard only what he heard: gross jests, taunts, and curses. The moment had passed. Her face was covered again, and she knew only that Savonarola's voice had passed into eternal silence.

***

On the evening of May twenty-second, 1509, five persons whose histories we have followed sat in a handsome upper room opening onto a loggia. From its right-hand corner, the view extended along the Borgo Pinti, over the city gate, and toward Fiesole and the solemn heights beyond.

At one end of the room, an archway led into a narrow inner recess where light fell from above upon a small altar draped in fair white linen. Over the altar hung a picture, discernible only as the small full-length portrait of a Dominican Brother, shaded from the overhead light by overhanging branches and wreaths of flowers. Fresh tapers stood unlit before it. The decoration seemed incomplete; part of the floor was strewn with a confusion of flowers and green boughs. Seated among them was a delicate, blue-eyed girl of thirteen, tossing her long, light-brown hair from her eyes as she selected blooms for the wreaths she wove. Occasionally, she looked up at her mother's work, offering instructions with a little air of superiority.

The mother, Tessa, was not particularly clever at weaving flowers or any other craft. Her fingers had not grown more adroit with the years; indeed, they had become much fatter. She worked slowly, turning her head frequently and asking Ninna's opinion with much deference, for Tessa never ceased to be astonished at the wisdom of her children. She still wore her contadina gown, only broader than the old one, with a silver pin in her rough, curly brown hair and the memorable necklace with its red cord resting against her bosom. Her rounded face wore a look of childish contentment that was even more perfect than in her youth; everyone was so good in the world, Tessa thought. Even Monna Brigida never found fault with her now, doing little else than sleeping—a practice Tessa found amiable and liked for herself.

Monna Brigida slept at that moment in a straight-backed armchair a couple of yards away. Her hair, parting backward under her black hood, possessed a soft whiteness unlike snow, simply the lovely whiteness of aged hair. Her chin had sunk upon her bosom, her hands resting on the elbow of the chair. She had not been weaving or working; she had only been looking on, as she usually did, before falling asleep.

The other two figures sat farther off at the wide doorway opening onto the loggia. Lillo sat on the ground with his back against the angle of the door-post, his long legs stretched out. He held a large book open on his knee, occasionally making a dash with his hand at an inquisitive fly with an air of interest stronger than that excited by the finely-printed copy of Petrarch kept open at one place, as if he were learning something by heart.

Romola sat nearly opposite Lillo, yet she observed him not. Her hands were crossed on her lap, her eyes fixed absently on the distant mountains. She was evidently unconscious of anything around her. An eager life had left its marks upon her: the finely-molded cheek had sunk a little, the golden crown of her hair less massive. Yet there was a placidity in Romola's face that had never belonged to it in her youth. It is but once that we can know our worst sorrows, and Romola had known them while life was new.

Absorbed thus, she was not at first aware that Lillo had ceased looking at his book and was watching her with a slightly impatient air, signaling his desire to talk but his uncertainty whether she wished it. Persevering looks eventually made themselves felt. Romola turned her eyes from the distance and met Lillo's impatient dark gaze with a brighter, brighter smile. He shuffled along the floor, keeping the book on his lap, until he got close to her and lodged his chin on her knee.

"What is it, Lillo?" Romola asked, pulling his hair back from his brow. Lillo was a handsome lad, though his features were turning out more massive and less regular than his father's. The blood of the Tuscan peasant ran in his veins.

"Mamma. Romola, what am I to be?" he asked, well contented that there was a prospect of talking until it would be too late to recite "Spirto gentil."

"What should you like to be, Lillo? You might be a scholar. My father was a scholar, you know, and taught me a great deal. That is the reason why I can teach you."

"Yes," said Lillo, hesitating. "But he is old and blind in the picture. Did he get a great deal of glory?"

"Not much, Lillo. The world was not always very kind to him, and he saw meaner men than himself put into higher places because they could flatter and say what was false. And then his dear son thought it right to leave him and become a monk; and after that, my father, being blind and lonely, felt unable to do the things that would have made his learning of greater use to men, so that he might still have lived in his works after he was in his grave."

"I should not like that sort of life," said Lillo. "I should like to be something that would make me a great man, and very happy besides—something that would not hinder me from having a good deal of pleasure."

"That is not easy, my Lillo. It is only a poor sort of happiness that could ever come by caring very much about our own narrow pleasures. We can only have the highest happiness, such as goes along with being a great man, by having wide thoughts and much feeling for the rest of the world as well as ourselves. And this sort of happiness often brings so much pain with it that we can only tell it from pain by its being what we would choose before everything else, because our souls see it is good. There are so many things wrong and difficult in the world that no man can be great—he can hardly keep himself from wickedness—unless he gives up thinking much about pleasure or rewards and gets strength to endure what is hard and painful. My father had the greatness that belongs to integrity; he chose poverty and obscurity rather than falsehood. And there was Fra Girolamo—you know why I keep to-morrow sacred: *he* had the greatness which belongs to a life spent in struggling against powerful wrong and in trying to raise men to the highest deeds they are capable of. And so, my Lillo, if you mean to act nobly and seek to know the best things God has put within reach of men, you must learn to fix your mind on that end and not on what will happen to you because of it. And remember, if you were to choose something lower and make it the rule of your life to seek your own pleasure and escape from what is disagreeable, calamity might come just the same; and it would be calamity falling on a base mind, which is the one form of sorrow that has no balm in it, and that may well make a man say, 'It would have been better for me if I had never been born.' I will tell you something, Lillo."

Romola paused for a moment. She had taken Lillo's cheeks between her hands, and his young eyes were meeting hers.

"There was a man to whom I was very near, so that I could see a great deal of his life, who made almost everyone fond of him, for he was young, clever, and beautiful, and his manners to all were gentle and kind. I believe, when I first knew him, he never thought of anything cruel or base. But because he tried to slip away
